# Trackwall architecture

Status: a proposal for discussion, 2026-10-23. The earlier working name "Pitwall" was changed to "Trackwall" (ADR-12).

This document describes how the app is split into components, where the boundaries between modules run, which protocols the components speak, what the data model is, what the key flows look like and which risks come with Windows as the main platform. The product vision (goal, modules, order of work) is an assumption of this document, not its content. Decisions worth being able to reverse later are collected at the end as ADRs.

## 1. Principles that order the rest

1. **The daemon is the single source of truth.** The UI, the MCP server and the `ws` CLI are three clients of the same service facade in the daemon. The dashboard and the agent never disagree about state, because they ask the same object.
2. **The daemon knows identifiers, not commands.** Every action from outside (MCP, CLI, UI) points to a repo, service, task or mise task by identifier. The daemon builds the command from configuration. The only exception is the interactive terminal in the UI.
3. **Process state is observed, not declared.** "Stopped" means the whole process group is dead and the daemon has checked it. "Running" means the health probe answered.
4. **Facts deterministically, the LLM only adds.** The repo card and the graph come from build and configuration files. A language model adds a description and suggestions, it is never the source of a technical field.
5. **Secrets do not pass through the agent.** The daemon injects them into the process environment and masks them in logs. MCP tools answer "set / missing", never with the value.
6. **Nothing company-specific in the code.** Addresses, repo names, paths and company-specific detection rules live in the local workspace, outside the project repository.
7. **Windows first.** Every mechanism (processes, PTY, paths, signals) is designed from the Windows side, and macOS gets the counterpart. The reverse order ends in a "slightly different on Windows" layer.

## 2. Overview

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  desktop (Tauri)                                                 │
│  WebView window ─ tray ─ autostart ─ sidecar supervision ─ update│
│      │ loads                                   │ starts          │
│      ▼                                         ▼                 │
│  ui (React + Tailwind + shadcn/ui)        daemon (TS, 1 binary)  │
│  panels: Tasks, Services, Logs,           ┌────────────────────┐ │
│  Task changes, Graph, Secrets, Audit      │ service facade     │ │
│  3D scene: @trackwall/scene (Pawn)        │  workspace  cards  │ │
│      │ HTTP + WebSocket (loopback, token)  │  graph      tasks  │ │
│      └────────────────────────────────────▶│  procs      leases │ │
│                                            │  logs       secrets│ │
│   CLI `ws` ──────── HTTP (loopback) ──────▶│  agents     audit  │ │
│                                            └───┬──────────┬─────┘ │
│   Copilot CLI / other CLIs ─ MCP (HTTP or stdio) ─┘          │     │
│                                                   SQLite + JSONL   │
│                                                   ~/.trackwall/    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Four kinds of processes on the user's machine:

| Process | Role | Alive when the window is closed? |
|---|---|---|
| `trackwall.exe` (Tauri) | window, tray, daemon supervision, update | yes, in the tray |
| `trackwall-daemon.exe` (sidecar) | all the logic: workspace, processes, logs, tasks, MCP | yes |
| service processes | `mise run dev` in a process group per service | yes |
| agent sessions | the agent CLI in a PTY owned by the daemon | yes |

The daemon can also run without Tauri (`trackwall-daemon --ui`), which gives the "local page in the browser" mode from the first stage of work. Tauri adds nothing to the logic, only to packaging and system integration.

## 3. Monorepo

```
trackwall/
  package.json            pnpm workspaces, root scripts
  pnpm-workspace.yaml
  mise.toml               tool versions for the project itself (node/bun, pnpm, rust) and the setup, dev, portable tasks
  packages/
    protocol/             event types, DTOs, JSON API schema (zod); zero runtime dependencies
    daemon/               the daemon binary; in src/ the api/ and mcp/ transports, the §4 modules in modules/, system and Bun APIs
                          in platform/, the rest of the infrastructure (discovery, state directory, errors) in infra/ (docs/development.md)
    ui/                   React, Vite; builds static files served by the daemon or Tauri; in src/ views
                          in views/<view identifier>/, shared components in components/, logic and hooks in lib/
    scene/                the 3D dashboard on Pawn: HomeModel snapshot -> entities (KeyedEntities), Orbit layout, models
    cli/                  `ws` (a thin HTTP client of the daemon); code shared with the daemon through protocol
  apps/
    desktop/              Tauri (Rust shell), tauri.conf, icons, the daemon sidecar binary
    docs/                 the feature documentation site (VitePress, ADR-56): one page per feature, published on GitHub Pages
  docs/
    architecture.md       this document
    development.md        repo layout, running from source, portable build, icons, recording sessions
    release.md            release process and installation
    release-notes/        release notes per version (v<version>.md), the GitHub Release description
    adr/                  architectural decisions (once they start piling up)
  .github/workflows/
    ci.yml                on PRs and on main (§10): lint, rustfmt, typecheck, test on Linux, audit; the E2E tests of the UI on all three systems (ADR-71); test, build, clippy, cargo test, smoke test, portable on Windows + macOS; the aggregate `CI ok` check
    release.yml           release from a vX.Y.Z tag (docs/release.md)
    pages.yml             publishes apps/docs on GitHub Pages after a push to main that changes it (ADR-56)
    contract.yml          the contract tests with the real Copilot CLI on Linux, Windows and macOS, called by ci.yml and nightly.yml (ADR-67)
    nightly.yml           every night: contract.yml on the newest Copilot CLI, an issue when it fails (ADR-67)
  .github/actions/setup/  shared job setup: pnpm, Node, Bun, pnpm install
  .github/dependabot.yml  weekly updates of actions, npm and cargo
  .github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/ issue forms (bug.yml, feature.yml), in English like the product (ADR-32)
```

`packages/protocol` is the only dependency shared by the daemon, the UI, the scene and the CLI (the documentation site takes from it only its own address, `TRACKWALL_DOCS_URL`, which agents get too). Thanks to that the UI imports nothing from the daemon (you cannot accidentally pull `node:child_process` into the WebView bundle), and the zod schemas are both input validation in the daemon and types in the UI.

## 4. Daemon: modules and boundaries

The daemon is a single process, but inside it is split into modules with an explicitly declared interface. Every module exposes a service class, and the facade (`Facade`) is the set of these services (plus the `openLink` function, which opens a link from a README in the system, without state and without a module, ADR-35) handed to the three transports (HTTP, MCP, events). A module does not import another module directly, only through the facade, so the dependency graph between modules is visible in one file.

In code: a module is the directory `packages/daemon/src/modules/<module>/`, the facade is `packages/daemon/src/facade.ts`. A module that needs another one declares an interface with what it uses on its own side (for example `WorkspaceVcs`), and `createFacade` gives it the instance of that module. Biome guards both sides: a module file does not import from a neighbouring module directory (from outside only the infrastructure files `src/infra/errors.ts`, `src/infra/atomic-write.ts`, `src/infra/map-limit.ts`, `src/infra/loopback.ts` (HTTP requests on loopback, for `services`, ADR-45), `src/infra/env-key.ts` (the spelling of an environment variable on Windows, for `agents` and `mise`, ADR-59), `src/infra/path-inside.ts` (path containment, for `agents`, ADR-59), `src/infra/xml.ts` (the small XML parser, for `cards` and `test-results`, ADR-60), `src/infra/glob.ts` (glob patterns without regular expressions, for `cards` and `workspace`, ADR-66), `src/infra/wait-limit.ts` (a wait with a time limit and cancellation, for `mise` and `verification`, ADR-64), `src/infra/file-key.ts` (whether a file changed between two `stat` calls, for `vcs` and `local-config`, ADR-86), `src/infra/regular-file.ts` (a regular file read without following a link, for `cards` and `local-config`, ADR-86) and the `src/platform/` directory, listed in `biome.json`, so a module is a flat directory), and code outside the modules (transports, startup) reaches the modules only through `facade.ts`.

| Module | Responsible for | Depends on | Persistent state |
|---|---|---|---|
| `workspace` | repos from the projects folder, cloning into it, sync, state per repo, browsing contents, code search across many repos, the repo watch (HEAD and refs of every repo compared every second, ADR-63), `ws sync` | `vcs` (`batch` composes sync with mise steps on top of them, §4.8) | `workspace.yaml` (ADR-13, ADR-18), `repos` table |
| `vcs` | adapters for git (local), GitHub and GitLab (through `gh` and `glab`: repo list, clone, and the merge request from a task branch with its pipeline and the address of the new merge request form, ADR-77); the push of a task branch (ADR-77); task worktrees (a new branch with a worktree, changes against the base commit, removal, ADR-31; the ignored files the repo root's `.worktreeinclude` names, copied into a new worktree, ADR-61); `git show` without a checkout; status, log, tree, files and `git grep` from HEAD; the HEAD and refs state from the git directory's files (ADR-63); for local config (ADR-86) which files a repo tracks or ignores, the lines it adds to a repo's `info/exclude`, and the commits and push of the local config store | – | – |
| `mise` | the list of a repo's tasks, running tasks by name in the repo root or in a task worktree, and `mise install` (§4.7); later generating `mise.local.toml` and detecting the stack | `procs`, `workspace` (repo root by identifier), `agents` (a task's worktree and whether its mise config is the trusted one, ADR-59; that the user trusted a repo root's config, ADR-92) | files in the repo |
| `batch` | the Batch command: sync, `mise install`, the `setup` and `build` tasks on selected repos (§4.8) | `workspace` (repo root, repo sync), `mise` (runs awaited until they end) | – (in memory) |
| `test-results` | the results of the latest test runs in a repo root or a task's worktree, from the JUnit XML reports the build tools left there (§4.9, ADR-60) | `workspace` (repo root by identifier), `agents` (a task's worktree), `logs` (masking) | – (read from the reports on every request) |
| `verification` | the verification of a task: the mise task `test` run in the task's worktree and its verdict from the exit code, the test reports the run wrote and its log errors (§4.10, ADR-64) | `agents` (a task's repo, whether it still exists), `mise` (the run and its end), `test-results` (reports since the run's start), `logs` (the run's errors); it hands every verdict to `agents` (`onVerdict`, ADR-68) | – (in memory, the last verification of every task) |
| `day-start` | "Start my day": a sync of every repo awaited to its end, the day report against the last day start (new commits on each repo's remote default branch, repos the sync did not update, open tasks and how far behind they are), and a new supervisor with a first message that asks for a briefing (§4.11, ADR-70) | `workspace` (repos, the awaited sync, `dayStart` from `workspace.yaml`), `vcs` (the remote default branch and the commits between two commits), `agents` (tasks and sessions, a new supervisor with a first message) | `day-start.json` in the state directory (the last report and the commit of each repo's remote default branch then, by repo root) |
| `merge-requests` | a finished task's way to a merge request: the task branch against the main remote, its push at the user's request, the repo host's new merge request form opened with the title and description the user approved, and the merge request from the task branch with its pipeline (§4.12, ADR-77) | `agents` (a task, its commits and uncommitted files), `vcs` (the main remote, the branch against it and its push, the repo hosts' merge requests and form), `workspace` (the repo hosts from the settings) | – (in memory, the repo host's last answer per task for 15 s) |
| `local-config` | the repos' local config files (`mise.local.toml`, `.worktreeinclude`): every 5 s the files that changed and differ from the store are copied into the local config store, committed there and pushed to its remote; their state against the store, Restore into a new clone and Ignore locally (§4.13, ADR-86) | `workspace` (repos, the projects folder), `vcs` (tracked and ignored files, `info/exclude`, the store's commits and push) | the local config store `.trackwall-local/<repo folder>/` in the projects folder, a git repo |
| `error-journal` | Trackwall's own errors: the daemon's unexpected errors, the UI's and the desktop shell's in one list, anonymized, with where in Trackwall's code each came from (§4.14, ADR-94) | `workspace` (the projects folder, the task worktrees folder, repos and repo hosts, to anonymize) | `logs/errors.json` in the state directory; the shell's `logs/shell-errors.jsonl` until the daemon takes it |
| `cards` | the repo card from build, configuration and code files in the HEAD commit (provided and consumed packages, Spring Boot apps, HTTP endpoints and clients, required tools, mechanisms), repo tags and the card warm-up in the background (§4.6, ADR-63); later completed by an LLM; for the `no-mise` tag also the mise tasks of `mise.local.toml` in the working directory (ADR-86) | `workspace` (repos from the folder, tag rules), `vcs` (reading the commit) | `cards/<commit>.json` in the state directory (ADR-25) |
| `graph` | the connection graph between repos: joining the packages and HTTP clients from the cards with the packages, apps and endpoints of other repos, package and app aliases and manual edges from `workspace.yaml`; the queries `who_calls`, `subgraph_for`, `find_endpoint` (§4.6) | `cards` (cards and tags of all repos), `workspace` (`graph` entries) | – (the last graph in memory, keyed by the set of commits and the configuration, ADR-29, ADR-30) |
| `procs` | process groups, start/stop with escalation, orphans, port -> owner | `logs` (group output, §4.2) | `runtime.json` (live groups, §4.1) |
| `logs` | the bus: ring buffer, JSONL, sequences, cursors, masking, `trace` | `secrets` (masks; today masks from the process environment) | `logs/<source kind>/<repo>/<mise task>.jsonl`, `logs/task-mise-task/<task>/<mise task>.jsonl` |
| `services` | service = repo + mise task `dev` + port + readiness; start with dependencies from the graph, endpoint call (§4.3, ADR-45); later leases | `mise` (the task `dev` and its run, in a repo root or a task worktree), `logs` (port from the log, errors, masking), `graph` (dependencies), `cards` (`server.port`), `agents` (the task port, ADR-61); later `leases` | – (in memory; planned: table `services`) |
| `leases` | who holds a service on which port; takeover by a task | – | `leases` table |
| `secrets` | the secrets repo (k8s `Secret` manifests), reading `git show origin/<app>:<file>`, injection into env, `check_secrets` | `vcs` | a clone outside the workspace |
| `agents` | Copilot CLI sessions in a PTY (§4.4, ADR-26), tasks in separate worktrees (ADR-31), state detection; later budget, recording | `procs` (group with a PTY), `workspace` (repo root by identifier, the projects folder for the supervisor, the session's tool permissions, ADR-66, the task worktrees folder for a new task, ADR-84, moving a repo out of the projects folder, ADR-83), `vcs` (task worktrees and branches); later `logs`, `leases` | today session files in `sessions/<session>/` (session plugin, `hooks.jsonl`), removed when a session closes and when the daemon starts, and tasks in `tasks.json` (with the trusted mise config of each worktree, ADR-59, and its task port and the files copied into it, ADR-61) and the task worktrees folder each was created in, ADR-84) with worktrees in `<task worktrees folder>/<repo>/<branch without prefix>`, removed at the user's request; eventually a `tasks` table and recordings; the unread review comments of each task only in memory (ADR-62), and so are task messages and task updates (ADR-68) |
| `mcp` | the MCP server: mapping tools to the facade, audit; today a transport in `src/mcp/` next to `api/` (§5.3, ADR-24), without audit | everything through the facade | `audit` table |
| `api` | HTTP + WebSocket for the UI and CLI (events `/ws`, session terminals `/ws/pty/<session>`), token, CORS, static UI files | everything through the facade | – (the last snapshot of each event topic in memory, ADR-36) |

Two modules are the "foundation": `procs` and `logs`. `logs` depends on no module, `procs` only on `logs` (it hands it the group output), and everything else stands on them, so they come first and get the most tests, including on Windows.

### 4.1 `procs`: process groups

- A start always creates a **group** (ADR-20): on Windows a Job Object with `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` through `bun:ffi`, on macOS and Linux a new session (`setsid`, `detached` in Node) and signals to `-pgid`. A mise child that runs mvn, which runs java, is one unit. On Windows the leader goes into the job right after `spawn` (Node cannot start a suspended process); a descendant created in that moment, before the leader is in the job, would fall out of the group, but `mise` takes a few milliseconds to start before it runs anything. The system code is in `platform/` (`GroupPlatform`), the `procs` module (`ProcessGroups`) knows only the interface.
- The group owner (`owner`) is the caller's key (today a mise task in a repo or `mise install` in a repo, built in the `mise` module and never parsed back); there is one group per owner, and a second start returns the state of the first until that one is confirmed empty. After the end the last state stays until the next start, in daemon memory. A stop during a start waits for the group, and once the daemon has started exiting no new groups are created. Whoever waits for the result rather than just showing the state (the Batch command, §4.8) gets it from `ended(owner)`: the `exited` state of the current group after confirming that it is empty; a group that cannot be killed never ends.
- A stop escalates: a gentle signal (macOS and Linux: SIGTERM to the group; Windows: closing the leader's stdin, because `GenerateConsoleCtrlEvent` needs a shared console, which a process with a hidden window does not have), after a grace period (5 s; 2 s on Windows, because most servers do not listen for stdin closing) killing the whole group (`SIGKILL` to `-pgid`, `TerminateJobObject`) and up to 3 s of waiting for an empty group, all within the 10 s the shell waits for the daemon to stop (§5.4). The same path stops orphans at startup. **A stop returns success only after confirming** that no process of the group is alive (`kill(-pgid, 0)` gives `ESRCH`, the job has zero active processes); otherwise the error `stop_unconfirmed`, and the run stays `stopping`. `procs` does not know ports: a service is `stopped` only once its port no longer answers either (§4.3).
- A leader that ended on its own takes the rest of the group with it: the daemon kills what is left, and only after confirming an empty group is the run `exited` (exit code, signal, whether the user stopped it, and the last lines of output). Until then it is `stopping`, and a group that could not be killed stays in `runtime.json` and blocks a new start of that owner. A stop that arrives in that time waits for this cleanup and returns the leader's result (its exit code, without `stoppedByUser`). A stop during a start waits for the start; when the start failed there is no group and the stop ends with `not_found`.
- The group registry in `runtime.json` (in the state directory): owner, command, directory, the leader's PID and its start time according to the system (`ps -o lstart` with fixed `TZ=UTC` and `LC_ALL=C`, to the second). A group goes there after the start and disappears after a confirmed end; an orphan that could not be killed stays until the next start. A file that cannot be read is set aside (`runtime.json.broken-<time>`), and a failed write goes to the log and does not change the in-memory state. At daemon startup, under the startup lock and before the API opens, orphans are cleaned up only when the leader is the same process: it is alive with the same start time (PIDs are recycled), or it is dead but the group with its id is alive (the system does not hand out a PID while a group with that number lives). A live PID with a different start time (or without a recorded time) is not killed: it goes to the log and disappears from `runtime.json`, because later starts would not settle whether it is our process either. On Windows under Bun there are no orphans, because closing the job handle when the daemon dies kills the group; under Node the daemon only reports the live PID.
- Daemon exit (Ctrl-C, a signal, `POST /api/daemon/shutdown`) stops all groups with the same stop, in parallel, before the API closes.
- **A leader in a PTY** (`startTerminal`, ADR-26): the same group, but the leader starts in a pseudo-terminal from `platform/terminal.ts` (`Bun.spawn({ terminal })`: ConPTY on Windows, a PTY with a new POSIX session on macOS and Linux, so the leader's PID is the group id as with `setsid`). The caller gets a terminal to write to and resize, and the PTY output goes to it and to the last lines of the `exited` state (masked by `logs.lastLinesOnly`, without a log, because a screen is not log lines, ADR-28). A gentle stop on Windows closes the terminal (ConPTY then sends `CTRL_CLOSE_EVENT` to the processes, as when a console window closes), on POSIX it is the same SIGTERM to the group. The owner of a group with a PTY must have no group or only an ended one: there is no "second start" that would return the existing one, but a session goes on under its id once it ended (ADR-96). Under Node there is no PTY (`terminal_unavailable`), because it would need a native addon.
- stdout and stderr are always read (an unread pipe blocks the child at about 64–192 KB) and go to the `logs` module (`attachOutput` in `process-groups.ts`): the caller gives the log source (`logSource`) in `PipedGroupSpec`, and `logs.open` gives the run output (`RunOutput`), which turns bytes into records and returns the last 20 non-empty lines (masked, without ANSI sequences and overwritten `\r` progress) for the `exited` state.
- The working directory must be the full path of an existing directory; the caller gives the root of the repo or worktree, or for the supervisor the projects folder (ADR-58). The environment is the daemon's environment without `TRACKWALL_HOME`, and on macOS with the `PATH` from the user's login shell (`user-env.ts`): an app opened from Finder gets only the system `PATH` from launchd, without Homebrew and `~/.local/bin`.
- `portOwner(port)` returns the owner with a confidence level: `exact` (a PID from our group), `high` (a descendant of our group), `medium` (the command matches a service), `low`. At `low` the daemon takes no automatic action. (Not done yet.)

### 4.2 `logs`: the bus

The first slice (ADR-28): the log of every mise task (from the repo details and from the Batch command) and of `mise install`, live in the repo details and in a JSONL file. The ECS parser, `trace`, search and the logs of services, tasks and the daemon are the next steps; the MCP tool `tail` reads the same log as the UI, and `get_errors` pulls errors out of the buffers of all sources (§5.3, ADR-42).

- Record: `{ seq, ts, runId, stream, level, msg }` (`LogRecord` in `packages/protocol`), in the file also `source`. The source (`LogSource`) is today `{ kind: 'mise-task', repo, miseTask }` for a mise task in a repo root, `{ kind: 'task-mise-task', repo, task, miseTask }` for one in a task worktree (ADR-59; `miseTaskLogSource` builds both) and `{ kind: 'mise-install', repo }` for `mise install` from the Batch command (§4.8); services, sessions and the daemon will come as further kinds, and the target fields (`trace_id`, `span_id`, `fields`, `raw`) will come with the ECS parser.
- One **global** `seq` for all sources. Each daemon lifetime starts it at its start time in milliseconds times 1000, so the numbers of consecutive lifetimes do not repeat, and a cursor from a previous lifetime is smaller than the cursor of the current empty log (start time times 1000). The `sinceSeq` cursor (`logs/tail` in the API and the MCP tool `tail`) works for one source, and for the whole workspace in `get_errors` (ADR-42; later also in `search`). A cursor older than the source's buffer returns `dropped: true` with records from the oldest in the buffer; a cursor from another daemon lifetime (smaller than the empty-log cursor, or larger than the last `seq` when the clock went back) returns `dropped: true` with the latest records, as without a cursor. A clock that went back by less than the previous daemon lifetime lasted can put an old cursor inside the current range; then the client gets the records after it without `dropped`, and the UI starts the log over after a connection change anyway.
- A ring buffer per source (2000 records, `LOG_BUFFER_RECORDS`, and at most 2 million characters of text, so that long JSON lines do not take tens of MB per task) for the UI and MCP, with the records of consecutive runs (`runId`), in memory only. JSONL on disk: `logs/<kind>/<repo>/<mise task>.jsonl` (`logs/task-mise-task/<task>/<mise task>.jsonl` for a task worktree, without the repo, which the task names, so the path stays short; not yet removed when the task ends, so its buffers stay until the daemon restarts and its files until the user deletes them; `logs/mise-install/<repo>.jsonl` for `mise install`) in the state directory, with names encoded to be safe on Windows and macOS (`%XX` for characters outside `[a-z0-9_-]`, including `:` and upper-case letters, a prefix for device names like `con`, a hash for names longer than 60 characters, so that the path fits in 260 characters with a state directory of up to about 100 characters). Writes in batches every 250 ms and at daemon exit; at 10 MB the file becomes `.1.jsonl` (the previous one is gone). A rotation that failed (on Windows a file opened without `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`, for example by `Get-Content -Wait`) does not stop writing: the file grows past the limit until the next successful rotation, and the error goes to the daemon log once. A failed write stays in the buffer until the next attempt (up to 4 MB, after that records do not reach the file), and the error and the loss of records go to the daemon log once; a write cut off halfway (no space left) may repeat some records in the file after a retry. An optional index `trace_id -> (file, offset)` in SQLite for `trace(trace_id)` comes with `trace`.
- Line: a UTF-8 decoder and an unfinished line separately for stdout and stderr, ANSI sequences removed, masking, then of a line with `\r` only what follows the last `\r` remains (progress in a terminal). An unterminated line longer than 64 KB becomes a record (the split never cuts a secret: it falls before it or before the last characters in which a secret could only be starting), a record has at most 16 KB of text, empty lines stay in the log (not in the last lines of the state).
- Level from the line text, until there is an ECS parser: `ERROR`/`FATAL`/`SEVERE`/`npm ERR!`/`npm error`, or `error`/`fatal` at the start of a line, is `error`, and so is a compiler diagnostic with a file location (javac `Foo.java:12: error:`, tsc `a.ts(3,5): error TS2322:`, kotlinc `e: ` with a file) and Vite's `Internal server error:`; `WARN`/`WARNING`, `warning` at the start, `npm warn`, `Foo.java:7: warning:` and kotlinc `w: ` with a file are `warn`; upper-case `DEBUG` and `TRACE`; everything else is `info`. The task prefix `[name] ` mise puts before the lines of dependencies does not change the level (a level is not a prefix: an upper-case level word alone or with a thread, like Maven `[ERROR]` or `[ERROR:main]`, or a lower-case one alone, like sbt `[info]`; so a task named exactly `debug` keeps its prefix). The stream does not decide, because many tools write ordinary progress to stderr.
- **Errors from logs** (`get_errors`, ADR-42): `errorBlocks` (`error-blocks.ts`) splits the records of one source into blocks, separately for each run and stream. A block starts at an unindented record at the threshold (`error`, with `minLevel: warn` also `warn`; an indented line at `error` level, like `b.old(Level.SEVERE);` under a javac warning, is context, not an error), a failed Gradle test (`OrdersTest > rejects FAILED`), an exception header (`java.lang.IllegalStateException: …`, `TypeError: …`, `Exception in thread "main" …`), a `Caused by:` without its beginning, or the opening of a region. It is extended by Java and Node stack frames (`at …`, `... 12 more`), `Caused by:`/`Suppressed:`, an exception header under a logger line (until the block has frames), indented and blank lines, a line of asterisks, Node's `Require stack:` with its `- path` lines, and further lines with the same level tag and no timestamp (Maven `[ERROR]`, `npm error`). Whatever starts an error (an `error` record, an exception header, `Caused by:`, a report) under a warning starts an error of its own, so an error does not depend on `minLevel`. Regions without indentation last until their end: the Spring "APPLICATION FAILED TO START" report until the blank line after the `Action:` paragraph, the Gradle `FAILURE:` report until `BUILD FAILED`, and both until a line starting with a date or time (the logger's next event), a Maven level tag or a Gradle `> Task` line (the build tool that ran the app), or another region's start, which matters for a Spring report without `Action:`; a block has at most 500 records. Outside a region any other line closes the block, and trailing blank lines are not part of it. `collectErrors` (`log-errors.ts`) merges the same errors of one source (the exception and its first frame, or without an exception the first 20 lines, after replacing numbers, UUIDs, hex ids and bracketed parts such as thread names and trace ids, but keeping file locations like `Orders.java:12` so different compiler errors stay apart) into one entry with `count` and `firstTs`, with the lines and `seq` of the latest occurrence, and trims the lines: the first 10 frames of each run of frames (recognised without the mise task prefix), the rest as one line `… N frames omitted`, at most 60 lines of 4000 characters (`omittedLines` counts what was left out). The entry's `exception` is the last `Caused by:` of the main chain (not one at or under the indentation of a `Suppressed:` exception) or the first header. The `sinceSeq` cursor skips errors whose first record is not after it, so a block still being written when asked comes once, with the lines it had at that moment. Errors are computed on every request from the in-memory buffer, without an index and without reading the JSONL files, so errors older than a source's buffer are gone with it.
- ECS parser (later): Spring Boot with `LOGGING_STRUCTURED_FORMAT_CONSOLE=ecs` gives JSON with `log.level`, `trace.id`, `error.stack_trace`. A line that is not JSON gets its `level` from the heuristic and goes to `raw`.
- Masking: the set of secret values of a given process is known to the `logs` module and replaced with `***` **before** being written to the buffer, the file and the last lines of the state. The agent and the file see the masked version only. Today a secret is the value of a variable from the process environment whose name has a segment `TOKEN`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD`, `PASSWD`, `PASSPHRASE`, `API_KEY`/`APIKEY`, `PRIVATE_KEY`, `CREDENTIAL(S)` or `PAT` (for example `GH_TOKEN`, `DB_PASSWORD`), at least 8 characters long; a multi-line value (a PEM key) contributes each of its lines separately, and overlapping occurrences of secrets are merged before the replacement. Secrets injected by the `secrets` module will join this set (gap: §8).
- ANSI is removed on write; colour in the UI comes from `level`. The view is a light list of lines under the task row in the "mise tasks" tab (the "Log" button; a run opens it by itself), which follows new lines until the user scrolls it up, and stderr has a marker on the side. The UI polls `logs/tail` from the cursor every second while the log is open, and immediately when a response is full; the WebSocket `log.append` (§5.2) will replace polling without changing the record. xterm.js stays for the PTYs of task sessions (ADR-28).

### 4.3 `services` and `leases`

A service is a pair (repo, mise task `dev`) plus a port and a probe. States: `stopped`, `starting`, `healthy`, `unhealthy`, `stopping`, `crashed(exitCode, lastStderr)`.

First slice (ADR-45, `modules/services/`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/services.ts`): a service is a repo with the mise task `dev` (`SERVICE_MISE_TASK`), one per repo root and one per task worktree (keyed by repo and task, ADR-61, below), run through `mise.start`, so it is the same process group and the same log source (`mise-task`, `dev`, or `task-mise-task` in a task worktree) as the button in the "mise tasks" tab, and a `dev` started there (or with `run_mise_task`) is adopted as a service by `list`, `start`, `stop` and `call`; its watch reads the log from the oldest buffered record (`logs.runStartCursor`), so a port logged long ago is still found.
- **Readiness** (`service-watch.ts`, `readiness.ts`, `port-probe.ts`): a watch per run checks every 500 ms (one check at a time; nothing changes the state after the end) the run state from `procs`, the new lines of its log and a TCP connection to the port on `127.0.0.1` and `::1` at once (Node dev servers on `localhost` often listen only on IPv6, and a refused connection on Windows takes about 2 s). The port is the first one the run logs (`Tomcat|Netty|Jetty|Undertow started on port N`, `Local: http://localhost:N`, `listening on port N`, `listening on http://localhost:N`; patterns of a server that listens, not of a client that connects), otherwise `server.port` of the apps on the card, which reads only the default profile. `healthy` once a port accepts a connection, or for a Spring Boot app without a port after its `Started … in N seconds` line; `unhealthy` when the port stops answering, or 5 minutes after the start of the run while still `starting` (Gradle and Maven compile first); `crashed` when the group ended by itself, `stopped` after a stop once the port no longer answers. A build tool may run the app outside the group (Gradle's daemon, Gradle's default, runs `bootRun`'s app as its own child), so the app shuts down only after the group is gone, or never when the daemon misses it: until the port closes the service stays `stopping`, with the reason after the group has ended, and the watch goes on checking. `stop_service` waits up to 15 s for that (`SERVICE_RELEASE_MS`) and returns the service as it is then; a stop of a service still `stopping` checks the port again and waits again, and a new start whose port still answers is skipped with that reason. The `mise run health` probe from the plan is not used: it needs a task in every repo, and an open port says the same for a web service.
- **Slice** (`slice-plan.ts`): a start with dependencies takes the repo and the repos it calls at runtime, transitively: graph edges `http` and `manual` without `low` confidence (paths that match endpoints of several repos); `package` edges matter only for the build. At most 12 repos (`MAX_SLICE_REPOS`), otherwise `bad_request`. Repos start in waves: one starts once every repo it calls within the slice has settled (healthy, unhealthy, crashed or skipped), so a config server or registry comes up before its clients; repos in a cycle start together. A member without `dev`, with mise unavailable or an untrusted config, or whose `server.port` already answers before its start (another service of the slice or a program Trackwall did not start; the reason names which) is `skipped`, the repo asked for too; the repo asked for without `dev` is `not_found` before anything starts. Two starts at once share one slice. A running service is not started again (`running`), so a second start waits further; a crashed one starts again. The start goes on in the background after the answer (`waitMs`, up to 55 s, MCP calls must end within the bridge limit).
- **Stop**: the service of the repo, and with `dependencies` the members its starts `started` (also an earlier start with dependencies before a start without them), except those another slice uses that is still starting or whose root still runs; the stops run in parallel, and the answer lists the services that were running. A stop during a start cancels it: no further wave, and no member starts after the stop unless another slice still starting needs it; a member whose start was already under way is stopped once it runs; the next start makes a new slice. A start with the other `dependencies` value while a start is in progress is `bad_request`. A repo never started (no slice and no running `dev`) is `not_found`; a stop after the root crashed still stops its dependencies.
- **Endpoint call** (`endpoint-call.ts`): an HTTP request with the caller's method, path, headers (without `host`, `content-length`, `transfer-encoding`, `connection`) and body (64 KB) to the loopback address and port that answered the probe of a `healthy` service, through `loopbackRequest` (not `fetch`, which sends loopback through `HTTP_PROXY` in Bun) with a deadline for the whole request, so an event stream with keepalives does not hold the call; the URL is built by the daemon, so the call does not leave the computer and reaches only a port of a service Trackwall runs. The response body is read up to 128 KB and returned up to 32 KB of text, and it and the headers are masked with the secret values of the service environment, as the log lines are (`logs.mask`). After the response the daemon waits 500 ms and returns the errors (`logs.errors`, ADR-42) of the task `dev` in every repo whose first record came after the cursor taken right before the request, so an exception in a downstream service is in the result too.
- UI: the "mise tasks" tab of a repo with `dev` has a "Service" panel with the state and port, "Start with dependencies" and "Stop with dependencies" and the state of each dependency of the last start. The API actions are `services/list`, `services/start` and `services/stop`.
- **From a task worktree** (ADR-61): `start`, `stop` and `call` take `task` (`null` for the repo root). The task's `dev` runs in its worktree through `mise.start` with the task as target, so it gets the task port in `SERVER_PORT` and `PORT` (§4.7), and the watch expects it on that port (`taskPort` from `agents`, in place of `server.port` from the card, `portSource: 'task'`; a port the task's log names still wins). A task that is not in the repo is refused before anything starts (`not_found` for an unknown or ended task, `bad_request` for another repo's, the rule `agents.taskOfRepo` keeps for the MCP tools too), and a task ended while its slice starts leaves its service `skipped`; whether there is a `dev` is read from the repo root's list, as the code the task started from, and `mise` refuses a start in a worktree whose `dev` the task removed. A repo's own mise `[env]` that sets `PORT` overrides the task port, as mise's environment wins over the inherited one: such a service takes its fixed port and is `skipped` or `crashed` next to the root's. The task port already answering before the start skips the service with the reason, as a taken card port does. Only the root of a slice runs from the worktree: its dependencies run from their repo roots, since the task did not change them, and a dependency already running from its root is `running`. So the same repo can run from its root and from one or more task worktrees at once, each on its own port, and `list` adopts a `dev` running in a worktree as that task's service; it forgets the service of an ended task once it no longer runs, so the list does not grow with every task. Callers are not rerouted: the services that call the task's repo keep calling the instance on the root's port, so the task's service is reached directly, with `call_endpoint` and its `task`. The UI lists only the repo roots' services in the "Service" panel; a task's service shows as its mise task `dev` in the task panel.
- Not yet: rerouting callers to a task's service (leases, below), several services in one repo root, secrets from the `secrets` module, the services in the graph view.

A lease says **which branch/worktree** holds a service on its port. By default the leaseholder is the main repo. An agent task that changes a service's code asks for the lease; the daemon stops the instance from the main repo, starts the instance from the task worktree on the same port, and gives it back when the task ends (or by hand). Thanks to that the rest of the graph does not need to know anything. Routing by header through a proxy is out of scope until two instances at once are needed; the `leases` interface is designed so that a proxy can be added without changing the other modules. Since ADR-61 a task's service runs next to the root's on the task port without a lease; a lease would come on top of it, when callers should reach the task's instance.

### 4.4 `agents`: tasks

- **CLI adapter** = a command template (`copilot --prompt {prompt_file} ...`), the way MCP configuration is passed, the session resume command, a set of state detectors. **The only adapter is Copilot CLI** (decision of 2026-10-23). The adapter interface stays, because it keeps the code in order, but we do not design for Claude Code or Codex and do not test their detectors.
- A session runs in a **PTY owned by the daemon** (ConPTY on Windows). The UI attaches xterm.js over a WebSocket, `ws attach` does the same in a terminal. Eventually the PTY output is recorded (asciicast format) to `sessions/<task>.cast`; today only the `copilot-record.ts` script records, and sessions in the daemon (below) do not. Copilot CLI takes the mouse and asks for every mouse move (mode 1003), so text is selected by dragging with Shift (Windows) or Option (macOS, `macOptionClickForcesSelection`). xterm.js clears the selection on any input it sends to the program, mouse reports included, so while text is selected and the program asks for moves, the session terminal keeps a move without a pressed button from reaching xterm.js (`keepsSelection` in `terminal-copy.ts`); otherwise the selection was gone before it could be copied. xterm.js turns Ctrl+V into a control character and cancels the key, so on Windows and Linux the session terminal leaves Ctrl+V and Ctrl+Shift+V to the browser, whose `paste` event xterm.js turns into pasted text (`pastesClipboard`); on macOS Cmd+V already does that. Copilot CLI also draws a selection of its own, which xterm.js does not know about, and copies it on Ctrl+C or a right click by sending OSC 52 (`ESC ] 52 ; c ; <base64>`), which xterm.js ignores by itself; the session terminal handles the sequence (`osc52Text` in `terminal-copy.ts`): only the clipboard target, never a query (`?`), so a program cannot read the clipboard, and only one sequence within 1 s of the user's Ctrl+C that goes to the program or right click in that terminal (`asksProgramToCopy`, `ProgramCopy`), so text an agent prints cannot fill the clipboard while the user types or clicks. Every copy from the terminal goes through `copyText` (`lib/clipboard.ts`): in the Tauri window the app command `copy_text` (ADR-51), because WKWebView refuses `navigator.clipboard` outside a key or click handler and an OSC 52 copy arrives later over the WebSocket; in a browser `navigator.clipboard`. Link hover listens to the same moves, so while text is selected in such a session a link responds to Ctrl+click or Cmd+click only after the selection is cleared.
- **State detection**, from the most reliable source: (1) CLI hooks, if the adapter has them, (2) the OSC 9 / 777 / 99 terminal sequences and the `ws notify <state>` command available in agent scripts, (3) screen patterns (on the daemon side, not in the UI; the first works on the output text alone, a VT parser will come with patterns that need it), and next to them the keys with which a human answers in the session terminal, for what the CLI does not report. Every state change carries `source` and `confidence`, so the UI can show "probably asking for approval".
- States: `Working`, `Input`, `Approval`, `Plan`, `Done`, `Error`, plus `Stale` (no signal for longer than a threshold). A budget of time and number of commits with forced termination.
- **Sessions in the daemon** (ADR-26, the "Sessions" view): `agents.start {repo, prompt?}` starts Copilot CLI (`copilot` from the user's `PATH`; on Windows, instead of `copilot.cmd` from npm, the native `copilot.exe` from the platform package, because `cmd.exe` mangles arguments, `findCopilot`) in the root of a repo from the projects folder, or with `repo: null` a free session in the projects folder itself (ADR-100), with `prompt` as its first message (`--interactive`) when there is one, through `procs.startTerminal`, with `--plugin-dir` of the session plugin, in a 120×30 PTY that takes the size of the last terminal window. The environment is `userEnv` with `TERM=xterm-256color` and `COLORTERM=truecolor`, and with `127.0.0.1` added to `NO_PROXY` (`withLoopbackNoProxy`, ADR-89), because Copilot CLI's MCP client would otherwise send the request to the daemon through the user's `HTTP_PROXY`. Several sessions can run at once; each has an identifier (UUID) and a directory `sessions/<session>/` in the state directory with the session plugin and `hooks.jsonl`. The plugin's hooks are HTTP hooks (ADR-88): Copilot CLI POSTs each hook's payload to the daemon's `POST /hooks/<event>` (§5.2) with the daemon token and the session token, and the daemon (`Agents.receiveHook`) appends a hook record to the file and answers with the hook's output: `{}`, or what waits for the session at the end of a turn or with a tool's result (ADR-85, below). The session environment gets `COPILOT_HOOK_ALLOW_LOCALHOST=1`, without which Copilot CLI silently drops a hook to `127.0.0.1`. The session reads new lines of the file every 200 ms (`HookTail`), together with the `Stale` threshold and the end of the group in `procs`. The screen of each session is kept by a windowless terminal emulator (`TerminalScreen`, `@xterm/headless`, 2000 lines of scrollback), from which the window in the UI gets the screen image on attach (§5.2). `stop` stops the group like any other (§4.1), and a stop by the user ends the state as `Done`, not `Error`, even though the process ends with a signal. An ended session stays on the list with its last screen until the user closes it (`dismiss`, which also removes its directory). The X on a grid tile and on a list row (ADR-48) closes a running session too: the UI calls `stop`, then `dismiss` (`lib/use-session-close.ts`, also behind the session panel's Close), and asks first while the agent is in the middle of a turn (`closeCutsOffTurn`); a task's session closes alone, and the task stays listed. Sessions live in memory: daemon exit stops them together with the other groups, and daemon startup removes the `sessions/` directory left by the previous daemon lifetime. The conversations survive (ADR-95, `resumable-sessions.ts`): a main session and the supervisor start with `--session-id=<session id>` (a task with its task UUID), and `resumable-sessions.json` in the state directory follows the running sessions at every check (session, repo, task, and whether Copilot CLI keeps a conversation: the session had a prompt). The daemon's stop stops following before it stops the groups, so the sessions its stop ends stay in the file; after a crash it holds those of the last check. The next daemon reads the file before its API starts and, once the API listens, starts them again one by one: a task's session as Resume does, the supervisor and a main session under their old id with `--resume=<id>`, or `--session-id=<id>` when there was no prompt yet (Copilot CLI refuses to resume a session without a conversation). One that cannot start (its task was ended, its repo is gone, a supervisor already runs) goes to the log; a file that fails its schema is moved aside (`.broken-<time>`) and nothing is resumed. A session "waits for you" (`Session.needsAttention`, computed in the daemon by `SessionAttention`, ADR-47) when it runs and its state is `Approval` or `Plan`, or `Input` the user has not seen yet. A session terminal in front of the user (focused and visible for at least 1 s, `FocusReporter` in the UI; the window sends `focus` over `/ws/pty`, §5.2) or a key typed into the session (not an answer xterm.js sends to a terminal query, which starts with ESC) marks the `Input` seen, the `Input` an Esc causes included, and a window in front of the user when the state changes sees the new state at once; only a change to another state makes it unseen again. `Approval` and `Plan` wait until the user answers, because the agent is blocked in the middle of the turn. The UI shows seen `Input` as "idle" (the dashboard state `idle`); the UI (the counter on the view bar, "Next waiting") and the tray (§5.4) only read the field. The first Copilot CLI window in a new directory (folder trust) and sign-in give no hook, so the session then has no state ("state unknown"), even though it is waiting for you. The daemon does not record sessions; recordings are made by the `copilot-record.ts` script. Every session gets the Trackwall MCP server (§5.3, ADR-24): the daemon writes `mcp-config.json` into the session directory, user-only, with an HTTP entry for its `/mcp` (ADR-89: `sessionMcpRequest`, the address with the daemon token and the session token as headers, ADR-39; `copilotHttpMcpServers` in `protocol`) and passes it as `--additional-mcp-config @<file>`, so the session has the Trackwall tools without `copilot mcp add` and without changing the user's configuration. In the same way it gets **session instructions** (`session-instructions.ts`, ADR-46): the file `instructions/trackwall.instructions.md` in the session directory with `applyTo: "**"`, whose directory the daemon appends, after a comma, to `COPILOT_CUSTOM_INSTRUCTIONS_DIRS` in the session environment (the user's own value stays first, under the user's spelling of the name on Windows). Copilot CLI loads it as a repository instruction next to the repo's `AGENTS.md` and `.github/copilot-instructions.md`, so the user's repo and `~/.copilot` stay untouched. The file opens with the session's environment: the Trackwall version that started it (`version` in the facade options, `VERSION` in the daemon) and that the user sees it in the "Sessions" view and is told when it ends its turn or asks for approval. Then it holds the session's role, built only from the repo and task ids, never from agent text: the supervisor (ADR-58) coordinates work across repos with `propose_task` and the session tools and does not change code itself, works out what a request changes before it delegates (a task is a worktree of one repo, so a request about the projects folder itself, such as removing a repo, is not proposed as a task; a repo removal it proposes with `propose_folder_action`, ADR-83) and sends a follow-up on a running task's topic to that task with `send_message` instead of proposing a new one (ADR-81), and, when the user asks to continue a task whose session no longer runs, resumes it with `resume_task` instead of proposing it again (ADR-90); a main session in the repo root works in the user's working copy and hands larger or parallel work, especially in another repo, over with `propose_task`; a task session works only in its worktree, commits on the task branch without switching branches, pushing, merging or opening merge requests unless the prompt asks for it (the user pushes and opens the merge request from the task panel, ADR-77), and ends (also when stuck) with `report_result`, written for a reviewer, because it becomes the merge request's description; it is told to build and test with `run_mise_task` and its own task, which runs in its worktree (ADR-59), that its mise config needs the user's trust once the agent changes it, and to verify its task with `verify_task` before `report_result` (ADR-64). The MCP instruction (§5.3) follows. A state directory with a comma in its path does not fit that variable, so the session then starts without the file and the daemon logs it. A task (below) adds a worktree, a starting prompt, resume and live changes to a session; a time budget is the next step.
- **Copilot CLI** (ADR-17, detectors in `modules/agents/`, recording in `scripts/copilot-record.ts`). The daemon gives the session a **session plugin** through `--plugin-dir`: a directory with `plugin.json` and `hooks.json`, outside the worktree and outside `~/.copilot`, so it changes neither the user's configuration nor the repo files. Each hook is a POST to the daemon (ADR-88; the recording script `copilot-record.ts`, which has no daemon, registers a program run without a shell, `exec` + `args`, from the plugin directory, not the repo, and with `--no-env-file`, since Bun would otherwise load `bunfig.toml` and `.env` from the user's repo), and its payload becomes a **hook record** in the session's `hooks.jsonl`: the event, the time and only the fields on the list (`CopilotHookPayload`: session id and time, start source, end reason, notification kind and trimmed text, tool name), and of the rest only the field names, so that recordings reveal the undocumented payload without storing the prompt, tool arguments and results. Events and states: `sessionStart` → `Input` (the first one → `Working` when a prompt is known to be on its way, below), `userPromptSubmitted` and `postToolUse` → `Working`, `notification` by kind: `permission_prompt` → `Approval` with `high` confidence (the kind from `@github/copilot-sdk`, confirmed in a session on macOS, ADR-17), `elicitation_dialog` → `Input` with `high` confidence (the agent's question from `ask_user`, seen on the real binary, ADR-66); other kinds, among them `agent_idle`, `agent_completed` and `shell_completed` about background agents and commands, do not change the state; without a kind `Approval` / `Plan` / `Input` by whole words of the text with `low` confidence, `agentStop` → `Input` (end of turn), `errorOccurred` → `Error`, `sessionEnd` → `Done` or `Error` by `reason`. Copilot CLI sends no hook until the first prompt (`sessionStart` comes with it), so until its first state a session that did not start with a prompt gets `Input` from its screen image: the input box (a line starting with `❯` between two rules) over the footer with hints, not over a status line (`● Loading`, `◉ Working`); source `screen`, confidence `medium` (`copilot-prompt-box.ts`, read at every check until the first state, and only while it could give one, ADR-69). While a prompt the daemon has just typed into such a session (ADR-62) is on its way (up to 5 s, `RETYPE_AFTER_MS`), the box does not give `Input`, so the session does not wait for you while its agent is about to start, and that prompt's `sessionStart` gives `Working`. A replayed recording has no screen image, so it does not give this state. No `preToolUse` and `permissionRequest`: a hook of theirs that crashes denies the agent the tool, and `permissionRequest` fires also for an automatically granted approval, so it does not mean "waiting for a human". Checked on the real binary (ADR-66): an exit code other than 0 or output that is not JSON from `permissionRequest` denies the tool, and Copilot CLI shows its approval request only after the hook has returned. From the terminal: OSC 9;4 (progress bar, the default `terminalProgress`) gives `Working` at state 1 or 3, `Input` with low confidence when the bar is removed and `Error` with low confidence at error state 2; Copilot CLI 1.0.103 sends it only when it recognises Windows Terminal (`WT_SESSION`), so on macOS and in a session of a daemon started outside Windows Terminal there is no bar, and during an approval request it keeps sending state 3 (ADR-17); the window title (OSC 0 / 2) is a description of the current work, not a state. ConPTY on Windows sets the title first to the path of the program it started, before Copilot CLI sets its own; the daemon leaves that title out (`windowTitleFromOsc`), so a new session shows no title rather than the path of `copilot.exe` (seen in the contract tests on Windows). From the keyboard (`copilot-keys.ts`, source `keyboard`): Copilot CLI sends no hook after an answer to an approval request (after an approval the first hook is `postToolUse` after the command ends) nor after a turn is interrupted with Esc, so the session in the daemon and the recording script look at single keys from the terminal window: Enter or a digit 1–9 (choosing an option without Enter) in `Approval` or `Plan` gives `Working`, Esc or Ctrl+C in `Working`, `Stale`, `Approval` or `Plan` gives `Input`, both with `medium` confidence. A key counts from the state after the hooks already in `hooks.jsonl`, not after the last read cycle (every 200 ms), so an approval request appended just before Enter does not stay in `Approval` after it. Only a whole chunk of input equal to one key counts, so text, a paste and arrows do not change the state, and neither do Enter or a digit while typing a prompt (the hook reports the turn, and a command like `/model` has no hook). Enter on the option "No, and tell Copilot what to do differently" gives `Working`, although Copilot is still waiting for text; the "No" digit also gives `Working` first, because the option number depends on the window, and the screen pattern (below) recognises the interruption right after. From the screen, one pattern (`copilot-screen.ts`, without a VT parser, on the output text alone): the message "Operation cancelled by user" with which Copilot CLI ends an interrupted turn (in recordings from macOS and from Windows a continuous text in one chunk of output) gives `Input` with `medium` confidence, but only up to 10 s after the session key that changed the state (an answer or an interruption), because an interruption always follows such a key, and the same text comes back when the screen history is redrawn. As the `screen` source it can only end `Working`; it catches an interruption the key did not recognise, for example when Enter on "No, and tell Copilot…" or the "No" option digit ends the turn at once. Other screen patterns will come from real recordings if hooks, OSC and keys turn out not to be enough.
- **Agent dialogs** (ADR-66, `copilot-dialog.ts`, `answer-dialog {session, dialog, answer}`, `AgentDialog` in `protocol`): while a session is in `Approval`, the daemon reads the approval request from the session's screen image (`TerminalScreen.lines()`, the visible lines as plain text) and gives it in `Session.dialog`: the heading, what it asks about (the command in the inner box, or the file and its lines), the question and the numbered options, Copilot CLI's wrapped lines joined again, each text cut to 300 characters and at most 20 detail lines; while the session is in `Input`, a question from the agent (the `ask_user` form under "Copilot needs information.") is a dialog of the kind `question`, with the question only. The state names the kind to read, so an approval not yet cleared from the screen hides no question. The layout is Copilot CLI 1.0.103's (fixtures from the real binary in `test/fixtures/copilot/dialog-*.txt`); a screen the reader does not recognise is no dialog, and the state and the terminal work as before. The dialog's box starts in the first column, and the agent's output, which could draw one, is indented, so only a box in the first column counts. An approval taller than the terminal (the JSON arguments of an MCP tool in a short task terminal) loses its top: Copilot CLI keeps its tab bar and a blank line on top and cuts the box above, heading included. Such a box still counts when only the first row (the tab bar) and blank lines are above its left border, which runs unbroken up from the footer; its heading is then the text of the approval's notification hook ("Use MCP tool: trackwall/verify_task", "Run command: …", kept by the session from the last hook if it was a `permission_prompt` notification, `Approval request` without one), and its detail starts with `…` (contract test on the real binary in a 14-row terminal). The task panel folds its sections above the terminal into one strip of task notices (ADR-91), unfolded they take at most a third of the panel, and the task session's summary shows two lines until opened, so a task terminal on a small screen is rarely that short. When the selection marker `❯` sits on an option that is not offered (the user moved it there in the terminal, where that option may already take text), there is no dialog until the state moves on. The state decides which dialog counts, so one left on the screen after a key is gone at once. The dialog's id is a digest of its text and of the number of the wait it belongs to (each entry into `Approval` or `Input`), so the same request asked again after an answer has a new id; the session reads the screen once per change of the screen image or the wait. `answer-dialog` reads it again and types the answer only while the same dialog of the same wait is there, as far as the emulator has processed the output: an option's digit (Copilot CLI selects options 1 and 2 at once; the options whose label starts with "No" need text in the terminal and are not offered) or Esc for `stop`, through `LiveSession.input`, so the key detectors and attention see it as the user's key (`Working` after a digit, `Input` after Esc); a dialog answered in the terminal or replaced by another is `dialog_changed` (409), and a question is `bad_request`. The UI shows the dialog in the tray panel (sessions asking for approval first) and in the Home details of a selected session (`AgentDialogCard`); the Sessions view has the terminal itself, so it shows no card (a tile not selected shows its state, and selecting it gives the terminal). The MCP tools leave `dialog` out (`AgentSession`, §8).
- **Tool permissions** (ADR-66, `toolPermissions` in `workspace.yaml`, `sessionToolPermissions` in `workspace/tool-permissions.ts`): every session starts with `--allow-tool=<pattern>` and `--deny-tool=<pattern>` for the patterns of the rules that apply to it, a rule without `repos` to every session, one with `repos` to sessions whose repo id matches one of its globs (ignoring case; never the supervisor or a free session). The supervisor also gets `--deny-tool=shell` and `--deny-tool=write` after them, unless `supervisor.changesCode` (`supervisorToolPermissions`, ADR-78); a bad `supervisor` key is `invalid_config` and the supervisor does not start, so a typo never lifts the denial. Copilot CLI applies them itself: an allowed tool runs without an approval request, a denied one is refused, a denial wins. The rules are read at every session start; a bad `toolPermissions` is `invalid_config` and the session does not start, so a denial the user wrote is never dropped.
- **Combining signals** (`TaskStateTracker`): the best source that has spoken wins, so after the first hook OSC and the screen do not change the state, and a session without working hooks gets its state from OSC. Exception: a weaker source may end `Working` (for example the progress bar being removed), because a lost end-of-turn hook would leave the session in `Working` and then in `Stale`, although it is waiting for a human; it can no longer start `Working` after a hook. A human key (`keyboard`) always passes and does not change the rank of the sources, because it speaks about what the hooks do not report; the key detector decides in which state a key means something. `Stale` only from `Working` after a threshold without output and signals (waiting for a human is not hanging), with the threshold time rather than the moment of checking, so live and from a recording the result is the same; output restores the state from before `Stale`. A process exit is final: `Done` at code 0, `Error` otherwise, unless the `sessionEnd` hook has already reported the end.
- **A recording** `.cast` has, besides the output, markers (`m`) with hook records, the kind of key (`enter`, `choice`, `escape`, `interrupt`) and the exit code, so the state can be computed again from the recording alone (`replayCopilotRecording`) by the same code that runs live. Live, the state is computed from the event time stored in the recording (full ms), not from the moment of computing, so a replay gives the same changes to the millisecond. Keyboard input text is not recorded, only each session key (a single Enter, a digit 1–9, Esc or Ctrl+C), including one that did not change the state. The script writes the recording synchronously at every chunk of output and reads `hooks.jsonl` every 200 ms, with the same code as the daemon (`HookTail`, `appendHookRecord`); when the daemon starts recording sessions, the write must become asynchronous. The script reaches into the module files directly, because it runs without the daemon.
- **Tasks** (ADR-31, `tasks.ts`, the "Sessions" view): `agents.create-task {repo, prompt}` creates the branch `trackwall/<slug>-<6 characters of the task UUID>` (a slug of up to 32 characters from the first words of the prompt without diacritics, only `[a-z0-9-]`, `taskSlug`) from the default branch, and its worktree (`git worktree add -b`) in `<task worktrees folder>/<repo>/<slug>-<6 characters>` (ADR-84): `worktreesDir` from `workspace.yaml` ("Task worktrees folder" in Settings, `set-worktrees-dir`, `null` back to the default) or by default `.trackwall-worktrees` in the projects folder, so a worktree sits on the repos' disk and under the same antivirus exclusion and Copilot CLI trusted folder. The repo list does not see it (a dot name, and no `.git` of its own one level down), and no repo's working directory gets an untracked directory: the folder may not be the projects folder itself or lie inside one of its repos (`workspace/worktrees-dir.ts`, checked on saving and again on every new task, because the projects folder may change). Each task keeps the folder it was created in (`worktreesDir` in `tasks.json`), so a changed setting applies to new tasks and never moves an existing worktree; a task saved before ADR-84 has `null` there and its worktree in `worktrees/` of the state directory. The default branch without asking the server: from `refs/remotes/<remote>/HEAD`, the local one when it exists (sync moves that one and unpushed commits sit on it), otherwise the remote one; a repo without this pointer gives the checked-out branch, and a detached HEAD the commit alone (`baseBranch: null`). The commit the branch started from is the task's **base commit**. Worktree changes in one repo (`worktree add`, `worktree remove`, `branch -D`) run one after another, because they fight over locks in `.git`; two tasks created at once in one repo get separate branches and directories. On Windows every git command gets `-c core.longpaths=true`, because a worktree two levels below its folder lengthens the paths of the repo's files. The task goes into `tasks.json` before the session starts, so the worktree does not disappear from the list if the daemon crashes; a failed session start (no Copilot CLI) removes the worktree and the branch, once git stops reading the worktree. A failed `worktree add` (for example a timeout with LFS) may leave a branch and a directory the list does not show; the daemon does not clean them up, because it does not know whether it created them (a branch with that name might have existed already), so they are removed by hand (`git worktree remove`, `git branch -D`). A task session is an ordinary session (above) in the worktree, with `--session-id=<task UUID>` and `--interactive=<prompt>`: Copilot CLI starts the TUI and sends the prompt as the first message (an option from `copilot --help` 1.0.103), and the `=` form makes sure a prompt starting with `-` is not an option. The prompt has at most 8000 characters, because it goes as one argument, and the Windows command line holds 32,767. Every task holds a **task port** (`Task.port`, ADR-61) while it exists: the lowest port of 41000–41999 (`TASK_PORT_FIRST`, `TASK_PORT_LAST`, below the dynamic ports systems hand to outgoing connections) that no other task holds, also one being created at the same moment; a task saved before ADR-61 gets one when the daemon reads `tasks.json`, and keeps it. Nothing is probed then: a port another program listens on shows when the task's service starts (§4.3). Right after `worktree add`, the **included files** (`Task.included`, `vcs/worktree-include.ts`, ADR-61) are copied: when the repo root has `.worktreeinclude` (`WORKTREE_INCLUDE_FILE`, `.gitignore` syntax, the file Claude Code reads too; it may itself be untracked), git lists the untracked files that match its patterns (`ls-files --others --ignored --exclude-from`) and keeps the ones the ignore rules ignore both in the root and in the new worktree (`check-ignore --stdin` in each; the worktree has the base commit's `.gitignore` and index, which may differ from the root's), so a file the worktree tracks is never overwritten, a copied file never shows as a task change the agent could commit, and an untracked file nobody meant to keep out of git is never copied; only regular files (a link could point anywhere on the user's disk), never over an existing file or through a directory the worktree has as a link, at most 100 files (more copies nothing) and 10 MB together (a file that does not fit is left out, the rest is copied); every file left out gets a reason in `included.problems`. A failure to copy does not stop the task. The task panel lists the copied files and the problems.
- **Resume** (`resume-task`, and the MCP tool `resume_task` from a session, ADR-90): a new session in the worktree with `--resume=<task UUID>`, so Copilot CLI goes back to the task's conversation; ended sessions of the task disappear from the list. A task with a running session gives `bad_request`, as does a second action on a task during the first. Tasks survive a daemon restart (`tasks.json`, atomic write), and the sessions that ran when the daemon stopped are resumed by the next one (ADR-95); a task whose session had already ended is "no session", to resume or end. The worktree directory is not stored, only computed from the repo identifier and the branch (`taskWorktree`), so a hand edit of the file cannot point to a directory outside `worktrees/`; the schema accepts only a `trackwall/[a-z0-9-]` branch, a full base commit hash and a full repo path. A file that fails it is set aside as `tasks.json.broken-<time>`, and the worktrees stay on disk.
- **Task changes** (`task-changes`, `TaskChanges`): the worktree's working directory with new files, without ignored ones, against the base commit, that is the task's commits and what the agent has not committed yet, together. New files (`git ls-files --others --exclude-standard`) go into a temporary index (a copy of the worktree index in a temporary directory, `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, removed after every request) as an entry with the hash of the empty blob (`git update-index --index-info`), as with `git add --intent-to-add`, but without writing that blob to `.git/objects`; a directory with its own repo is, as in `git add`, a submodule entry with that repo's HEAD, and a repo without commits or in another object format is skipped. Computing changes therefore writes nothing to the repo: the index the agent uses stays untouched, and several "Changes" windows at once do not write the same object (on Windows the second write ended with "Permission denied"). Then `git diff <base commit>` (working directory) without rename detection, external diff programs and `textconv`, the diff with the prefixes `a/` and `b/` whatever the user's `diff.noprefix` or `diff.mnemonicPrefix`, since the UI reads file paths from its headers (ADR-62): files with the kind of change and number of lines, the diff cut to 1 MB after its last whole line, so a reader never gets half a line or half a character (the same truncated-result marker when the list of new files exceeds 8 MB), the number of commits since the base and of `git status` entries. The UI polls every 2 s while the "Changes" tab is open and shows at most 4000 lines of diff. For an agent (`get_task_changes`, §5.3) git pathspecs narrow both `ls-files --others` and every `git diff`, and the diff limit is the agent's (at most 200,000 bytes). Polling instead of a file watcher: git has to compute the diff anyway, and a recursive `fs.watch` on a worktree with `node_modules` or `target` gives event storms and behaves differently on Windows, macOS and Linux. The copy of the index keeps the original's mtime, because git trusts a file whose size and mtime match its index entry unless the index was written no later than that mtime; with a fresh mtime a same-size edit made in the second the index was written (right after the worktree was checked out) would be missed.
- **Review comments** (ADR-62, `task-reviews.ts`, `send-review {task, comments}`, `ReviewComment` in `packages/protocol/src/review.ts`, the task's "Changes" tab): the user comments on lines of the task's diff and sends the comments to the task's agent as one review. A comment names the file, the side (`new`: an added or unchanged line, numbered in the worktree's file; `old`: a removed line, numbered at the base commit), the line number, the line's text (up to 300 characters, so the agent finds the line after the file has moved on) and the user's text (up to 2000); a review holds 1 to 15, so a review of ASCII text fits the 64 KiB request body (a longer one, with many escaped or non-ASCII characters, gets the body-size `bad_request`), and at most 60 comments of a task wait unread, so `get_review_comments` stays a size an agent's context takes (a review beyond that is `bad_request`). The UI reads the place of every line from the diff (`lib/diff-lines.ts`: the file from the `---`/`+++` header, git's quoted names included, the numbers from the hunk header) and keeps the drafts in the task panel, so switching tabs keeps them; while a comment form is open the tab stops polling, so a change by the working agent cannot move the lines under the form. The daemon queues the comments per task in memory (`TaskReviews`; a daemon restart drops the unread ones, like task proposals) and asks the task's running session to read them by typing a fixed one-line prompt into its PTY (`LiveSession.askToRead`, ADR-68): at once while the session waits in its input box, otherwise as soon as it does again. The session is in its input box when its last hook record that changes the state is `agentStop` or a `sessionStart` without a known prompt, or, before its first hook, when no prompt is on its way, and in either case its screen shows the empty input box now (`CopilotSessionState.inPromptBox`, ADR-69; a background shell's notification changes nothing; Copilot CLI sends `agentStop` before it draws the box again, ADR-68), its state is `Input` (or none yet, before the first hook), the screen does not show the agent's question (the dialog reader of ADR-66, since ADR-68: the hooks can put the session in its input box while the question is still open), and the user has not typed or pasted text since their last message was submitted (`userPromptSubmitted`, or the box on the screen empty for 2 s since their last text: text cleared with Ctrl+C, a slash command and text Copilot CLI took into a running turn send no hook; an answer in an approval or plan dialog and Esc are not text, nor a digit or Enter in the middle of a turn while the screen shows an approval whose hook has not come yet, ADR-71); the prompt is decided once after a batch of hook records, so a turn that ended and started again within one read gets nothing; the state alone is not enough, because a question dialog (`elicitation_dialog`), a guess from the screen or the user's Esc also give `Input` (and the tracker drops an `Input` that follows another `Input`), and the prompt with Enter would answer the dialog, race the interrupt or join the user's unsent text. So the text never lands in the middle of a turn or answers an approval; after an Esc the review waits for the end of the next turn. One prompt is outstanding at a time: once typed it counts until the session leaves `Input`, and a second review meanwhile types nothing, because the prompt asks for everything unread (a request it does not cover, such as a task message, goes in after the turn it starts, ADR-68), and the read cycle looks after it (`LiveSession.#followTypedPrompt`); the typed prompt is the way into an idle session, while a session that is working gets it through its hooks' output instead (ADR-85): `agentStop` is answered with a block whose reason is the prompt, which starts the next turn with it as the user's message without typing (not while the user has a draft, and not at the end of a turn a block started, `stop_hook_active`, so an agent that does not read is not kept in a loop; then the typed prompt takes over once the box is back), and `postToolUse` gets it as `additionalContext`, once per turn and prompt, so the agent reads what waits before the turn ends; an agent that did not act on the context still gets the block. Only what `askToRead` asked for goes out this way, so a new supervisor or main session is not handed old updates. A blocked `agentStop` is no `turn_ended`, because the turn goes on with the daemon's prompt. A block counts as a typed prompt: not taken after 5 s, it gets Enter again while its text still sits in the box (the TUI took the text and Enter as one paste), or it is typed again, read anew, into an empty box it was lost from, at most twice, then the daemon waits for the next request; while the screen shows no box (Copilot CLI busy) it waits; the text and Enter go as two writes 100 ms apart, because a TUI reads one write as a pasted string, in which a carriage return does not submit. Without a running session the prompt goes to the next session of the task, the one `resume-task` starts. The result says which of the three happened (`typed`, `queued`, `on_resume`) and how many comments are unread. The agent reads them with the MCP tool `get_review_comments` (§5.3), which returns the unread comments of the calling session's own task and marks them read, already when it returns them, so a call the agent cancels loses them; the user's text thus reaches the agent as tool data, never as keystrokes the Copilot CLI input box would interpret (`@` opens the file picker, `/` a command, a line break submits). Ending the task drops its unread comments. A prompt typed by the daemon does not count as the user's key, so it does not mark the end of a turn seen (ADR-47).
