- **Shared files** (`task-shared-files {task}`, result `{shared: TaskSharedFiles[]}`, ADR-44): the files on a task's list of changes (as in `task-changes`, without the diff) that are also on the list of changes of another task of the same repo, per such task. Changes of the other task that cannot be read (being ended, worktree deleted by hand, broken index) are left out, because this is only a warning; a failure to read the task's own changes is returned as in `task-changes`. It is computed on request, one git read per task of the repo, at most 3 at a time; the task panel in the UI (`SharedFiles`) asks every 5 s and shows a warning above the tabs with a button to the other task.
- **mise tasks in the worktree** (ADR-59, `task-mise-config.ts`): a task's mise tasks run in its worktree (§4.7), so its agent builds and tests its own changes. The worktree's **trusted mise config** (`trustedMiseConfig` in `tasks.json`) is the files mise reads in the worktree root, each with a SHA-256 of its content: the config files by name (`mise.toml`, `.mise.toml`, `mise.<env>.toml`, the local ones git ignores such as `mise.local.toml`, `.config/mise*.toml`), `.tool-versions`, and everything in `mise/`, `.mise/`, `.config/mise/`, `mise-tasks/` and `.mise-tasks/` (config, `conf.d`, file tasks), names matched without regard to case as mise finds them on Windows and macOS; a linked file counts by its target's content when the target is inside the worktree, as mise follows links; a link out of the worktree, a broken link, a linked directory, anything but a regular file, more than 500 files, 8 MB or a path over 1024 characters is `mise_unavailable`, which the task panel shows. It is read when the worktree is created, which is the base commit's config from a repo whose config the user trusted, after the included files were copied (ADR-61), so a `mise.local.toml` copied from the root counts as trusted like the root's own. `task-mise-config {task}` (`TaskMiseConfig`) gives the files the agent added, changed or deleted since then, with the text of each (up to 64 KB, from the same read as the digest) for the user to read, and a digest of the current files; `trust-task-mise-config {task, digest}` stores the current files as trusted only when the digest still matches (otherwise `bad_request`), so the user trusts the version they saw. Both only in the UI: the task panel shows the changed files and their text with "Trust this config" and polls every 5 s, but keeps the version it showed until the user asks for a newer one, so the digest it sends is that of the text on screen; no MCP tool trusts. A task saved by an older version has no trusted config (`null`, `neverTrusted`), so its mise files count as added until the user trusts them once. `trustedTaskWorktree(task, repo)` gives the `mise` module the worktree only while it is a task of that repo, is not being ended and has the trusted mise config, and otherwise refuses with the changed files. A session refused that way, or for an untrusted repo root, hears when the user trusts the config (ADR-92, below). The task panel also lists the mise tasks running in the worktree (pushed with `mise.running.changed`) with their log and "Stop".
- **Ending** (`remove-task {task, keepBranch, discardChanges}`, result `null`): only without a running session and without a process group working in the worktree (a mise task, ADR-59; `procs.liveIn` compares the group's working directory), because their processes write in the worktree and on Windows lock its files; it waits for all ongoing change computations (several windows), and while ending, `task-changes` refuses. Without `discardChanges` the daemon computes what would be lost: the worktree's `git status --untracked-files=all` entries, commits from the worktree's detached HEAD that are on no branch or tag (they leave the worktree regardless of `keepBranch`), a worktree directory git no longer knows (an interrupted removal, `worktree prune`, a repo cloned anew in the same place; what is in it cannot be computed), and without `keepBranch` also the commits `<base commit>..<branch>`, and on any of these it refuses with the `unsaved_changes` error listing what would be lost; the UI shows it and only then asks "Remove anyway". Files ignored by git (dependencies, build output) are always lost, and the UI says so before ending. Then `git worktree remove --force`; git removes the registration before deleting the files, so a removal interrupted by a file held by another process leaves a plain directory, which the daemon deletes itself after confirmation, and `git worktree prune` clears the registration of a directory removed by hand. Without `keepBranch` also `git branch -D`; the task's sessions disappear from the list. A task from a repo that no longer exists (moved, disconnected drive) cannot be checked with git: like a directory git does not know, without `discardChanges` it is refused with `unsaved_changes` when the worktree is on disk, and after confirmation the daemon deletes the worktree directory (it lies in the task's worktrees folder, because it is computed from that folder, the repo and the branch), the branch stays in the moved repo, and `git worktree prune` clears its registration there. `open-task-editor` opens the worktree in VS Code (`vscode://`).
- **Folder trust**: Copilot CLI asks for trust in a directory before it works there. An entry in `trustedFolders` (`~/.copilot/config.json`) covers subdirectories, and a worktree whose main repo is trusted is not asked about (both checked on 1.0.103), so the new task form suggests the projects folder, which covers every repo and, by default, the task worktrees folder too (ADR-84); the daemon does not change the Copilot CLI configuration. The `sessions` list also returns the tasks (`tasks`, newest first, without sessions), and the UI joins a task with its newest session (`Session.task`) and shows them as one item next to the sessions in repo roots.
- **Main session and task proposals** (ADR-39, `task-proposals.ts`, `session-wait.ts`, the "Sessions" view): a session (or a supervising agent outside Trackwall) delegates work to another agent with the MCP tool `propose_task {repo, title, prompt, model?}`. The daemon stores a **task proposal** (`TaskProposal`: identifier, repo, a title of up to 80 characters on one line, a prompt of up to 8000 characters, the **task model** (`model`, ADR-98: a Copilot CLI model id, or `null` for Copilot CLI's default), `proposedBy` with the session identified by the session token, or `null` for an agent outside Trackwall) in memory only, at most 20 waiting at once; nothing starts. The `sessions` list returns them in `proposals`, and the view shows them with "waiting for your decision" and counts them on the view bar together with the sessions that are waiting. The view's list is a tree (`sessionRows` in `lib/session-entries.ts`): a proposal or task with `proposedBy.session` sits under the entry that holds that session (a session in the repo root, or a task, also when it is an older session of the task), in the same order on every level (proposals first, then most recently started), and an entry with children has a "main" badge with their count; proposals from an agent outside Trackwall or from a session no longer listed are at the top level. The proposals of one session are one entry (ADR-55: `kind: "proposals"`, oldest first inside it), and selecting a proposal selects that entry; its panel (`ProposalInbox`) lists them all with their prompts, each opens to be read and edited, and the user starts one, or ticks several and starts them with "Start N selected", one after another (`startProposals` in `lib/proposal-decisions.ts`; a failed start does not stop the rest and leaves that proposal waiting with its error). A proposal for a repo that already has tasks says so, since which files it will change is not known before the start (the shared-files warning of ADR-44 needs changes). A proposal can be ticked only after it has been opened, so a prompt is never started unread. A decision first selects the group by its proposer (`{kind: "proposals", proposer}`), so the panel stays while its proposals turn into tasks one by one; afterwards, if the user is still there (`showsGroup`), the view opens the next proposal of the same session that still waits, then the first task just started, and after the last rejection the session that proposed them (`selectionAfterDecision`). A proposal selected from the tray, a notification or a `trackwall://task/` link selects its group and opens there. The "main" badge counts each proposal of a group. A grid tile with proposals (ADR-48) shows every prompt in full with "Start task" and "Reject…"; editing and starting several are in the full panel. "Next waiting" and Alt+J go through proposals as well as waiting sessions, in list order; a group is one stop, while the count on the button and the view bar counts each proposal. Proposals also show in the dashboard's "Needs you" queue (§5.5) and in the tray menu (§5.4). "Start task" (`approve-proposal {proposal, prompt, model}`, with the prompt and the model the user may have edited; the panel's model field is empty for the default, and a grid tile starts with the proposed model) creates a task like `create-task`, but with the proposal's identifier, its title (`Task.title`; the branch slug comes from it, not from the prompt), origin (`Task.proposedBy`) and model (`Task.model`, ADR-98: every start of the task's session, the first and each resume, passes it as `--model=<id>`). While it is being approved the proposal stays on the list, so that the panel in the UI does not disappear together with an error and the edited prompt, but a second approval or a rejection gives `not_found`, and waiting sees `starting`; the `sessions` list stops returning it as soon as a task with its identifier is on the list, and a proposal selected in the UI switches to that task (during a decision the group stays selected instead, and when it is gone its newest task stands in, ADR-55). When the task was not created, the proposal waits for a decision again (a failed `worktree add` cleans up the branch git creates before the checkout, so a retry with the same branch succeeds; when the cleanup itself fails, for example because of a file held on Windows, the error names the directory and branch to remove by hand), and the user ends a task left behind by a failed cleanup themselves. "Reject…" asks for an optional reason of up to 1000 characters (`reject-proposal {proposal, reason?}`, ADR-55), removes the proposal from the list, and the daemon remembers the last 100 rejections with their reasons, so that a waiting session gets `proposal_rejected` with `rejectionReason` (`null` without a reason) and can propose differently. A task session reports its result with the tool `report_result {summary}` (up to 4000 characters): the daemon stores it in `Task.summary` with the time (in `tasks.json`, so it survives a restart), and the UI shows it above the task tabs; only a session that has a task can call it, and only for its own. `wait_for_session {session | task, timeoutSeconds ≤ 55}` reads the state from daemon memory every 200 ms and stops waiting when the proposal was rejected, the session does not respond (`Stale`), is waiting for a human (`needsAttention`) or reported an error (`Error`, because it is unknown whether an end of turn follows), has ended, or the task has no session; after the time limit it returns `proposal_pending`, `starting` (a proposal being approved, or a task with an ongoing action, for example a session start) or `working`. Waiting on a task follows its newest session, and waiting on one's own session is refused. A task session with a starting prompt (`--interactive`) gets the state `Working` instead of `Input` from the first `sessionStart` hook (the next one, after `/clear` or `/resume` in the session, no longer carries the prompt), because it works on the prompt right away; otherwise waiting would end before the agent starts, and the tray would flash "waiting for you". Its input box on the screen before that hook does not give `Input` either (ADR-69). A **repo removal** (ADR-83) is a proposal of another kind (`TaskProposal.kind: "remove_repo"`; a task proposal has `"task"`), from the MCP tool `propose_folder_action {action: "remove_repo", repo, reason}`: its title is made by the daemon ("Remove <repo> from the projects folder") and its `prompt` holds the reason, up to 2000 characters. A task session cannot propose one (it works inside its worktree; it tells its proposer instead), and the daemon keeps the folder the identifier named at the time of the proposal, so an approval after the projects folder changed moves nothing. It waits, counts and shows in the tray, the dashboard and the session tree like a task proposal; its panel shows the reason and what happens instead of a prompt to edit, it cannot be ticked for "Start N selected", and its button is "Remove repo" (`approve-repo-removal {proposal}`); `approve-proposal` refuses it and `approve-repo-removal` refuses a task proposal. The daemon refuses the removal while a session runs in the repo's root, while the repo has a task (its worktree and branch belong to the repo, so the user ends the task first), while `procs` has a live group with its working directory in the repo or under it (a service or mise task), or while a clone writes into it; the proposal then keeps waiting with the error. The last check runs right before the rename, with nothing awaited in between, so nothing starts in the repo between the check and the move. Otherwise `workspace` moves the repo's folder into `<projects>/.trackwall-removed/` (`repo-removal.ts`, under the same name, or `name-2`, `name-3` when that is taken), so the repo leaves the list on the next scan and the user can move it back or delete it for good; a file held open on Windows (`EPERM`, `EBUSY`) leaves the repo in place with an error that asks to close the program and approve again, and any other error passes through. The proposer gets `repo_removed` with the folder it went to, and the other proposals for the repo are rejected with the reason that it was removed. **Session token** (`session-tokens.ts`): every session gets a random token (32 bytes), which its HTTP MCP entry in `sessions/<session>/mcp-config.json` (ADR-89) and its hooks (ADR-88) send in the `x-trackwall-session-token` header, and the daemon keeps only its SHA-256 hash in memory and recognises the calling session by it. The same session sees its own identifier through `list_sessions` (`caller`).
- **Supervisor** (ADR-58, `startSupervisor` in `agents.ts`, `start-supervisor`, `SupervisorPanel` in the UI): the one session above all others, Copilot CLI in the projects folder (`workspace.projectsDir()`, `bad_request` while it is not set) rather than in a repo, so its `Session.repo` is `null` and `Session.supervisor` is `true` (`isSupervisor` in `protocol`), and `Session.task` is `null` too. At most one runs: `start-supervisor` returns the running one, and two requests at once share one start; when the last one has ended, the start launches a new one and only then closes the ended ones (`dismiss`), so a failed start keeps the last screen. The start says which conversation it goes on with (`{conversation: "last" | "new"}`, ADR-96, `last-supervisor.ts`): `last-supervisor.json` in the state directory follows the newest supervisor the daemon lists (its id and whether it had a prompt) and keeps it after the user closed it and across daemon restarts, `SessionList.lastSupervisor` names it while it had a prompt, and a start with `last` launches the supervisor again under that id with `--resume=<id>` (`--session-id=<id>` without a prompt), closing its ended session first, since the id is listed once (so a failed start of it loses the last screen; Resume stays); with `new`, or without a supervisor that had a prompt, it starts a new one. The panel offers Resume and New while `lastSupervisor` is set, otherwise one start of a new one; Start my day always starts a new one. It is an ordinary session otherwise (process group, session plugin, session token, hooks, `needsAttention`), with its own role in the session instructions and, unless `supervisor.changesCode` in `workspace.yaml` is `true`, without Copilot CLI's `shell` and `write` tools (`--deny-tool`, after the user's own rules; ADR-78), and it has no more rights than any other session: it sees all sessions and tasks through `list_sessions` and `get_session` like every session, proposes with `propose_task`, and every proposal waits for the user. What it proposes carries `ProposedBy.supervisor: true` (a task keeps it in `tasks.json`; files from before ADR-58 read as `false`), so the Sessions list puts that work, and whatever it delegated further, under the "Supervisor" heading at the top (`sessionRows`: such roots first at depth 1, `SessionRow.supervised`), also after the supervisor session is gone. The supervisor is not a list entry and has no position: it lives in the **supervisor panel** on the right of every view of the window (`App.tsx`), with its terminal, its state, Stop, or "Start my day" (the day start, §4.11, ADR-70) and "Start again", and "Delegated", the rows under its heading; a selection or jump that points at the supervisor session (tray, notification, tray panel, `trackwall://session/` link) opens the panel instead of the Sessions view (`selectsSupervisor`), and Alt+0 pins it open and gives its terminal the keyboard. The panel is pinned open or folded to a strip with a bell while the supervisor or a proposal under its heading waits; that choice is kept in the window's `localStorage` (`supervisor-pin.ts`, pinned by default). The terminal counts as in front of the user (ADR-47) only while the keyboard is in the panel, and then the terminal selected in a view does not (`KeyboardElsewhere` in `lib/keyboard-elsewhere.ts`); Alt+0 without a supervisor session leaves the keyboard where it is (`showingTakesKeyboard`), and opening anything else from the panel, or focus landing anywhere outside it (a `focusin` on the document, since removing the focused terminal fires no blur), takes it out. The Sessions view's counter leaves the waiting supervisor out (its panel shows it), but counts its proposals; "Next waiting", Alt+J and the global shortcut go to the supervisor's panel only when no list entry waits (`nextWaitingTarget`); the tray says "Waiting: Supervisor" and the notification "Supervisor". The dashboard scene puts sessions at their repos, so the supervisor is not on it.
- **Free session** (ADR-100, `start {repo: null}`, `isFreeSession` in `protocol`, `NewSession` in the UI): Copilot CLI in the projects folder that is not the supervisor: `Session.repo` is `null` and `Session.supervisor` is `false`. Any number run at once, next to the supervisor; it has the tool permissions of the rules without `repos` and none of the supervisor's denials, and its session instructions (`freeRole`) say that the repos are the user's working copies, that its changes there go through no review, and that larger or parallel work goes to a task proposal. To MCP it is a main session in no repo: it proposes tasks in any repo and steers them like a main session (its proposals carry `supervisor: false`), and the tools that run in one repo (`run_mise_task`, `start_service`, `verify_task` and the ones that stop them) refuse it as they refuse the supervisor. The list a restart resumes keeps `supervisor` with each session (a file from before has none, and its session without a repo was the supervisor), so a free session comes back as a free session. In the Sessions list it is an ordinary entry named "Free session"; Home shows no free session, neither on the scene, since it has no repo to stand on, nor in its "Needs you" list, whose entries are the scene's objects; the view bar counter, the tray and Alt+J count a waiting free session.
- **Task messages and task updates** (ADR-68, `task-coordination.ts` with `proposer.ts`, `task-messages.ts`, `task-updates.ts` and `inbox-prompt.ts`): coordination between a task's **proposer** (`Task.proposedBy`: the session that proposed it, or for the supervisor's work whichever supervisor runs now, `proposerOf`) and the task's session, both ways, in daemon memory only. Down: the proposer sends a **task message** with `send_message {task, text}` (up to 4000 characters, `visibleText`), only to a task it proposed (another session, an agent outside Trackwall writing to a session's task, and anyone writing to a task the user typed in the form get `bad_request`); the daemon keeps the last 20 messages of each task, read ones included, refuses a new one while 20 are unread and refuses the sixth sent since the user last took part (text the user typed or pasted into the sender's session or a session of the task, `LiveSession.onUserText`, or a review of the task), and the task's agent reads the unread ones with `get_messages`, which marks them read. Up: the daemon queues a **task update** for the proposer (at most 50 unread, the oldest dropping out and counted in `dropped`) when the task's session ends a turn (its `agentStop` hook, `SessionEvent` from `LiveSession`; not the `Input` after a start or resume, nor the one a question dialog or an Esc gives mid-turn, and not a turn the user drove by putting text into the task's session or pressing Esc there since the previous end; an Esc or text before the daemon's own prompt does not count, and an answer to the agent's question is not such text), reports an error or exits (`turn_ended`, `error`, `exited`; a crash is only `exited`; the end of a turn comes from its `agentStop` hook record, so no turn is missed between two session cycles, except an `agentStop` the daemon blocks with its prompt, since the turn goes on (ADR-85), and an error and the exit are seen when the session reads its hooks or its process state, every cycle or sooner), when it calls `report_result` (`result_reported` with the summary), when a verification of the task has a verdict (`verified`, from the `verification` module's `onVerdict`), and when the user rejects one of the proposer's proposals (`proposal_rejected` with the reason); the proposer reads them with `get_updates`, which marks them read. An agent outside Trackwall has no input box and gets no updates, and it sends no messages, since it is not told apart from another agent outside Trackwall (both `bad_request`; it keeps `wait_for_session`). Updates are queued only for a proposer still on the list (the supervisor's always). Both ways the daemon only types its fixed one-line prompt (`inboxPrompt`: a sentence naming `get_review_comments`, `get_messages` or `get_updates` for each that has something unread) with the mechanism of review comments above (`LiveSession.askToRead`): into the task's running session for a message (at once, after its turn, or `on_resume` into the session the user resumes, as `SendMessageResult.delivery` says), into the proposer's running session for an update (not into a supervisor or main session as it starts: the user starts those to talk, and the next update asks them). The prompt is read when it is typed, so it names what is unread then and nothing when everything was read meanwhile; a request the typed prompt does not cover (a message arriving while the review prompt waits to be taken) goes in after the turn that prompt starts. The task panel shows the task's messages with their sender and whether the agent has read them (`SessionList.messages`, `TaskMessages` in the UI). Ending a task drops its messages; closing a session drops the updates for it, except the supervisor's, which wait for the next supervisor. When the user took part is kept per task and per sending session, so a task resumed in a new session still counts the user's text in the old one.
- **Trust notices** (ADR-92, `mise-trust-notices.ts`): `run_mise_task`, `verify_task` and `start_service` from a Trackwall session go through `Agents.runNeedingMiseTrust`, which remembers the session's last refusal with `mise_unavailable` and its target (the repo root or a task worktree) and forgets it on the session's next try. When the user trusts a config, with "Trust this config" in the task panel (`trust-task-mise-config`) or with `mise trust` in the repo details (the `mise` module's `onTrusted`, only once mise lists the tasks), every running session refused there (for the repo root: anywhere in the repo, since a worktree run needs the root trusted too) is asked to read (`askToRead`), and the prompt gains a sentence (`Unread.trustNotice`) that the user trusted the mise config that refused its last `run_mise_task`, `verify_task` or `start_service`, to run it again if it still needs to. The sentence is the whole news, so no tool reads it: it stays in every prompt the daemon sends the session until the end of the first turn after one of them was typed or blocked the end of a turn (`LiveSession`'s `onPromptSent`; context with a tool's result does not count, and a turn the user drove ends without `turn_ended`), until the session tries again, or until it closes. A failure to ask one session is logged and does not fail the user's trust. Only in daemon memory, per session.

### 4.5 `workspace` and `vcs`: repo list, browsing, cloning from a host and sync

The first slice of the `workspace` module (stage 3 in §11, done earlier at the user's request): repos from the projects folder, browsing them, cloning from GitLab or GitHub into the folder, and syncing all repos (fetch and fast-forward, without the mise step and extractors; the Batch command gives mise steps on selected repos, §4.8).

- **The projects folder** (`projectsDir`, ADR-18) is the only source of the repo list. Its full path is the `projectsDir` key in `workspace.yaml` (ADR-13); it is set in the "Settings" view (`set-projects-dir`, the directory must exist) or by hand in the file. The file is read on every request, so a hand edit works without a restart; a write from the UI changes only this key and keeps comments and the other keys. There is no default: until the folder is set, the list is empty, and the UI leads to the settings. The task worktrees folder (`worktreesDir`, ADR-84, §4.4) is the second folder setting, saved the same way.
- **The repo list** is built on every request from the direct subdirectories of the folder that have `.git` (a directory or a file, as in a worktree); directories starting with a dot are skipped, a link to a directory counts as a directory. Trackwall does not look deeper. The repo identifier comes from the directory name (`orders-bff`; on a collision, for example `Zażółć` and `zazolc`, a `-2` suffix in name order, so a new directory that sorts earlier takes over the identifier without a suffix). The folder is read, and checked on write, asynchronously (it may sit on a network drive or a sleeping external drive), and the repo status on the list is fetched for at most 8 repos at once. A repo cloned or deleted outside Trackwall appears or disappears at the next list request; in Trackwall a repo is added only by cloning from a host (below), and there is no removal. A folder that disappeared after being set (for example a disconnected drive) gives the error `projects_dir_unavailable` with its path. A folder dropped on the window (ADR-51) does not add a single repo either: `dropped-folder` says what it is (a listed repo or a folder inside one, the projects folder itself, a git repo elsewhere, a repo in the projects folder whose name starts with a dot, a folder with repos directly inside it, a folder without any, a file), and the UI opens the listed repo or offers the folder, or the folder that holds the dropped repo, as the projects folder through `set-projects-dir`.
- **Show in Explorer/Finder** (`open-folder`, ADR-51) opens the folder of a listed repo in the system's file manager, like `open-editor` opens VS Code: the action takes a repo id, `Workspace.openFolder` looks the path up in the list and hands it to the `openFolder` the facade injects (`explorer.exe` with the path in quotes on Windows, because Explorer splits its command line at commas, `open` on macOS, `xdg-open` on Linux, `infra/system-open.ts`), and the daemon does not wait for it. It works in the page opened from the daemon too.
- **Repo hosts** (`repoHosts`, ADR-19) are a list in `workspace.yaml` (`- kind: gitlab` + `host: gitlab.example.com`, likewise `github`), set in the "Settings" view (`set-repo-hosts`) or by hand, without duplicates (the `RepoHosts` schema); every `workspace.yaml` key is checked separately, so a bad entry in `repoHosts` breaks only the hosts, not the repo list; only the user's file knows the address of the GitLab instance (ADR-7). The daemon asks the host through the program the user is signed in to it with: `glab api --hostname <host> projects?membership=true…` for GitLab and `gh api --hostname <host> user/repos?affiliation=owner,collaborator,organization_member…` for GitHub. The token stays in those programs (`glab auth login`, `gh auth login`); Trackwall neither reads nor stores it. **Search** (`remote-repos {query}`) in GitLab runs on the GitLab side (`search` with `search_namespaces`, so the group path matches too), one page of 100 projects, most recently changed first, because a company instance has thousands of them; a full page is `truncated` and a request to narrow it down. GitLab matches fragments only from 3 characters, so the daemon matches a shorter query itself on the first page without searching. The GitHub API for the user's repos has no search, so the daemon fetches up to 10 pages (1000 repos) and filters them itself by name and description. Fetched pages serve later queries for 60 s (a failed fetch does not), so typing in the search box does not ask the host at every character. The list is the repos the user is a member of (`membership=true` in GitLab; in GitHub own, collaborator and organisation repos), not all visible public projects. Archived repos are skipped, and a name outside `RemoteRepoName` (segments `[A-Za-z0-9_][A-Za-z0-9._-]*`) does not reach the list. A host that does not respond (no program, no sign-in or no network) is on the list as `unavailable` with the program's message and does not take away the results of the others; the UI suggests the sign-in command. An API page has a 30 s limit.
- **Clone** (`clone {host, fullName}`) is `glab repo clone <full name> <folder>/<last segment>` or the same with `gh`, with `GITLAB_HOST` / `GH_HOST`, so the protocol (SSH or HTTPS) and credentials are as the user set them in those programs. The daemon rejects a host outside the settings, an unset folder and a directory name that is already in the folder (a case-insensitive comparison, because that is how Windows and macOS drives work); a remote repo with that name is on the list as "Already in the folder". Cloning runs in the background: the action returns it at once in the `running` state, and `clones` returns the clones into the current projects folder since the daemon started (`running`, `done`, `failed` with a message), kept in memory only. A second click on the same repo in the meantime returns the same clone, and another repo with the same directory name waits for it to end. The repo reaches the list by itself, because it lies in the folder (ADR-18), so nothing is stored; during the clone its directory may already be on the list. A clone has a 30-minute limit, because a large repo takes minutes to clone, and ssh waiting for a host key confirmation in a hidden console would wait forever; after the limit the clone has failed, and a retry goes through only once git finishes by itself and removes its directory. A clone cannot be cancelled from the UI, and killing `glab` or `gh` (the limit, daemon exit) does not stop the git it started, because the clone is not in a process group: a gap to close together with the `procs` module (§8). Without a terminal and with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` git does not wait for a password; ssh started from the daemon's terminal (development mode) may still ask about the host key in that terminal.
- **Sync** (`sync`, ADR-21) is the "Sync all" button on the repo list: for every repo from the projects folder, at most 4 at once, `git fetch` of the main remote, then a fast-forward of the local default branch to the remote one. The Batch command (§4.8) composes mise steps (`mise install`, `setup`, `build`) with sync through `syncRepo {id}`: the same sync of one repo, with an error when a clone into the repo is in progress. The extractors from §7 do not exist yet, and the `ws sync` CLI will come together with the `ws` CLI.
  - **Remote and branch.** The main remote is `origin`, and without it the only remote. The default branch is `refs/remotes/<remote>/HEAD` (set by `git clone`); when it is missing or points to a branch that no longer exists (the host changed the default branch, and a fetch with `prune` removed the old one), the daemon asks the server once through `git remote set-head --auto`. It does not detect a change of the default branch whose old version still exists on the remote.
  - **Moving.** A checked-out default branch moves through `git merge --ff-only`, so the working directory gets the new files, and an untracked file in the way ends in a git error instead of being overwritten. A branch that is not checked out moves through `git fetch . <remote>:<local>` (without `+`): git accepts only a fast-forward and refuses when the branch is in use in some worktree, also during a rebase or bisect, when HEAD is detached; then the repo ends with an error carrying git's message, and the branch stays. The checked-out branch and the working directory stay unchanged.
  - **Skips.** Sync never merges, rebases or switches branches. A repo without a remote, without a default branch (remote or local), with uncommitted changes in tracked files of the checked-out default branch, with a diverged branch or unpushed commits, or with the default branch checked out in another worktree is skipped with a reason (`SyncSkipReason`) and a sentence for the user. Untracked files do not block, and changes on a feature branch do not get in the way of moving a default branch that is not checked out. A git error (network, sign-in, host key, time limit) is a `failed` result for that repo with git's message and does not stop the others.
  - **Result and working directory** (ADR-34). Every result without an error (`updated`, `up-to-date`, `skipped`) also has the state of the working directory after sync (`SyncWorkingTree`): the checked-out branch and the number of changed tracked files and, separately, of new untracked entries, from one `git status` at the end (when it fails, `workingTree: null`, and the result stays); `updated` and `up-to-date` also have the remote branch (`origin/main`). The UI builds a sentence from this for every repo, including an up-to-date one: what happened to the default branch, whether the files in the working directory changed (no, when another branch is checked out), and that uncommitted files were left untouched, split into changed and new.
  - **No prompts.** Commands that connect to the server (`fetch`, `remote set-head` and `merge`, because the LFS filter downloads objects) get, besides `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, an empty `GIT_ASKPASS`, `GCM_INTERACTIVE=never` (Git Credential Manager without windows) and `GIT_SSH_COMMAND="ssh -o BatchMode=yes"` (`networkEnv` in `vcs/git.ts`), unless the user has set an ssh program themselves (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_SSH` or `core.sshCommand`): then theirs stays, and only the 5-minute limit protects against hanging. A clone through `gh` and `glab` does not get this environment yet (only `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`).
  - **In the background.** Like cloning: `sync` returns the sync at once with the repos in the `waiting` state, and `sync-run` returns the last sync of the current projects folder (in daemon memory, until a restart). One runs at a time: a second click returns the running one, and a start in another folder meanwhile is `bad_request`. A repo being cloned into is skipped without an entry. List entries with the same `.git` directory (a repo and its worktree, or a link in the folder) sync one after another, because two simultaneous fetches in one `.git` fight over refs. The UI polls progress every 1.5 s during a sync and every 10 s outside it, and shows the result of every repo, including "up to date", under the repo status on the list (a sentence in the tooltip) and in full in the sync panel (in place of the repo details), with the number of up-to-date repos next to the moved, skipped and failed ones. The `sync.progress` event (§5.2) will replace polling together with the WebSocket.
- **The remote address** (`Repo.remote`, ADR-21) is the address of the main remote in the form `host/path`, without the scheme, user (also with a token written into the URL), port and `.git`, with the host in lower case: `git@gitlab.example.com:shop/api.git` and `https://gitlab.example.com/shop/api` give `gitlab.example.com/shop/api`. It identifies a repo next to the directory name: it does not change when the directory is renamed, and it links SSH and HTTPS clones. A local path, `file://`, no remote, or several remotes without `origin` give `null`. The address is computed after `url.<base>.insteadOf` shorthands, as in git, and `transport::` (for example `ext::`) also gives `null`, because it is a command, not an address. The daemon reads it from `git config -z --list` and keeps that configuration per repo until the repo's `config` or `config.worktree`, the user's global `.gitconfig` or `git/config` under `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` changes (found as git finds them: `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL`, `HOME`, on Windows `HOMEDRIVE`+`HOMEPATH`) (time, size and file identity; `ConfigMemo` in `vcs/config-memo.ts`, ADR-79), so a refresh of the list, the tags or the graph starts no process for it; a change only in the system configuration or in an `include.path` file shows after a daemon restart. The UI shows it in the repo header under the path.
- **The repo status** (`RepoStatus`) is observed on every request with one `git status --porcelain=v2 --branch`; the last commit's author, date and subject come from `git log -1` only for a HEAD hash not seen before (`CommitMemo` in `vcs/commit-memo.ts`, ADR-79), so a list refresh is one git process per repo: the branch, the last commit, the number of changes in the working directory (with new untracked entries separately, `untracked`, ADR-34; a new directory is one entry, as in `git status`; the UI writes "3 changed, 1 new"), ahead/behind against the tracking branch. None of this is stored. A repo git cannot read (a broken `.git`, no permissions) stays on the list as `unavailable` with git's message.
- **Working directory changes** (`changes {repo}`, the "Changes" tab in the repo details, ADR-34) are the files and diff of the working directory against HEAD, with new files and without ignored ones, by the same read as task changes (`workingTreeChanges` in `vcs/worktree-changes.ts`, ADR-31): a copy of the index in a temporary directory with the new files as `--intent-to-add`, so the user's index stays untouched, and at most the empty blob reaches `.git/objects` (not a new version of every file), a diff without external diff programs and `textconv`, cut to 1 MB (`PATCH_MAX_BYTES`). A repo without commits is compared with the empty tree (`git hash-object -t tree --stdin`, so it works for SHA-1 and SHA-256). Read only: committing and discarding changes stay in the editor. The UI polls the tab every 10 s after the previous response while it is open, and at once after a HEAD change; the file list is cut to 500 in the UI, and `--name-status`/`--numstat` output cut in the daemon gives `truncated`; the "Working tree" tile with changes opens this tab.
- **Browsing** reads from the HEAD commit, not from the working directory (ADR-14): one `cat-file --batch-check` says whether HEAD and `HEAD:<path>` exist, what type and size they are, then `ls-tree` or `cat-file blob`. The path from the request is a path in the commit's tree, so it cannot lead outside the repo or hit a file ignored by git. The file and README preview is cut to 512 KB; a file with a NUL byte in its first 8000 bytes is binary (the same heuristic as in git).
- **Code search** (`searchCode`, MCP tool `search_code`, ADR-40) reads from the HEAD commit, like browsing: for each chosen repo, at most 4 at once, `git grep -n -z -I --column --no-textconv --no-recurse-submodules -C<context>` with `-F` (fixed string) or `-E` (POSIX extended regular expression, without `\d` and `\s`), optionally `-i`, the pattern after `-e` and git pathspecs after `--` (`code-search.ts` in `workspace`, `grep.ts` in `vcs`, where `commitGrep` owns these flags together with reading source files for cards). It searches the commit's objects, so uncommitted changes are invisible, a path cannot leave the tree, ignored files are never in the result, and the repo makes git run no program (no `textconv` or filters) and pull in no submodule. A repo without commits is skipped. The pattern is one line: git would split a pattern with a newline into several, and an empty line matches everywhere. A match is told from context by the column field of the output line (`-z` with `--column`: a match has a column, context does not). **Limits**: git output 2 MB per repo, `git grep` 30 s, lines cut to 500 characters, at most `limit` matches from one repo; repos start in list order, so once finished repos have given `limit` matches no further repo is searched; repos not started because of that, or 60 s after the start, go to `skipped`; line text in the whole result at most 100,000 characters (the result goes to the agent twice, like the README in `get_repo`). Each of these cuts sets `truncated`, and in `searched` also on the repo it affects (so `truncated` can be true when the skipped repos had no matches). A repo git could not search (invalid pattern, time limit, broken `.git`) goes to `failed` with git's message and does not take away the results of the others. The MCP tool builds the tag filter from the card tags (`cards`), and `workspace` gets a ready list of ids; an unknown id is `not_found`, also when tags are given. There is no index: every query reads the repos again.
- **Git, `gh` and `glab`** are run through `runProgram` (`modules/vcs/run.ts`): `spawn` with an argument array, without a shell, with a time limit, bounded output and the end of stderr as the error message. **Git** gets `-C <repo>`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES` on the parent of the repo root after resolving links (with an empty or broken `.git` git does not look for a repo higher up and does not show the repo the projects folder lies in; an explicit `--git-dir` would disable the repo ownership check, `safe.directory`, so we do not use it), `-c core.fsmonitor=false -c log.showSignature=false` (§8), a 15 s time limit (`git grep`: 30 s; sync commands: 5 minutes and the no-prompt environment, above), `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` (reading the status does not take `index.lock` when the user happens to be committing) and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, without inherited variables that choose a repo (`GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`…), which would override `-C`. The result is settled without waiting for the pipes to close, because on Windows `git` from `PATH` can be a launcher whose grandchild holds them after the time limit. Output is parsed from machine formats (`-z`, `--porcelain=v2`), so it does not depend on git's language. **The git budget** (ADR-79): on Windows the daemon asks `git --exec-path` once and starts the real `<root>\mingw64\bin\git.exe` (`ucrt64`, `clangarm64` in other builds; `vcs/git-program.ts`) with `<root>\mingw64\bin` and `<root>\usr\bin` in front of `PATH`, as the launcher `cmd\git.exe` or a package manager's shim would, instead of the launcher, which would start a second process; network commands keep `git` from `PATH`, so credential helpers and ssh run as from the user's shell. At most 6 short git calls run at once in the whole daemon (`Slots` in `vcs/slots.ts`; a call waits for a slot before its time limit starts), and network commands and local ones with a longer limit (a worktree, a search) take no slot. The HEAD commit is read from the git directory's files (`HEAD`, the branch's ref file, `packed-refs`; `vcs/head-commit.ts`), and only the reftable backend and unusual refs go to `git cat-file`. `git-budget.test.ts` counts the processes of a refresh with 20 repos.
- **"Open in VS Code"** opens the address `vscode://file/<repo root>` with the system's URL-opening mechanism (the same as `--ui`), without running `code` through a shell. With the same mechanism the facade opens an `http` or `https` link clicked in a README (`workspace/open-link`, ADR-35); `overview` gives the README format (`markdown` or `text`).
- **Tool permission rules** (`toolPermissions`, ADR-66) are a list in `workspace.yaml`, by hand only, like `repoTags`: `allow` and `deny` with Copilot CLI permission patterns (`ToolPermissionPattern`: `kind` or `kind(argument)` on one line), and optionally `repos` with globs of repo ids; `agents` asks for the patterns of one session (`toolPermissions(repo)`, §4.4).
- `workspace.yaml` is written through a temporary file and `rename` (`atomic-write.ts`, shared with `daemon.json`), with a retry on Windows when an antivirus or an editor holds the file for a moment. The projects folder path in the file must be full (on Windows with a drive letter or UNC; `\projects` would depend on the daemon's current drive). The `repos` key from version 0.2.0 is no longer read, but a write does not remove it.
- **The repo watch** (`RepoWatch` in `workspace/repo-watch.ts`, ADR-63) compares, every second from the daemon's start, the set of repos in the projects folder and the HEAD and refs of each of them, read from the files of the git directory without a `git` process (`GitStateReader` in `vcs/git-state.ts`: the `stat` of `HEAD`, of the file of the branch HEAD points to, of `packed-refs`, of `reftable/tables.list` and of every directory under `refs/`, without a `.git` the directory is not a repo to warm up, and a file that cannot be read for a moment (a sharing violation, a network timeout) keeps the repo's previous state; `HEAD` is read again only after it changed, and a directory under `refs/` is listed again only after its time changed and once its time is over 2 s old, because on a file system with whole-second times a subdirectory created in the same tick would be missed; in a worktree `.git` is a file with `gitdir:` and the refs are in the `commondir`; what a read remembers is kept only for the repos of the last comparison). The state has two parts: HEAD (the `HEAD` file, the file of its branch and, with the reftable ref backend, `tables.list`, which holds every ref, compared by time, size and file identity, so a commit is seen within one timestamp tick) and the other refs. Any change, and a repo added to or removed from the folder (also a directory that gets its `.git` later, as with `git clone` in a terminal), bumps the revision (`RepoWatchState`: `startedAt` of the watch and `revision`, the `repos.changed` topic on `/ws`, §5.2); the first comparison is revision 0. A repo that is new or whose HEAD moved goes to the card warm-up (§4.6), the most recently used first (the time of `logs/HEAD`); a fetch or a new branch changes only the refs, so it does not start a card computation (with reftable every ref change counts as HEAD moving, and the order falls back to the time of the `HEAD` stub, because reftable keeps no `logs/HEAD`). `git status` and uncommitted changes change nothing: the index and the working directory are not read on purpose. A repo list that cannot be read (a missing folder, a `workspace.yaml` saved with an error for a moment) keeps the previous states and goes to the log once per identical error, so the repair is no change (but a list that becomes readable only after the start is a change); one comparison runs at a time (16 repos read at once), so a slow network drive does not stack them. Next to the `stat` calls a comparison reads `workspace.yaml` and the projects folder like a list request. The watch is a second service of the `workspace` module in the facade (`repoWatch`), started in `main.ts` after the API and stopped first at shutdown. Comparing `stat` results instead of `fs.watch`, see ADR-63.
- The UI fetches the list at once after every `repos.changed` event (the count of changes is the `refreshKey` of `useRemote`, `lib/use-repo-watch.ts`; a change while a request is on its way, or while the window is hidden, makes one fetch after the response or when the window shows, `lib/refresh-gate.ts`), and polls it only for the working directory state, which the watch does not see: every 30 s while the events are live, every 10 s without an event connection (`REPO_LIST_PACE` in `lib/repo-watch.ts`), counted from the previous response (a slow git does not pile up requests) and only while the window is visible; a new HEAD in the list fetches the overview and the tree again. The repo list itself is not a `/ws` topic (ADR-36), because its snapshot is a `git status` of every repo; the event carries only the revision.

### 4.6 `cards` and `graph`

A card is built in two steps. The deterministic step reads the repo's files: `pom.xml` / `build.gradle(.kts)` (artifacts, dependencies on Feign/WebClient clients, OpenAPI configuration), `application*.yml` (URLs, ports, topic names, environment variable names = secret candidates), `package.json` (packages, scripts), OpenAPI files, Kafka definitions. Extractors are separate files per build system, each returns only facts with a position in a file. The LLM step gets the card and selected fragments and returns a description and connection proposals, marked `origin: 'llm'`.

The first slice (ADR-25): the deterministic step for build files, tools and the mechanism inventory, repo tags with a filter in the repo list, and the card in MCP. The second (ADR-30): Spring Boot apps, HTTP endpoints and clients from Java and Kotlin code, Spring configuration with profiles, and backend addresses in the frontend. Topics, environment variable names (secret candidates) and the LLM step are the next steps.

- **Reading the commit** (`read-snapshot.ts`): `git ls-tree -r -z --long <commit>` gives the list of files with sizes, and `git cat-file --batch` in one process the contents of the files some extractor selects (each has its own `reads…File` function, combined in `readsCardFile`): `pom.xml`, `build.gradle(.kts)`, `settings.gradle(.kts)`, `gradle/libs.versions.toml`, `package.json`, `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, mise configuration and `.tool-versions` in the repo root, `.nvmrc`, `.node-version`, `.java-version`, `.sdkmanrc`, Maven and Gradle wrapper properties, Spring configuration (`src/main/resources/application*` and `bootstrap*`), `.env*` files and frontend proxy configuration. OpenAPI and Swagger specs are chosen by content, whatever their name (ADR-87): `git grep` finds the `.yaml`, `.yml` and `.json` files outside test code with a top-level `openapi` or `swagger` key, or the quoted key in JSON (`SPEC_SEARCH`, at most 200), read with the build files. Besides these, Java and Kotlin source files (ADR-30): `git grep -l -z -I -E` in the commit (without textconv) selects those with a controller, mapping or client annotation, `WebClient`, `RestClient`, `RestTemplate`, `ApiDelegate` (a class implementing a delegate generated from a spec, ADR-87) or a constant with a path (`JVM_SOURCE_PATTERN` in `spring.ts`), from `src/main/` at any depth, and without it only outside `src` directories (no tests; `isJvmSource`); they go through the same `cat-file --batch`. Then the read pulls in the type files those files name (ADR-38): capitalised names from the tokens (`referencedTypeNames`) point to source files with that name (`OrderDto.java`, `OrderDto.kt`, with the same `isJvmSource`), in at most three rounds (controller, DTO, field type, nested field type) and at most 3000 files, each round with one `cat-file --batch` within the shared byte limit. These files go into `RepoSnapshot.typeFiles`: only the contracts read them, not the Spring extractor, so the HTTP facts depend only on the files from the search; type files that did not fit within the limits give one card problem with their count. Files in `node_modules` and `bower_components` do not exist for the card (neither for reading nor for mechanisms). Limits: 1 MB per file, 2000 build and configuration files, 3000 source files (4 MB of `git grep` list) and 64 MB in total; a file git did not return within the limit is a card problem, so an incomplete card says what it is missing. The commit is HEAD read once before both commands (from the git directory's files, otherwise through `cat-file --batch-check`, ADR-79), so a commit made meanwhile does not mix two trees. The working directory does not matter (ADR-14). Files with a BOM and `\r\n` are normalised before the extractors see them. Last, within the same byte limit, the read takes every Java and Kotlin source file for the endpoint reach (`RepoSnapshot.callSources`, `CALL_SOURCES` in `extract-card.ts`, ADR-82): with its blob id from `ls-tree`, and with its text only when no earlier card read that blob (`CallReads` in `call-files.ts`; the kept read is taken right then, so another card cannot drop it before the parse). A kept read counts against the limit with its size as if fetched, so whether the reach fits depends on the commit, not on what the daemon remembers. The extractors do not see these texts, so the HTTP facts still depend only on the files from the search. A cut file list, more than 20,000 such files, one over 1 MB or one that does not fit within the limit leave the card without call sources, with a problem.
- **Extractors** are pure functions on a commit snapshot (`RepoSnapshot`: the list of files and whether git gave all of it, the contents of the ones read, type files and read problems), in `modules/cards/`: `maven.ts` (with the `xml.ts` parser), `gradle.ts` (with the version catalogue in `gradle-catalog.ts` and the comment scanner in `gradle-code-lines.ts`), `npm.ts`, `tools.ts`, `mise-tasks.ts` (ADR-34), `mechanisms.ts`, `spring.ts` and `frontend-http.ts` (ADR-30, below), combined in `extract-card.ts`; `repo-path.ts` handles paths in the repo (with `/`). Endpoint contracts and DTOs (`endpoint-contracts.ts`, ADR-38) read syntax trees from `syntax-tree.ts` (ADR-37): `loadSyntaxParsers()` once per process, `withParseBudget` with a total time for each card (`cards.ts`), then a synchronous `read(language, text, reader)` with a tree-sitter syntax tree, freed after the read, so only values leave the read; a file whose parsing exceeds `PARSE_TIME_LIMIT_MS` gives the result `timeout` instead of a tree, and a read after the card's budget is spent gives `over-budget`. When the grammars cannot be loaded, the card is built without contracts, with a problem (`unavailableParsers`). The files are untrusted (§8), so no regular expression backtracks quadratically on a long line, and workspace globs are matched by dynamic programming, not a regular expression (`src/infra/glob.ts`). A build file that cannot be read (bad XML, JSON, TOML, YAML) is a card problem (`problems`), and the rest of the card is built without it. Every card problem has a code (`CardProblemCode`, ADR-80): `files-not-read` (a read limit or a failed `git grep`: facts from those files are missing), `file-invalid` (a file an extractor could not parse), `parse-incomplete` (Java or Kotlin without syntax trees: a parse time limit, the card's budget, no grammars), `truncated` (the card keeps the first part of a list, such as DTOs, configuration addresses or Spring profiles, or the beginning of a type notation; the rest is missing). `path` is the file or module directory the problem concerns, empty for the repo root or the whole repo.
  - **Build roots.** A build file without a file of the same kind in any parent directory is a root; a deeper one belongs to the build only when the root names it (a Maven module, also from a profile; a Gradle `include`; a workspace package from `workspaces` in `package.json` or from `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, patterns with `*`, `**` and `!`). Thanks to that a `pom.xml` from test fixtures does not reach the card, and a repo with `backend/pom.xml` and `frontend/package.json` without a root has both builds. Every build file on the card has `memberOf`: the file that names it, or `null`.
  - **Maven** reads the pom with its own XML parser with line numbers (`xml.ts`; without attributes, namespaces and DTDs). `groupId` and `version` without a value of their own inherit from `<parent>`; `${…}` in versions substitutes the properties of the pom, of its parents from the repo (`relativePath`, by default `../pom.xml`) and `project.*`, and leaves an unknown property (or one whose value would exceed 4096 characters) in the text. It provides the coordinates of every module; it consumes a parent from outside the repo (`parent`), dependencies (with `scope`, by default `compile`), BOMs from `dependencyManagement` in the `import` scope, and plugins from `build/plugins` (`plugin`, default group `org.apache.maven.plugins`). It skips ordinary `dependencyManagement` entries and dependencies from profiles. The Java version from `maven.compiler.release`, `java.version` or `maven.compiler.source` is a tool hint.
  - **Gradle** does not run scripts (they are Groovy or Kotlin code), it only reads lines in the typical form, without comments (strings are skipped when looking for them, so `exclude("META-INF/*.SF")` does not start a comment; lines over 2000 characters are skipped): `rootProject.name` and `include` from settings (also spread over several lines), `group` and `version` (of the project or the root project), dependencies `configuration("g:a:v")` (also inside `platform(...)`) and from the version catalogue (`libs.…`), plugins `id("…")` with a dot in the name and `alias(libs.plugins.…)`, the Java version from `JavaLanguageVersion.of(N)` or `sourceCompatibility`. A project has `group:name` coordinates only with a known group and name. A dependency composed in code (a variable, a loop, `project(...)`) does not reach the card.
  - **npm** reads the fields of `package.json` separately (a wrong type of one does not take away the rest): a package with a name and without `"private": true` is provided, dependencies from `dependencies`, `devDependencies`, `peerDependencies` and `optionalDependencies` (the scope is the field name). The package manager from `packageManager`, and without it from the lock file in the root directory (`pnpm-lock.yaml`, `yarn.lock`, `bun.lock(b)`, `package-lock.json`, by default npm); workspace packages inherit it from the root. `engines.node` and the version from `packageManager` are tool hints.
  - **Packages from the repo** are not on the list of consumed ones (a module that depends on another module of this repo, a private workspace package), and every consumed package is on the card once, with its first occurrence.
  - **Tools** (`ToolRequirement`): `[tools]` from the mise configuration in the repo root (`mise.toml`, `.mise.toml`, `mise/config.toml`, `.mise/config.toml`, `.config/mise.toml`, `.config/mise/config.toml`; without `mise.local.toml`, `mise.<environment>.toml`, `conf.d/` and configuration in subdirectories) and `.tool-versions` (source `mise`), and hints (source `hint`) from the root and the build directories: `.nvmrc`, `.node-version`, `.java-version`, `.sdkmanrc`, versions from the distribution URLs of the Maven and Gradle wrappers, and from the build extractors the Java version, `engines.node` and the package manager. Not from `mise ls --json` (ADR-25).
  - **Mechanisms** (`Mechanism`) are an inventory from the rules in `mechanisms.ts`: a package by `<ecosystem>:<name>` (Spring Boot, Feign, WebClient, OpenAPI, Kafka, RabbitMQ, Eureka, Config Server and client, Spring Cloud Kubernetes, Jib, React, Angular, Vue, Svelte, Express, NestJS) or a file by path (OpenAPI and Swagger files, `Chart.yaml`, `Dockerfile`, `compose.yaml`, `kustomization.yaml`, YAML in a `k8s/` or `kubernetes/` directory; manifests in other directories, for example `deploy/`, are not recognised), with at most three pieces of evidence. The rules are general; company rules will come from the workspace (ADR-7).
  - **Spring configuration** (`spring-config.ts`, ADR-30): a module is the directory above `src/main` (a repo with several apps has several modules); everything under `src/test`, including a sample project in test resources, is test code and is skipped (code and configuration, `isTestPath`). `application` and `bootstrap` files (`.yml`, `.yaml`, `.properties`) from `src/main/resources`, the profile from the file name (`application-prod.yml`) or from a document activated by `spring.config.activate.on-profile` (formerly `spring.profiles`; YAML `---` documents, `#---` in `.properties`). A profile's view is the configuration without a profile overridden by the profile's documents, and a profile file (`application-prod.yml`) overrides an `on-profile` document from the file without a profile, as in Spring Boot (every `bootstrap*` is below every `application*`); the `default` profile is the configuration without a profile, and a profile with the same address value as `default` is not listed separately (at most 32 profiles per module). Keys are compared as in Spring Boot (ignoring case, `-` and `_`, so `baseUrl` is `base-url`). `placeholders.ts` expands `${key}` and `${key:default}`: nested, with a default value, an unknown key stays as text, and a cycle, a depth over 10 and a result over 4096 characters stop the expansion. Environment variables are not known, so `${PORT:8081}` gives `8081`.
  - **App** (`ProvidedApp`, `kind: "app"`): the module's `spring.application.name` with the port from `server.port` (a number 1–65535 after expansion, otherwise `null`) and evidence from the name line.
  - **HTTP endpoints** (`ProvidedEndpoint`, `kind: "endpoint"`, `spring-endpoints.ts`): a class with `@RestController` or `@Controller`, the path from `server.servlet.context-path` (or `spring.webflux.base-path`), the class's `@RequestMapping` and the method's annotation (`@GetMapping`, `@PostMapping`, `@PutMapping`, `@PatchMapping`, `@DeleteMapping`, `@RequestMapping` with `method` or without it, which means every method: `method: null`), every combination of paths and methods separately (at most 20 per method). Mappings from an interface the controller implements (API-first style, an interface from the OpenAPI generator) count as its own, with evidence in the interface file. Paths are in `value`, `path` or the first argument, in an array `{…}` (Kotlin `[…]`, `arrayOf(…)`), also from constants (`ApiPaths.ORDERS + "/{id}"`, `static final` and `const val` constants from the files read); a constant not in the files read stays as `<Class.NAME>`. The code is read by a tokenizer (`java-tokens.ts`: comments, strings, text blocks) and a simple declaration parser (`java-source.ts`, `java-expr.ts`), without compiling, so an annotation in a comment or a string is not a mapping, and a mapping composed in code (for example `RouterFunction`) does not reach the card. `${…}` in a mapping path is expanded from the module's configuration like an address, with the default after the colon (`@RequestMapping("${openapi.shop.base-path:/api}")` from a generated interface), and an unknown key stays in the path (ADR-87). Such an endpoint has `source: "code"`.
  - **Endpoints from OpenAPI specs** (`openapi-spec.ts`, `openapi-generators.ts`, `openapi-endpoints.ts`, ADR-87), only in a repo with a Maven or Gradle build (a frontend keeps the specs of the backends it calls): a file the search found is a spec when it has `openapi: 3…` or `swagger: "2.0"` and `paths`; JSON goes through the same YAML parser, which gives lines, aliases are not followed, and at most 2000 operations per spec (more is a `truncated` problem; a spec that does not parse is `file-invalid`). An operation is a method key under a path (without `trace`). Where the app serves it follows what the OpenAPI generator writes, checked on real builds: with `interfaceOnly` the generated interface maps the bare path from `paths`, so the path is the context path, the mapping of the implementing controller class and that path; without it the generated controller maps `${openapi.<title in camelCase>.base-path:<server path>}`, expanded from the module's configuration; a spec the build does not generate from documents the API, so the path is the path of the first server address (`servers[0].url` with variables replaced by their defaults, or Swagger `basePath`) and the path from `paths`, with the context path in front unless the server path already starts with it. An operation has `operationId`, a summary (`summary`, else the first line of `description`, at most 200 characters), the JSON request body schema and the schema of the first 2xx response as type names (`Order`, `List<Order>`, `Map<string, Order>`, `string`; local `$ref`s to request bodies and responses are followed). A spec the build generates a client from is not this repo's API: the executions of `openapi-generator-maven-plugin` (and `swagger-codegen-maven-plugin`) and the `openApiGenerate` or `GenerateTask` blocks of `build.gradle(.kts)` name the spec (`inputSpec`, relative to the module, `${project.basedir}`, `$projectDir` or `$rootDir`; with another property in front only its end is compared) and the generator with its `interfaceOnly` option; `spring` without the `spring-cloud` or `spring-http-interface` library, `jaxrs-*` and `*-server` generate a server, every other generator a client. A spec without a generator, or with a server one, belongs to the app of the generating module, else of the module above `src/main`, else to the repo's only app. An operation the code maps (same method and path, variable names aside, else the only mapped endpoint whose handler is named like the `operationId`) puts its `operationId`, summary and spec line into that endpoint's `operation`; any other becomes an endpoint with `source: "openapi"`, the spec as evidence and as handler the method named like the `operationId` in camelCase (as the generator names it: `get-order` → `getOrder`) of a controller or an `…ApiDelegate` implementation without a mapping of its own in the spec's module; with none or several, `handler: null` and no reach. An endpoint with a handler gets its reach like a mapped one (ADR-82).
  - **Endpoint contracts and DTOs** (`endpoint-contracts.ts` with `contract-java.ts` and `contract-kotlin.ts`, ADR-38): an endpoint gets, from the syntax tree of the method in which its mapping line lies (in the controller or the API interface), the type of the `@RequestBody` parameter (`request`) and the return type without wrappers that do not change the content (`ResponseEntity`, `HttpEntity`, `Mono`, `Optional`, `CompletableFuture`, `CompletionStage`, `Callable`, `DeferredResult`, `WebAsyncTask`; `response`; also with Kotlin's `?`; `Flux<T>` stays, `void`, `Unit`, `Void`, `?`, `*` and a wrapper without an argument are `null`, as is a Kotlin function without an explicit type). Types are in type notation: the text from the tree without annotations, with a space only before a word following a word or after `?` (`List<? extends Line>`), longer than 256 characters cut with "…" (DTOs are discovered from the full notation). The method is identified by the name from the handler and the mapping line inside the method (a file may have several classes and overloads), and without the line or without such a method by the first method of the file with that name. `RepoCard.dtos` are the repo's types reachable from these types through field types, in discovery order, at most 500 types, each with at most 200 fields or constants: a record (fields from the header), a `data class` (constructor parameters and properties from the body), a class with fields (instance fields without `static`; in Kotlin properties from the constructor and the body; a type without fields, like a service or an empty record, is not a DTO), an enum (constants). A type is identified by its simple name, and in a qualified name (`Api.Summary`, `java.math.BigDecimal`) by its last part: first the file with that name, then the first file read in which the text `class|record|enum|object <Name>` occurs, so two types with the same name in the repo give the first one, and a type from outside the files read (a library, the JDK) is not a DTO. Parsing the whole card has a total budget of 10 s (§8), checked before every file, so exceeded at most by the time of the last file (parsing up to 1 s and a linear tree read); after it the rest of the endpoints stay without types, and the card has a problem.
  - **Endpoint reach** (`ProvidedEndpoint.reaches`, ADR-82): the client calls on the same card that an endpoint's handler reaches through the repo's Java code, so the graph can say which endpoint makes a call, not only which repo. `call-files.ts` parses every Java source file (`java-calls.ts`: package, imports, types with their supertypes and fields, methods with parameters, local variables and the calls in their bodies; plain values, not nodes) and keeps the read by blob id across repos and commits (the newest 50,000), so a new commit parses only the files it changed. The parse runs on the daemon's thread, so it gives way to other work every 50 ms and stops after 60 s (`CALL_PARSE_BUDGET_MS`). `call-types.ts` resolves a simple type name as the file sees it (single-type import, own package, wildcard imports, then by simple name, preferring the file's module, because services of one repo often copy a class), `call-graph.ts` builds the call graph: a node per method, an edge per method a call can run, the receiver's type from a local variable or parameter (a `var` through the expression it is set to), a field of the class or its supertypes, a capitalised name (a static call) or a method's return type; a call runs the nearest method of the receiver's type and supertypes with the call's argument count (by name alone only when none has it) and each override in a subtype, but no overrides when there are more than 8 subtypes (`MAX_DISPATCH`: a generic DAO or visitor would make every endpoint reach everything), and a call on a type from outside the repo ends the path. A nested type is known by its name within the file (`Outer.Inner`) as imports name it, and an enum's methods count like a class's. A client call belongs to every method whose lines hold its line (a `RestTemplate`, `WebClient` or `RestClient` call in a body, the mapping of a Feign or `@HttpExchange` method). `endpoint-reach.ts` collapses the graph's strongly connected components (`strong-components.ts`, Tarjan with an explicit stack) and computes each component's calls once in reverse topological order, so the cost is linear in the graph, without a depth limit. The handler is `Class.method` from the endpoint: the overload whose lines hold the mapping, or all overloads when the mapping is elsewhere (an API interface); a handler the graph does not find gives that endpoint `reaches: null`. A client whose address differs by profile is on the card once per address with the same calls, which the reach counts once. The result answers "may call": every branch and every implementation counts. Not seen: calls made by the framework (`@EventListener`, `@Scheduled`, `@Async`, security filters, interceptors, AOP), lambda parameters without a type, unqualified use of an outer class's fields and methods from a nested class, and a client chosen at run time; a call reached only from those is reached by no endpoint. A repo with Kotlin source files (no problem: it is not a gap of this commit), or one whose Java files were not all read and parsed (with a problem), has `reaches: null` on every endpoint and `reachTraced: false` on the card, because a reach without part of the code would be wrong rather than incomplete; parsing that ran out of time leaves the card only in memory, like missing syntax trees.
  - **HTTP clients** (`ConsumedHttp`, `kind: "http"`) have a kind (`via`), a name (interface, class or key), the name of the target app (`app`) or an address (`url`) in profiles (`profiles`, with the configuration entry the address comes from: `urlSource`) and calls (`calls`: method and path with a line). An address with `${…}` gives a separate client for every profile in which it has a different value (`default` first). `lb://name` is an app name (the Spring Cloud load balancer). Kinds: `feign` (`@FeignClient`: the app name from `name`, `value` or `serviceId`, the address from `url` or from `spring.cloud.openfeign.client.config.<name>.url` / `feign.client.config.<name>.url`, the prefix from `path` and the interface's `@RequestMapping`, calls from the methods' mapping annotations), `http-interface` (an interface with `@HttpExchange` and `@GetExchange`…), `webclient`, `rest-client` and `rest-template` (`imperative-clients.ts`: the base address from `baseUrl`, `rootUri`, `create(…)` or a `@Value("${…}")` field, calls from `.uri(…)` with the method from the chain (`.get()`, `.method(HttpMethod.POST)`) and from `RestTemplate` methods (`getForObject`, `exchange` with `HttpMethod`…); a part concatenated after a string, for example `"/orders/" + id`, is a path variable `{}`), `config` (`config-clients.ts`: a configuration key with an HTTP or `lb://` address in some profile that the code did not use as a client address, for example `spring.cloud.gateway.routes[0].uri`; without calls).
  - **Backend addresses in the frontend** (`frontend-http.ts`): variables with an HTTP address in `.env*` files next to `package.json` (`via: "env"`, the variable name), the `proxy` field in `package.json` and quoted addresses in the development server's proxy configuration (`vite.config.*`, `webpack.config.*`, `vue.config.*`, `next.config.*`, `proxy.conf.*`, `setupProxy.*`; `via: "proxy"`, the file name). It does not run JS configuration.
  - **Secrets in addresses**: every address on the card loses its user and password (`http://***@host`), query and path segments that look like a token (`withoutCredentials` in `protocol`), and call paths lose their query, before they reach the card (§8).
- **Card cache** (`card-cache.ts`): a card depends only on the commit's tree and the extractor version (`EXTRACTOR_VERSION`), so it is computed once per commit. It lives in daemon memory (the last 1000) and in `<state directory>/cards/<commit>.json` (written through `writeFileAtomic`; a failed write goes to the log). A file with another extractor version or not matching the schema counts as missing (version 6 since the `no-mise` tag stopped depending on problems with code files, 7 since ADR-82, 8 since ADR-87, so cards from before them are computed again). A card for which the parsers did not give some tree because of time or missing grammars (`CardParsers.complete`, `CallFiles.complete`) depends on the moment of computing, so it stays only in memory, and the next daemon start computes it again. Daemon startup removes files beyond the 2000 newest. Two simultaneous requests for the same commit read git once.
- **Card warm-up** (`card-warmup.ts`, ADR-63): the repos the repo watch hands over (§4.5: all of them at the first comparison after the start, then the new ones and those whose HEAD moved) are computed in the background, two at once, the ones handed over last first; a waiting repo handed over again moves to the front, and one being computed is queued again, because its HEAD has moved meanwhile. A request (`cards/card`, the graph, MCP) never waits for the queue: it computes its card itself, so the repo open in the UI is never behind the queue, and the card cache gives both the same job. A repo whose card fails (git cannot read it) is counted as done, its error goes to the daemon log once per repo and message, and comes back to whoever asks for the card. At shutdown the warm-up starts no further card. The progress (`CardWarmupProgress`: `running`, `done`, `total`, counted from the moment the warm-up was last idle) is `cards/warmup` and the `cards.warmup.changed` topic; the "Daemon" view shows it as "Repo cards: computing 12 of 80" or "up to date", and "Overview" shows it while the first graph is computed. The graph does not come in parts during the warm-up: a graph without the repos still being computed would look like a repo nobody calls (`who_calls`), so a graph asked for right after the start still waits for all cards, only no longer for repos the warm-up has already computed.
- **Repo tags** (`RepoTag`, `tags.ts`) are computed on every request from the card, the repo identifier, the remote address and the rules, each with a reason: `java` (a Maven or Gradle build file), `typescript` (a `typescript` dependency), `frontend` (React, Angular, Vue, Svelte), `backend` (Spring Boot, Express, NestJS), `bff` (the word `bff` in the identifier, the remote address or the name of a provided package), `library` (provides a package, and has no app framework or deployment files), `monorepo` (a build file names at least two modules or packages, or at least two build roots in different directories), `no-mise` (neither the commit nor the working directory's `mise.local.toml` has a mise task, below, ADR-34, ADR-86). Tags are orthogonal: "a BFF in Java" is `java` + `bff`. On top of that, **tag rules** (`repoTags` in `workspace.yaml`, by hand only): a tag and patterns of the identifier or the remote address (`*` without `/`, `**` with `/`, ignoring case), for example `core-api: ["*-core", "gitlab.example.com/core/**"]`; the names of a company's teams and groups stay in the user's file (ADR-7).
- **API and UI**: `cards/card {repo}` returns the repo's card and tags, `cards/tags` the tags of every repo from the folder (at most 4 repos at once; a repo git cannot read is `unavailable` with a message). The repo list shows tags under the repo status (reasons in the tooltip) and a filter: every tag with its number of repos, and the selected tags narrow the list to repos that have all of them. Tags are fetched again when the set of repos or the HEAD of one of them changes, and every 5 minutes while the repo events are live, every minute without them (a hand change of the rules, `REPO_TAGS_PACE`). The `no-mise` tag (ADR-34) goes to a repo whose commit stores no mise task (`RepoCard.mise`: mise configuration files in the root and the tasks from their `[tasks]` and from files in the task directories `mise-tasks/`, `.mise-tasks/`, `mise/tasks/`, `.mise/tasks/`, `.config/mise/tasks/`), with the reason "no mise config in the repo root" or "mise.toml declares no tasks"; the repo list shows it as the warning "no mise tasks", and a filter on it picks the repos to normalise. The tag only speaks about the absence of any task; a repo with tasks but without `setup` or `build` learns that from a skipped step of the Batch command (§4.8). The configuration is the files from the root (`mise.toml`, `.mise.toml`, `mise/config.toml`, `.mise/config.toml`, `.config/mise.toml`, `.config/mise/config.toml`, `.config/mise/mise.toml`) and `*.toml` from `conf.d` directories; a config file the card did not read (limits) or a commit file list cut at its limit disables the tag, because it is unknown whether the repo has tasks; a problem with other files (a failed source file search, unread type files) does not. Tasks from the user's global configuration and from `task_config.includes` are not counted, and the name of a file task is the path in the task directory with `:` (without the extension shortening mise does; `_default` gives the directory name); the "mise tasks" tab gives the full list, §4.7.
