The "Card" tab in the repo details, the one a repo opens on (the README tab is last, `lib/repo-tabs.ts`), shows tags with reasons, problems ("Incomplete", each with its kind), mechanisms, tools, apps (name, port), HTTP endpoints (method, path, request and response types, handler), DTOs (kind, name, fields with types or enum constants), HTTP clients (kind, name, app or address with profiles, calls), provided and consumed packages, and build files, every fact with evidence.
- **MCP**: `list_repos` gives the tag names of every repo and accepts `tags` (repos with each of the given ones), `get_repo` the repo's tag names, and `get_repo_card` the whole card with tags and reasons (§5.3). Tags do not take away the rest: `get_repo` for a repo whose card cannot be computed has empty tags, and `list_repos` without a filter with a bad `repoTags` gives repos without tags (with a filter, a configuration error). `list_repos` always computes tags, so a call after startup with hundreds of new commits reads git for every repo the warm-up (above) has not computed yet; after that the cards are in memory.

The graph = joining `consumes` with `provides` by keys: URL/base-path, artifact name, topic name, environment variable name with a host. Every edge has `confidence` and `evidence` (file, line). MCP queries work on this graph and return identifiers with evidence, not prose.

The first slice of the graph (ADR-29): edges from packages, package aliases and manual edges from `workspace.yaml`, the "Overview" view and the MCP tools `who_calls` and `subgraph_for`. The second (ADR-30): HTTP edges from the clients and endpoints on the cards, app aliases and `find_endpoint`. Topics and service states in the subgraph will come together with the extractors and services.

- **A node** (`GraphNode`) is a repo from the list (today a repo, not a service): the identifier, the remote address, tag names, the number of distinct packages it builds, the names of Spring Boot apps and the number of HTTP endpoints from the card, and the reason when the card cannot be computed (`problem`; the repo stays in the graph without edges from cards).
- **An edge from a package** (`kind: "package"`): repo A consumes a package (`consumes`) that repo B builds (`provides`), in the same namespace (`PackageEcosystem`: Maven `group:artifact` also from Gradle, npm, Gradle plugins). The direction is from the repo that consumes to the one consumed. Evidence from both sides: the build file and dependency line in A (with version and scope), and the file and line in which B builds it. Confidence `exact`, and when several repos from the list build the same package (two clones of one repo, a fork), an edge to each with `medium` confidence; a repo that builds a package with two build files (for example while moving from Maven to Gradle) counts once. The dependency scope (for example `test`) is in the evidence, it does not change the edge.
- **An HTTP edge** (`kind: "http"`, ADR-30, `http-index.ts` and `http-edges.ts`): an HTTP client from repo A's card calls repo B. The target is the first of: an app with the name from the client (`app`: Feign `name`, `lb://name`) in B's card; an app alias from `workspace.yaml` for that name or the address's host; an app or repo with a name equal to the first part of the host (`http://payments-service:8080`, `orders-api.shop.svc.cluster.local`; a repo's name is its identifier or the last part of its remote address); an app with the port from an address on `localhost` (also `127.0.0.1`, `host.docker.internal`), like an address from a frontend's `.env` in development mode. Without any of these the graph looks for a repo whose endpoints match the call paths (by method and path: a variable `{id}`, `{}` or `*` matches one segment, `**` the rest; a path without a fixed segment, like `/{id}`, does not point to a repo). An endpoint without a fixed segment (`/**`, `/{path}`: forwarding everything to a SPA) is neither a target nor evidence of a call. An app name, alias or port that points only to the client's own repo ends the search without an edge (an app calls another app from the same repo). The edge key is the app name, alias, host, `localhost:<port>` or, for a match by path, the client name. Confidence: `exact` when the app name points to one repo and every call of the client has a matching endpoint in it; `high` for the app name or an alias alone; `medium` for a host equal to a repo name, a port on `localhost`, paths matching the endpoints of one repo, or an app name in several repos (a clone, a fork); `low` for paths matching the endpoints of several repos. Evidence: the client (kind, name, address with profiles), the configuration entry with the address, the target app, and call–endpoint pairs (at most 5 per client). A client in several profiles is one edge with evidence from each (at most 20 pieces of evidence), with the higher confidence. A repo does not connect to itself, and a client without a target (for example a Zipkin address) gives no edge.
- **User entries** (the `graph` key in `workspace.yaml`, schema `GraphConfig`, by hand only, like `repoTags`): `packages` are package aliases (package name → the repo that delivers it), working only for a package no repo from the list builds, with `high` confidence; `apps` are app aliases (an app name or an address host, ignoring case → repo), for when the name from a client is not the `spring.application.name` of any card (an address behind a gateway, DNS from another name), with `high` confidence (ADR-30); `edges` are manual edges (`from`, `to`, `note`, kind `manual`, confidence `exact`), for example an HTTP call through a gateway the cards do not see. A repo is given by its identifier or its remote address (`host/path`, ignoring case; the address survives a directory rename, ADR-18). The evidence of an entry is `workspace.yaml` (`repo: null`, full path) and a sentence with the entry's name. An empty subkey (`packages:` without entries) means no entries. An entry pointing to a repo outside the list, or a manual edge from a repo to itself, goes to the graph's `configProblems`. A bad `graph` key (for example `edges` is not a list) or `repoTags` is not a graph error: the graph is built without user entries (or without tags from rules), and the message goes to `configProblems`, so the edges from cards stay.

```yaml
graph:
  packages:
    com.example:orders-client: orders-api            # package -> repo (identifier or host/path)
  apps:
    orders.internal.example.com: orders-api          # app name or host -> repo
  edges:
    - from: gitlab.example.com/shop/shop-web
      to: orders-bff
      note: HTTP /api/orders
```

- **Computing** (`modules/graph/`): `Cards.all()` gives the cards, addresses and tags of all repos (at most 4 repos at once, cards from memory by commit), and the pure function `buildGraph` joins them with the `graph` entries; the graph has no persistent state: the module keeps only the last computed graph with the input key (commits, addresses and tags of all repos, reasons for missing cards, and the `graph` configuration), because HTTP edges compare calls with the endpoints of all repos (ADR-30), so a new commit, a new repo in the folder and a hand change of `workspace.yaml` work at once. The cost of a request is the cost of the tag list: reading HEAD and the remote of every repo. Edges are sorted, and the same pair of repos, kind and key is one edge. Package names from repos do not reach the object prototype (aliases are in a `Map`).
- **Queries**: `who_calls(repo)` are the edges to the repo (who consumes it), `subgraph(repo, depth)` the repos within `depth` edges in both directions (1–3, `MAX_SUBGRAPH_DEPTH`) and the edges between them. A repo outside the list is `not_found`. `findEndpoint(query, method?)` (ADR-30, ADR-87, `find-endpoint.ts`, `endpoint-query.ts`) are the endpoints from the cards of all repos matching the query, best match first (`match`): `path` when the query is a path that matches the whole endpoint path (also a concrete one: `/api/orders/42` finds `/api/orders/{id}`), `path-end` when the shorter of the two paths is the end of the longer one with at least one fixed segment equal on both sides (the query lacks the context path, or has a gateway prefix), `text` when every word of the query is in the path, handler (`Class.method`), app name, `operationId` or summary, ignoring case. A query is a path when it is one word with `/` (the leading `/` is optional); a whole address loses its scheme, host and query, and an HTTP method in front of the path narrows like the `method` argument, which wins over it. With a method only endpoints of that method and without a method. Each found endpoint has `source` and `operation` from the card. Each with the repos that call it (`callers`: repo, client kind and name, full call path, confidence as for an edge, evidence, and `from`: the endpoints of the caller repo whose reach holds that call, ADR-82) and the endpoints of other repos it calls (`calls`: its reach joined with the client's targets as for an edge), at most 50 (`truncated`). `from` is `[]` when no endpoint reaches the call (a scheduled job, a listener) and `null` when the card did not trace (`reachTraced`) or an endpoint of the repo has no reach, since that one might make the call; `calls` is `null` when the endpoint has no reach. `endpoint-calls.ts` joins them by client name and the call's file and line, each callee once (a client split by profile can lead to one repo twice). Following `from` and `calls` hop by hop is the impact of a change to an endpoint across repos.
- **API and UI**: `graph/graph` returns the whole graph (`RepoGraph`). The "Overview" view (`GraphView`, Ctrl+4) draws it at full width in columns: a column is the longest path from a repo nobody consumes (frontends on the left, libraries on the right, like the scene layers in §5.5; edges going back in a cycle are skipped when computing columns), and the order within a column comes from the average position of the neighbours (barycentre), so that lines cross less often. The UI computes the layout without a graph library (`lib/graph-layout.ts`): repos are HUD cards, the edges of one pair of repos are one curve in SVG, manual ones dashed. Repos without edges are below the drawing ("No connections"). Selecting a repo highlights its neighbourhood, and the panel on the right shows the repo's apps, the number of packages and endpoints, whom the repo consumes and who consumes it, with the kind (package, HTTP, manual), keys, confidence and evidence; without a selection the panel has the counts, errors in the `graph` entries and the path of `workspace.yaml`. "Overview" fetches the graph at once after a `repos.changed` event, otherwise every 5 minutes while the events are live and every 30 s without them (`GRAPH_PACE`), and on a button.

### 4.7 `mise`: repo tasks and running them

The first slice of the `mise` and `procs` modules (ADR-20): the "mise tasks" tab in the repo details shows the mise tasks of a repo from the projects folder, runs them and stops them; since ADR-59 they also run in a task's worktree. Task logs are in the `logs` module (§4.2, ADR-28); services (port, probe, leases) are the next step.

- **Log**: the task's output goes to the `logs` module as a `mise-task` source (§4.2), live in the tab under the task row; a run in a task worktree is a `task-mise-task` source, shown in the task panel.
- **The task list** is `mise tasks ls --json` in the repo root on every request (the UI polls while the tab is open: every 2 s when something runs, otherwise every 10 s, because in a trusted repo mise evaluates `[env]` while doing it), without hidden ones (`hide`) and without names mise would take for an option. No `mise` on `PATH`, configuration without trust (`untrusted`) and another mise error (for example `mise.toml` syntax) are list states, not API errors (likewise an exceeded time limit); mise warnings (`mise WARN`) that are not the cause are removed from the message. A running task stays until it is stopped even when it disappeared from the configuration or mise stopped giving the list (for example an error in `mise.toml` while it works): a state without a list carries `running`.
- **A run** is `mise run <task>` in the repo root through `procs`, only for a task from the current list (§1.2); a running task returns its state instead of a second group. No mise at start is `mise_unavailable`, a task outside the list `not_found`. Every method that acts on a run takes a target (`MiseTarget`): the repo root (`task: null`) or the worktree of a task of that repo (ADR-59). A run in a worktree has its own group owner (`task-mise:<task>:<repo>:<mise task>`, a prefix no repo id can give, and reached only through its task's repo), so the same mise task can run in the root and in a worktree at once, and its list (`mise tasks ls`) comes from the worktree. A run in a worktree gets the task port in `SERVER_PORT` and `PORT` (`TASK_PORT_ENV`, ADR-61), replacing the user's own values, so a server it starts (Spring Boot reads `SERVER_PORT` as `server.port`) does not take the root's port.
- **Trust**: mise does not read configuration with `[env]`, templates or hooks until the user trusts it, and `mise run` itself trusts its configuration automatically. The `trust` action (`mise trust` in the repo root, without parent directories) is only in the UI, with a warning that mise configuration can run programs (§8). A task worktree (ADR-59) is never trusted with `mise trust`, which would trust its path for good, whatever the agent writes there later. A run there needs the repo root's config trusted (`mise tasks ls` in the root succeeds) and the worktree's mise config unchanged since the user trusted it (§4.4); then the daemon adds the worktree to `MISE_TRUSTED_CONFIG_PATHS` (mise's list of trusted directories, separated like `PATH`, after the user's own) in the environment of that one `mise tasks ls` and `mise run`. Otherwise `mise_unavailable` names the changed files and says the user trusts them in the task panel. A successful `trust` calls `onTrusted`, so `agents` tells the sessions this repo's config refused (ADR-92).
- **For the Batch command** (§4.8) the module has two runs awaited until the group ends: `runToEnd` (a task from the current list, `null` when the repo does not have it; a task already running, for example from the repo details, does not start a second time, and the result is its end) and `installTools` (`mise install` in the repo root, its own group owner `mise-install:<repo>`, because a task may be called `install`). Both take an `AbortSignal`: an abort before the start runs no process, and after the start it stops the group with the same stop as the repo details; when the stop cannot be confirmed, the result is the error `stop_unconfirmed` instead of waiting further.
- **The agent through MCP** (ADR-41, §5.3): `run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `wait_for_mise_task` and `list_running_mise_tasks` are the same `start`, `stop` and `running` as in the UI (with an optional `task`, ADR-59), plus `waitForEnd`: the current run after the end of the group confirmed in the system (`procs.ended`) or after the time limit, and an abort by the client ends only the waiting, not the task. Tasks run by the agent and from the UI are the same groups, so the agent sees and stops also a task started with the button, and the UI the agent's task.
- **Running tasks of all repos** (`mise/running`, `RunningMiseTask` with `task`: the task whose worktree it runs in, `null` in the repo root): a read from the module's memory (tasks started by the daemon whose group has not ended yet), without asking mise and without reading the folder; from it the dashboard (§5.5) knows in which repo something is running, without `mise tasks ls` on every repo every few seconds.
- Short `mise` calls (`tasks ls`, `trust`) go through `mise-cli.ts`: an argument array, without a shell, a 15 s limit and 4 MB of output; they do not need a process group.

### 4.8 `batch`: the Batch command

The Batch command (ADR-27) is selected steps on selected repos with one button in the "Repos" view: sync, tools, dependencies and build, with the result of every step in every repo. The module runs nothing itself, it only composes `workspace` and `mise` on top of them.

- **Steps** (`BatchStep`) always run in a repo in this order, regardless of the order in the request: `sync` (sync of one repo, §4.5, ADR-21; `vcs` syncs one `.git` directory at a time anyway, so a parallel "Sync all" does not get in the way; a repo being cloned is a step error), `tools` (`mise install` in the repo root: the JDK, Node and the rest from the repo's mise configuration), `setup` (the mise task `setup`, that is dependencies) and `build` (the mise task `build`). The task names are the convention from `CONTEXT.md`; a repo without a given task has the step `skipped` with a reason and goes on. The daemon builds the commands from the step name (§1.2); the client gives only repo identifiers and steps from the list.
- **Step result** (`BatchStepState`): `waiting`, `running`, `done` (sync with the branch and number of new commits), `skipped` (with a reason; a skipped sync does not stop the repo, because the next steps work on what is there locally), `failed` (message and the last lines of the process output; the repo's remaining steps are `skipped`, and the other repos go on) or `cancelled`. A mise step ends `done` at code 0, and `cancelled` when the user stopped the group: by cancelling the command, by stopping the task in the repo details, or by daemon exit.
- **Parallelism**: at most 3 repos at once (a Java or frontend build takes several cores and GBs of memory, and `mise install` downloads JDKs), step by step within a repo. Tasks go through `procs` under the same owner as from the repo details, so the repo details show them as running and can stop them, and a second start of the same task joins the running one instead of starting a second group.
- **Cache**: a rerun is cheap thanks to mise, not to Trackwall. mise skips a task with `sources` and `outputs` when the outputs are newer than the sources, and `depends` runs a task's dependencies once per `mise run`. `build` with `depends = ["setup"]` will run `setup` a second time in the same command, unless `setup` has `sources` and `outputs`; a deliberate choice (ADR-27). With the `setup` and `build` steps mise also installs missing tools itself, so the `tools` step exists to make the installation visible as a separate result.
- **Cancellation** (`cancel`): waiting repos and steps that have not started end as `cancelled`, and an ongoing mise step gets a group stop (`AbortSignal`, §4.7). An ongoing sync cannot be cancelled, because git is not in a process group (§8); it ends by itself. A group whose killing cannot be confirmed ends the step with the error `stop_unconfirmed` (the group stays `stopping` in the repo details, from where the stop can be retried), so a cancellation always ends the command. Without cancellation the command waits for a group whose end cannot be confirmed (for example a task from the repo details after a failed stop) and shows that step as ongoing. A cancellation also stops a task the command joined, although the user started it from the repo details. Daemon exit cancels the command before stopping the groups and rejects the start of a new one, including one that is just checking repos.
- **State** lives only in daemon memory, like sync: `run` returns the last command, one runs at a time (a second start is `bad_request`), and an unknown repo rejects the whole request (`not_found`) before anything moves. The UI polls every 1.5 s during a command and every 10 s outside it; the Batch command may become another `/ws` topic (ADR-36).
- **Choosing repos** is done by the client: the Batch command panel (in place of the repo details, like the sync panel) has a list of repos with checkboxes, all selected by default. The list is the repos after the tag filter of the repo list (ADR-25), so "build all `java` + `bff`" is two clicks in the filter; a filter change starts the selection over (all from the new group).
- **The same directory, another entry**: a link in the projects folder to another repo on the list is a separate repo (ADR-18), so the command groups repos by directory on disk (`realpath`, ignoring case), and the repos of one group run one after another: two `pnpm install` runs or builds at once in one directory would break each other's files. Before every step the command also checks that the repo identifier points to the same directory as at the start, because an identifier from a directory name can move to a new directory (ADR-18); otherwise the step ends with an error.
- **mise monorepo mode** (`monorepo_root`, `mise run '//...:build'`) was checked as an engine and rejected (ADR-27): it requires a configuration file in the projects folder and one process for all repos, and it stops the others after the first error.

### 4.9 `test-results`: results of test runs

The module answers "which tests failed and why" without the agent scrolling a Gradle or Maven log (ADR-60, MCP tool `get_test_results`, §5.3). It runs nothing: it reads the JUnit XML reports a test run left on disk, in the repo root or in a task's worktree (`TestReportsTarget`, the same target as a mise task, ADR-59; the MCP tool refuses a task of another repo with `bad_request`, as for `tail`), on every request (`TestReports.results` in `test-reports.ts`).

- **Finding reports** (`report-files.ts`) needs no configuration: files named `TEST-*.xml` (Maven Surefire and Failsafe, Gradle, Ant) and `junit.xml` (jest-junit, Vitest). The walk goes breadth first and is bounded (20,000 directories, 2000 reports, depth 10, `truncated` when a limit stops it); it skips hidden directories, `node_modules` and `src`, and inside `target` and `build` enters only `surefire-reports`, `failsafe-reports` and `test-results` (with everything under them, as Gradle writes `test-results/<task>/`), so compiled classes are never walked, and under them skips `junitreports`, where TestNG writes a second copy of the results Surefire already has in `TEST-TestSuite.xml`. Symbolic links are not followed. Reports written before `since` are counted (`earlierReports`) and dropped during the walk, before the report limit, so old reports of one module do not crowd out the fresh ones of another. A missing checkout is `not_found`.
- **Reading a report** (`junit-report.ts`) uses the shared parser in `src/infra/xml.ts` (elements with attributes and lines; a `>` inside a quoted attribute value does not end the tag). The root is `<testsuites>` or `<testsuite>`, suites may nest, and the counts come from the `<testcase>` elements and their `<failure>`, `<error>` and `<skipped>` children rather than from the suite attributes. Every `<testcase>` counts, also one repeated with the same class and name: jest and Vitest repeat titles for different tests (`test.each` without a placeholder), so merging them could hide a failure, at the cost that every attempt of Gradle's test-retry plugin counts too; Surefire's `flakyFailure` and `rerunFailure` children are not failures. A failure keeps its type, message (up to 2000 characters) and the start of its stack trace (12 lines) plus every `Caused by` with the frame after it, each line cut to 500 characters, because the first line repeats the message and an assertion on a large object writes hundreds of KB into it. A file larger than 20 MB, not XML or with another root goes to `problems` instead of failing the read.
- **The result** (`TestResults`): totals over every report, the reports newest first with their counts and `modifiedAt` (at most 100), the failures in that order (at most 50) and up to 20 problems, with `truncated` when any list or the walk was cut. `since` leaves out reports written before a time, because a report stays until the next run of its test class overwrites it (Surefire leaves the report of a deleted or filtered-out test class for good). Every text from a report (suite, class, test name, type, message, stack, and the text of a problem) is masked with the values of secrets from the user's environment, as log lines are (§4.2), before it is cut, so a cut never leaves part of a secret. `earlierReports` without `reports` tells the agent the run wrote no report, for example a Gradle `test` task that was up to date.

### 4.10 `verification`: one verdict on a task's changes

A **verification** (ADR-64, `modules/verification/verifications.ts`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/verification.ts`) answers "do the task's changes pass?" with one button in the task panel ("Verify") and one MCP tool (`verify_task`, §5.3), so the user and the agent read the same result. The module runs nothing itself: it composes `mise`, `test-results` and `logs`, as `batch` composes `workspace` and `mise`.

- **The run** is the mise task `test` (`VERIFY_MISE_TASK`, the convention from `CONTEXT.md`, like `setup` and `build` in the Batch command) in the task's worktree, through `mise.start` with the task as target (ADR-59), so it is the same process group, log source (`task-mise-task`) and task port as `run_mise_task` with that task. `start` returns once the run has started, so a worktree without `test` (`not_found`) or with a mise config the user has not trusted (`mise_unavailable`) is the caller's error and leaves no verification. A `test` already running in the worktree (the agent's own `run_mise_task`) is joined, as `mise.start` returns the running run; so is a verification already running, and two starts at once share one (`#starting`). `cancel` stops the run with the same stop as the task panel, only while that run is still the worktree's current `test` (once it has ended and the verdict is being read, a new run there is the agent's), and a stop by the user or the daemon's exit ends it `cancelled`.
- **The verdict** comes once `mise.ended` confirms the group is empty (§4.1): `passed` when the exit code is 0, no test in the reports the run wrote failed or errored, and every report it wrote could be read (a report too large or not JUnit XML may hold a failed test, `unreadReports`), otherwise `failed` with a `message` (the exit code or the signal, or the failed count when the build tool exited with 0 anyway, for example with `ignoreFailures`). The test counts are `testReports.results` with `since` set to the run's start (§4.9), so reports of earlier runs do not count; a run that wrote none passes on its exit code, and the message says so, with the count of earlier reports when Gradle found the tests up to date. On a failure the verification keeps the first 5 failed tests (`VERIFICATION_MAX_FAILURES`, masked as in `get_test_results`), the first 5 errors of this run's log (`logs.errors` over the whole buffer of the source, filtered by the run's `runId`, oldest first, because the first error is usually the cause; `VERIFICATION_MAX_ERRORS`; errors older than the source's buffer are gone, §4.2) and the run's last lines; `get_test_results` and `get_errors` have the rest. When the reports cannot be searched at all, `tests` is `null` with the reason in `message`, and the verdict then rests on the exit code.
- **State** lives in memory only, the last verification of every task, replaced as a whole rather than changed, so a caller holds one moment of it. `get` drops the verification of an ended task. Entries of ended tasks are dropped at the next start. On the daemon's exit `procs` refuses new groups, so no verification starts after the groups were stopped. A verification does not notice later edits in the worktree: it says when it started, and the next one replaces it.
- **The agent** (`verify_task {repo, task, runId?, timeoutSeconds}`) starts or joins the verification and waits for its verdict up to the limit (55 s, like the other waits, counted from the call, so a slow start of mise does not push the call past the MCP client's limit), then returns it `running`; the next call with its `runId` waits for that run and returns it also when it finished in between (`not_found` once a newer verification replaced it), while a call without `runId` after the verdict starts a new verification. The session instructions tell an agent without a `test` in the repo not to add one (a changed mise config stops its mise tasks until the user trusts it, ADR-59) but to say so in its result. The repo and session gate is that of `run_mise_task` (a task session only its own task, a session in a repo root only tasks of its repo, the supervisor none, told to read the verdict in `get_session`); the supervisor and the delegating session read the last verification in `get_session`. The session instructions of a task session ask it to verify its task before `report_result` (§4.4), so a finished task usually shows its verdict already.
- **UI** (`TaskVerification.tsx`, `lib/use-task-verification.ts`, `lib/verification.ts`): "Verify" in the task panel's header (the run is then left out of the panel's mise tasks of the worktree, which would list it a second time); below the summary the "Verification" section with the verdict, the mise task, when it started and how long it took, the test counts, the message, the failed tests (class without package and test name, the message) and the first lines of each log error, the run's last lines when neither says why, "Log" with the run's log and "Cancel" while it runs. The panel polls `verification/get` every second while it runs and every 5 s otherwise; a verification started by the agent shows within those 5 s.
- Not yet: a verdict on the Sessions list rows and the dashboard, a mise task other than `test` per repo (a setting in `workspace.yaml`), a fingerprint of the worktree to mark a verdict as outdated after later edits, keeping verifications across a restart, running `build` or a service probe as part of the verification.

### 4.11 `day-start`: Start my day

The **day start** (ADR-70, `modules/day-start/`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/day-start.ts`) is one action for the start of a working day: "Start my day" in the supervisor panel (`dayStartApi.start`, `lib/use-day-start.ts`, polled like a sync) syncs every repo, computes the **day report** and starts a new supervisor whose first message asks it to brief the user from that report. The daemon computes, the supervisor judges: the report is one `get_day_report` call (§5.3) instead of `get_repo` for every repo, whose README alone is up to 32,000 characters.

- **Steps** (`DayStart`, `DayStartRun.step`): `sync` (`workspace.syncAndWait`, the same sync of the projects folder as "Sync all", joined when one runs, ADR-21), `report` and `supervisor`. One runs at a time, in memory like a sync; a second start returns the running one. It is refused before anything runs (`bad_request`) while a supervisor starts or runs (`agents.assertNoSupervisor`): the user stops it first, so no conversation ends without them; without a projects folder; and with an invalid `dayStart` key (`invalid_config`). A step that throws ends the run with `error`, which the panel shows with the step.
- **The report** (`day-report.ts`, `DayReport`) reads only git refs and objects after the sync, never the network (`vcs/commit-range.ts`): for each repo the commit of its main remote's default branch (`refs/remotes/<remote>/HEAD`, as sync resolves it), compared with that repo's commit at the last day start: `new` with `total` and the newest five commits (`DAY_REPORT_COMMITS`), `rewritten` when the old commit is no longer an ancestor (a force push; then the newest commits), `first-seen` for a repo the last day start did not know, nothing when the branch has not moved. The remote branch rather than the local default branch, because sync leaves a dirty or diverged local branch where it was, and the news is what came onto the server. A repo is listed only with commits or with a sync result other than `updated`/`up-to-date` (a repo without a remote, skipped `no-remote` every day, is quiet); the rest are counted (`quietRepos`). Every open task gets its newest running session's state and `needsAttention`, when it last reported, and `behind`: the commits on its repo's remote default branch that its base commit does not have. A repo git cannot read loses its commits (logged), not the report.
- **State**: `day-start.json` (`DayStartFile`) keeps the last report and the commit of each repo's remote default branch, by repo root as the sync joins its results (a repo whose branch or commits could not be read keeps the commit from before, so what lands meanwhile is reported next time), so "since last time" survives a restart and an update, and `get_day_report` returns the same report until the next day start. It is written once the report is computed, before the supervisor starts, so a failed supervisor start keeps the report. An unreadable file is logged and read as no day start: the next one compares with nothing and writes a good file.
- **The supervisor** (`agents.startSupervisorWith`): a new supervisor in place of the ended ones (as `start-supervisor`, ADR-58) with `--interactive=<first message>` and `promptOnStart`, as a task's session gets its prompt (ADR-31), so it works from the start without typing into its terminal. The first message (`first-message.ts`) is Trackwall's general part (read the report, brief, use the repo host and issue tracker tools if there are any, at most three proposals) followed by the user's `dayStart.prompt` from `workspace.yaml`, for what only the user knows, such as an issue tracker query (ADR-7). How a repo and a task map onto the repo host (the remote's path is the project, a task's branch is its merge request's source branch) is in the supervisor's session instructions, which every supervisor gets.
- Not yet: a repo whose directory was renamed or moved with the projects folder comes back `first-seen` (its commit is kept by repo root); sessions in a repo root that wait for the user (the supervisor reads them with `list_sessions`); differences of cards and graph between two day starts, persistent task proposals and rejections, a "Today" card with the briefing in Home, a day start at the first window of a working day, `mise install` from the briefing when a repo's mise config changed.

### 4.12 `merge-requests`: from a finished task to a merge request

A task's **merge request** (ADR-77, `modules/merge-requests/`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/merge-requests.ts`, host specifics in `vcs/hosted-merge-request.ts` and `vcs/branch-push.ts`) is where a finished task leaves Trackwall: the user pushes the task branch from the task panel, opens the repo host's own new merge request form with the title and description they approved, and creates the merge request there; the panel then follows that merge request and its pipeline. The daemon writes nothing on the repo host: the only write is the push, with the user's own git credentials.

- **The branch against the remote** (`get`, `TaskBranchPush`): the main remote (`origin` or the only one, as for sync, ADR-21), the task's commits since its base and its uncommitted files (`agents.taskChangesSummary`), and the task branch against `refs/remotes/<remote>/<branch>`: whether it is published, how many commits are not pushed and how many the remote has that the task does not. Only refs, never the network, so a push by someone else shows after the next sync or push.
- **The push** (`push`): `git push --no-follow-tags --no-recurse-submodules -- <remote> refs/heads/<branch>:refs/heads/<branch>` in the task's worktree, with `core.hooksPath` set to an empty directory the daemon makes for the push `remote.<remote>.receivepack` pinned to `git-receive-pack` and `push.gpgSign=false`, with the network environment of sync (no prompts, §4.5) and a 5-minute limit, in the same per-`.git` queue as sync, because both write remote-tracking refs. Never forced: a remote that moved on (a colleague's commit, an amended commit of the agent) refuses it with git's message (`repo_unavailable`), and the panel shows the branch behind. Refused (`bad_request`) without commits, and while another push of the task runs; refused too when the remote is on no known repo host (`project: null`), when its address is not HTTPS or SSH (a local path, `file://`, `ext::` or `git://`, which let configuration run a program or reach a repo the agent wrote), and when any address git would push to (`git remote get-url --push --all`: every `pushurl`, or every `url` without one, after `pushInsteadOf`) is not the fetch address, compared as `host/path`, because the user approves the repo host the panel names. Without any hooks, because `core.hooksPath` may point into the worktree (`.husky`), where the agent writes (§8): `--no-verify` would skip only `pre-push`, and git also runs `reference-transaction` when the push updates the remote-tracking branch. A pre-push hook such as Git LFS's therefore does not run either.
- **The repo host** (`hosted-project.ts`): the remote's address (`host/path`, ADR-21) is a project on a repo host from the settings with the same host name (its port kept for links), or on GitHub at `github.com`, which needs no entry; any other host is `project: null`, and the panel says to add it in Settings. The path must be a `RemoteRepoName`, since it goes into API paths and the form's address.
- **The form** (`openForm`, `MergeRequestDraft`): the daemon opens the repo host's new merge request page in the browser (`openUrl`): on GitLab `-/merge_requests/new` with `merge_request[source_branch]`, `[target_branch]`, `[title]` and `[description]`, on GitHub `compare/<target>...<branch>?quick_pull=1` with `title` and `body`. The target is the task's base branch, or the host's default when the task has none. The description is at most `MERGE_REQUEST_DESCRIPTION_MAX` (4000) characters, and the daemon cuts it further (by code points, ending with "…") until the whole address fits in 7000 characters (`FORM_URL_MAX`): GitLab behind nginx refuses addresses over 8 KB, and escaped Polish or emoji grow up to nine times. Refused while the branch has unpushed commits, so the form never shows less than the user reviewed, and while the branch already has an open merge request. The draft comes from the UI (`lib/merge-request.ts`): the task's title or the first line of its prompt, and the agent's `report_result` summary (or the prompt) followed by the verdict of the last verification; the user edits both before the form opens.
- **The merge request and its pipeline** (`Hosting.mergeRequest`, `MergeRequestLookup`): found by the task branch as its source branch, the most recently updated one in any state, through `glab api` (`projects/<path>/merge_requests?source_branch=` and then the one merge request, which carries `head_pipeline`) or `gh api` (`repos/<path>/pulls?head=<owner>:<branch>`, then the check runs (up to five pages of 100; more than that counts as `pending`, so an unread failure never shows as a pass) and the combined status of its head commit, summed up worst first: a failed check fails the pipeline, one still running keeps it running). So a merge request made in any way (the form, the user's own push, another MCP server) shows the same. Looked up only once the branch is published, and kept for 15 s per task (a push drops it), so several windows and the panel's 30-s poll make one call; a failed lookup is `unavailable` with the message of `gh` or `glab` and is not kept.
- **UI** (`TaskMergeRequest.tsx`, `lib/use-task-merge-request.ts`, `lib/merge-request.ts`): a "Merge request" section below the verification, once the task has a commit: the remote and the branch in one line ("2 commits · 1 not pushed · 3 files not committed"), "Push", "Open merge request" with the draft's title and description, and once one exists its number and title, its state and its pipeline, each a link to the repo host.
- Not yet: the merge request on the Sessions list rows and the dashboard, a notification when the pipeline fails, the pipeline's failed jobs and their logs for the task's agent, the merge request in `get_session` for the supervisor (today it reads the repo host with the user's own MCP servers, ADR-70), Git LFS objects on push, forks.

### 4.13 `local-config`: local config backed up outside the repo

**Local config** (ADR-86, `modules/local-config/`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/local-config.ts`, git in `vcs/local-files.ts` and `vcs/backup-repo.ts`) is the files a repo keeps outside git on purpose: `mise.local.toml` (mise tasks, tools and variables only for this clone, which mise reads on top of `mise.toml`) and `.worktreeinclude` (ADR-61) (`LOCAL_CONFIG_FILES`, nothing else, so no `.env` with secrets goes along by accident). When a repo's rules do not allow pushing mise configuration, the user keeps the mise tasks they worked out in `mise.local.toml`, and Trackwall makes sure the file survives a deleted folder, a new clone and a second computer.

- **The local config store** is `.trackwall-local` in the projects folder (`LOCAL_CONFIG_STORE_DIR`), so it lies on the same disk and under the same antivirus exclusion as the repos, and the repo list skips it like every name with a dot. A repo's files go to `<store>/<repo folder>/<file>`, by the name of the repo's folder, which does not change when its identifier gets a suffix (ADR-18). The store is a git repo on `main` the daemon creates with the first backup (`ensureBackupRepo` in `vcs/backup-repo.ts`). An agent can write into it like into any folder, so before every commit and push the daemon checks it: the folder and its `.git` are real directories (not a link, not a `gitdir:` file pointing at another repo), HEAD is on `main`, and its own `.git/config` holds only the keys `git init`, `git clone` and `git remote add` write; any other key (a filter, a `receivepack`, an `sshCommand`, a `pushurl`) stops the backup with a problem naming it, until the user removes it.
- **The backup** (`LocalConfig.backUp`, started by `main.ts` every 5 s like the repo watch, ADR-63): the store's last commit is the backup. For every repo and file a `lstat`, compared by file key (`infra/file-key.ts`) with the last comparison, so an unchanged file is not read, and the store's `main` is read from its files: a pass with nothing changed starts no git process (a test with 20 repos guards it, as ADR-79), and git runs only for a changed file, through the same slots as every local git call. A changed file is read with `infra/regular-file.ts` (a regular file only, never through a link or a FIFO, at most 256 KB, `MAX_LOCAL_CONFIG_BYTES`) and compared with the file in the store's last commit; a different one that the repo's index does not track (`git ls-files`; a tracked file is the repo's, not local config) is committed. All files of one pass are one commit, built from the content the daemon read with plumbing and a temporary index (`hash-object -w --no-filters`, `write-tree`, `commit-tree`, `update-ref` with the old value), so no filter or attribute applies, every command with `core.hooksPath` pointed at an empty directory (even `read-tree` and `update-index` run the `post-index-change` hook), under the identity `Trackwall <trackwall@localhost>`, without signing (`Back up orders: mise.local.toml; web: .worktreeinclude`); then the paths are updated in the store's index and written to its folder (a new file renamed over the old one), so `git status` there stays clean. A file is marked as compared only once its commit is made, so a failed commit is tried again with the next pass; a file that cannot be read is a problem of its own and does not hold up the repo's other file. The repo's file wins: when the store's last commit changes under the daemon (a pull, a projects folder that moved, a restart) every file is compared again, and a file that differs is committed on top, with the other version kept in the history. A file deleted from a repo stays in the store. Problems of a pass (a link, a file over the limit, a refused store, a git error) are logged once and shown with the store until a pass has none.
- **The push**: whenever `main` is at a commit the daemon has not pushed since it started, `main` goes to the branch of the same name on the store's main remote (`origin` or the only one, ADR-21), in the background, one at a time, so a hanging remote never holds up a backup, never forced, without hooks and with `receivepack` pinned (as ADR-77). Only to an HTTPS or SSH address (`networkAddress` in `vcs/remote.ts`, as ADR-77): a local path or a network share is a repo an agent may have written, and its `pre-receive` hook would run as the user, beyond the reach of `core.hooksPath` on the pushing side. A failed push is tried again with the first pass a minute later. The daemon adds no remote: the user adds one with git (a private repo on a repo host), and without one the backups stay on this computer. A push the remote refuses (another computer pushed first) stays failed until the user pulls in the store; the next pass then commits this computer's files on top where they differ. The daemon never pulls.
- **State and actions** (`localConfigApi`): `store` (the folder, the last backup from the store's log, the remote as `host/path`, or only its name when it is not an HTTPS or SSH address, the last push, the problem), `repo` (each file against the store's last commit: `absent`, `tracked`, `backed_up` or `pending` with whether git ignores it, or `missing`: only the store has it), `restore` (writes the missing files from the store's last commit into the repo root, never over a file: one that appeared meanwhile is skipped, then `ignore` for those written) and `ignore` (adds `/<file>` for every local config file git neither tracks nor ignores to `info/exclude` of the git directory the repo shares with its worktrees, appended and only once, never through a link, so a task worktree ignores it too and an agent's `git add -A` does not take it into a merge request). Restore and Ignore are only in the UI (§8).
- **Task worktrees** get `mise.local.toml` as before through `.worktreeinclude` (ADR-61), which Restore brings back with it, and the trusted mise config of a worktree includes it (ADR-59).
- **The repo card** (§4.6) reads `mise.local.toml` from the working directory on every tag request (`cards/local-mise.ts`, not cached with the card, since it changes without a commit), so a repo whose mise tasks are all local does not get `no-mise`.
- **UI**: the "Local config" section above the mise tasks in the repo's "mise tasks" tab (`RepoLocalConfig.tsx`, `lib/local-config.ts`) with each file's state, "Restore from the store" and "Ignore locally"; the "Local config store" section in Settings (`LocalConfigStoreSurface.tsx`) with the folder, the last backup and the push.
- Not yet: a pull from the remote, a list of repos that can be restored in the Repos view, other local files (a local Spring profile) by a setting, the store's own folder as a setting.

### 4.14 `error-journal`: Trackwall's own errors

**The error journal** (ADR-94, `modules/error-journal/`, contract in `packages/protocol/src/error-journal.ts`) keeps Trackwall's own errors on this computer, so that one seen yesterday is still there today with the place in Trackwall's code it came from. Nothing leaves the computer: there is no sending, telemetry or issue form.

- **What goes in.** The daemon: an API action or MCP tool that failed with an exception other than a `DomainError` (the transport records it with the action's path, `api:/api/workspace/list`, `api:hooks` for a session hook, `api:other` for any other path that is not one of the API's actions, so a path a client made up never reaches the journal, or the tool, `mcp:list_repos`; arguments a schema refuses, `bad_request`, and every `DomainError` are the caller's or the repo's, not Trackwall's), an exception nobody caught or a rejected promise nobody handled (`uncaught`, `unhandled-rejection`: `main.ts` records it, writes the journal at once and exits with 1, as the runtime would have, also when writing fails), and a start that failed (`startup`, into a journal of its own when the facade does not exist yet). The UI: what React caught in a boundary or nobody caught (`createRoot`'s `onCaughtError` and `onUncaughtError`), `window.onerror` and an unhandled rejection, sent to `record-ui-error` (`lib/ui-error-report.ts`; the same error once a minute, at most 20 a minute; a failed send is dropped; a kind that is not an identifier becomes `Error`). The desktop shell: what it used to write only to stderr when a call failed (`report_error!` in `error_journal.rs`, with the shell file as the area) and a panic (a hook before the default one), one JSON line each in `logs/shell-errors.jsonl`, since the shell has to keep them also without a daemon; a failed daemon start too, with the first line of its message. Expected conditions the shell meets stay on stderr only: a window effect the system lacks, a global shortcut another app holds.
- **Where in the code.** Each error keeps up to 8 frames of Trackwall's own code as paths in its repo with a line (`stack-frames.ts`): the daemon from source gives absolute paths, the compiled daemon paths from its package root (`--sourcemap` in the build), both become `packages/<package>/src/…`; the UI's frames are addresses of built chunks, which the daemon maps through the chunk's source map in the UI folder it serves (`ui-source-map.ts`, Vite's `build.sourcemap`; a map over 32 MB or an asset name outside `assets/<name>.js` is not read; the last four maps stay parsed, by file and modification time); a shell location is `apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/<file>.rs:<line>`. Frames of libraries and the runtime are left out. A UI error's area is the folder of its top frame (`ui:views/repos`), else the page (`ui:window`, `ui:tray-panel`).
- **Counted once.** The same source, area, kind and top frame (or, without a frame, the message with its numbers left out) is one error with a count, the first and last time and the last version (`errorFingerprint`). At most 100 errors, the ones seen longest ago dropped. The list is written to `logs/errors.json` at most once a second, through a temporary file; a file that is not the expected JSON starts an empty journal. The daemon takes the shell's file by renaming it to `shell-errors.taken.jsonl` before reading, so a line the shell appends afterwards goes into a new file, read the next time; a line the shell was writing at the moment of the rename may be cut and is then skipped, and a taken file a stopped daemon left behind is read first.
- **Anonymized** before it is kept, and every kept message again when the workspace's values are read and before each write (`anonymize.ts`, `known-values.ts`). A message is cut to 2000 characters first, since the patterns' work grows with the text. First the values the daemon knows, in one pass of one compiled pattern with the longest value winning at each place (a whole-word value that is not a whole word there leaves the place to the values inside it), never inside a label already written, so anonymizing twice changes nothing, in every spelling a message can carry them (both slashes, JSON's doubled backslash, URL encoding): the state directory (`<trackwall>`, the only path whose rest stays, because it is Trackwall's), the home directory, the projects and task worktrees folders, repo folders (`<home>`, `<projects>`, `<worktrees>`, `<repo>`, each taking the rest of its path with it), the user and computer names and the Windows domain (as whole words), repo names (as whole words outside a path, so a repo `api` leaves `/api/…` alone), repo hosts, and every environment variable value of 8 characters or more and every entry of a list variable (`PATH`) as `<env>`; then patterns for what nobody named: a path between quotes whole, spaces included; addresses with a scheme and git remotes in scp form (`git@host:group/repo.git`); e-mail; Windows (also URL-encoded), UNC, `~/` and POSIX paths under users' folders, with a space inside when another separator follows before the next space (`Acme Client\billing`), and any other absolute POSIX path of three or more folders except `/api/…`; IPv4 addresses other than loopback; host names with a top-level domain from a list that leaves words of code alone (`this.app`); `Bearer` values, a base64-looking `Basic` value and the value of any key ending in `token`, `secret`, `password`, `api_key`, `authorization` or `credentials` (`access_token`, `refreshToken`); GitHub and GitLab tokens; long hexadecimal or letter-and-digit runs. A message is cut to 500 characters. A kept error's fingerprint follows its message when it is anonymized again, so its next occurrence counts on it. The workspace's values are read when the journal is read and kept for the errors in between, so an error that ends the daemon is anonymized with what it knew.
- **The previous start log.** The shell renames `daemon-start.log` to `daemon-start.prev.log` before a new daemon starts, so the last lines of a daemon that ended without writing the journal (killed, a crash of the runtime) are still on disk.
- **Actions** (`errorJournalApi`): `errors` (the journal with the shell's new lines, newest first), `record-ui-error` (`UiErrorReport`: page, name, message up to 4000 and stack up to 20 000 characters) and `clear` (the shell's file too). No MCP tool: the journal is for the person looking into Trackwall, not for an agent's work.
- **UI**: the "Trackwall errors" section at the end of Settings (`ErrorJournalSurface.tsx`, `lib/error-journal.ts`): each error with the part of Trackwall and its area, kind, count, when last seen and the version, the message and the top three frames, with "Clear" and "Refresh".
- Not yet: errors a module meets in the background and only logs (a failed card warm-up, a failed backup), an issue form filled from the journal (put off on purpose, ADR-94), and the environment checks ("doctor") in the same part of Settings.

## 5. Protocols

### 5.1 Discovering the daemon

At startup the daemon writes `~/.trackwall/daemon.json`: `{ pid, port, token, startedAt, version }` with permissions for the user only. The port is random from the loopback range, listening only on `127.0.0.1`. A second daemon exits when `/api/health` of the existing one answers with the same version; with another version it asks for a restart from the UI. Clients (Tauri, `ws`, the MCP stdio bridge) read this file; nothing is configured by hand.

### 5.2 Daemon ↔ UI and CLI: HTTP + WebSocket

- **HTTP JSON** for commands and queries: `POST /api/<module>/<action>` with a body validated by zod from `packages/protocol`. The response is `{ ok, data }` or `{ ok: false, error: {code, message} }`. One convention for the UI and `ws`. A body has at most 64 KB; an empty one means `{}`. An action is described in `packages/protocol` by one `Endpoint` object (path, body schema, `data` schema): the daemon validates input with it (`api/routes.ts`), and the UI types the body and checks the response, so the two sides cannot pair them differently. Today these `workspaceApi` actions work: `list` (`{ projectsDir, repos }`), `settings` (`{ projectsDir, configFile }`), `repo-hosts` (the list of hosts), `set-projects-dir {path}`, `overview {repo}` (README and 30 commits), `tree {repo, path}`, `file {repo, path}`, `changes {repo}` (`WorkingTreeChanges`: files and diff of the working directory against HEAD, ADR-34), `open-editor {repo}`, `open-folder {repo}` (the repo folder in Explorer or Finder, `null`, ADR-51), `dropped-folder {path}` (`DroppedFolder`, ADR-51), `open-link {url}` (an `http` or `https` address from a README opened in the system browser, ADR-35), `set-repo-hosts {repoHosts}` (the stored list), `remote-repos {query}` (`{ projectsDir, hosts }`), `clone {host, fullName}` (a clone in the `running` state), `clones`; `open-editor` returns `data: null`. Then `agentsApi` (§4.4, ADR-26): `sessions` (`SessionList`: sessions, newest first, with the process group `RunState`, the last state change, the window title and whether it is waiting for you), `start {repo}` (`Session`), `stop {session}` (returns after a confirmed end of the group) and `dismiss {session}` (only an ended session, otherwise `bad_request`); for tasks (ADR-31) `create-task {repo, prompt}` and `resume-task {task}` (`Task`), `task-changes {task}` (`TaskChanges`), `open-task-editor {task}` (`null`) and `remove-task {task, keepBranch, discardChanges}` (`null`); `sessions` also returns the tasks and the directory of their worktrees. And `miseApi` (§4.7): `tasks {repo}` (`MiseTasks`: a list with `RunState`, or `not_installed` / `untrusted` / `error`), `start {repo, miseTask}` and `stop {repo, miseTask}` (`RunState`; `stop` returns after a confirmed end of the group), `trust {repo}`, `running` (`RunningMiseTask[]`: running tasks of all repos, from daemon memory). Then `batchApi` (§4.8): `start {repos, steps}` (`BatchRun`: a command started in the background), `run` (the last command or `null`) and `cancel`. Then `logsApi` (§4.2): `tail {source, sinceSeq?, limit}` (`LogTail`: `records`, `nextSeq`, `dropped`; `limit` up to 1000). Then `cardsApi` (§4.6): `card {repo}` (`RepoCardResult`: the card, or `null` in a repo without commits, and tags) and `tags` (the tags of every repo from the folder, or `unavailable`). And `graphApi` (§4.6, ADR-29): `graph` (`RepoGraph`: nodes, edges with evidence, errors in the `graph` entries and the path of `workspace.yaml`). And `verificationApi` (§4.10, ADR-64): `start {task}` (`Verification`, running in the background), `get {task}` (the last one or `null`) and `cancel {task}`. Validation messages are in English (the default zod locale, ADR-32). An error a module raises deliberately (`DomainError`) comes back with its code and text: `bad_request` 400, `not_found` 404, `repo_unavailable` 409, `projects_dir_unavailable` 409 (the projects folder disappeared), `invalid_config` 500 (a bad `workspace.yaml`), `mise_unavailable` 409 (no mise, configuration without trust, or a mise error when starting a task), `stop_unconfirmed` 500 (the group is alive after killing), `agent_unavailable` 409 (no Copilot CLI on `PATH`, or no PTY in this runtime), `unsaved_changes` 409 (ending a task would delete changes the caller did not confirm); any other exception is `internal` without details.
- **WebSocket `/ws`** for events (ADR-36, `api/event-socket.ts`, `api/event-hub.ts`): JSON text frames `DaemonEvent` `{ type, payload }` (`packages/protocol/src/events.ts`), in which `payload` is the whole **topic snapshot**, the same as the response of the HTTP action: `sessions.changed` (`SessionList`, like `agents/sessions`), `mise.running.changed` (`RunningMiseTask[]`, like `mise/running`) and `cards.warmup.changed` (`CardWarmupProgress`, like `cards/warmup`, §4.6), with one exception, `repos.changed` (below). After connecting the daemon first sends the snapshot of every topic, then an event only when a topic's snapshot changed, so a client reconnects after a broken connection and needs no event buffer, event number or `resync`; one connection gives the order. The daemon detects a change by comparing the snapshot's JSON every 200 ms (as often as the session cycle) while someone is listening, and serialises an event once for all clients; the compiler checks the set of topics (`TOPICS` in `event-hub.ts`). A topic whose read failed goes to the daemon log once per identical error, and when clients already had its snapshot, the daemon closes their connections with code 1011: after reconnecting they get snapshots without that topic, so the UI goes back to HTTP polling for it and shows the error instead of an old snapshot as live state. The client sends nothing (a frame over 1 KB ends the connection), and a client that does not receive (over 4 MB in the buffer) gets a close with code 4429 (`EVENTS_CLOSE_SLOW`) and reconnects from the snapshots. The connection goes through the same checks as a session terminal (below, `api/sockets.ts`), with the `trackwall.events` subprotocol next to the token; in `vite dev` the Vite proxy adds the token, as for the terminal. The UI (`lib/daemon-events.ts`) keeps one connection per daemon connection, reconnects after 1 s and then less and less often, up to 10 s, and gives the topics as a `LiveFeed` to `useRemote` (the `live` option): a snapshot replaces the data like a response, HTTP polling waits while the topic has a snapshot from the current connection, and the first fetch, `reload` after an action and time without a connection stay on HTTP. One counter numbers requests and snapshots (`LoadOrder`: a request when sent, a snapshot when it arrives), and the response to a request sent before a snapshot does not replace a newer snapshot; a snapshot read in the daemon just before an action but received after it counts as newer than the action until the next one (at most 200 ms later) corrects it. `repos.changed` (`RepoWatchState`, the revision of the repo watch, §4.5, ADR-63) is the only topic without an HTTP action, because polling it would be the polling it replaces: it is not data for a view but a signal, so the UI fetches the repo list, tags and the graph again after every new revision, polls them rarely while the topic is live and as before without it, and after a failed read of the topic simply goes back to that polling. Further topics (logs `log.append`, services, sync and cloning) are further variants of `DaemonEvent` and entries in `TOPICS`; the Tauri shell still polls `sessions` over HTTP (§5.4).
- **PTY** as a separate WebSocket `/ws/pty/<session>` (ADR-26, `api/pty-socket.ts`, the `upgrade` handling and checks shared with events in `api/sockets.ts`, the `ws` library, because Node has no WebSocket server): after connecting the daemon sends the session's screen image (a text frame from `@xterm/addon-serialize`, with terminal modes and the alternate screen), then the PTY output in binary frames, exactly from where the image ends. The client sends JSON text frames `PtyClientMessage`: `input` (keys and pasted text, up to 65,536 characters) and `resize` (columns and rows). Keys typed into a terminal before its first connection opens wait in the window and go out in order after the first `resize` (`InputBeforeOpen` in `packages/ui/src/lib`, up to 4,096 characters; past that, those keys and every key after them are dropped with a notice, so no Enter goes without its text), so typing at once after switching to a session loses nothing. Only the first attempt: when it fails, or for keys typed while a lost connection comes back, the keys are dropped, because the screen they answer may have changed by the time a connection opens. A window that comes to the front sends `resize` again for each of its terminals, so with the same session in two windows (ADR-49) the window the user works in sets the PTY size. The client also sends `focus {focused}` after the window has been focused and visible for 1 s and when it stops being so; a focused window marks the session seen (ADR-47), and a closed connection counts as unfocused. In the session grid (ADR-48) only the selected tile reports `focused: true`; the other tiles report that they are not in front. The connection goes through the same `Host` and `Origin` checks as the rest of the API; the token is a second subprotocol (`bearer.<token>`, `SOCKET_TOKEN_PREFIX`, constant-time comparison), and the server confirms only `trackwall.pty`, because a browser cannot set a header on a WebSocket, and an address ends up in logs and history. The socket's address and subprotocol go together (`DaemonSocket`, `ptySocket`, `EVENTS_SOCKET`, `daemonSocketAt` in `packages/protocol/src/sockets.ts`), so the daemon, the Vite proxy and the UI cannot combine them differently. The token is checked before the address (without a token 401 also at an unknown address), then the address (404) and the socket's subprotocol (400). An unknown, closed (`dismiss`) or non-UUID session closes the connection with code 4404 (`PTY_CLOSE_NOT_FOUND`, the window does not reconnect), a window that does not receive output (over 8 MB in the buffer) with code 4429 (`PTY_CLOSE_SLOW`). A request whose address cannot be parsed is refused, and an error in handling a connection ends only that connection, not the daemon. In `vite dev` the Vite proxy adds the token, as for `/api` (§8). The session state reaches the UI with the `sessions.changed` event from `/ws` (above); without an event connection the UI polls `sessions` every 1 s with the view open and every 3 s outside it (the counter on the view bar).
- **Session plugin hooks** (ADR-88, `api/hooks.ts`): `POST /hooks/<event>` with the daemon token and the session token in `x-trackwall-session-token`, a body of at most 32 MB (a payload carries a tool's arguments and result). The answer is the bare hook output Copilot CLI reads, not `{ ok, data }`: `{}`, or for `agentStop` a block with the daemon's prompt and for `postToolUse` the prompt as `additionalContext` (ADR-85, `LiveSession.hookOutput`). A missing or unknown token is 401, an event outside the session plugin's list 404, and the record is written before the answer.
- **Authentication**: the token from `daemon.json` in the `Authorization: Bearer` header, constant-time comparison. Requests whose `Host` or `Origin` is not loopback are rejected (DNS rebinding, CSRF from a page in the browser). Tauri gives the token to the WebView through a Tauri command, not through a query string.

### 5.3 Daemon ↔ agent: MCP

The daemon exposes an MCP server over the **Streamable HTTP** transport at `/mcp` with the same token. For CLIs that only support stdio there is the stdio bridge `trackwall-daemon mcp-stdio`, which forwards JSON-RPC messages to `/mcp`. A Trackwall session does not use the bridge: its Copilot CLI connects to `/mcp` over HTTP directly (ADR-89). Eventually the "connect an agent" feature in the UI adds an entry to the given CLI's MCP configuration without touching the rest of the file; today the UI shows the command that does this on the Copilot CLI side (below).

The first slice (ADR-24) gives a **supervising agent** (Copilot CLI started by the user, for example in the projects folder) a map of the repos, read only; ADR-39 adds delegating tasks to other sessions through proposals the user approves, and ADR-41 running and stopping mise tasks from the repo's trusted configuration:

- **The server** (`src/mcp/endpoint.ts`) is `createMcpHandler` from `@modelcontextprotocol/server`, without MCP sessions: every request gets a fresh `McpServer` with the same tools, because the facade holds the state. `handle()` in `api/server.ts` recognises a request with the `x-trackwall-session-token` header (a Trackwall session's HTTP MCP entry, ADR-39, ADR-89) through `agents.callerSession`, and the calling session goes to the server factory as `authInfo.clientId` and to every tool call; an unknown token (a closed session) is `401 unauthorized` before the SDK, so that the session does not look like an agent from outside Trackwall. It supports revision 2026-07-28 and older clients with `initialize` (statelessly; GET and DELETE of a session give 405). `handle()` in `api/server.ts` checks `Host`, `Origin` and the daemon token (one token check for the API and MCP; only `GET /api/health` and the UI files are public) before the request reaches the SDK; a body has at most 256 KB, and a disconnected client aborts the request in the SDK too. `main.ts` builds the MCP server from the facade and passes it to `startApi`. The server announces `instructions` with the MCP instruction (`MCP_INSTRUCTIONS` in `protocol`, ADR-46): what Trackwall is and when to reach for which tool (the repo card before reading build files, `search_code` to find code across repos, `find_endpoint` and `who_calls` before changing an API, `get_errors` (then `tail`) to diagnose a failure, `run_mise_task` and `wait_for_mise_task` to build and test, `propose_task` and `wait_for_session` to hand work over, `report_result` at the end of a task, the documentation site and its `llms.txt` (`TRACKWALL_DOCS_URL` in `protocol`) when the agent or the user is unsure what Trackwall can do). Tool names come from `mcpTools`, and a test requires the instruction to mention every tool, so a new tool cannot land without a sentence on when to use it. Copilot CLI 1.0.103 adds `instructions` to the prompt only for servers on its own allowlist (unless started with `--allow-all-mcp-server-instructions`), so Trackwall sessions get the same instruction in their session instructions too (§4.4).
- **Tools** (`src/mcp/tools.ts`): `list_repos`, `get_repo`, `get_repo_card`, `tail` (mise task log, §4.2), `get_errors` (errors from logs, ADR-42), `get_test_results` (test results from JUnit reports, §4.9, ADR-60), `who_calls`, `subgraph_for` and `find_endpoint` (connection graph, §4.6), `search_code` (code search, §4.5, ADR-40) and `list_sessions`, `get_session` and `get_task_changes` (sessions and tasks, §4.4, ADR-36, ADR-39), and `get_day_report` for the day start (§4.11, ADR-70), and for delegating work `propose_task`, `report_result` and `wait_for_session` (§4.4, ADR-39), and `get_review_comments` for the user's review of a task (§4.4, ADR-62), and `send_message`, `get_messages` and `get_updates` between a task's proposer and its session (§4.4, ADR-68), and for mise tasks `run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `wait_for_mise_task` and `list_running_mise_tasks` (§4.7, ADR-41), and `verify_task` for a task's verification (§4.10, ADR-64), and for services `start_service`, `stop_service`, `list_services` and `call_endpoint` (§4.3, ADR-45). A tool's contract (name, description for the agent, input and result schema) is an `McpTool` object in `mcpTools` (`packages/protocol/src/mcp.ts`), like `Endpoint` for the API; the daemon only supplies its execution on the facade (`defineTool`), and the UI takes the names from it. The annotations come from the contract: `readOnlyHint` from the `readOnly` field, and a tool with `readOnly: false` must give `destructive` (`destructiveHint`). `propose_task` and `report_result` store only text for a human, and `send_message` only text for another agent (`destructive: false`; `get_review_comments`, `get_messages` and `get_updates` mark what they return read, but are `readOnly`, since that changes only Trackwall's state and an approval would stall the agent the daemon asked, ADR-62, ADR-68); `run_mise_task` and `verify_task` run repo code, and `stop_mise_task` can kill a process the user needs (a server, a migration), so they have `destructive: true`, and an MCP client that asks for approval of destructive tools will ask about them. Each returns `structuredContent` checked against the schema and the same JSON as text for clients that do not read it. An error raised by a module (`DomainError`) comes back as a result with `isError` and the text `<code>: <message>`, arguments the tool's schema refuses as `bad_request` with what is wrong, and anything else as `internal` without details, which also goes into the error journal (§4.14). Every call goes to the daemon log (tool, calling session, time, result, without arguments); a destructive tool (`run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `verify_task` and the service tools) adds a line with its arguments, that is the repo, the task and the mise task, before acting (ADR-41, ADR-59). The `audit` table will come with SQLite. A further tool is a further `mcpTools` entry with its execution in `runs` in `tools.ts` (its absence is a compile error) and a registration whose completeness the `tools/list` test checks.
- **The stdio bridge** (`trackwall-daemon mcp-stdio [--home <dir>]`, `src/mcp/stdio-bridge.ts`, for an agent outside Trackwall) reads JSON-RPC lines from stdin and sends each separately to `/mcp` with the port and token from `daemon.json`, read at every message: a daemon restart (new port and token) needs no change to the agent's configuration, and the token never goes into it. The time limit of one request is 120 s, with room for `wait_for_session` (up to 55 s). Messages go in parallel; a JSON or SSE response comes back as lines on stdout, and the bridge's own messages only on stderr. The bridge adds the version from `initialize` to later requests as `MCP-Protocol-Version`. A transport error the daemon returns with `id: null` (for example a header the SDK rejected) the bridge sends back with the request's `id`, because a stdio client pairs responses by `id`. The bridge rejects the revision 2026-07-28 probe (`server/discover`) like a server without that method (`-32601`), because in that revision an HTTP request repeats content fields in headers, which stdio does not have; the client then falls back to `initialize` (as Copilot CLI 1.0.103 does). Without a running daemon, with a rejected request, or with a response that has no message for that `id` (a stream broken midway), the bridge answers with error `-32000` and an instruction to start Trackwall; it does not start the daemon itself. A line that is not JSON gets `-32700`, and JSON that is not a JSON-RPC object (including a batch, which MCP has not had since revision 2025-06-18) `-32600`. After stdin ends the bridge waits for the responses in flight and exits by itself once stdout drains.
- **Connecting Copilot CLI**: `/api/daemon/info` gives `mcpStdioCommand`, this daemon's bridge command (the release binary, recognised by the Bun virtual file system in the script path, `isCompiledBinary`; when running from source the runtime and the script, under Bun with `--no-env-file` and `--config` pointing to the empty `packages/daemon/bunfig.toml`; a non-default state directory as `--home`). The "Daemon" view shows the command `copilot mcp add trackwall -- <bridge command>` built from it (quoted for PowerShell on Windows, where arguments with `,` and `@` and those starting with `-` are in single quotes too, because PowerShell eats a bare `--` before the `copilot.ps1` script; sh on macOS) and the same entry for `mcp-config.json` in the user's `.copilot` directory to copy. Copilot CLI starts the bridge at startup and keeps it until the session ends, so Trackwall must be running before.

Tools (all take identifiers; `list_repos`, `get_repo`, `get_repo_card`, `tail`, `get_errors`, `get_test_results`, `who_calls`, `subgraph_for`, `find_endpoint`, `search_code`, `list_sessions`, `get_session`, `get_day_report`, `get_task_changes`, `propose_task`, `report_result`, `get_review_comments`, `send_message`, `get_messages`, `get_updates`, `wait_for_session`, the mise task and service tools and `verify_task` are done, the rest is the plan):

| Tool | Input | Returns |
|---|---|---|
| `list_repos` | `tags`? | `projectsDir` and the repos from the projects folder (`AgentRepo`): `Repo` (identifier, path, remote address, repo status with `untracked`), `lastSync`, the repo's result in the last sync since the daemon started, with its time (joined by path, with the remote branch and the working directory state, ADR-34), or `null`, and the repo's tag names (ADR-25); with `tags` only repos with each of them |
| `get_repo` | `repo` | the same repo (with tag names; empty when the card cannot be computed), the README from HEAD cut to 32,000 characters, the 10 latest commits and the mise tasks (`MiseTasks`: a list with `RunState`, or `not_installed` / `untrusted` / `error`) |
| `get_repo_card` | `repo` | the repo card (`RepoCardResult`, §4.6): provided and consumed packages, apps, HTTP endpoints and clients (ADR-30), tools, mise configuration and tasks (`mise`, ADR-34), mechanisms, build files and problems with their code (ADR-80), every fact with evidence, plus tags with reasons; `card: null` in a repo without commits |
| `tail` | `repo`, `task`?, `miseTask`, `sinceSeq`?, `limit` (up to 1000) | the mise task's `LogTail` (with `task`, of its run in that task's worktree, ADR-59), the same as `logs/tail` (§4.2): `records`, `nextSeq`, `dropped`; an unknown repo or task is `not_found`, a task of another repo `bad_request` |
| `get_errors` | `repo`?, `task`? (only the sources of that task's worktree, ADR-59), `miseTask`?, `minLevel` (`error` or `warn`, default `error`), `sinceSeq`?, `limit` (default 20, up to 100) | `LogErrors` (§4.2, ADR-42): errors from the sources matching the filter (without `miseTask` also `mise install`), most recent first, each with its source, `runId`, stream, level, the cause's exception, trimmed lines, `seq`, time, `firstTs` and `count`; `truncated`, `nextSeq` (the last `seq` of all sources) and `dropped` as in `tail`; an unknown repo or task is `not_found` |
| `get_test_results` | `repo`, `task`? (that task's worktree instead of the repo root, ADR-59), `since`? (ISO 8601) | `TestResults` (§4.9, ADR-60): the searched directory, totals (tests, failures, errors, skipped, time) over every JUnit report found, the reports newest first with counts and `modifiedAt` (at most 100), the failed tests (suite, class, name, `failure` or `error`, type, message, the start of the stack trace with every `Caused by`, masked; at most 50), unreadable reports in `problems`, `earlierReports` (left out by `since`) and `truncated`; with `since` only reports written at or after it; an unknown repo or task is `not_found`, a task of another repo `bad_request` |
| `who_calls` | `repo` | the connection graph edges to the repo (who consumes it: packages, HTTP, manual) with kind, key, confidence and evidence (ADR-29, ADR-30); later also `service` |
| `subgraph_for` | `repo`, `depth` (1–3, default 1) | a subgraph: the repos within `depth` edges in both directions and the edges between them (ADR-29); later service states |
| `find_endpoint` | `query` (a path, or a fragment of a path, handler or app), `method`? | endpoints (`FindEndpointResult`): repo, app, method, full path, handler, file and line, plus the repos whose clients call them, with confidence, evidence and the calling repo's endpoints that reach the call (`from`), and the endpoints of other repos each one calls (`calls`); at most 50 (ADR-30, ADR-82) |
| `search_code` | `pattern`, `regex`?, `ignoreCase`?, `repos`?, `tags`?, `paths`? (git pathspecs), `context`? (0–10, default 2), `limit`? (up to 500, default 100) | `git grep` matches in the HEAD commit of the chosen repos (`SearchCodeResult`): repo, path, line, column, line text and context lines before and after (at most 100,000 characters in total), plus the searched repos with their commit and `truncated`, the repos git could not search with its message, and the `skipped` ones; an unknown repo in `repos` is `not_found` (§4.5, ADR-40) |
| `list_sessions` | `repo`? | Copilot CLI sessions from the "Sessions" view, newest first (`AgentSession`: `Session` with repo (`null` for the supervisor, ADR-58), start, process group, the last state change with source and confidence, window title, `needsAttention` and task identifier, but an ended group without the last screen lines, §8) and tasks (`Task`: prompt, branch, base branch and commit, worktree); task proposals waiting for a decision (`TaskProposal`) and `caller`, the identifier of the calling session (`null` outside Trackwall, ADR-39); with `repo` only the sessions, tasks and proposals of that repo, and an unknown repo is `not_found` (ADR-36) |
| `get_session` | `session` | the same session, its task (`null` in a repo root and for the supervisor) and the task's changes without the diff (`TaskChangesSummary` from `agents.taskChangesSummary`: at most 200 files (`TASK_SUMMARY_MAX_FILES`) with line counts and `filesTruncated`, commits since the base, `git status` entries; git then does not compute the diff content); when the changes cannot be computed (a worktree removed by hand, the task is being ended, a git error), `changes: null` and the reason in `changesError`, and the session and task stay; `verification`, the task's last verification (§4.10, ADR-64) or `null`; an unknown or closed session is `not_found` (ADR-36) |
