| `get_day_report` | — | `report` (`DayReport`, §4.11, ADR-70): the report of the last day start, as `day-start.json` keeps it, `null` before the first; the same report until the next day start |
| `get_task_changes` | `task`, `paths`? (git pathspecs, up to 20), `maxPatchBytes` (1000–200,000, default 32,000) | `AgentTaskChanges` from `agents.agentTaskChanges`: the task changes (`TaskChanges`, the same as the "Changes" tab) narrowed to `paths`, with the diff cut after the last whole line within `maxPatchBytes` (`truncated`, which also covers a cut file list) and at most 200 files (`filesTruncated`); the commit and `git status` counts stay those of the whole worktree; an unknown or ended task is `not_found` (ADR-39) |
| `propose_task` | `repo`, `title` (up to 80 characters, one line), `prompt` (up to 8000), `model`? (a Copilot CLI model id, ADR-98) | `proposal`: a task proposal with the identifier the task will get, its `model` (`null` without one) and `proposedBy`; nothing starts before approval in the UI, at most 20 wait at once, an unknown repo is `not_found` (ADR-39) |
| `propose_folder_action` | `action` (today only `remove_repo`), `repo`, `reason` (up to 2000 characters) | `proposal`: a proposal of the kind `remove_repo`, which the user approves with "Remove repo" in the UI; the daemon then moves the repo into `.trackwall-removed` in the projects folder unless a session, a task or a process still runs in it; an unknown repo is `not_found` (ADR-83) |
| `report_result` | `summary` (up to 4000 characters) | `task`: the calling session's task with the new `summary`; only from a task session, otherwise `bad_request` (ADR-39) |
| `get_review_comments` | — | `comments`: the review comments the user sent on the calling session's own task and the session has not read yet, oldest first, which then count as read; only from a task session, otherwise `bad_request` (ADR-62) |
| `send_message` | `task`, `text` (up to 4000 characters) | `message` (`TaskMessage`: identifier, task, `from` as on a proposal, text, time, `readAt`) and `delivery` (`typed`, `queued`, `on_resume`); only to a task the caller proposed (the supervisor: one a supervisor proposed), at most 20 unread and 5 since the user last took part, otherwise `bad_request`; an agent outside Trackwall gets `bad_request` (ADR-68) |
| `resume_task` | `task` | `task` and `session` (`AgentSession`): the same as Resume in the task's panel, a new session in the task's worktree with `--resume=<task UUID>`, which then reads what waits for the task (review comments, messages); the supervisor resumes any task, a main session only one it proposed, a task session none (`bad_request`); an agent outside Trackwall, the user's program, any; a running session or another action on the task is `bad_request`, an unknown or ended task `not_found` (ADR-90) |
| `get_messages` | — | `messages`: the messages to the calling session's own task it has not read yet, oldest first, without `readAt`, which then count as read; only from a task session, otherwise `bad_request` (ADR-68) |
| `get_updates` | — | `updates` (`TaskUpdate`: `turn_ended`, `error`, `exited` with exit code, `result_reported` with the summary, `verified` with the verdict and message, `proposal_rejected` with the reason, `repo_removed` with the folder the repo was moved to; each with task, session and time) on the work the caller proposed, oldest first, which then count as read, and `dropped`; an agent outside Trackwall gets `bad_request` (ADR-68) |
| `wait_for_session` | `session` or `task` (exactly one), `timeoutSeconds` (1–55, default 30) | `status` (`proposal_pending`, `proposal_rejected`, `starting`, `working`, `stale`, `needs_attention`, `exited`, `no_session`), the proposal, the task and the session (`AgentSession`), and `rejectionReason`, the user's reason with `proposal_rejected` (ADR-55), otherwise `null`; returns at once except for `proposal_pending`, `starting` and `working`, and for those after the time limit; on one's own session `bad_request`, an unknown identifier `not_found` (ADR-39) |
| `run_mise_task` | `repo`, `task`?, `miseTask` | `run` (`RunState` with `runId`): `mise.start`, only a task from the current `mise tasks ls` list, in the root of a repo from the projects folder, or with `task` in that task's worktree (ADR-59); a running task returns its state; a configuration without trust (and no mise), and in a worktree a mise config changed since the user trusted it, is `mise_unavailable`, a task outside the list `not_found`; a Trackwall session only in the repo it works in, and a task session only in the repo root or its own task's worktree, otherwise `bad_request` (ADR-41, ADR-59) |
| `stop_mise_task` | `repo`, `task`?, `miseTask` | `run` after confirming the group is dead (`stop_unconfirmed` when it survived), also a task started from the UI; one not run since the daemon started is `not_found`; a Trackwall session only in its own repo, a task session only in the root or its own worktree (ADR-41, ADR-59) |
| `wait_for_mise_task` | `repo`, `task`?, `miseTask`, `timeoutSeconds` (1–55, default 30) | `run`: `exited` with the exit code when the group ends, or the current state after the time limit; an ended one returns at once, one not run is `not_found` (ADR-41) |
| `list_running_mise_tasks` | — | `miseTasks`: the running (and stopping) mise tasks of all repos and task worktrees since this daemon started (`RunningMiseTask` with `task`, the same as `mise/running`), without asking mise (ADR-41, ADR-59) |
| `verify_task` | `repo`, `task`, `runId`? (keep waiting for that verification), `timeoutSeconds` (1–55, default 30) | `verification` (`Verification`, §4.10): starts the task's verification (the mise task `test` in its worktree) or joins the running one, and returns its verdict (`passed`, `failed` with the first failed tests and log errors, `cancelled`) or, after the time limit, `running`; without `test` `not_found`, an untrusted mise config `mise_unavailable`; the gate of `run_mise_task`; `repo` and `task` go to the audit line (ADR-64) |
| `start_service` | `repo`, `task`? (the service from that task's worktree on the task port, ADR-61), `dependencies` (default true), `timeoutSeconds` (1–55, default 30) | `slice` (`ServiceSlice`): dependencies in start order and the repo last, each with `calledBy`, `outcome` (`pending`, `started`, `running`, `skipped` with `problem`), `service` (`Service`: state, port and its source, run, reason) and for `crashed`/`unhealthy` up to 5 errors of its run; `settled: false` when the wait ended first; without the task `dev` `not_found`; a Trackwall session only for its own repo (ADR-45) |
| `stop_service` | `repo`, `task`?, `dependencies` (default false) | `services`: the stopped services (the root after a crash is not among them, its dependencies are); never started `not_found`; a Trackwall session only for its own repo (ADR-45) |
| `list_services` | — | `services`: services started or adopted since the daemon started, without asking mise (ADR-45) |
| `call_endpoint` | `repo`, `task`?, `method` (default GET), `path`, `headers`, `body`, `timeoutSeconds` | `url`, `method`, `response` (status, headers, body up to 32 KB masked, `truncated`) or `failure`, `durationMs` and `errors` of the task `dev` of every repo during the call and 500 ms after it; a service that is not `healthy` is `service_unavailable`; only `repo`, `task` and `method` go to the audit line (ADR-45, ADR-61) |
| `trace` | `trace_id` | records from all services in `seq` order |
| `search` | query, `source`?, range | records with `seq` |
| `required_secrets` | `repo` | variable names from the card |
| `check_secrets` | `repo` | `{name, set: boolean}` without values |

`run_mise_task` runs any task from the list, also `dev`, and the agent stops its server with `stop_mise_task`. `start_service` (ADR-45) runs the same task `dev` with its dependencies and waits for its port, and a `dev` started with `run_mise_task` is adopted as its service; with `task` both run in the task's worktree on the task port (ADR-61), and a task session may pass only its own task. Once leases exist, starting and stopping a service will go through them. Eventually every call goes to the `audit` table (client, tool, arguments, time, result hash), visible only in the UI; today only to the daemon log, without arguments (ADR-24) except for the destructive tools (`run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `verify_task`, `start_service`, `stop_service`, `call_endpoint`), which log the arguments their contract names (`auditArgs`) before acting (ADR-41, ADR-45).

MCP resources (`resources`) expose the repo card and the current graph snapshot as read-only documents, so that the agent can load them without a series of calls.

### 5.4 Tauri ↔ daemon

Tauri starts the daemon as a sidecar with the argument `--parent <pid>`, waits for `/api/health`, shows a splash screen, and after a failure a dialog with the startup log. The window shows only after the UI has loaded, with the background effect already set (ADR-11). Closing the main window hides it to the tray. The shell can have several windows (ADR-49, `src-tauri/src/windows.rs`): the main window from `tauri.conf.json` (label `main`) and extra windows the user opens with "Open in new window" in the context menu of a view icon on the view bar, Ctrl+Shift + the view's digit (⌥⌘ on macOS, where ⌘⇧3 and ⌘⇧4 take screenshots; `viewKeyAction`, not for a held key), Ctrl+Shift+N (⌘⇧N; the current view) or "New window" in the tray (the UI's first view, Home). The context menu is the native menu (`@tauri-apps/api/menu` under `core:default`), one per window, reused for every right click. An extra window is built from the main window's configuration (`WebviewWindowBuilder::from_config`) in an async command or the async runtime, never on the main thread, where building a window deadlocks on Windows, and one at a time (`Windows::opening`), so two quick opens do not pick the same label; its label is `window-<n>` with the lowest free number from 2, its page is `index.html?view=<view>&sessionLayout=<layout>` (only a lowercase word or number reaches the address, `window_url`), and it gets the background effect on the main thread and the same reveal after page load as the main window (`WindowEffects`, per label, with the entry created before the window, so a reveal from its page or the tray always lands on it). Every window is the same UI with its own view and its own connection to the daemon (`daemon_connection`, `/ws`, `/ws/pty`), so sessions and changes are live in all of them; the capability `default` covers `main` and `window-*`. The window's CSP (`app.security.csp` in `tauri.conf.json`) lets the page connect only to loopback, where the daemon listens (`http://127.0.0.1:*` and `ws://127.0.0.1:*` in `connect-src`), and to Tauri's IPC, and refuses code from strings (no `'unsafe-eval'`): the UI and its libraries must work without it. The E2E test of the window under that CSP (ADR-71) checks Home with the 3D scene; the other views under the CSP are a gap in §8. Closing an extra window closes it; only the main window hides to the tray. On macOS the shell sets its own menu bar (`app_menu.rs`) instead of Tauri's default: the app menu with About, Settings… (⌘,), Services, Hide and Quit; Edit with the system's items, which copy and paste in the web view need; View with the view bar's views and their shortcuts (⌘1…⌘4, ⌘9; `apps/desktop/test/shell-events.test.ts` keeps the list equal to `VIEWS` in `App.tsx`) and full screen; Window (`WINDOW_SUBMENU_ID`, so macOS lists the open windows by title and adds "Bring All to Front") with New Window (⌘⇧N, as on the view bar), Minimize, Zoom and Close Window; and Help, where macOS puts its search. A Trackwall item sends the event `app-menu` with `{kind: "view", view}` or `{kind: "new-window"}` to the last focused window (`Windows::last_focused`, shown first, because the app can be active with every window hidden), whose UI shows the view or opens its current view in a new window (`use-app-menu.ts`). The web view gets a key press first and the menu only one the page did not handle (wry), so a shortcut the UI takes (⌘1, ⌘⇧N) acts once, and the menu's item acts when focus is elsewhere. A click when the window's page is still loading is lost: the page shows itself (ADR-11) and has no listener yet. The title of a window is the name of its view from the same table (`app_menu::view_name`). Windows has no menu bar, because there it would be a bar inside every window. `tauri-plugin-window-state` keeps the position, size, maximized and full-screen state of each label in `.window-state.json` in the shell's configuration directory (not the visibility, which stays with the reveal) and restores it when a window with that label is created; a saved position is used only if a monitor still contains it, so a window from an unplugged monitor opens at the default position. The file is written when an extra window closes (`windows::closed`) and when the shell exits; a killed shell loses the moves since then. The open extra windows are saved in `windows.json` in the same directory (`saved_windows.rs`: label, view and session layout of each, written through a temporary file and a rename whenever a window reports what it shows or the user closes one), and the shell's `setup` opens them again under the same labels (the list enters the registry first and `Windows::opening` is held for the whole restore, so a window reporting or closing meanwhile does not drop the others and "New window" cannot take a saved label; a window that cannot be built stays in the list, since the failure may be the app quitting mid-restore; the file is written with the registry locked, so writes from the restore and from the main thread do not overtake each other), so each comes back on its monitor with its view and layout. Quitting the app closes windows without `CloseRequested`, so they stay in the list; closing an extra window removes it. The file is read like any external input: entries whose label is not `window-<n>` (n ≥ 2), whose view or layout is not a lowercase word or number, repeated labels and anything past 16 windows are dropped, and a file that is not the expected JSON opens no windows. Every exit from the shell stops the daemon: the shell aborts an ongoing start, sends `POST /api/daemon/shutdown` (when the start failed, to the daemon from `daemon.json`), the daemon stops services and sessions with the same gentle stop as Ctrl-C, and Tauri waits until it stops answering and does not start a new one after that. A daemon that already answers gets the gentle stop also when the exit came during the start; only a sidecar that has not come up yet is killed. "Quit" from the tray hides the windows and the icon, and stops the daemon in the background before the event loop ends, so the single instance is held until the end. Cmd+Q and "Quit" from the Dock on macOS (and, according to tao's code, also the end of a Windows session, where the system may end the process before the waiting ends) end the loop skipping `ExitRequested`, so the `RunEvent::Exit` handler does it, after hiding the windows and removing the tray icon. The shell asks the daemon for sessions every 2 s (`POST /api/agents/sessions`, with the same hand-written HTTP client), because the WebView of a hidden window may have its timers suspended, and it does not listen to events from `/ws`, because they would need a WebSocket client in Rust, and 2 s is enough for flashing the window (ADR-36): a session that goes from `Working` or `Stale` to waiting for a human triggers `request_user_attention` (flashing on the Windows taskbar, a bounce in the macOS Dock), and every waiting session has a tray menu item "Waiting: <repo>" that shows the window and asks the UI for a jump to `{kind: "session", id}`. The window is the one for sessions (`Windows::session_window`, ADR-49): the last focused window that shows the Sessions view (each window reports its view and session layout with `window_shows` when either changes; the view's name becomes the window's title, `<view> – Trackwall`, so windows differ in the taskbar and the macOS Window menu), otherwise the last focused window, otherwise the main window; the event goes only to that window (the UI listens on its own window, `getCurrentWebviewWindow().listen`, because a global `listen` also gets events sent to other windows), and `request_user_attention` flashes it too. Task proposals work the same way (ADR-39, from `proposals` in the same reply): the item "Proposal: <repo> · <title>" above the sessions asks for a jump to `{kind: "proposal", id}`, and a proposal that was neither a proposal nor a task at the previous poll (a proposal being approved is already a task, and comes back after a failed start) calls `request_user_attention`; at the first poll after the shell starts none is new. `&` in a menu item's text is doubled, because it marks a mnemonic. The icon tooltip says how many sessions and proposals are waiting. The daemon does not exit when its parent dies (the "runs with the window closed" mode), it checks `--parent` only to tell a start from Tauri from a manual one. After the shell crashes or is killed the daemon keeps running, and the next start of the app connects to it through `daemon.json`.

A jump (`Jump` in `jump.rs`: `repo`, `session`, `task` or `proposal` with an id) goes to the window for sessions (ADR-49) or, for a repo, to the main window. The shell keeps it with that window's label and sends that window the event `jump-requested` without a payload; the window's UI takes the jump with the command `take_jump`, which answers only the window it is meant for, on the event and once when it starts, so a jump that comes before the page listens is not lost. A newer jump replaces one not taken yet. `trackwall://` links (ADR-52, `link.rs`, `tauri-plugin-deep-link`) become jumps the same way: `trackwall://repo/<repo id>`, `trackwall://session/<session id>` and `trackwall://task/<task id>` (a task link also finds a task proposal with that id). The shell checks the shape (scheme and kind without regard to case, exactly one id of letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-` up to 64 characters, no query, fragment, port or user); the UI checks the id against `RepoId`, `SessionId` and `TaskId`, the only definition of an id. Anything else only shows the main window. A jump to a window whose page is still loading does not show it early (`windows::show_once_loaded`, ADR-11); that page shows itself and takes the jump when it starts. A jump waiting for a window that closes is dropped, because a new window may get the same label. macOS delivers a link to the running app, or starts it, through `Info.plist`, which the bundle gets from `plugins.deep-link` in `tauri.conf.json`. On Windows a link starts a second `trackwall.exe` with the link as its only argument; `tauri-plugin-single-instance` with its `deep-link` feature hands it to the running instance, and a link that starts the app is read from the arguments. A second start that carries a link does not bring the main window forward (`link::in_args`): the link shows its own window. The portable build has no installer, so the release shell registers the scheme for the current user (`HKCU\Software\Classes\trackwall`, pointing at the running `trackwall.exe`) at every start, which follows the folder when the user moves it; with two copies, the one started last gets the links. `tauri dev` does not register, so it does not take links away from the installed app. A `trackwall://` link printed in a session terminal opens on Ctrl+click (Cmd+click on macOS) through the command `open_link`, which applies the same check without the round trip through the system; the click also reaches Copilot CLI, which takes the mouse. A link split over rows is joined (`terminal-links.ts`): over a row xterm wrapped as it is, and over an unwrapped row, after its indentation, only when the row above ends in a link cut where nothing else could follow (inside the scheme or the kind, right after the kind, or inside a session or task UUID), because Copilot CLI breaks long words of its answer into indented rows itself; a repo id at the end of a row may be whole, so it is not joined with an unwrapped row. A session or task link whose id is not a whole UUID is not a link. A split link is underlined row by row, each part over its own characters only, so the indentation and box borders between the parts are neither underlined nor clickable. Copilot CLI shows a Markdown link (`[orders](trackwall://repo/orders)`) as an OSC 8 hyperlink whose visible text is the label; the terminal's `linkHandler` opens a `trackwall://` hyperlink through `open_link` and an `http` or `https` one in the system browser through the daemon (`open-link`, like a README link), both on Ctrl+click or Cmd+click, and any other scheme not at all, although it is underlined like the others (xterm.js on its own opens only `http` and `https` hyperlinks, with `window.open`, which does not reach the system browser from the Tauri window). Known limits: a wide character earlier in the row shifts the underline, and a repo link cut at the end of a row by Copilot CLI is found only up to the cut, so Ctrl+click opens the shorter id (or nothing, when no repo has it).

The shell also gives the window three things that need the system (ADR-51). The app command `copy_text` writes text to the system clipboard (`arboard` in Rust; nothing in the shell reads the clipboard), for copies from a session terminal. The app command `pick_projects_folder` opens the system folder picker (`tauri-plugin-dialog`, called from Rust) and returns the chosen full path, which the UI saves with `set-projects-dir`; the plugin's own JavaScript commands stay unreachable, because the windows' capability is still only `core:default` and the app's own commands. Files dropped on the window come as Tauri's webview drag-and-drop events (`onDragDropEvent`: full paths and a point); the point is in physical pixels only on Windows (WebView2), while on macOS and Linux wry hands over CSS pixels under the same type, so the UI divides by the device pixel ratio on Windows only. The drop target that holds the point (`[data-file-drop]`, today the session terminal) is highlighted while dragging and gets a `FileDropEvent` with the paths, which it pastes; a drop outside every target goes to `dropped-folder` (§4.5), a drag without paths (text, a link, an image) is ignored, and so is a drop while the UI has no daemon connection. Tauri's drag-and-drop handling turns off HTML5 drag and drop inside the page on Windows, so the UI does not use it.

System notifications, the global shortcut and the tray panel (ADR-50; `notification.rs`, `global_shortcut.rs`, `tray_panel.rs`, jumps through `jump.rs`): at the same moments that flash the window (a session that starts waiting, a new task proposal), the shell also shows a system notification, but only while no Trackwall window has the focus: "Session in <repo>" (for the supervisor, "Supervisor", ADR-58) with what the session waits for, in the words of the UI's state labels, or "Task proposal for <repo>" with the proposal's title; new proposals are held until three polls in a row (about six seconds) bring no new one, so a plan the main session writes over several `propose_task` calls makes one notification, "N task proposals" with their repos and titles, which opens the first; proposals decided meanwhile are left out (`HeldProposals` in `attention.rs`, ADR-55). The taskbar flash waits with it. Clicking it does what the tray menu item does. On Windows the toast goes out under the app's AppUserModelID (the app identifier, with `.dev` in a debug build), which the shell registers at every start under `HKCU\Software\Classes\AppUserModelId` with the display name "Trackwall" and the app icon, written to `notification-icon.png` in the state directory, because the portable build has no installer and no Start menu shortcut. The click handler is attached to the toast in the running shell, so a click on the toast on screen opens the entry; whether a click on it later in the notification centre does too is to be checked on a real machine, and after Trackwall has quit such a click does nothing, because the shell registers no COM activator. On macOS the notification is an `NSUserNotification` under the app's bundle identifier (a debug build runs outside a bundle and shows it under another app's name), and a thread waits until it is clicked, closed or cleared; up to eight notifications wait like that at once, and the ones past that are shown without a click action. On Linux nothing is shown. Win+Alt+J on Windows and ⌃⌥J on macOS, registered system-wide from Rust (`tauri-plugin-global-shortcut`, with no permission for the UI), jump to `{kind: "next-waiting"}` in the window for sessions (ADR-49), for which the UI opens the next waiting session or task proposals after the selected entry, like Alt+J (ADR-48, ADR-55); the window comes up even when nothing waits. When another app holds the shortcut, the shell starts without it and logs why. A left click on the tray icon opens the tray panel next to it (a double click on Windows opens the main window, as a single click did before): the frameless window `tray-panel` from `tauri.conf.json`, created hidden at start, which loads the UI with `#tray-panel`, takes the daemon's address and token with `daemon_connection` like the main window (and again, with a fresh list, each time the shell shows and focuses it, since the daemon may have been restarted with a new port and token), and lists the task proposals and the waiting sessions, live from `/ws`, with the sessions asking for approval first and their agent dialogs answered in the panel (ADR-66, §4.4). A click on an entry or on "Open Trackwall" calls the command `open_from_tray_panel`, which hides the panel and then jumps like the tray menu (or shows the main window). The panel is not a window for sessions: its focus does not count in the window registry, and the window effect leaves it alone. The panel hides when it loses the focus; a press on the icon that finds the panel open, and a double click, only close it. It sits above the icon when the icon is in the lower half of the screen and below it otherwise, always inside the work area of the icon's monitor; Windows places it in physical pixels, macOS in points, because macOS reports each display in pixels of its own scale; the display is the one that holds the icon read in its scale and whose menu bar is as tall as the icon (`screen_for_icon`). With the menu bar hidden automatically that check has nothing to compare, and next to a Retina display the panel may open on the wrong one.

App updates (ADR-53) are the shell's too: the UI asks for them through the shell commands `check_update`, `install_update` and `cancel_update` and follows the install in the `update-progress` event, one per stage (`connecting`, `downloading` with the bytes and total at most every 250 ms, `verifying`, `installing`, `restarting`), each stage and the first bytes of the download also written to the shell's stderr; in the browser (`trackwall-daemon --ui`) there is no shell, so nothing is offered. The update goes through the same exit as "Quit": `request_restart` ends the event loop, `RunEvent::Exit` stops the daemon, and only then does the new shell start, so the new shell starts its own daemon instead of finding the old one. While an agent is in the middle of a turn (`agentsInTurn` in `lib/session-state.ts`: a running session in `Working`, `Approval` or `Plan`, the rule that makes Close ask first), the offer is "Update after their turn" and "Update now" instead of "Update and restart" (ADR-97): the first sets `afterTurns` on the available update of `nextUpdate` (a later check moves it to the version the shell found last, which is the only one `install_update` takes), and the window that holds it calls `install_update` once the session list, which comes over `/ws`, has no agent in a turn (`dueAfterTurns`); `updateOffer` picks the text and buttons.

The shell also keeps Trackwall present in the system (ADR-65). Start at sign-in (`autostart.rs`): the app commands `autostart_enabled` and `set_autostart` read and switch the system's own start entry through the `auto-launch` crate, a value named after the app in `HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run` (the current user only, the program path in quotes; a value for all users that the user cannot remove makes turning off an error) and a LaunchAgent in `~/Library/LaunchAgents` on macOS with the app's bundle identifier (a path with `&`, `<` or `>`, which the crate would not escape in the XML, or a translocated app is refused); a debug build uses its own name (`Trackwall-dev`). The entry is the only record of the setting, so an entry turned off in Task Manager reads as off; on macOS the crate reads the LaunchAgent file, so turning Trackwall off under Login Items does not show in Settings. The entry starts the shell with `--autostart`, and such a start marks the main window as revealed without showing it (the page loads and connects, the window stays hidden) and holds the build of the saved extra windows (`windows::restore_when_shown`, with the saved list already in the registry, so "New window" meanwhile takes another label) until the main window first shows, so sign-in puts only the tray icon on screen. An update's restart repeats the start's arguments, so `install_update` leaves the marker `restart-shows-windows` with the time in the shell's configuration directory first, and a start removes it and shows its windows when it is less than two minutes old (a marker left by a restart that never came does not show the windows at the next sign-in). At every start an enabled entry is written again with the running app's path, which follows a moved portable folder. The waiting badge (`badge.rs`): the poll that updates the tray (above) also puts the count of waiting sessions and task proposals, the number in the tray tooltip, on the app's icons, and 0 (with an empty tray tooltip and menu) after three polls in a row without a list, when no daemon runs or it no longer answers this shell: the Dock badge on macOS (`set_badge_count`, on a change of the count), an overlay drawn by the shell (a disc with the number up to 9, then "9+", from a 3×5 pixel font) on the taskbar button of every visible window on Windows (`set_overlay_icon`, set on every visible window when the count changes and on a window that has become visible or failed before, because Windows drops the button of a hidden window and makes a new one without the overlay), and a dot on the tray icon (a cut-out ring around a black dot on the macOS template, a dark ring around an amber dot on the coloured icon elsewhere; set together with the template flag). Window zoom (`zoom.rs`): Ctrl+= or Ctrl++, Ctrl+- and Ctrl+0 (⌘ on macOS) are taken by the page (`zoom-shortcut.ts`, capture phase, only in the shell, so a browser keeps its own zoom; they no longer reach a session terminal) and sent as the async app command `zoom_window` with `in`, `out` or `reset`; the shell steps through the browser levels from 0.5 to 2 (an index into the list), calls `set_zoom` on the calling window, sets the page's attribute (below) and, once the web view has zoomed, saves the level per window label, outside the lock of the levels but in the order of the steps, in `zoom.json` in its configuration directory (only `main` and `window-<n>` labels and known levels are read back). A page that starts loading gets its label's level, and one that has loaded gets the attribute `data-zoom` with it, which the UI needs for the drop point on macOS, where wry reports points that are CSS pixels only at actual size (`cssPoint` in `file-drop.ts`). On macOS the View menu has Actual Size, Zoom In and Zoom Out, which zoom the last focused window in the shell, off the main thread; the page takes the keys first, so a key press zooms once. The tray panel does not zoom.

### 5.5 Daemon ↔ 3D scene

`@trackwall/scene` has no transport of its own and does not know the daemon API (ADR-33). From the same responses as the repo list, the "Sessions" view and "Overview", the UI computes the dashboard snapshot `HomeModel`: repos with a status (`clean`, `behind`, `changed`, `unavailable`), the number of changes or commits to pull and the running mise task (from `mise/running`, §4.7), sessions with a state (`working`, `attention`, `idle`, `error`, `done`; `idle` is `Input` the user has seen, ADR-47), connections from the graph, the number of layers and whether the daemon is running. A repo's layer is the column from the graph layout in "Overview" (the longest path from a repo nobody consumes), so the dashboard and the graph do not disagree about layers; a repo without connections has the layer `null`. `HomeScene.apply(model)` takes successive snapshots and keeps one Pawn entity per object (`KeyedEntities`): a new object gets an entity, a vanished one loses it together with its sessions and connections, each stands in its place in the new layout, and a three.js model is rebuilt only when something visible changed. Sessions and running mise tasks reach it from `/ws` events (ADR-36), the rest from polling; the scene does not know where a snapshot comes from.

The "Orbit" layout (`orbitLayout`, a pure function): the daemon in the middle, sessions on the inner track opposite their repo, repos on the outer ring in the arcs of the layers, and repos without connections in the last arc. The kind of a repo's solid follows from its layer: an entry (a terminal), a gateway (a cylinder with an antenna), a backend (a cabinet), a library (a crate). The scene decides which objects have a label (repos that are not clean, and sessions that are waiting or have an error), and the UI writes the label's text; colour and shape say the rest. A click selects an object and highlights its repo's connections, a double click reports going to the session or repo, and pointing reports the object under the cursor for the preview card. Colours come from UI tokens converted to `#rrggbb`, because three.js does not read `oklch`. With `prefers-reduced-motion` the models stand in their pose at time 0.

The "Home" view (`views/home/`, Ctrl+1, the first screen after startup) builds the snapshot in `lib/home-model.ts` (`homeModel`) from the repo list (like the "Repos" view: at once after a `repos.changed` event, otherwise every 30 s with events and every 10 s without them), sessions (the same list as the view bar, from `sessions.changed` events), the graph (at once after a `repos.changed` event, otherwise every 5 minutes with events and every 30 s without them, `GRAPH_PACE`) and running mise tasks (from `mise.running.changed` events; without an event connection sessions every 1 s and `mise/running` every 3 s, §5.2). The daemon "runs" while it answers: a repo list error with a code (for example `invalid_config`) came from it, and when the projects folder needs fixing (`projects_dir_unavailable`, `invalid_config`), the view shows the error and a way to "Settings" instead of the old list, like "Repos". On the left the "Needs you" queue (sessions that are waiting or have an error, with the state from the "Sessions" view, task proposals from the session list, ADR-39, and repos that are not clean; proposals are neither in the snapshot nor on the scene, and a click opens one in "Sessions" right away, because the decision, with an edited prompt, is made there), in the middle the canvas, on the right the details of the selected object with an "Open repo", "Open session" or "Open Daemon" button (without a selection a summary of the environment), and in the corner of the canvas a preview card of the object under the cursor. The tone and text of a dashboard object's status have one place (`homeRepoStatus`, `HOME_SESSION_STATUS`, `homeDaemonStatus`), from which the queue, the details, the preview and the scene labels take them; a repo with commits to pull is not clean on the dashboard, although the repo list counts only changes in the working directory. Selecting from the queue and from the scene is the same state, and the selection of an object that vanished from the snapshot is dropped: the scene highlights the object, and `CameraRig` points the camera at it; without a selection the camera goes back to the starting angle and looks at the middle from a distance chosen for the canvas's proportions (`lib/home-camera.ts`), also after the window is resized, so that the whole ring is in frame. A double click or the button goes to the "Sessions" view with that session, to "Repos" with that repo (only on that transition) or to "Daemon". The canvas (`HomeCanvas`) is loaded lazily (`React.lazy`), because three.js and Pawn are most of the view's weight: the rest of the UI takes only `@trackwall/scene/model` from the scene (the entry `src/model.ts`: keys, snapshot types and the palette, without three.js), which Biome guards, and the build removes the unused Draco decoder files three.js reports despite there being no glTF (`drop-unreferenced-draco.ts`). `lib/scene-palette.ts` reads the scene's colours: it paints every token on one pixel of a 2D canvas over the `--background` and reads back sRGB, so translucent tokens come out as they look in the app. When WebGL 2 does not work (checked on a separate canvas before the engine, because a failed `Engine` constructor leaves a koota world behind, and koota has at most 16 of them) or the context is lost (`webglcontextlost`), and also when the canvas throws (its chunk does not load, the scene fails to build; an `ErrorBoundary` around it), in place of the canvas there is a message with a "Try again" button (a chunk that failed to load fails again until the window reloads, because React's `lazy` and the browser both keep the failed import), and the "Needs you" queue and the details keep working. Errors nothing else catches stop at two more boundaries: one around the current view (the view bar and the other views keep working) and one around the whole page (a message and a reload instead of a blank window). A hidden window (`visibilitychange`) stops the engine loop, because Pawn does not pause it itself; rendering only on change is a request filed with Pawn.

## 6. Data model

The types live in `packages/protocol`. Below is the core; auxiliary fields are left out.

```ts
type RepoId = string;      // slug from the directory name in the projects folder, e.g. "orders-bff"
type ServiceId = string;   // usually = RepoId; a repo can have several services
type TaskId = string;      // ulid

// Today in protocol: { id, path, remote, status: RepoStatus } (§4.5; remote as host/path, ADR-21); the other fields come with cards.
interface Repo {
  id: RepoId; path: string; remote: string; vcs: 'github' | 'gitlab' | 'git';
  defaultBranch: string; head: string;
  analysis?: { commit: string; at: string; cardId: string };
  lastSetup?: RunResult; lastTest?: RunResult;
}

// Today in protocol (cards.ts, http.ts, ADR-25, ADR-30): RepoCard without `repo` (a card depends only on the commit) and without
// `description`; provides are kind 'package' (ecosystem 'maven' | 'npm' | 'gradle-plugin', name, version), 'app'
// (spring.application.name, port, module) and 'endpoint' (method, path, app, handler, request and response types, ADR-38,
// and `reaches`: the client calls its handler reaches, ADR-82), consumes are 'package'
// (with scope) and 'http' (client kind, name, app or address with profiles, calls); instead of `stack`
// and `runtime` there are build files, tools, mechanisms and problems.
interface RepoCard {
  commit: string;
  buildFiles: BuildFile[];   // { path, tool: 'maven'|'gradle'|'npm'|'pnpm'|'yarn'|'bun', name, memberOf }
  provides: Provided[];   // { kind: 'http'|'topic'|'package', key, evidence }
  consumes: Consumed[];   // { kind: 'http'|'topic'|'package'|'env-url', key, evidence }
  dtos: Dto[];            // { name, kind: 'record'|'data-class'|'class'|'enum', fields: { name, type }[], values, evidence } (ADR-38)
  reachTraced: boolean;   // whether every endpoint got its reach (ADR-82): false with Kotlin code or Java files not read or parsed
  tools: ToolRequirement[];   // { tool, version, source: 'mise'|'hint', evidence }
  mise: MiseSetup;            // { configFiles, tasks: { name, evidence }[] }: mise configuration and tasks from the commit (ADR-34)
  mechanisms: Mechanism[];    // { id: MechanismId, evidence: (Evidence & { detail })[] }
  problems: CardProblem[];    // { code: 'files-not-read'|'file-invalid'|'parse-incomplete'|'truncated', path, message } (ADR-80); path: file or module directory, '' for the repo root or the whole repo
  runtime: { ports: number[]; databases: string[]; secretNames: string[] };   // plan: from application*.yml
  description?: { text: string; origin: 'llm'; model: string; at: string };
}

interface RepoTag { name: string; reason: string }   // from the card and from the `repoTags` rules in workspace.yaml

// Today in protocol (graph.ts, ADR-29, ADR-30): GraphEdge between repos (RepoId), kind 'package' | 'http' | 'manual',
// EdgeEvidence with a repo (or null for workspace.yaml) and a `detail` sentence.
interface Edge { from: ServiceId; to: ServiceId; kind: Consumed['kind']; key: string;
                 confidence: 'exact' | 'high' | 'medium' | 'low'; evidence: Evidence[] }

// Today in protocol (services.ts, ADR-45): Service { repo, task (null for the repo root, ADR-61), state, port, portSource
// ('card' | 'task' | 'log'), run (RunState of the task dev), since, reason }, one per repo root and task worktree, in memory; no
// groupId, lease or lastStderr (errors come from logs).
interface Service {
  id: ServiceId; repo: RepoId; port?: number;
  state: 'stopped'|'starting'|'healthy'|'unhealthy'|'stopping'|'crashed';
  groupId?: string; exitCode?: number; lastStderr?: string;
  lease: Lease;
}

interface Lease { service: ServiceId; holder: { kind: 'main' } | { kind: 'task'; taskId: TaskId };
                  worktree: string; since: string }

// Today in code: `RuntimeGroup` in runtime.json (owner as a text key) and `RunState` in protocol (§4.1).
interface ProcessGroup { id: string; leaderPid: number; command: string[]; cwd: string;
                         startedAt: string; owner: { kind: 'service'|'task'|'run'; id: string } }

// Today in protocol: LogRecord without source and ECS fields, LogSource = { kind: 'mise-task'; repo; miseTask } (§4.2, ADR-28).
interface LogRecord { seq: number; ts: string; source: LogSource; stream: 'stdout'|'stderr'|'pty';
                      level: 'trace'|'debug'|'info'|'warn'|'error'; msg: string;
                      traceId?: string; spanId?: string; fields?: Record<string, unknown>; raw: string }

// Today in protocol (agents.ts, ADR-31, ADR-39): Task with prompt, branch, baseBranch, base (base commit), worktree,
// createdAt, title and proposedBy (from a task proposal, with supervisor: whether the supervisor proposed it, ADR-58), model (ADR-98) and summary ({ text, reportedAt } from report_result),
// port (the task port) and included ({ files, problems }: the ignored files copied from the repo root, ADR-61); a session points
// to its task (Session.task); without adapter, budget, leases, changedPaths and recording, and the task state is its session's state.
interface Task {
  id: TaskId; title: string; repo: RepoId; branch: string; worktree: string;
  adapter: 'copilot' | 'claude' | 'codex'; prompt: string;
  state: 'Working'|'Input'|'Approval'|'Plan'|'Done'|'Error'|'Stale';
  stateSource: 'hook'|'osc'|'screen'|'keyboard'|'timeout'|'exit'; stateConfidence: 'exact'|'high'|'medium'|'low';
  stateSince: string;   // TaskState, TaskStateChange: packages/protocol/src/task-state.ts
  budget: { maxMinutes?: number; maxCommits?: number };
  leases: ServiceId[]; changedPaths: string[]; recording: string;
  summary?: string;   // the summary block from the completion protocol
}

interface AuditEntry { id: number; at: string; client: string; tool: string;
                       args: unknown; durationMs: number; ok: boolean; resultDigest: string }
```

Persistence: SQLite (`~/.trackwall/trackwall.db`) for repos, cards (today files `cards/<commit>.json`, ADR-25), edges (a cache; today the daemon keeps only the last graph with its input key in memory, ADR-29, ADR-30), services, leases, tasks, audit; JSONL for logs; session recordings as files; `runtime.json` only for live process groups. The workspace configuration is `workspace.yaml` (projects folder, repo hosts, repo tag rules, package aliases and manual graph edges, port overrides, the pointer to the secrets repo), which the user can keep in a private repo; until a workspace directory is chosen it lives in the daemon's state directory (ADR-12). State never mixes with configuration.

## 7. Flows

**App start.** Tauri → sidecar → `daemon.json` → `/api/health` → the UI fetches the views' data with HTTP actions and opens `/ws`, from which it gets the event topic snapshots (ADR-36). At startup the daemon: cleans up orphans according to `runtime.json`, restores the task list, and once the API listens resumes the sessions the previous daemon left running (ADR-95).

**`ws sync`.** For every repo in parallel (with a concurrency limit): fetch, fast-forward of the default branch, if `head` ≠ `analysis.commit` then dependencies (`mise run setup`) and the card extractors; after all of them, recomputing the graph and a `graph.updated` event (once the graph gets a cache; today it is computed on every request, ADR-29). A repo in which a fast-forward would touch local changes (uncommitted changes in the checked-out default branch, a diverged branch or unpushed commits) is skipped with a warning; changes on another branch do not get in the way (ADR-21). Done (ADR-21, §4.5): fetch and fast-forward from a button in the UI, with a skip reason per repo. The Batch command gives dependencies with the `mise install`, `setup` and `build` steps on selected repos (ADR-27, §4.8), for now always, not only on a new `head`; extractors, the graph and the CLI command will come together with those modules.

**Starting a service.** Today (ADR-45, §4.3): UI or `start_service` → `services.start {repo, task, dependencies}` (with `task`, the root from that task's worktree on the task port, ADR-61) → plan from the graph's `http` and `manual` edges → waves from the dependencies: `mise.tasks` of the repo root (is there `dev`?) → `server.port` from the card, or the task port for a task's root, free → `mise.start({repo, task}, 'dev')` through `procs` (in the task's worktree with the task port in its environment) → observed: log (port, `Started`) and a TCP connection → `healthy`, `unhealthy` or `crashed` with errors from its log → next wave → `call_endpoint` to the port that answered, with errors from every `dev` log. Eventually: UI → `services.start(id)` → `leases.check` → `secrets.resolve(repo)` (git show of the `Secret` manifest from the `origin/<repo>` branch, keys from `data` decoded from base64, from `stringData` taken as they are, into env) → `mise.run('dev')` through `procs.spawn` in a group → `logs` registers the source and masks → the `mise run health` probe periodically → `service.state`. Failure: `crashed` with the code and the last stderr line on the service row, without restarting in a loop (at most three attempts, then giving up).

**Agent task.** Today (ADR-31, §4.4): UI → `create-task {repo, prompt}` → `vcs.addWorktree` from the default branch → `tasks.json` → a Copilot CLI session in the worktree with `--interactive=<prompt>` → the "Changes" tab polls `task-changes` → after the session ends, resume (`--resume`) or `remove-task` (worktree, optionally the branch). While the task runs, its panel also asks `task-shared-files` for the shared-files warning (ADR-44). From a main session (ADR-39): session → `propose_task` → a proposal in the "Sessions" view → the user reads the prompt, edits it and clicks "Start task" (`approve-proposal`) → the same as `create-task`, with the proposal's identifier → the task session works and calls `report_result` → the main session, which ended its turn, gets the daemon's prompt to call `get_updates` (`result_reported`; or it waited inside its turn with `wait_for_session`), reads the changed files through `get_session` and reviews their diff through `get_task_changes` → to steer the task it calls `send_message`, and the task session gets the prompt to call `get_messages` (ADR-68). Eventually: the UI creates a task (repo, prompt, adapter, budget) → `vcs.worktree.add` → the prompt written to a file → the adapter builds the command and the MCP configuration (the `/mcp` address, a session token) → `procs.spawnPty` → state detectors → a `sessions.changed` event with the session snapshot (ADR-36) → tray and "jump to the waiting one". When the agent changes a service, it asks for the lease (`Approval` in the UI, or automatically depending on a setting). Ending: the summary block → `summary`, a PR proposal, leases given back, recording closed.

**Start my day.** Today (ADR-70, §4.11): supervisor panel → `day-start/start` → refused while a supervisor runs → `workspace.syncAndWait` (sync of every repo, ADR-21) → `dayReport` against `day-start.json` (remote default branches, sync results, open tasks) → `day-start.json` → a new supervisor with `--interactive=<first message>` → it calls `get_day_report` and briefs the user, proposing at most three tasks that wait for approval (ADR-39).

**A cross-repo error.** The user sees an error in the FE → clicks a record with a `trace_id` → `trace(trace_id)` returns the records from the BFF and the services in `seq` order → a timeline view per service → "create a task with this context" pastes the service identifiers and the `seq` range into the prompt, and the agent pulls in the details itself through MCP.

## 8. Security

- Loopback only, a token, the `Host`/`Origin` check, constant-time comparison.
- The error journal (ADR-94, §4.14) keeps error messages from the user's computer; they are anonymized before they are saved, by known values and by patterns, and nothing sends them anywhere. Anonymization of free text is best effort: a name nobody told the daemon and no pattern catches (a client's name in a message of a library) can stay, which is why the journal has no way to send itself and its section in Settings says what it holds. `record-ui-error` takes text from the UI behind the token, bounded by its schema, and the journal keeps at most 100 errors; it opens only `assets/<name>.js.map` in the folder the daemon serves the UI from.
- The session token (ADR-39) next to the daemon token: the daemon knows which session calls MCP, so `report_result` writes only to its own task, and the call goes to the log with the session, and `run_mise_task` and `stop_mise_task` work only in the session's repo (ADR-41); later it will write the session into the audit. The token lies in the session directory in the state directory, as a header in the session's `mcp-config.json` (ADR-89; user-only on macOS and Linux), not in process arguments, because other users of the system can see those; the daemon keeps only its hash. A process running as the same user can read another session's token (like `daemon.json`), so the token says who is calling but does not protect against a user process; it also grants no permission the daemon token does not have. The session plugin's `hooks.json` and the session's `mcp-config.json` in the same directory hold the daemon token and the session token as headers (ADR-88, ADR-89), readable like `daemon.json`; a hook only appends a record to its own session's `hooks.jsonl`.
- `resumable-sessions.json` and `last-supervisor.json` (ADR-95, ADR-96) name which Copilot CLI conversations the daemon resumes; the schemas take only UUIDs, so neither file reaches a path or an argument beyond `--resume=<uuid>`, but a process of the same user that writes them can point the supervisor at another session's conversation. Such a process already reads the tokens next to them, so this is left as it is.
- A process's working directory is always inside a repo or worktree, apart from the supervisor, which runs in the projects folder itself (ADR-58, below); paths from input are normalised and checked for escaping the root. Repo browsing reads only objects from the HEAD commit, and the `RepoPath` schema validates the path in the repo (no `.`, `..`, empty segments and control characters; `\` is an ordinary name character).
- The daemon runs `git` in every subdirectory of the projects folder that has `.git` (ADR-18), so the list also gets a repo the user did not knowingly choose (for example an unpacked archive). The repo configuration (`.git/config`) applies as in a terminal, except for keys with which the repo would make git run a program: the daemon disables `core.fsmonitor` (`git status`) and `log.showSignature` (`git log` would call `gpg.program`). Repo browsing and status commands (`status`, `log`, `ls-tree`, `cat-file`) run no hooks and no `textconv` (sync does, below), but `git status` can run a `clean` filter (for example git-lfs) defined in `.git/config` for paths from `.gitattributes`: a gap we leave, because disabling filters would break the status of LFS repos; only an allowlist of filters or asking the user for trust at the first repo will close it. A new git command in the daemon must check this again.
- Cloning from a host (ADR-19) runs `glab` or `gh` with the full repo name from the UI request. The `RemoteRepoName` schema validates the name (segments do not start with `-` or `.`), so it will not be taken for a program option or produce a directory outside the projects folder; the host must be in `workspace.yaml`. The host's token stays in `glab` and `gh`. The API response is external data: parsed with a schema, and an error does not contain its content. A cloned repo is subject to the same rules as every repo in the folder (the `clean` filter gap above). Gap: `glab`/`gh` and the clone's git are not in a process group, so a clone interrupted by the time limit or by daemon exit may keep running in the background until git finishes by itself (§4.5).
- A mise task is a program from the repo configuration: running it from the UI is the user's consent to executing the repo's code, like `mise run` in a terminal. The daemon runs only a task from the current `mise tasks ls` list, in the repo root, without a shell on the daemon side; mise refuses to give the list itself for configuration without trust. `mise trust` (the `trust` action) is only in the UI and applies only to the configuration in the repo root; MCP has no tool that would trust. On macOS the daemon runs the user's interactive login shell once (`$SHELL -ilc`) to learn the `PATH`, so it executes their shell startup files, like every new terminal.
- The Batch command (ADR-27, §4.8) runs `mise install` and the `setup` and `build` tasks with one click in every selected repo, by default in all of them from the projects folder, including one the user did not knowingly choose (ADR-18). Choosing repos and steps in the UI is consent, like running a task from the repo details. Tasks come only from the `mise tasks ls` list, which mise does not give without trust. Gap: `mise install` does not ask for trust when the repo configuration has only `[tools]` (checked on mise 2026.10.18: `mise trust --show` says `untrusted`, and the installation goes ahead), so it downloads and runs the installer of a tool the repo names (for example an asdf plugin with an address from `mise.toml`), like `mise install` in a terminal. Requiring `mise trust` before the `tools` step, or an allowlist of tool backends in the settings, will close it.
- Cleaning up orphans trusts `runtime.json` in the state directory; a user process that writes someone else's PID there could kill that process itself anyway. A group whose leader is dead is killed without checking the start time: the exception would be a new process with the same PID that created its own POSIX session and then ended, leaving descendants in it.
- Under Node on Windows (the compatibility mode, ADR-20) a group is a tree with `taskkill /T`: an empty group means only "the leader is dead", a descendant orphaned by an earlier death of its parent falls out of the tree and survives the stop, and after the daemon is killed hard nothing is cleaned up (a live PID from `runtime.json` only goes to the log). The release is a Bun binary with Job Objects, so the gap concerns running from source under Node.
- Short queries to `mise` (`tasks ls`, `trust`) go outside `procs`, like `git`: after the limit the daemon ends `mise` itself, and a program mise started from the `[env]` of a trusted configuration may survive.
- Sync (ADR-21) runs `git fetch` and `git merge --ff-only` in every repo from the projects folder, including one the user did not knowingly choose (ADR-18). Gap: fetch runs programs named in the repo's local `.git/config` (`core.sshCommand`, `remote.<name>.uploadpack`, `credential.helper`), and `merge` the repo's hooks (`post-merge`, `reference-transaction`) and `smudge` filters (for example git-lfs), like `git pull` in a terminal. It is the same class of gap as the `clean` filter above, but wider, because it also covers keys the daemon does not disable; we leave it, because disabling credential helpers, ssh and LFS would break syncing ordinary repos. Only reading these keys from the user and system configuration alone, or asking for trust at the first repo, will close it. The remote name from `.git/config` goes to git after `--`, so it will not be taken for an option. The remote address on the list contains no user or token from the URL, and `transport::` (with a command) does not reach the list at all. Gap: the sync's git is not in a process group, so when interrupted by the time limit or daemon exit it may keep running in the background (like a clone, §4.5).
- The repo card (ADR-25) reads files from a repo the user may not have chosen (ADR-18), so their content is untrusted: git reads objects (`ls-tree`, `cat-file --batch`) without filters, textconv and hooks, and paths from the tree go to `cat-file` through stdin, not as arguments. Extractors execute nothing (a Gradle script is read as text), the XML parser knows no DTDs or external entities, and the limits (1 MB per file, 2000 build and configuration files, 3000 source files, 3000 type files pulled in for endpoint contracts, 64 MB in total, 64 MB of file list, 4096 characters of a value after substituting Maven properties) bound the memory per repo. Time too: the extractors' expressions have no adjacent `\s*` or classes that give quadratic backtracking on a long line (the wrapper address matched only in the file name, `${` without nesting), Gradle lines over 2000 characters and paths over 1024 characters in the mechanism inventory are skipped, key lines (evidence for npm dependencies and mise tools) are computed in one pass over the file, workspace globs from the repo are matched by dynamic programming (pattern × path), and build roots check only a file's ancestors; `cards-limits.test.ts` keeps each of these cases under 1–2 s. A key from the repo does not reach the object prototype (`version.ref = "constructor"`). Knowingly left: the `k8s/…/*.yaml` pattern in the mechanism inventory is quadratic in the path length, but a path has at most 1024 characters (a fraction of a millisecond per file), and the Gradle scanner does not treat `\` as an escape in triple quotes (right for Kotlin; in Groovy it shows at most a few more lines, it never hides a line). The facts on a card are text from the repo: the UI shows them as text, and the agent gets them through MCP as data that may contain an attempted prompt injection (like the README from `get_repo`). A card file in the state directory is named only by the commit hash checked against a pattern and is parsed with a schema on read.
- Java and Kotlin code, Spring configuration and frontend files (ADR-30) are as untrusted as build files: nothing is compiled or executed (a Vite or webpack configuration is text), `git grep` gets a fixed expression from the daemon and paths from the tree do not reach the arguments, and source files have the same read limits (3000 files, 4 MB of list). The tokenizer and the declaration parser go through a file once, a stack counts nested brackets, and concatenating constants has a depth limit (8) and a length limit (2048 characters); `${…}` in configuration expands with memoisation, a depth limit (10) and a length limit (4096), so neither a cycle nor keys that double each other take memory; YAML has a depth limit (64), and proxy configuration lines over 2000 characters are skipped. Bracket pairs are computed once per file, an unclosed bracket does not drag an expression to the end of the file, and of a call's argument list longer than 256 tokens only the arguments that fit within the limit as a whole are taken (the address and method come first). YAML is read without checking for repeated keys (the library compares each with each), a file has at most 20,000 entries across all documents, a module at most 32 profiles (more is a card problem), and in each profile only keys with an address or `${…}` are expanded. An error or time limit of `git grep` is a card problem, it does not take the card away (the card stays without facts from code). The number of facts is bounded too (20 endpoints per method, 200 calls per client, 2000 `.uri(…)` and `RestTemplate` calls taken apart per file, 500 addresses from configuration per module, and beyond that last limit a card problem). `.env*` files are read from the whole commit (choosing a file knows only its path), but only variables with an HTTP address from files next to `package.json` reach the card, without the user, password and query; the other values stay in daemon memory while the card is computed. Addresses on the card lose the user and password (up to the last `@` before the path, and when there is no host with a port before the first `/`, up to the last `@` before the query, because a password may contain `/`; a part before the path that is still not a host with a port disappears entirely), the query, the fragment and path segments that look like a token (20+ characters with letters and digits, for example a webhook key), and call paths lose their query, because an address from `.env` or `application.yml` may carry a password or an API key, and the card goes to the UI and to the agent. Matching calls to endpoints in the graph compares a call only with endpoints with the same number of segments that have its rarest fixed segment (or a variable) at the same position (and with `**`), so thousands of endpoints in a few dozen repos are computed in a fraction of a second; `cards-limits.test.ts` keeps these cases under 1–2 s.
- The tree-sitter parser (ADR-37) gets the code from the repo unchanged: it runs in WASM memory without access to files and the network, but synchronously in the daemon's thread, and the Kotlin grammar grows in time faster than linearly on malicious input. That is why every parse has a time limit (`PARSE_TIME_LIMIT_MS`, 1 s), after which it is aborted and the file has no tree, and the whole card a total budget of 10 s (ADR-38), after which it parses nothing more. Gap: the first computation of the card of such a repo can stall the daemon API for about 11 s (the budget and the time of the last file: parsing up to 1 s and reading the tree; once per commit, and once more after a daemon restart, because such a card does not go to disk); parsing in a worker thread would close it. Since the card warm-up (ADR-63) this happens without anyone asking, right after the start and after a commit in such a repo, so a stall can come while the user types in a session terminal. The endpoint reach (ADR-82) parses every Java file of the repo, not only those from the search, so it gives way to other work every 50 ms (a request waits at most that long plus one file) and stops after 60 s and 20,000 files; what it parsed is kept by blob id (at most 50,000 reads, in memory), so a large repo costs seconds once and a commit then costs only its changed files.
- The connection graph (ADR-29) reads nothing from the repo beyond the card: the edges from cards carry the same untrusted strings (package names, paths; a package name does not reach the object prototype), and the `graph` entries come from the user's `workspace.yaml`. No MCP tool changes them; `who_calls` and `subgraph_for` only read the graph, `subgraph_for` has a bounded depth (3) so that the result does not grow to the whole graph, and `find_endpoint` a bounded number of results (50) and query length (300 characters).
- Test results (ADR-60) read XML files from a repo or a worktree, written by the build and possibly by the task's agent, so they are untrusted like build files: the walk stays inside the checkout (no symbolic links followed, bounded in directories, depth and reports), a report over 20 MB is not read, the parser builds plain elements (attributes in a `Map`, no entity expansion beyond the five XML entities and character references, no DTD), and a broken report is a problem in the result, not an error. Every text taken from a report is masked with the secrets of the user's environment before it is cut, and each stack line is cut to 500 characters, so one failure cannot fill the agent's context; a secret a test printed into `system-out` never leaves the file, because `system-out` is not read. Gap: a secret known only to the test run (from mise `[env]` or a `.env` the test loaded) is not masked, as in logs (§4.2). The parser runs in the daemon's thread, so a checkout with many large reports slows other requests for a moment.
- The secrets repo is cloned outside the workspace, in a directory invisible to worktrees; in a task's profile the secrets repo is excluded from the agent's file tools (adapter configuration).
- Environment variables never reach logs or the audit; values are masked before the buffer (§4.2). Gap: the daemon masks only the values of variables with secret names from the environment it starts the process with itself. A secret the process gets otherwise (from the `[env]` of a mise configuration, from a `.env` file, from a password manager), or one with a name outside the list, reaches the log in the UI, the JSONL file in the state directory and the agent through the MCP tool `tail` in plain text, as it reaches the terminal. The `secrets` module, which knows the values it injects itself, will close this; reading `[env]` from mise (`mise env --json`) is a possible addition. Log files lie in the state directory with user permissions, like the rest of the state. Known edge cases of masking: a secret cut by an ANSI sequence in the process output, a line of a multi-line key shorter than 8 characters (for example the last base64 line of a PEM key), and an unterminated line longer than 64 KB made entirely of overlapping occurrences of secrets (then the split falls at the end).
- The session terminal (ADR-26) accepts any keys from the UI: the only exception to the rule of identifiers instead of commands (§1.2), like the terminal in VS Code. The daemon starts the session with a command it builds itself (a program from the user's `PATH` and the plugin directory), in the root of a repo from the projects folder. The terminal and event WebSockets have the same protections as HTTP, with the token in the subprotocol (`api/sockets.ts`). Copilot CLI gets the user's environment (`userEnv`), as in their terminal; the session screen goes neither to logs nor to MCP, only to the user's window, so only the last lines of the session state are masked (`logs.lastLinesOnly`, §4.2). The plugin's hook is the daemon binary run by Copilot CLI from the plugin directory (not the repo), which only appends a record to the session file, without the daemon token. The `vite dev` proxy adds the token also to a terminal connection without an `Origin` header, so in development mode any local process gets the session terminal and the `/ws` events through Vite, as it gets `/api` today; the release has no such proxy. Gap: Copilot CLI started from a PTY can itself start programs outside the process group if they create their own POSIX session (`setsid`) or leave the job with `BREAKAWAY_OK`; such processes survive a session stop.
- Agent dialogs (ADR-66) are screen text the agent and the repo produced (a command, a file's lines), so the UI shows them as plain text, and the MCP tools leave them out: like the last screen lines, one session's screen does not reach another. Answering one is a key typed into the session for the user, only from the UI (`answer-dialog`, with the daemon token), never from MCP, so no MCP tool lets an agent approve its own or another session's tools; a process of the user that reads the token from `daemon.json` (§5.1), a task session's shell command among them, can call `answer-dialog`, as it could already type into a session over `/ws/pty`. The daemon types only a digit of an option it read on the screen, or Esc, and only while the screen still shows the dialog the user saw in the same wait (its id), so a click does not answer a request asked after it, even an identical one; the check sees the screen as far as the emulator has processed it, so output that has not reached the emulator yet is not seen, the same as for a key typed in the terminal at that moment. The session's process draws the screen, so it can draw a dialog of its own; answering it only sends that process a key, which it could have acted on without asking. Tool permission rules come from the user's `workspace.yaml` and go to Copilot CLI as one argument each (`--allow-tool=<pattern>`, a pattern on one line without control characters), never through a shell.
- A task's state from hooks and OSC comes from the session's process, so the session can forge it (append a record to `hooks.jsonl`, print OSC 9;4). The state is only information for a human and gives the session no permission; it is to stay that way. A hook record stores only the fields on the list (without the prompt, tool arguments and results), and the hook payload is parsed with a schema like every input from outside. Exceptions to remember with fixtures: the trimmed notification text (it may contain the command or full path the agent asks about) and the window title in `states.jsonl` (a description of the current work), and a `.cast` recording has the whole session screen (from the keyboard only the kind of session key, without content). The state from session keys comes from the terminal window through `/ws/pty/<session>`, but the session's process runs as the same user, can read the token from `daemon.json` and send a key over the same WebSocket, so it can forge this state too, like hooks and OSC.
- A task (ADR-31) passes the user's prompt from the UI to Copilot CLI as one argument `--interactive=<prompt>`, without a shell (so does the day start with its first message and the user's `dayStart.prompt` from `workspace.yaml`, ADR-70): the prompt is content, not a command, and the `=` form does not let it become an option. The daemon builds the branch and the worktree directory from the prompt only from `[a-z0-9-]` characters. `git worktree add` fills the working directory like `git checkout` in a terminal: it runs the repo's filters (`smudge`, LFS) and `post-checkout` hook; the same consent to the repo's code as an agent session in it. `tasks.json` lies in the state directory and can be changed by hand, and ending a task deletes the worktree with `--force` and the branch with `branch -D`, so the worktree directory is not in the file (the daemon computes it in `worktrees/`), and the read accepts only a `trackwall/[a-z0-9-]` branch and a full base commit hash, which goes to `git diff`. Task changes are computed by `git diff` without external diff programs and `textconv` from the repo configuration; the working directory diff runs the `clean` filter, like `git status` (the `clean` filter gap above). The same applies to the "Changes" tab of a repo from the projects folder (ADR-34), including a repo the user did not knowingly choose.
- Review comments (ADR-62) are text the user typed in the UI, so they need no `visibleText` check, but they still do not go to the session as keys: the daemon types only its own fixed prompt into the PTY, and only while the session waits in its input box, and the comments reach the agent through `get_review_comments`, which returns only the caller's own task's comments (session token, ADR-39). What the comments ask for is up to the user, as with a task prompt. Text the user types or pastes in the session terminal holds the prompt back until the user's next message is submitted, so the prompt does not join text the user is writing; text recalled from history with the arrow keys is not detected, and the daemon does not clear the box first, since Copilot CLI has no documented key for that. Gap: `send-review` is an HTTP action with the daemon token, which any process of the user can read from `daemon.json`, so a session's agent could send a "review" to another task's session and its text would reach that agent as the user's; the same holds for every API action today (§5.2), and only the MCP tools tell sessions apart (ADR-39).
- Sessions in MCP (ADR-36): every session with the Trackwall MCP server sees all sessions and tasks through `list_sessions` and `get_session`, with the task prompts and window titles, and the changes of every task's worktree with their diff through `get_task_changes` (files a session process could read from disk anyway); these are the user's prompts in their own daemon, and the tools only read. The session screen and the last lines of an ended group (`run.lastLines`) do not reach MCP: the last lines are masked only with the values of secrets from the process environment (§4.2), and the agent also prints in the terminal what it read from files (`.env`) or got in the prompt, so a secret printed in one session's terminal does not pass to another. Gap: the window title (`windowTitle`, from OSC 0 / 2) goes to MCP without masking; Copilot CLI writes a description of the current work there, but the session's process can print any text there. The session token (above) will later make it possible to narrow what a session sees; today each sees everything, including task proposals.
- Task proposals and summaries (ADR-39) are text from an agent that may have read untrusted content from repos (READMEs, code, task output), so they carry the same class of prompt injection as tool results: a proposal's prompt will reach another session as its first message, and a summary will reach the session that is waiting. A human is the gate: a proposal starts nothing, the UI shows the whole prompt to read and edit before "Start task", and a task from it has the same boundaries as a task from the form (a worktree in the state directory, a command built by the daemon, the prompt as the content of `--interactive=`). Automatic approval, if it ever exists, will only be a setting in the UI, never an MCP tool. A repo removal (ADR-83) carries no command at all: the session names an action from a closed list and a repo identifier the daemon resolves, the daemon makes the title, and only the reason is agent text, shown as text; what the daemon does on approval (a move inside the projects folder, refused while anything runs in the repo) is fixed in code. The UI shows the prompt, title and summary as text, and the MCP tools reject characters in them that a human will not see but a model will read (`visibleText`: format characters, with hidden tag characters U+E0000–E007F and direction changes, private-use characters, line separators; not unassigned ones, because their list depends on the engine's Unicode tables); at the cost that emoji joined with U+200D will not pass in proposals. The limits (20 waiting proposals, 80 characters of title, 8000 of prompt, 4000 of summary, 55 s of waiting in one call) bound how much daemon memory and time a session in a loop takes. The diff from `get_task_changes` is not filtered this way: it is the task's code, which the reviewing session could read from the worktree itself, and rewriting it would make it differ from the files; so a task agent misled by a repo can put invisible characters in a comment that the reviewing session reads as instructions, which the "Changes" tab does not show the user either. Gaps: a session can propose endlessly after every decision, and the list does not say how many proposals this session has already made. Approval in the UI is a gate for an agent that uses the MCP tools, not a security boundary: the session's process runs as the same user, can read the token from `daemon.json` and call `approve-proposal` or `create-task` itself through the HTTP API, just as it can start any process without Trackwall. Only a separate UI token the session processes cannot read will close this.
- The supervisor (ADR-58) has the rights of any session: it reads every session and task and proposes work, and every proposal waits for the user; no MCP tool lets it type into another session's terminal or start a session or task on its own (`resume_task` only continues a task the user already approved, in its own worktree, ADR-90), and it sends task messages only to the tasks a supervisor proposed (ADR-68, below). It runs in the projects folder, so Copilot CLI's own tools in it (shell, file edits) would reach every repo of the folder, not just one; since ADR-78 it starts with `--deny-tool=shell` and `--deny-tool=write`, which Copilot CLI applies before any approval, also before the user's own `toolPermissions` and an approval for the session, so it reads files but changes none and runs no command (contract test). The user lifts that with `supervisor.changesCode: true` in `workspace.yaml`; then its session instructions ask it to leave changes in a repo to its tasks, which is an instruction to the model, not a boundary, and Copilot CLI's folder trust and approval prompts are the boundary, as for any session. Not covered: the user's own MCP servers (a repo host's server may commit or merge), which Trackwall does not know and leaves to the user's own rules. The `ProposedBy.supervisor` mark is set by the daemon from the calling session's token, never from the tool's arguments. `run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `start_service` and `stop_service` refuse the supervisor (`bad_request`, `assertCallerRepo`): a session runs processes only in its own repo, and the supervisor has none.
- Task messages (ADR-68) are text from one agent to another without a human reading each one, so they carry the class of prompt injection of task proposals (ADR-39): a proposer misled by repo content can tell its task's agent to do something the user did not ask for. The bounds: a message goes only to a task the sender proposed, whose prompt the user read and approved, so a session cannot steer work the user started or another session delegated; it never reaches the terminal as keys (the daemon types only its fixed prompt, and the text comes through `get_messages`, as review comments do); `visibleText` rejects characters the user would not see in the task panel, where every message stays visible with its sender; the task's session instructions say that a message updates the task's prompt (the proposer drafted it) but that what the user typed into the task's session or sent as a review ranks above a message, and that text elsewhere claiming to come from the proposer is no message; and at most 5 messages go to a task until the user takes part again, so two agents cannot keep each other working, each turn starting the other's, without the user.
