The approved prompt does not limit what a message asks for: a message updates the task and wins over its prompt where they differ (ADR-68), also over wording the user changed when approving the proposal, so the bound on a misled proposer is the panel where the user sees each message, the cap and the user's own text ranking above a message. Task updates go the other way with a fixed shape: the daemon writes them from what it observed, and the only agent text in them is the task's summary from `report_result`, which the proposer could read through `get_session` anyway. Gap: the sender is recognised by its session token, but like every MCP tool this is a guard for the agent, not a boundary (a session process can call the HTTP API with the daemon token, §5.2); and "the user took part" counts any text typed into the sender's session, also text unrelated to the task.
- The README in the UI (ADR-35) is content from the repo: HTML goes through sanitisation with GitHub's schema, a Mermaid diagram is rendered in `strict` mode, images do not load, and a link opens outside the window only as `http` or `https` in canonical form. `workspace/open-link` makes the system open such an address; whoever has the daemon token can do it without a click, like `open-editor`. Gap: the daemon sends no CSP header with the UI files, so in browser mode (`--ui`) an image from the internet in a Mermaid diagram label (`strict` mode lets `<img>` through) will load; in the Tauri window CSP blocks it. The Tauri window also has no navigation guard (`on_navigation`), so the protection against a link from a README is only intercepting the click in the UI; the WebView context menu ("open in new window") remains to be checked manually.
- mise tasks through MCP (ADR-41): `run_mise_task` runs code from the repo's mise configuration, like the button in the UI and like `mise run` in the agent's terminal. Boundaries: a task only from the current `mise tasks ls` list (mise does not give it for configuration without trust, and trust can be given only in the UI), the command built by the daemon from the name (§1.2), in the repo root through `procs`, a Trackwall session only in the repo it works in, a line in the daemon log with the repo, task and session before the start, and `destructiveHint` (also on `stop_mise_task`), so that the MCP client asks for approval as for a shell command (Copilot CLI asks about every MCP tool until the user allows all of them). The task's output comes back to the agent through `tail` with masking and the gap with secrets from `[env]` (above), and `lastLines` in `run` is masked the same way. Gaps: mise trusts the configuration's path, not its content, so an agent that can change a trusted `mise.toml` or a task file in the repo root (a session in the repo root, the supervising agent) can run any command through `run_mise_task`; it is the same consent the MCP client gives anyway for writing a file and for a shell command, but consent to the MCP tool alone does not show the task's content. mise's `paranoid` mode (trust in a content hash) or running only tasks from the commit (the repo card knows them from `RepoCard.mise`) will close it. A task session runs its mise tasks in its worktree only when it passes its task (ADR-59, the next point); without it they run in the repo root, on the branch the repo is on. Calls go only to the daemon log, because there is no `audit` table. Limiting a session to its repo works only for calls from its bridge (with the session token): the session's process can read the daemon token from `daemon.json` itself and call `/mcp` without a session token, like an agent outside Trackwall, so it is a gate for an agent, not a security boundary (like approving proposals, ADR-39).
- mise tasks in a task worktree (ADR-59): the worktree's config is not trusted with `mise trust`; the daemon trusts the worktree path for one run (`MISE_TRUSTED_CONFIG_PATHS`) only while the repo root's config is trusted and the worktree's mise files are those the user trusted: as the worktree was created from the base commit, or as the user trusted them later in the task panel, by a digest of the version they saw. So a command that `mise run` builds comes from a mise config the user consented to, and an agent that rewrites `mise.toml`, adds `mise.local.toml` (which git ignores) or a file task in its worktree runs nothing through MCP until the user has read the files in the task panel; a symbolic link among the mise files counts by its target inside the worktree, and one out of it is refused, since mise follows it to a file outside the check. Not covered, knowingly: the code the mise task runs is the agent's own work, so a test, a build script (`build.gradle`, `package.json` scripts) or a file a config names elsewhere (`task_config.includes`, `[env] _.source`) can run any command the agent wrote, the same as in the repo root (ADR-41) and as the agent's own shell; the trusted path also covers subdirectories, so a mise task that changes into a subdirectory with its own `mise.toml` (a monorepo) loads a config outside the checked files. The check runs once per start, before mise lists the worktree's tasks and runs one, and the trusted path stays in the environment of the whole run, so a config the agent swaps in that moment, or while a long run calls `mise` again, is loaded unchecked; a mise task can also start in the moment between the check of a running process and the start of a task's removal. A link's target is checked by its real path and then opened by name, so an agent that swaps a directory on that path for a link in between can make the panel show a file from outside the worktree (up to 64 KB, to the user only). The panel shows the text as UTF-8 as it is, so invisible or direction-changing characters ("Trojan Source") can make it read differently from what mise reads; the digest still names the bytes. Only a session's own bridge is held to its own task's worktree, as with the repo gate above.
- Verifications (ADR-64) run the mise task `test` from the worktree's trusted mise config, the same as `run_mise_task` with the task: the button is the user's consent, and `verify_task` has the session gate of `run_mise_task` and is destructive, so a client that asks before destructive tools asks. The tests themselves are the agent's code (§4.4); their verdict is information for a human, not a check that lets anything through, since nothing merges on a pass.
- The task worktrees folder (ADR-84): ending a task deletes `<folder>/<repo>/<branch>` with the folder taken from `tasks.json` (a full path, at most 4096 characters), also without asking git when git no longer knows the directory and the user confirmed the loss. The file is the daemon's own state, like `repoDir` in it, which already decides where git runs; someone who can write it can do more as the user anyway. Not closed: the daemon does not check that the folder is one a setting ever named, and the check that the folder is not in a repo compares paths as typed, so a link into a repo (or a differently cased path on macOS) gets past it; the cost is a worktree showing as untracked files in that repo, not a deletion.
- Task worktrees ready to run (ADR-61): the files `.worktreeinclude` names are copied from the repo root into every new task worktree, and they are typically local config with secrets (`.env`, a local Spring profile). The task's agent can read them, as it can read the same files in the root with its own shell, and they stay in the worktree until the task ends; Trackwall does not show them to other sessions (`get_task_changes` leaves ignored files out). The file names what to copy, the repo's ignore rules decide whether: only ignored, untracked, regular files inside the root, so `.worktreeinclude` cannot pull in a tracked file or anything outside the repo. A copied `mise.local.toml` is part of the worktree's trusted mise config from the start. The task port is reserved only among tasks: a program outside Trackwall listening on it makes the task's service `skipped` (checked before the start, as for the card port), and the gap of ADR-45 remains (a process that takes the port after the start gets the requests).
- Services over MCP (ADR-45): `start_service` runs the same task `dev` from the trusted config as `run_mise_task`, also in the repos the service calls; which repos comes from the graph (cards and `workspace.yaml`), not from the agent, and a Trackwall session names only its own repo. A repo can thus get its dependencies started because its code or config names them; they are repos from the user's projects folder, with a config the user trusted. `call_endpoint` sends the agent's method, path, headers and body only to `127.0.0.1` or `::1` and only to the port that answered the probe of a service Trackwall started, so it is not a proxy to other hosts or to other local programs; within that it can change data (a `DELETE` reaches the service's database), hence `destructiveHint`. Unlike start and stop, a Trackwall session may call the service of any repo: checking a change end to end means calling the services behind its own, and these are the user's local dev services, which the session could reach with `curl` anyway. The response is masked with the secret values of the service environment, as its log; a secret the service returns from elsewhere (its own config, a database) reaches the agent, as it would with `curl` in the agent's terminal. The path, headers and body do not go to the daemon log (`auditArgs`). Gap: a port that answers is assumed to be the service's own; a process Trackwall did not start that takes the logged port after the start (`portOwner`, §4.1, is not done) would get the requests.
- The clipboard (ADR-51): the page can write text to the system clipboard through `copy_text`, never read it. `copy_text` takes up to 1 MiB and needs no gesture, so script running in the page (an XSS) could set the clipboard. A session's program sets the clipboard with OSC 52 only once per Ctrl+C that goes to it or right click, within 1 s, and a query (`OSC 52 ; c ; ?`) is never answered. Gap: the first OSC 52 after such a gesture is copied whatever it holds, so output that reaches the terminal in that second with a sequence of its own (a file the agent shows) takes the place of the selection.
- Folder picker, Show in Explorer/Finder and drag and drop (ADR-51): `dropped-folder` reads any full path the UI sends (a `stat` and the folder's entries) and answers what it is, which tells a token holder no more than `set-projects-dir` already does; it saves nothing. `open-folder` takes only a repo id, so the system opens a path from the list, never one from the request. A path dropped on a session goes into its terminal as pasted text, the same as the user typing it. The window still gets no plugin commands: the picker is the app's own `pick_projects_folder` command. The dialog plugin also replaces `window.alert` and `window.confirm` in every page with calls to its `message` command, which the capability does not grant: `alert` would fail and `confirm` would return a Promise, which is always truthy, so Biome bans both globals in `packages/ui/src` and the test `apps/desktop/test/ui-dialogs.test.ts` also catches calls through `window` and `globalThis`.
- The shell on Windows writes two registry values at every start (`HKCU\Software\Classes\AppUserModelId\<app identifier>`, `DisplayName` and `IconUri`, ADR-50) and the icon file `notification-icon.png` in the state directory, and never removes the registry key, so a portable build deleted with its folder leaves it behind (the portable `README.txt` says so). A notification for a task proposal shows its title, which is agent text (ADR-39), as plain text. The tray panel window has the same capability as the main window (`core:default` and the app's commands); `open_from_tray_panel` only shows windows and passes the target on to the UI, which parses it with a schema. The global shortcut is registered from Rust, and the UI has no permission of the shortcut plugin.
- `trackwall://` links (ADR-52) can come from anywhere: a web page, a chat, an agent's answer. A link only switches the view in the window: the shell turns only `repo`, `session` or `task` with one id of safe characters into a jump, the UI parses the id with the protocol schemas, and no link starts, stops, approves or changes anything. Gap: a page the user has allowed to open `trackwall://` links without asking can raise the window and switch views as often as it likes; a jump to a repo also resets the "Repos" view. A link to a session or repo that no longer exists shows the first entry of the list instead, without a message. Registering the scheme on Windows writes the current user's `HKCU\Software\Classes\trackwall`; a moved or deleted portable folder leaves a key that points nowhere until the next start.
- Services run by a build tool outside their process group (ADR-45): Gradle's daemon, Gradle's default, runs `gradle bootRun`'s app as its own child, outside the process group of the mise task `dev`. A stop kills the group, Gradle's daemon notices its client is gone and ends the app, and the service is `stopped` only once its port has closed (§4.3). A daemon that does not notice leaves the app running, and Trackwall does not kill it, because only the port ties it to the service and killing whatever holds a port could hit another program: the service stays `stopping` with the reason, and the user stops it (`gradle --stop`, or `org.gradle.daemon=false` in the repo's `gradle.properties`).
- Merge requests from tasks (ADR-77): the push is one of the daemon's two writes to a server (the other is the local config store's, ADR-86), made with the user's git credentials at a click in the task panel, of one branch by its full name, never forced, so it cannot change another branch or overwrite a commit on the remote. The task's agent shares the repo's git configuration and writes in the worktree, so the push takes nothing from either that could run its code or send the branch elsewhere: no hooks (`core.hooksPath` pointed at an empty directory, since `--no-verify` skips only `pre-push` and `reference-transaction` still runs from a hook path in the worktree), no tags or submodules, `receivepack` pinned, no signed push (`push.gpgSign=false`, so no `gpg.program`), only HTTPS or SSH to a repo host from the settings or GitHub, and every push address the fetch address. The cost is that Git LFS objects are not uploaded and the remote refuses such a push. Gap, as for sync: the connection itself still follows the repo's local `.git/config`, which the agent can change: `core.sshCommand` (a program), `credential.helper` (a program), `http.proxy` and `http.<url>.sslVerify` (the branch and the credentials through a proxy the agent picks). The merge request is created by the user in the repo host's own form, so no MCP tool and no daemon call creates, approves or merges one, and `gh` and `glab` only read; their token stays theirs (ADR-19). The draft in the form's address is the agent's summary, text the user reads and edits before the form opens; the address goes to the system's browser as one argument, without a shell. The merge request and pipeline addresses the repo host returns are opened only as `ExternalLink` (`http` or `https`).
- Local config (ADR-86): the backup reads only the two fixed file names (`LOCAL_CONFIG_FILES`) with `infra/regular-file.ts`: a regular file of at most 256 KB, opened with `O_NOFOLLOW` and `O_NONBLOCK` where the system has them and checked against its `lstat` after opening (on Windows the identity of the opened file), so a link or a FIFO swapped in is refused; the card's read of `mise.local.toml` uses the same function. The store is untrusted like any folder an agent can write: the daemon refuses a store that is a link, whose `.git` is not its own directory, or whose `.git/config` has a key beyond those git writes itself, so no filter, `receivepack`, `sshCommand`, credential helper or push redirection set in the store reaches git; commits are made with plumbing from the content the daemon read, so no filter or attribute applies, and every git command that writes there runs without hooks and signing. The push goes to the remote the user added, only to an HTTPS or SSH address, since hooks on the receiving side are beyond the reach of `core.hooksPath` and a local or shared folder may be one an agent wrote, never forced, with `receivepack` pinned. Restore writes only those names, from the store's last commit, into the repo root, never over a file (`wx`); Restore and Ignore locally are UI actions without an MCP tool, and Ignore never writes through a link. Known gaps: `mise.local.toml` may hold secrets in `[env]`, and the backup puts them into the store's history and onto its remote, so the documentation says to keep secrets out of it or the store's remote private; a file an agent commits into the store reaches a repo only through the user's Restore of a file the repo lacks; an agent that rewrites its `mise.local.toml` in a loop grows the store's history by up to 256 KB per 5 s; the user's global git configuration is trusted, as for every other git call.
- Autostart and "connect an agent" only from the UI; the agent has no tool for changing the daemon configuration.
- Start at sign-in (ADR-65), known gap: when an administrator has set a `Trackwall` value for all users (`HKLM\...\Run`), `auto-launch` reads the entry as on, so the shell's refresh at start also writes the user's own value and Trackwall starts twice at sign-in; the second start reaches the single instance and shows the main window. Turning it off in Settings reports that only an administrator can.
- App updates (ADR-53) install only a package whose minisign signature matches the public key in `tauri.conf.json` and names the version the manifest announces, so neither a changed `latest.json` nor a changed file on GitHub installs anything, and an older signed package cannot pose as a new version; only a newer version than the running one is offered. The private key lives only in the repo secrets (`TAURI_SIGNING_PRIVATE_KEY`, with its password) and with the owner, and only the signing step of the release workflow gets it. `TRACKWALL_UPDATE_ENDPOINT` changes only where the manifest comes from, not the key. The install runs only after the user's click in the window; neither the daemon nor an MCP tool can start it. The Windows install unpacks only entries inside the package's `Trackwall` folder, without `..` and symlinks. Gap: whoever holds the private key can ship code to every installed copy; a leaked key is replaced in a new version, and installed copies trust the old key until a manual install. The check tells GitHub the IP address and that Trackwall runs (no version or identifier goes with it).
- The MCP server (ADR-24) accepts the daemon token, and Trackwall sessions add a session token (ADR-39): the supervising agent is the user's program, and the tools read, store a proposal and a summary, or run and stop mise tasks (ADR-41). The stdio bridge takes the token from `daemon.json`, so it does not reach the Copilot CLI configuration the user keeps; a user process can read this file anyway, as the UI can. A Trackwall session's own files do carry it (ADR-88, ADR-89): the headers of its HTTP hooks in `sessions/<session>/plugin/hooks.json` and of its MCP entry in `sessions/<session>/mcp-config.json` hold the daemon token and the session token, written user-only on macOS and Linux (on Windows the profile's permissions apply), and they stop working when the daemon restarts or the session ends. Whether Copilot CLI writes hook or MCP headers into its logs is not checked. The session's `NO_PROXY` with `127.0.0.1` (ADR-89) only keeps loopback requests away from the proxy, which is where they belong anyway. Only `HTTP(S)_PROXY` with `NO_PROXY` is checked (the contract tests); a proxy Copilot CLI took from another setting would get the request with both tokens, and the session would fail to reach its MCP server. The session environment's `COPILOT_HOOK_ALLOW_LOCALHOST=1` lets any hook configuration Copilot CLI loads (a repo's `.github/hooks`, the user's hooks) POST to loopback, and the agent's own processes inherit it; such a configuration could already run a command hook, and without the daemon token it gets nothing from the daemon. A task proposal becomes a task only after the user clicks in the UI, and only `run_mise_task` starts processes (above). Content from repos in tool results (READMEs, commit subjects, mise task descriptions, task output in `tail`, code lines from `search_code`) is external data for the agent too: a repo can slip the agent instructions this way (prompt injection); Trackwall does not filter it. `search_code` (ADR-40) returns any committed text from every repo in the projects folder, including secrets someone committed (`.env`, keys, passwords in `application.yml`), and to every session with the Trackwall MCP server, not only the one working in that repo; Trackwall does not mask them (the masks from §4.2 cover only values from process environments). The pattern and pathspecs from the agent reach git only as arguments after `-e` and `--`, without a shell; a pathological regular expression keeps git busy for at most 30 s per repo, and the whole search starts no new repo after 60 s, but a running `git grep` is not stopped when the MCP client gives up. `get_repo` asks mise for the task list just like the tab in the UI, so in a trusted configuration mise evaluates `[env]` while doing it (§4.7); it does not read configuration without trust, and trust can be given only in the UI.
- The daemon binary reads neither `.env` nor `bunfig.toml` from the current directory (`--no-compile-autoload-dotenv`, `--no-compile-autoload-bunfig` in `scripts/build.ts`), because the MCP bridge starts in the agent's directory, that is in the user's repo; from source under Bun, `--no-env-file` and `--config` with the daemon package's empty `bunfig.toml` in `mcpStdioCommand` do the same.
- OpenAPI specs from the repo (ADR-87) are untrusted input: the key test before parsing matches blanks within a line only (no backtracking over a file of blank lines), YAML aliases are not expanded, local `$ref`s and schema depth are capped, and at most 200 specs and 2000 operations per spec are read. Known gaps: a spec over the 1 MB file limit is not read and no problem says so, and a Gradle generator whose `inputSpec` or `generatorName` is not a quoted literal (a `val`, a computed path) is not recognised, so a client spec configured that way counts as the repo's own API and its endpoints point callers to the wrong repo.
- If database tools ever appear: a role without superuser and a read-only transaction.
- Vulnerabilities are reported privately as described in `SECURITY.md` (GitHub Security Advisories).
- Only Home is tested under the Tauri window's CSP (ADR-71). The other views' libraries (Streamdown with Mermaid and KaTeX in the README tab, xterm.js in Sessions, the Overview graph) are not; the README chunks carry `Function(...)` calls that are guarded or unreached as far as read, which a refusal would show as a README in plain text (`ReadmeFallback`) rather than a blank window.

## 9. Windows: risks and how we check them early

| Risk | What can go wrong | How we check |
|---|---|---|
| Job Objects with Bun/Node | no native API; needs N-API or `koffi`; a child process already in another job (for example started from the VS Code terminal) cannot be added without `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_BREAKAWAY_OK`; under Node (without FFI) a group is a tree with `taskkill /T`, from which a descendant falls out after its parent dies, and orphans after a hard kill of the daemon remain | settled in stage 1 (ADR-1, ADR-20): the `procs` module with a test that stops the whole group together with grandchildren (`procs.test.ts`); in the compiled binary only by hand before a release (gap in ADR-1) |
| ConPTY + `node-pty` in Bun | a native addon may not work under Bun; `bun build --compile` does not embed `.node` | settled in stage 1 (ADR-1, ADR-26: `Bun.spawn({ terminal })`); in the compiled binary only by hand before a release (gap in ADR-1); plan B: Node 22 + SEA, plan C: PTY on the Rust side in Tauri (`portable-pty`) with a bridge to the daemon |
| Gentle stop on Windows | no SIGTERM; `GenerateConsoleCtrlEvent` needs a shared console; `taskkill` reports success with the process still alive | a stop is always confirmed by observation; for Spring Boot additionally `POST /actuator/shutdown`, if enabled |
| Detecting Copilot CLI state | hooks from `--plugin-dir` do not start on Windows (the `exec` path, AppData), ConPTY loses or reorders OSC 9;4; the ConPTY screen differs from Unix; `Bun.spawn({ terminal })` is described in the Bun documentation as POSIX only, although the stage 1 spike passed on Windows (ADR-1) | stage 2 (ADR-17): `copilot-record.ts` on a real Windows and macOS following the list in the PR description; screen patterns only from recordings, if hooks, OSC and session keys are not enough (the first one, "Operation cancelled by user", comes from recordings on macOS and Windows). Sessions in the daemon (ADR-26): the `agents.test.ts` test with a real PTY and a fake Copilot CLI in the CI matrix, on a real Windows the list of manual tests in the PR description |
| Portable exe | WebView2 may not be installed; SmartScreen blocks an unsigned file; auto-update must replace a running file | stage 1: build with `tauri.conf` → bundle `none` + a WebView2 check at startup; a test on a real Windows; signing with Azure Trusted Signing before publication. The update renames the running exe instead of replacing it (ADR-53); `cargo test` on `windows-latest` renames its own running binary and unpacks a zip made by the system `tar` like the release, the sidecar smoke test renames the running Bun-compiled `trackwall-daemon` and puts it back, and the first real update is checked by hand (docs/release.md) |
| Long paths and file locks | a task's worktree in `<task worktrees folder>/<repo>/<branch>` (branch shortened to 32 characters, ADR-31, ADR-84) lengthens the paths of the repo's files and can exceed 260 characters; `git worktree remove` does not delete files held by a process | `\\?\` and `core.longpaths` (the daemon calls git with `-c core.longpaths=true` on Windows); ending a task only without a running session, that is after a confirmed stop of the group, and the daemon deletes the directory left by an interrupted `worktree remove` itself after confirmation |
| Port → owner | `netstat`/`Get-NetTCPConnection` are slow; a PID without a tree | `GetExtendedTcpTable` through N-API, the tree from `runtime.json` and the Job Object |
| Antivirus / EDR | random slowdowns, the daemon quarantined; every process start outside the excluded folders is scanned, also the ones an agent waits for | no process per session hook (HTTP hooks, ADR-88), a git budget (ADR-79), task worktrees in the projects folder (ADR-84); portable without UPX packing, an early test on a real Windows |

## 10. Tests and CI

- `procs` and `logs`: integration tests that start real child processes (a script that starts a grandchild and ignores a gentle stop), on both systems in the CI matrix (every PR, ADR-22).
- `cards`: extractors, tags and mechanisms tested on in-memory commit snapshots (small `pom.xml`, Gradle scripts, `package.json`, `mise.toml`) without git and network; the module with commit reading and the card store on a real git repo in a temporary directory.
- `agents`: sessions tested with a real PTY, a process group, the hooks POSTed to the daemon's hook route (`test/support/hook-server.ts` for an `Agents` without the API) and an interactive fake Copilot CLI (`test/fixtures/fake-copilot-session.ts`), tasks likewise on real git (a clone from `origin/HEAD`, `tasks.test.ts`), also through the terminal WebSocket (`pty-socket.test.ts`); state detectors tested on `.cast` recordings replayed by `replayCopilotRecording`; recordings of real Copilot CLI sessions go into the repo as fixtures only after anonymisation, so the `.cast` fixture is synthetic (`test/fixtures/copilot/`, marked in the recording title), and the full path (PTY, session plugin, hook processes, recording) is checked by the test with the fake Copilot CLI. What the fake assumes about Copilot CLI is checked by the contract tests (ADR-67, `packages/daemon/contract/`): the daemon starts the real Copilot CLI of the pinned version in BYOK mode against a scripted model, with the hooks, the MCP stdio bridge and the session instructions of `main.ts`, and the tests check the hook events and payload fields, the approval request and the agent dialog the daemon reads from the screen and answers (ADR-66), a `toolPermissions` denial refused without an approval request, the Trackwall MCP tools and the instructions in the model's prompt, and a task's prompt from `--interactive=`, a new session waiting for you from its input box before any hook, and a review typed into a resumed task session before the user's first message (ADR-69). The fake replays the Copilot CLI reference of its system (`test/support/copilot-reference.ts`, `test/fixtures/copilot/reference/<platform>.json`): the hooks with their payloads (the first only with the first prompt, at once with `--interactive=`), the window titles, the input box and the approval dialog's box, approved with a "Yes" option or Enter as in Copilot CLI; the contract tests compare a live session with that reference once all of them are done and print what they saw when it differs (the dialog by what the daemon reads from it, since a long temporary path wraps its lines differently), so the fake cannot drift from the pinned version unnoticed. Every night `nightly.yml` runs the same contract tests on the newest stable Copilot CLI on npm (`TRACKWALL_COPILOT_CLI`) on all three systems, outside `CI ok`; a failure opens an issue for that version, and the next green night closes it, so a new Copilot CLI that breaks the daemon is known before the pinned version is raised.
- UI: logic without React (`packages/ui/src/lib`) in unit tests of `packages/ui/test`; the key paths through the views in E2E tests (ADR-71, `packages/daemon/e2e/`, `pnpm test:e2e`): the daemon of the session tests with the fake Copilot CLI serves the built UI to headless Chromium driven by Playwright's library under `bun test`. Five paths, each through the daemon, the event and terminal WebSockets and a view: a session asking for approval answered from the tray panel (ADR-66), a main session's proposal edited and started in Sessions (ADR-55), an agent task verified (ADR-64) and commented on in Changes, with the review reaching its agent (ADR-62), an agent task on a small screen (1280×650) whose terminal still shows the whole approval dialog under its summary and verdict, answered from the tray panel, and on the same screen Home with the supervisor panel pinned keeping its 3D scene at least 320 pixels wide, and Home in the main window as the Tauri shell shows it, under the window's origin and CSP, drawing the 3D scene and receiving the daemon's events, and, with the scene's chunk failing to load, showing a message in its place while the other views keep working. The tests read what the user sees and check what the agent got through its session's MCP tools; the Tauri shell itself (WebView2, WKWebView, tray, notifications, shortcuts) stays outside them, apart from the window's origin, CSP and `daemon_connection`, which the fourth path imitates.
- `api`/`mcp`: the contract from `packages/protocol`, tests through a real MCP SDK client (`@modelcontextprotocol/client`): through Streamable HTTP in both revisions, through the bridge in the same process and through the bridge command from `/api/daemon/info` with the daemon process under Bun and under Node; the binary smoke test (`scripts/smoke.ts`) does `initialize` through `mcp-stdio`; a session's HTTP MCP entry, also behind a proxy that answers nothing, through the real Copilot CLI in the contract tests (ADR-89).
- Java builds (ADR-72, `packages/daemon/jvm/`, `pnpm test:jvm`): the unit tests of `cards`, `graph`, `services`, `test-results`, `verification` and `logs` work on handwritten build files, reports and log lines; whether real build tools still write what those assume is checked on two Spring Boot projects in `jvm/projects/` (`orders` with Maven, `checkout` with Gradle calling it through a `RestClient`), built by real Maven and Gradle through real mise (the JDK and the build tool from each project's `mise.toml`, installed with `mise install`), through the MCP tools: the cards, `find_endpoint`, `get_test_results` after `run_mise_task`, `start_service` with the dependency from the graph and `call_endpoint`, `verify_task` on a task whose change fails a test (Maven) or the compilation (Gradle), and a task's service that fails to start; `checkout` builds with Gradle's daemon, Gradle's default, so a stop shows the port closed although the app ran outside the group. `jvm.yml` runs them every night from `nightly.yml` on all three systems, outside `CI ok`, with an issue while they fail on `main`, and on a PR that touches the daemon, the protocol or the dependencies.
- CI (ADR-22): every PR and push to `main` goes in parallel through lint, `cargo fmt`, typecheck, tests and the documentation site build (ADR-56) on `ubuntu-latest`, the dependency audit, the `contract` job (the contract tests with the real Copilot CLI on all three systems, ADR-67, from `contract.yml`, which `nightly.yml` also runs every night on the newest Copilot CLI outside `CI ok`, together with the tests with real Java builds, ADR-72), the `e2e` job (the E2E tests of the UI on all three systems, ADR-71), the `test` job (tests) and the `desktop` job (daemon build as a binary and Tauri → clippy → `cargo test` → sidecar smoke test → portable → artifacts) on `windows-latest` and `macos-latest`. The aggregate `CI ok` job is green only when all the others are green; it is meant to be the only required check in the protection of `main`, which the repo owner sets. A vulnerability without a fixed version goes into `auditConfig` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` (`pnpm audit --ignore`) with a justification, so that it does not block every PR. CI also runs on documentation-only changes, because the required check must report on every PR. A new push to a PR branch cancels its previous run, and every `main` commit has its own run that nothing cancels. Manual runs remain for branches without a PR. A release from a tag (`release.yml`): the GitHub Release is created only after both builds pass, described in `docs/release.md`.
- Known differences between the fake Copilot CLI and the real one, which the contract tests found and a PR of their own fixes: with a prompt from `--interactive=` the real Copilot CLI runs that turn to its end, while the fake sends only `sessionStart` and waits for a typed line; every turn of the fake asks for an approval, while the reference also has a turn that ends in text. Not in the reference, so not in the fake: a rejected approval ("No", Esc) and the OSC 9;4 progress bar, which Copilot CLI 1.0.103 does not send with the scripted model (the daemon still reads it, ADR-17, tested on `.cast` fixtures).
- Found by the E2E tests (ADR-71), not by a contract test: the fake draws at a fixed width and does not redraw its screen when the terminal shrinks, as Copilot CLI does, so a dialog drawn wider than the session terminal wraps and the daemon cannot read it. The E2E tests keep the terminal wider than the fake's screen; a fake that redraws on resize would lift that.

## 11. Milestones (mapping to the order of work)

1. **Skeleton**: monorepo, `protocol`, daemon with `/api/health`, UI with one panel, Tauri portable on Win and mac, CI. Spikes: Job Objects, ConPTY/node-pty under Bun. Result: the Bun vs Node decision (ADR-1 confirmed or reversed).
2. **Copilot CLI prototype**: PTY in the daemon, xterm in the UI, state detectors, a test on a real Windows. Result: which state sources work and with what confidence. Done: detectors from hooks, OSC 9;4 and session keys, combining signals, recording and replay (ADR-17). Then PTY in the `procs` module, `/ws/pty/<session>` and xterm in the UI: the "Sessions" view with many sessions at once (ADR-26). Left: results from a real Windows (ADR-17).
3. **Workspace**: `workspace.yaml`, sync, mise generator, `procs` + `logs` in full, the Services and Tasks views, the `ws` CLI. Done: projects folder (ADR-18), mise tasks in the repo details with process groups (ADR-20), clone from a host (ADR-19), sync without mise and extractors (ADR-21), the Batch command with sync, `mise install`, `setup` and `build` (ADR-27), mise task logs live and in JSONL (ADR-28).
4. **Knowledge**: card extractors, graph, MCP server with the tools from §5.3, audit. Done: MCP server with the stdio bridge and the `list_repos`, `get_repo` (ADR-24), `tail` (ADR-28) and `get_errors` (ADR-42) tools, repo cards from build files, repo tags and `get_repo_card` (ADR-25), the connection graph from packages and user entries, the "Overview" view, `who_calls` and `subgraph_for` (ADR-29), HTTP edges from Spring and frontend code and `find_endpoint` (ADR-30), code search `search_code` (ADR-40), sessions and tasks in `list_sessions` and `get_session` (ADR-36).
5. **Completion**: 3D scene, leases, `trace`, secrets in the target version (sops + age).

## 12. Decisions (ADRs in brief)

**ADR-1 Daemon runtime: Bun, keeping compatibility with Node.** Bun gives `bun build --compile` into one binary and built-in SQLite. The code is written so that it also runs under Node 22 (`node:` APIs, no Bun-specific APIs outside the `platform/` layer). If the stage 1 spike shows that ConPTY or Job Objects do not work under Bun, we move to Node 22 + SEA without changes to the modules. Addendum (2026-10-23): compatibility with Node is guarded by a tool, because without one it drifted (a constructor parameter property in `api/guard.ts` blocked the start under Node). The daemon and `protocol` have `erasableSyntaxOnly` and required extensions in imports, Biome rejects Bun APIs outside `platform/`, and the daemon process tests run under Bun and under Node with the flags from the `start:node` script (Node 22.12+ runs the source code without compilation). Result of the stage 1 spikes (Bun 1.4.2, Windows x64, macOS arm64, Linux, also in a binary built with `--compile` moved outside the repo): Job Objects through `bun:ffi` work on Windows, a process group (`setsid` + `kill(-pgid)`) on macOS; PTYs come from `bun-pty` and `Bun.spawn({ terminal })` (ConPTY on Windows), while `node-pty` does not work under Bun and does not load `.node` in `--compile`. ADR-1 is therefore confirmed. The spike code (`spikes/job-objects`, `spikes/conpty`) was removed from the repo and from CI on 2026-10-24, because the answer is here, and their solutions live in the daemon's `platform/` and its tests on Windows and macOS; the code is in git history (last in `9e1c1bd`). Deliberate gap: the spikes also ran the Job Object and the PTY in the compiled binary. The daemon tests do it from source, and the binary smoke test (`scripts/smoke.ts`) checks only the start, `/api/health`, `/api/daemon/info`, MCP and the stop, so a Bun version change that breaks FFI or PTY only in `--compile` is caught only by the manual step before the tag in `docs/release.md`. A smoke test that starts a session with a fake Copilot CLI in the binary will close it. Addendum (2026-10-24, PR #46): Bun 1.4.2 on Windows crashed (segfault) or hung `bun test` about 1–2 s after a test file had run four ConPTY sessions, at a random point that did not depend on how the session was stopped (closing the terminal or killing the leader), on garbage collection or on `--isolate`; macOS and Linux were fine. Bun 1.4.4 passes the same tests, so `mise.toml` and both workflows moved to 1.4.4. What would reverse it: the crash coming back on a later Bun, which would mean running the PTY test files in separate `bun test` processes.

**ADR-2 UI transport: HTTP + WebSocket, not only Tauri IPC.** This way the same UI works in a browser (stage 1) and in Tauri, and the `ws` CLI and tests use the same API. Tauri IPC remains for native matters: tray, autostart, token.

**ADR-3 MCP in the daemon, Streamable HTTP transport, stdio as a bridge.** One process holds the state, so the MCP tools need no separate channel. A stdio bridge for CLIs that do not support HTTP. The first slice (a server from the SDK, stateless, read-only, Copilot CLI through the bridge): ADR-24.

**ADR-4 Persistence: SQLite + JSONL, no external database.** One person, one machine; SQLite is enough, and JSONL is readable by hand and cheap to write.

**ADR-5 Leases instead of a proxy.** One service instance on a port and a takeover by a task solve 90% of cases without a proxy; the `leases` interface leaves room for routing by header. Clarification (2026-10-25): ADR-61 runs a task's service as a second instance on the task port without a lease; a lease remains the way to route callers to it.

**ADR-6 Facts from extractors, an LLM only in the `description` field and in proposals with `origin: 'llm'`.** Repeatable analysis and tests without a model.

**ADR-7 Nothing company-specific in the repo.** All company-specific rules (URL patterns, topic names, a GitLab adapter with the instance address) are workspace configuration, and the extractors take them as parameters.

**ADR-8 Copilot CLI only.** (2026-10-23) One agent adapter. The adapter abstraction stays as a boundary in the code, but is not developed for other CLIs.

**ADR-9 No auto-merge and no planner, permanently.** (2026-10-23) A task ends with a summary and a PR proposal; merging and splitting goals is done by a human. The data model has no place for dependencies between tasks or a queue.

**ADR-10 The secrets repo holds k8s `Secret` manifests.** (2026-10-23) The `origin/<repo>` branch contains YAML files with `kind: Secret`; `secrets.resolve` reads `data` (base64) and `stringData` (plain text) and maps the keys to environment variables 1:1. Key names are compared with `runtime.secretNames` from the repo details, hence `check_secrets`. The target sops + age encryption does not change the format, it only adds a decryption step before parsing.

**ADR-11 A translucent window with a system effect, an opaque background as a fallback.** (2026-10-23) The Tauri window is transparent (`transparent`, theme `Dark`), and the shell in `setup` tries effects in turn through `window-vibrancy`: on Windows Mica (Windows 11), then Acrylic (Windows 10 1809+), on macOS the `UnderWindowBackground` vibrancy. After the page loads, the shell sets the `data-window-effect` attribute on `<html>` with the name of the effect it got and shows the window (it starts with `visible: false`) only after this script has run, so an opaque background does not flash at startup; if the page did not report the end of loading, the window shows after 10 s. Reloading the page (F5) paints it opaque until loading ends, which we leave as a rare case. The attribute removes the page background and makes surfaces translucent; without it (no effect, a browser) the UI stays opaque, and `prefers-reduced-transparency` restores opacity also with a working effect. Tauri's `set_effects` is out, because it takes only the first effect from the list and does not say whether it worked. `macOSPrivateApi` is not needed: since Tauri 2.12.1 (the lower bound in `Cargo.toml`) transparency on macOS works without this flag. What would reverse it: noticeable Acrylic stutter when dragging the window on Windows 10 (then Windows 10 gets an opaque background) or poor readability on light wallpapers (then less surface transparency). Clarification (2026-10-24, ADR-49): the effect and the reveal are kept per window label (`WindowEffects`), so an extra window gets the same effect, applied on the main thread right after it is built, and shows only after its page has set the attribute; a page that loads before the effect is applied shows its window opaque at first, and the effect reaches it as soon as it is applied. Clarification (2026-10-27): on Windows a window that was hidden when `windows::show` shows it (the first reveal, the tray, a second launch) gets a one-pixel resize, held for 100 ms, and back (`repaint.rs`). After the first reveal of the transparent window with Mica, the backdrop looked washed over, as if everything were selected, and the page did not repaint ("Connecting to the daemon…" stayed after the UI had connected) until the user resized or clicked the window; the likely cause is that WebView2 and the compositor keep the frame composed while the window was hidden. A maximized or full-screen window is left alone, since a size change would take it out of that state; one hidden to the tray while maximized is not repainted on show. What would remove the workaround: the frame repaints on show without it on current WebView2 and wry.

**ADR-12 Name: Trackwall.** (2026-10-23) The working name "Pitwall" was taken by existing developer tools (quanticstudios/pitwall, aenzenith/pitwall-vscode), so the project is called Trackwall: packages `@trackwall/*`, binaries `trackwall` and `trackwall-daemon`, data directory `~/.trackwall`, variables `TRACKWALL_*`, app identifier `dev.trackwall.desktop`. The name keeps the motif of the wall by the track and at the time of the decision was free on npm (also as a scope), crates.io and PyPI. The state from version 0.1.0 in `~/.pitwall` is not migrated, because apart from the token and logs there is nothing in it. What would reverse it: a name collision with a tool or a trademark found before publication.

**ADR-13 `workspace.yaml` in the daemon's state directory, until a workspace is chosen.** (2026-10-23) The repo list (since ADR-18: the projects folder) is in `<state directory>/workspace.yaml` (`~/.trackwall/workspace.yaml`). This way the first version needs no workspace selection screen, and the file already has its target format and can be moved. What will reverse it: choosing the workspace directory in the UI (for example a private repo with configuration): then the daemon gets the path to the file, and only a pointer to it stays in the state directory. The manual `repos` list in this file was replaced by ADR-18; the location of the file still applies.

**ADR-14 Repo browsing from the HEAD commit, not from the working directory.** (2026-10-23) The file tree, README and preview read git objects (`ls-tree`, `cat-file`). A path from the UI does not touch the file system, so there is no risk of escaping the repo, symbolic links, files locked by processes or accidentally showing secrets from ignored files (`.env`). The cost is that uncommitted changes are not visible in the browser; the number of changes in the repo status shows them, and eventually a diff (Monaco). What will reverse it: a need to edit files in the UI or to preview the working directory; then a separate route with path validation against the root.

**ADR-15 CI on PRs only on Linux; Windows and macOS on `main`, manually and at release.** (2026-10-23) In a private repo a Windows runner minute counts double against the GitHub Actions limit, and macOS ten times, and the full matrix on every PR and push used up the limit. A system-specific bug in a PR without a manual run comes out on `main` at the latest, and the release (`release.yml`) runs the tests on both systems before publication. What will reverse it: making the repo public (standard runners are then unlimited) or a self-hosted runner: then the Windows + macOS matrix comes back to every PR. Superseded by ADR-22.

**ADR-16 UI look: one dark HUD theme with one accent, tokens only in `index.css`.** (2026-10-23) The UI has one theme, dark, in the spirit of a HUD: a black background (the same as `ACRYLIC_TINT`, so ADR-11 works without changes in the shell), cool glass surfaces with thin lines and a grid under the content, cyan as the only accent for what is active, and green, amber and red only for state (`success`, `warning`, `destructive`). All tokens are in `packages/ui/src/index.css`: colours in `:root`, in `@theme` the typefaces, the `text-2xs` size, the `tracking-hud` letter spacing, the dimensions `--spacing-activity-bar` (`w-activity-bar`) and `--spacing-sidebar`, the `shadow-glow-*` glows, and the utilities `surface`, `hud-label` (section and field labels, mono in capitals; not for sentences), `hud-grid` and `hud-active-edge`. Components use only tokens, and the test `packages/ui/test/design-tokens.test.ts` rejects raw colours and arbitrary appearance values in `.tsx`. Other spacing is the Tailwind scale. Shared building blocks: `Surface` (a surface with a header and a footnote), `StatusLabel` (a state dot with text; the tone from `repoStatusTone`), `Loading` and the shadcn/ui components in `components/ui`. Fonts are system fonts (Segoe UI Variable and Cascadia Mono on Windows, SF on macOS), because the app works offline and we do not want to add font files. The direction comes from the inspiration for the graph and the 3D scene (stage 5), so that the rest of the UI matches them; the main view with the scene or the graph is to have the layout "the scene at full width, details of the selected node in a panel on the right". What would reverse it: poor readability on a real Windows with Mica/Acrylic or a need for a light theme; then a second colour set under a class and a switch.

Clarification (2026-10-23): the app's views are switched by a view bar at the left edge of the window (`ActivityBar`, like the activity bar in VS Code) instead of tabs in the header, because a vertical bar fits more views (sync, graph, Copilot CLI sessions) without squeezing the header and leaves the whole window height to the content. Work views are at the top of the bar, Daemon and Settings at the bottom edge; each has an icon, a tooltip with the name and shortcut, and a Ctrl (⌘ on macOS) + key shortcut (digits by physical key, also from the numeric keypad, comma by character, because in AZERTY and Dvorak it sits elsewhere, and its key may hold a letter with its own shortcut; in Cyrillic layouts the comma needs Shift, so there only the icon opens Settings): Repos 1, Daemon 9, Settings comma, further work views the next digits. A view appears on the bar only together with working content, with no empty placeholders. Tabs (`TabBar`) remain only inside a view, for example in the Repos view. Clarification (2026-10-24, ADR-48): the view shortcuts listen in the capture phase on `window` and stop the event, because xterm.js turned Ctrl + digit into a control character for the session and stopped it, so with the focus in a session terminal the views did not switch. Clarification (2026-10-24, ADR-49): Ctrl+Shift + digit (⌥⌘ + digit on macOS) opens the view in a new window, and Ctrl+Shift+N (⌘⇧N) opens the current view; Settings has no such shortcut, because Shift and Option change the comma on most layouts.

**ADR-17 Copilot CLI state from the session plugin's hooks, OSC 9;4 as a fallback, no screen parser at the start.** (2026-10-23) According to its documentation Copilot CLI has hooks for the end of a turn (`agentStop`), notifications (`notification`, including approval requests), errors and the end of a session, and a plugin given through `--plugin-dir` loads them for one session only, without changes in `~/.copilot` and without files in the repo. Checked on Copilot CLI 1.0.103 (Linux): `copilot plugin list` shows such a plugin, and its `sessionEnd` hook with `exec` + `args` fired already on the folder trust screen, before login; the other events could not be triggered without a logged-in account. A hook is a program with `exec` + `args`, without a shell, so the same definition works on Windows and macOS. Copilot CLI sends the OSC 9;4 progress bar by default, so it gives `Working` also when the hooks do not work (only with `WT_SESSION`, clarification of 2026-10-24 below). We put off the screen parser (VT in the daemon), because it needs dependencies and patterns that cannot be checked without recordings. The considered `copilot --acp` mode (Agent Client Protocol: JSON-RPC on stdio with exact state and approval requests) is out for now, because it replaces the Copilot CLI TUI with our own conversation view in the UI; it comes back if hooks turn out unreliable or the UI gets its own session view anyway. Other sources rejected: `copilot -p --output-format json` gives JSONL events, but only without the TUI and with `--allow-all-tools`, so it has no `Approval` state and no conversation with a human; the `~/.copilot/session-state/<session>/` directory and the logs in `~/.copilot/logs` have an undocumented, internal format that may change with every version. What would reverse it: hooks from `--plugin-dir` not working on Windows (then a plugin in the worktree's `.github/hooks` excluded through `.git/info/exclude`), hooks working only partly, for example without `notification` (after the first hook OSC can only end `Working`, so then a per-event combining rule or screen patterns), or `notification` kinds that do not tell approval from a question (then screen patterns for `Approval`). The PTY in the prototype comes from `Bun.spawn({ terminal })` (ADR-1); if it did not work in ConPTY, `bun-pty` from the spike comes back (code in git history, ADR-1). Result from macOS (2026-10-24, Copilot CLI 1.0.103 with a logged-in account, `copilot-record.ts` with `-p`): hooks from `--plugin-dir` all arrive (`sessionStart`, `userPromptSubmitted`, `postToolUse`, `agentStop`, `sessionEnd` with `reason: complete`), and an approval request is a `notification` with the field `notification_type: permission_prompt`, the title "Permission needed" and the path or command in the text, so `Approval` comes from the kind, not from the text. Copilot CLI asks only about what is not on its list of safe actions (for example writing outside the session directory); it runs `ls` and `date` without asking. Clarification (2026-10-24): sessions with the TUI in the "Sessions" view on macOS gave the same `notification_type: permission_prompt` for an approval request to write and delete a file, so `Approval` from this kind has `high` confidence (UI: "asking for approval"); `elicitation_dialog` stays `medium`, because no recording has shown it yet. Left: Windows, `elicitation_dialog` and the notification kind for a ready plan. Clarification (2026-10-24, five sessions with the TUI on macOS, `copilot-record.ts` in a 120×30 PTY): after an answer to an approval request Copilot CLI sends no hook. After approval the first one is `postToolUse` after the command ends (many seconds for tests), after "No, and tell Copilot…" with a message, the first one is the next tool's `postToolUse`, and after Esc, which interrupts the whole turn ("Operation cancelled by user"), nothing until `sessionEnd`; so the state hung in `Approval` and the session was "asking for approval" while working or after the interruption. Hence the key detector (§4.4): Enter in `Approval` or `Plan` → `Working`, Esc and Ctrl+C → `Input`. Copilot CLI sends OSC 9;4 only with `WT_SESSION` (without it, also with `TERM=xterm-256color` and `COLORTERM=truecolor` as in the daemon, not once): state 3 from the start of the turn, then every 5 s, also throughout an approval request, and state 0 about 20 ms before `agentStop`; so the OSC fallback works only in Windows Terminal and does not tell `Approval` from `Working`. Setting `WT_SESSION` in the session environment to get the bar outside Windows Terminal too is put off: Copilot CLI could change other behaviour under Windows Terminal as well. The window title (OSC 0) does not change on an approval request, a refusal or the end of a turn. Further sessions (2026-10-24): Ctrl+C works like Esc, with one press, during an approval request and while working, without a hook and without asking for a second Ctrl+C; after the interruption the screen shows "Operation aborted by user" at the tool and "Operation cancelled by user" at the end, both as continuous text, so the second one is a screen pattern (§4.4). The same refusal on Windows (the test thread) gave "Operation cancelled by user" without any hook, and the state held `Approval` for over a minute. Sessions with the fix (keys and replay) gave the same changes live and from the recording. Still left: Windows with the fix, `elicitation_dialog`, the notification kind for a ready plan and choosing an option with a digit (whether Copilot CLI accepts it). Clarification (2026-10-24, Windows after PR #38): in the "Allow path access" window the digit of the "No" option works at once, without Enter, and ends the turn with the message "Operation cancelled by user" without a hook, so a digit 1–9 in `Approval` or `Plan` is an answer (`choice`), like Enter. Clarification (2026-10-25, ADR-66, Copilot CLI 1.0.103 with a stand-in model): `elicitation_dialog` comes with the agent's `ask_user` form, so it is `high`; in an approval request the digits of options 1 and 2 select them at once, the digit of "No, and tell Copilot…" only moves the selection there. Clarification (2026-10-25, contract test with the real Copilot CLI 1.0.103 in the TUI, ADR-67): `sessionStart` does not come when the session starts but together with the first prompt, right before `userPromptSubmitted` (the earlier recordings used `-p` or `-i`, where the prompt is there from the start). Until the user's first prompt a new session therefore has no state ("state unknown" in the UI) and does not count as being in its input box (ADR-62). The fake Copilot CLI of the tests sends `sessionStart` at once and is wrong about it; it is corrected in its own PR together with the daemon's handling of a new session (§10). Done in ADR-69. The read-only `list_repos` of the Trackwall server ran without an approval request, while `touch` in the repo asked. Since ADR-88 the hooks are HTTP hooks that POST to the daemon; the events, the record and its fields stay as they are.

**ADR-18 The repo list is the git repos in the projects folder, not a manual list.** (2026-10-23) `workspace.yaml` points to one folder (`projectsDir`), and the repo list is its direct subdirectories with `.git`, read on every request. That is how the user keeps repos anyway (one folder with clones), so the configuration is one path instead of a list, and a repo cloned from a terminal or deleted from disk does not drift from Trackwall. The same model will handle adding repos by choosing from the list available on GitHub or GitLab: the clone goes into the folder and is on the list at once, without a separate write (done: ADR-19). Adding by path by hand and removing from the list disappear; the repo identifier now depends on the directory name, so renaming the directory changes the identifier, and a new directory with a colliding name that sorts earlier can take over an existing repo's identifier (today nothing stores it; the `tasks` table from §6 will have to store the path too; cards are keyed by the commit hash, so they are not affected, ADR-25). The scan goes one level only, because nested repos (submodules, examples in a repo) would give false entries, and scanning the whole tree is expensive with `node_modules`. What would reverse it: a need for repos outside the folder (then an extra `extraRepos` list next to `projectsDir`), a folder layout with groups as in GitLab (`<group>/<repo>`, then the scan depth in the configuration) or several folders at once (`projectsDir` as a list).

**ADR-19 Repos into the projects folder from a GitLab and GitHub search through `glab` and `gh`.** (2026-10-23) A repo is added by searching for it on a host from `workspace.yaml` (`repoHosts`) and clicking "Clone"; the clone goes into the projects folder, so the repo list picks it up by itself (ADR-18). The daemon queries the host and clones through `glab` (GitLab) and `gh` (GitHub) logged in on the system, not through its own HTTP client: login (also SSO and company instances), the token in the system keychain, the proxy and the clone protocol choice (SSH or HTTPS) are already set in these programs, and Trackwall does not touch the token, so it does not have to protect or mask it. The cost is two programs to install and log in to, and a dependency on their output, limited to `api` (JSON from the REST API, stable) and `repo clone`. GitLab is primary: a company instance has thousands of projects, so GitLab does the search (one page of results), while for GitHub, where the user's repo list is small and has no search, the daemon filters itself. A clone runs in the background, and its state lives only in daemon memory, because after it ends the directory in the folder is the truth. What would reverse it: no `glab` on the user's machine without a way to install it (then an HTTP client in `vcs` with the token as a secret, §4 `secrets`), a need to clone from another host (Bitbucket, Gitea: another program or an HTTP client) or GitLab groups as subdirectories of the folder (then together with the scan depth change from ADR-18).

**ADR-20 Process group: a Job Object under Bun on Windows, a POSIX session on macOS, no Effect and no native addon.** (2026-10-23) The `procs` module runs every mise task as a group: on Windows a Job Object with `KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` through `bun:ffi` (the stage 1 spike, ADR-1), on macOS and Linux `setsid` and signals to `-pgid`; the `exited` state and the success of a stop only after the system confirms the group is empty. The system code sits in `platform/` behind the `GroupPlatform` interface, so the module does not depend on Bun. Under Node on Windows there is no FFI: a group is a tree with `taskkill /T /F`, without orphan cleanup; Node is the compatibility mode (ADR-1), and the release is a Bun binary. A gentle stop on Windows is closing stdin, because Ctrl+C needs a shared console. Orphans on macOS are cleaned up by the next daemon start according to `runtime.json` with the leader's start time from `ps`. Effect (cancellation, resources, time limits as a library) considered and put off: the stop sequence is a few steps with time limits that read plainly, and Effect would change the style of the whole daemon, so it is a separate decision. The `ProcessGroups` interface (promises) can be wrapped later without changes in `platform/`. What would reverse it: a leader escaping the job before assignment (then a start suspended by `CreateProcessW` with `CREATE_SUSPENDED` through FFI), servers on Windows that need Ctrl+C for a clean shutdown (then a separate console and `GenerateConsoleCtrlEvent`), or a release under Node (then a Job Object through a native addon).

**ADR-21 Sync is a fetch and a fast-forward of the default branch, nothing more; the remote address as a repo identifier.** (2026-10-23) "Sync all" fetches the changes of every repo from the projects folder and moves the local default branch to the remote one only when it is a clean fast-forward and does not touch uncommitted changes; every other case leaves the repo untouched with a reason. The user usually works on feature branches, so a default branch that is not checked out moves without touching the working directory (`git fetch . <remote>:<local>`, which itself refuses for a branch in use, also during a rebase and bisect), and a checked-out one through `merge --ff-only`; merging, rebasing and `git pull` with the user's configuration (`pull.rebase`) are out, because nobody resolves a conflict in the background. The default branch comes from `refs/remotes/<remote>/HEAD`, because the name (`main`, `master`, `develop`) differs between repos. Considered multi-repo tools (`meta`, `mu-repo`, `gita`, `myrepos`) are out: they need their own file with the repo list, whose source is already the projects folder (ADR-18), and run arbitrary commands through a shell, while the daemon builds commands itself (§1.2). Fetch gets an environment without password and host key prompts, because the daemon has no terminal; an ssh program set by the user takes precedence over batch mode. Sync runs in the background, 4 repos at once (a company GitLab and a VPN can limit concurrent SSH connections), and the state lives only in memory, like clones (ADR-19). The address of the main remote (`host/path`, without the protocol and user) goes to every repo on the list as an identifier independent of the directory name (ADR-18), to which cards, the graph and MCP will attach; it is computed on every request, because nothing is stored yet. What would reverse it: a need to update feature branches too (then a fast-forward of every branch to its tracking branch, with the same rules), repos with several remotes without `origin` at the user (then a remote choice in `workspace.yaml`) or a list too slow with hundreds of repos on Windows (then the address stored in the `repos` table and refreshed on sync).

**ADR-22 Full CI on every PR: Windows and macOS in parallel with Linux, one required check `CI ok`.** (2026-10-23) The repo is public, so standard GitHub Actions runners have no minute limit, and the reversal condition of ADR-15 has been met. Every PR runs tests and the build on `windows-latest` and `macos-latest`, without a manual `workflow_dispatch`; it is on Windows that most bugs Linux does not see come out. Tests and the build are separate jobs without waiting for Linux, so a run takes as long as the longest job (the build on Windows), not the sum. The protection of `main` (a repo setting, done by a human) requires one check `CI ok`, which collects the results of the other jobs: a change of the matrix or a job name needs no change in the repo settings, and a skipped or cancelled job gives a red result (GitHub counts a skipped required check as passed). For the same reason CI has no `paths-ignore`: a documentation-only PR must also get a verdict, and a test checks `docs/release-notes`. Every `main` commit has its own concurrency group, because a shared group cancels a waiting run when the next one comes, and then nobody knows which commit broke `main`. The dependency audit is part of `CI ok`: a new high-severity vulnerability blocks every PR until someone bumps the dependency or, when there is no fixed version, writes it with a justification into `auditConfig` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`; we prefer that to a quiet release with a vulnerability. What would reverse it: going back to a private repo, the audit blocking PRs more often than it catches a vulnerability introduced by a PR (then the audit outside `CI ok` and separately before a release), or a macOS runner queue so long that waiting for it slows PRs more than a bug found only on `main` costs.

**ADR-23 The "Node" icon from one SVG; the tray on macOS as a template, outside macOS the colour app icon.** (2026-10-23) The app icon is a cyan ring (Trackwall) connected to four nodes (repos and agents), one of which glows as active, on a dark tile in the UI theme (ADR-16). Chosen from five proposals, because it shows the connection graph with Trackwall at its centre plainly, and the silhouette stays readable at 16 px. The sources are `apps/desktop/assets/icon.svg` and `tray-icon.svg`, and `apps/desktop/scripts/icons.ts` generates from them the files from `bundle.icon`, the UI favicon and the tray template; the generated files are in the repo, so that the build does not depend on SVG rendering. The tile in the SVG has the margin of the macOS icon grid, and `.ico`, `32x32.png` and the favicon are made from a crop without this margin, because Windows icons fill almost the whole field. The tray on macOS gets a monochrome template (`icon_as_template`), which the system recolours for the light and dark menu bar; on Windows and Linux the colour app icon stays, because the notification area can be light or dark, and a black or white mark would disappear on one of them. What would reverse it: an unreadable colour icon in the Windows notification area (then separate tray marks for the light and dark theme, switched by the system theme) or task state shown on the tray icon (then icon variants with a state marker from the same SVG).

**ADR-24 MCP for the supervising agent: a server from the SDK in the daemon, stateless, read-only, Copilot CLI through a stdio bridge.** (2026-10-23) The daemon exposes `/mcp` through `createMcpHandler` from `@modelcontextprotocol/server` 2.x (dependencies: only `zod` and `@modelcontextprotocol/core`), with the `list_repos` and `get_repo` tools mapped to the facade. The SDK instead of our own JSON-RPC, because the protocol changes every few months (the 2026-07-28 revision added the `server/discover` probe, headers with content fields and a new envelope), and the SDK supports both it and clients with `initialize`; version 1.x (`@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`) was out, because it pulls Express, Hono and an OAuth client into the daemon binary, and the `@modelcontextprotocol/node` adapter for the same reason (Hono), so `IncomingMessage` is turned into a `Request` in a dozen or so lines in `endpoint.ts`. The server is stateless, because the tools only read the facade; sessions and notifications will come when there is something to announce (for example `graph.updated`). Copilot CLI connects through the stdio bridge (`copilot mcp add trackwall -- <trackwall-daemon> mcp-stdio`), not through an HTTP entry with a header, because the daemon's token and port change on every start, and the bridge reads them from `daemon.json` for every message and leaves no token in `~/.copilot/mcp-config.json`. The bridge speaks only the protocol with `initialize`; it rejects the 2026-07-28 probe, because over stdio there are no headers that this revision requires in HTTP. The Copilot CLI configuration is written by its own `copilot mcp add` command run by the user, and the UI only shows it: the daemon does not touch files in `~/.copilot`, and the format stays Copilot CLI's business. The daemon token is enough as long as the tools only read (§8). Checked with Copilot CLI 1.0.103 on Linux without login: Copilot starts the bridge at startup, the `server/discover` probe gets `-32601`, `initialize` (revision 2025-11-25) and `tools/list` pass; a tool call cannot be triggered without a logged-in account. What would reverse it: the first tool that changes state or starts processes (then a task token and an audit in SQLite before it), a need for server notifications (then Streamable HTTP sessions and a GET stream, and in the bridge a channel from the daemon), Copilot CLI requiring the 2026-07-28 revision without falling back to `initialize` (then the bridge rewrites the content fields into HTTP headers) or SDK 2.x changing its API every release (then our own JSON-RPC server for the revision with `initialize`). Changed by ADR-39 (2026-10-24): the server is no longer read-only, because `propose_task` stores a task proposal and `report_result` a task summary, although no tool starts anything still; Trackwall sessions add a session token to the daemon token, and the call goes to the log with the calling session. The `audit` table in SQLite still does not exist: ADR-39 explains why it is not a precondition for these two tools. Changed by ADR-41 (2026-10-24): the `run_mise_task` and `stop_mise_task` tools run and stop mise tasks. Changed by ADR-89 (2026-10-26): a Trackwall session reaches `/mcp` over HTTP with the tokens in its own `mcp-config.json`, which the daemon writes per session and which stops working with the session; the bridge stays for agents outside Trackwall, whose configuration the user keeps.

**ADR-25 The repo card deterministically from the files of the HEAD commit, cached by commit hash; repo tags from the card and from workspace rules.** (2026-10-23) The `cards` module reads build files and tool configuration from the git objects of the HEAD commit (`ls-tree` and `cat-file --batch`, without a checkout and without filters), extracts from them the provided and used packages (Maven, Gradle, npm), the required tools and the mechanism inventory, every fact with a file and line, and caches the card by commit hash in memory and in `cards/<commit>.json`. From the card, the identifier and the remote address it computes repo tags with reasons, and the `repoTags` rules in `workspace.yaml` add tags from names (for example a team's) that are not visible in the files. Reasoning: the commit instead of the working directory, because the card is to describe what is in the repo, not unsaved changes on a feature branch, and because only then is the commit hash the cache key: hundreds of repos are computed once, and a daemon restart does not read them again. Without running the build (Maven, Gradle, `mise ls --json`): that would run code from the repo (Gradle scripts, plugins, mise configuration requiring trust, ADR-20), need installed tools and network, take seconds per repo and read the working directory and global configuration instead of the commit. The price is that Gradle is read from typical line forms, and a dependency assembled in code does not reach the card; the reliable graph edges (shared packages) use typical forms anyway. Our own XML parser (about 140 lines, with line numbers, without DTDs and external entities, so without XXE) instead of a dependency, because evidence needs lines, and a pom needs no namespaces or attributes; TOML through `smol-toml` (no dependencies, pure JS, works in `bun build --compile`), because the Gradle version catalog and mise are full TOML, and YAML through the existing `yaml` dependency. Tags are orthogonal and short (`java`, `bff`, `frontend`, `monorepo`…), instead of compound ones like `#java-bff`, because the filter combines them itself (selected tags are AND), and one tag per combination would multiply rules. Tags without an LLM and without state: computed on every request from the cached card, so a rule change works at once. The card gives provided and used packages in one namespace, but joining them into edges (who uses what) is a separate step: the `graph` module and MCP tools over it, the next task. What would reverse it: a need for facts that cannot be read from files without running the build in many of the user's repos (then an optional step with a build in a worktree, marked with a different source), or a card computed so slowly that it must be kept in SQLite together with the graph (§6).

**ADR-26 Copilot CLI sessions in a daemon PTY, a terminal in the UI through a WebSocket with a screen image, hooks through a session file.** (Partly superseded by ADR-95: the sessions that ran when the daemon stopped are resumed by the next one.) (2026-10-23) The "Sessions" view runs Copilot CLI in a repo from the projects folder in a PTY owned by the daemon (`Bun.spawn({ terminal })`, ConPTY on Windows), as a process group of the `procs` module, several sessions at once; a session runs with the window closed and ends together with the daemon. The terminal window is xterm.js connected through the `/ws/pty/<session>` WebSocket. The daemon keeps every session's screen in a windowless emulator (`@xterm/headless`) and on connection sends its image (`@xterm/addon-serialize`), because the Copilot CLI TUI works on the alternate screen, and replaying the whole output from the start grows with the session's time; both packages are pure JavaScript that works in `bun build --compile` and under Node. The token goes in the WebSocket subprotocol (a browser will not set a header), not in the address. The session plugin's hook (ADR-17) is the daemon itself with the `copilot-hook` subcommand, which appends a record to the session's `hooks.jsonl`, and the daemon reads the file every 200 ms: an HTTP request to the daemon would require giving the hook the token in `hooks.json`, while a file in the state directory requires nothing. "Waiting for you" is `Input`, `Approval` and `Plan` of a running session, computed in the daemon (`needsAttention`); the UI shows the number of such sessions on the view bar and switches to the next one, and the Tauri shell polls the daemon itself (the WebView of a hidden window may have its timers suspended) and, when a session goes from working to waiting, flashes the window on the taskbar (a bounce in the Dock) and adds the session to the tray menu. (Note 2026-10-24: `Input` waits only until the user has seen it; superseded in this part by ADR-47.) System notifications (toasts) are out for the start: on Windows they need a registered AppUserModelID, which a portable app does not have. Considered and rejected: `node-pty` (does not work under Bun, ADR-1), PTY on the Rust side (`portable-pty`, plan C from §9; it adds a bridge between the shell and the daemon, and sessions are to run without a window) and the `--acp` mode (ADR-17). Every session gets the Trackwall MCP server (ADR-24) through `--additional-mcp-config` with a file in the session directory, on the same stdio bridge as the supervising agent. Under Node there are no sessions (`agent_unavailable`), because Node has no PTY without a native addon, and Node is the compatibility mode (ADR-1). The daemon does not record sessions (`copilot-record.ts` does that), and sessions do not survive a daemon restart. What would reverse it: `Bun.spawn({ terminal })` not working in ConPTY on a real Windows (then `bun-pty` from the spike behind the same `TerminalPlatform`; code in git history, ADR-1), the screen image from `@xterm/headless` drifting from the TUI screen (then replaying the output since the last screen switch), a need to resume sessions after a daemon restart (then `copilot --resume` with the session identifier from the `sessionStart` hook) or a release under Node (then a PTY from a native addon). Superseded in its hook part by ADR-88: the hook is a POST to the daemon with the token in `hooks.json` instead of a process of the daemon's `copilot-hook` subcommand, which is gone, because the process start cost more than the token in the file; the session file `hooks.jsonl` stays.

Clarification (2026-10-24): toasts came back with ADR-50, under an AppUserModelID that the shell registers itself.

**ADR-27 The Batch command as a daemon module over `workspace` and `mise`, one `mise run` per repo, without the mise monorepo mode.** (2026-10-23) The Batch command (§4.8) is a fixed list of steps (sync, `mise install`, the `setup` task, the `build` task) on the selected repos, at most 3 repos at once, in each repo in order, with the result of every step in every repo and cancellation. Every mise step is a separate `mise run <task>` or `mise install` in the repo root, in a `procs` process group under the same owner as from the repo details, so the state, stop and cleanup after a crash are the same as for a task run by hand, and caching and dependencies between tasks stay in mise (`sources`, `outputs`, `depends`). An engine checked and rejected: the mise monorepo mode (mise 2026.10.18, `monorepo_root = true` in the `mise.toml` of the projects folder, `mise run '//...:build'`). The tasks of all repos then run in one process with output prefixed by `[//repo:task]`, so the per-repo result would have to be read out of text, and only the whole can be stopped; the first error interrupts the other repos (without `-c`); the projects folder needs a configuration file with `monorepo_root` and an explicit `[monorepo].config_roots` list (automatic discovery is deprecated), which Trackwall would have to write and maintain, and mise to trust; repos without `mise.toml` and git sync are outside it, and the mode is experimental. Checked on a folder with three repos: `mise run '//...:build'` with one repo's build ending with code 3 interrupted the running build of another, and mise warns that `experimental_monorepo_root` and automatic discovery are deprecated.
