Separate `setup` and `build` steps instead of `build` alone with `depends` are deliberate: the dependency result is visible separately, at the cost of a second `setup` run without `sources` and `outputs` when `build` depends on it. What would reverse it: a monorepo mode with a stable, machine-readable per-task result (for example JSON) and without requiring a file in the projects folder, or a need for a dependency graph between repos (building a library before its consumers), which does not exist today: then the order comes from the graph of cards, and mise can stay the engine.

**ADR-28 mise task logs: an in-memory buffer and JSONL in the `logs` module, a light list in the UI instead of xterm.js, polling from a cursor until the WebSocket.** (2026-10-23) The output of every process group with output through pipes goes from `procs` to the `logs` module, which turns it into records with a global `seq`, masks secrets and strips ANSI before writing to the buffer (2000 records per source) and to a JSONL file rotated at 10 MB (§4.2). The last lines of the `exited` state are also made in one place, so they are masked too; that is why `procs` depends on `logs` (table in §4). The UI shows the log as a list of lines coloured by the record's level, not as an xterm.js terminal: a record is already text without ANSI (the same for the UI, the file and the MCP tool `tail`, so it can be masked, filtered and searched), the list has ordinary selection and wrapping, and xterm.js would add a few hundred KB to the UI and its own screen buffer with no benefit for output without interaction. ANSI colours the tools print themselves are lost; the level from the line content (`ERROR`, `WARN`) highlights what matters. Live means polling `logs/tail` from the cursor every second while the log is open: reading from memory is cheap, and the `/ws` WebSocket does not exist yet. The `seq` number starts from the moment the daemon starts (ms × 1000), so that a cursor from a previous daemon lifetime can be recognised without a separate lifetime identifier in every cursor. The MCP tool `tail` is a thin mapping to `logs.tail` with `repo` and `miseTask` instead of `source`, so that the agent gives what it knows from `get_repo`. Groups with a leader in a PTY (task sessions, ADR-26) have no log, because a TUI screen is not lines; their last state lines go through the same masking (`logs.lastLinesOnly`). What would reverse it: a need for interactive output (that is the PTY, ADR-17) or ANSI colours in the log (then `raw` with the sequences next to `msg`), and a `log.append` WebSocket will replace polling without changing the record.

Addendum (2026-10-24, ADR-36): the `/ws` WebSocket exists now, but logs are not one of its topics: their snapshot is the whole source buffer, so they will come as `log.append` with records from a cursor, and until then the UI polls `logs/tail` as above.

**ADR-29 The connection graph between repos computed on every request from cards and the `graph` entries in `workspace.yaml`, drawn in the UI without a graph library.** (2026-10-23) The `graph` module joins the used and built packages from the cards of all repos (ADR-25) into edges from the repo that uses to the repo that is used, with evidence from both sides, and adds package aliases and manual edges from the `graph` key in `workspace.yaml`; the MCP tools `who_calls` and `subgraph_for` and the "Overview" view read the same graph. Reasoning: a shared package is the most reliable connection that can be read from files without knowing the company's infrastructure (discovery, Config Server, Feign or WebClient), so the graph starts from it, and connections the files do not show are added by the user in a file they keep locally anyway (ADR-7) and that the agent can read but not change (§8). A node is a repo, because services (several per repo) do not exist yet. Without state and without SQLite: cards are in memory by commit, and joining a few dozen cards takes milliseconds, so a graph computed on every request does not drift from the cards, a new repo or a manual change of `workspace.yaml` and needs no `graph.updated` event. An alias works only for a package no repo on the list builds, because a fact from build files takes precedence over an entry that may have gone stale; a repo in the entries is given by identifier or remote address, because the identifier changes with the directory name (ADR-18). A bad `graph` or `repoTags` key does not take away the edges from cards, it only goes into `configProblems`, just as a bad `repoTags` does not take away the repo list in `list_repos`. The layout in the UI is columns from the longest path (the same layers as in the 3D scene, §5.5) and a barycentre in a few dozen lines (`lib/graph-layout.ts`), instead of a library (React Flow, Cytoscape, ELK), because for a few dozen repos it gives a readable drawing from frontends to libraries, without a dependency in the WebView bundle and with the look from the HUD tokens (ADR-16); the three-dimensional scene is a separate package (Pawn). What would reverse it: a graph with hundreds of repos and thousands of edges (then zoom, a filter by tags and maybe a library with a layered layout), edges whose computation stops being cheap (HTTP from `application*.yml` and code, then caching the graph by the set of commits and a `graph.updated` event), or services as nodes (then a `ServiceId` node and the repo as a group). Clarification (ADR-30): HTTP edges from code stopped being cheap to compute on every request, so the module keeps the last graph with its input key (the set of commits and the configuration); a `graph.updated` event is still not needed, because the key changes together with the commits.

**ADR-30 HTTP edges from Spring code and configuration with profiles, read deterministically from the commit, with a confidence that depends on what links the client to its target.** (2026-10-23) The repo card gets apps (`spring.application.name`, port), endpoints from controllers (`@RestController`, `@*Mapping`, also from API-first interfaces) and HTTP clients (Feign, `@HttpExchange`, `WebClient`, `RestClient`, `RestTemplate`, addresses from `application*.yml` and `.properties` in every profile with `${…}` expanded, addresses from `.env` and the frontend proxy), every fact with a file and line; the graph links a client to a repo by app name, an alias from `workspace.yaml`, host, port on `localhost` or by call paths that match endpoints, and the MCP tool `find_endpoint` gives an endpoint with the repos that call it. Reasoning: the user does not know what their company uses (discovery, Kubernetes, Feign or WebClient), so the extractor recognises all the typical forms and each gives a fact with evidence, and whether a connection is certain is said by the edge's `confidence`, not by guessing: an app name under which every call has an endpoint is `exact`, a bare path matching several repos is `low`. Profiles are separate clients, because a local address (`localhost:8081`) and a production one (`orders-api.svc`) point to the same repo in different ways, and an agent asking "who calls" needs both. Without compilation and without a Java parser from a dependency: a tokenizer and a declaration parser (about 470 lines) are enough for annotations, constants and call chains, work the same on Java and Kotlin, execute no code from the repo (ADR-25) and have a linear cost on a malicious file; the price is that a path assembled in code (`RouterFunction`, an address from a method) does not reach the card or stays as `<Class.NAME>`. Source files are chosen by `git grep` in the commit, because reading the whole code of hundreds of repos would cost gigabytes, and the facts are only in files with annotations and clients. Without an LLM: the LLM step (§4.6) can later describe what the rules do not see, with a different `origin`. The graph keeps the last result with its input key, because matching calls to the endpoints of all repos stopped being free when polled every 30 s (the condition from ADR-29). The word "app" instead of "service", because a service is a process started by Trackwall (§4.3). What would reverse it: a company with another HTTP framework (Micronaut, Quarkus, JAX-RS; then another extractor next to `spring.ts`), many false edges from paths (then path matching only on demand or a threshold), addresses in Config Server or in Kubernetes manifests instead of in the app repo (then an extractor for the configuration repo and an alias from a `ConfigMap`), or a need for paths computed in code (then the LLM step or analysis with a compiler in a worktree).

**ADR-31 An agent task in its own worktree in the state directory, the prompt through `copilot --interactive`, changes by polling git.** (2026-10-23) The "Sessions" view creates a task: a new branch `trackwall/<prompt slug>-<6 characters of a UUID>` from the repo's default branch, its worktree in `worktrees/<repo>/` of the state directory and a Copilot CLI session (ADR-26) in that worktree with the prompt as the first message (`--interactive=<prompt>`) and the task's UUID as the conversation identifier (`--session-id`), so resuming is `--resume=<UUID>`. Tasks are in `tasks.json` and survive a daemon restart; the worktree and the branch disappear only at the user's request, and changes that would be lost doing so, only after confirming the `unsaved_changes` refusal (§4.4). A task's changes are the working directory against the base commit, computed by git with a temporary index, and the UI polls them every 2 s. Checked on Copilot CLI 1.0.103: `--session-id` in the help is "set the UUID for a new session", and a try with it created `~/.copilot/session-state/<that UUID>`, so `--resume=<task UUID>` hits the task's conversation; `--interactive=<prompt>` with `=` is accepted also for a prompt starting with `-`. Reasoning: a worktree gives every task its own working directory and branch, so several tasks in one repo and the user's work in the main repo do not get in each other's way, without a copy of the repo; the state directory instead of `<repo>/.worktrees` from the earlier plan (§4.4), because a worktree in the repo is an untracked directory in the user's `git status` (without an entry in `.git/info/exclude`) and another repo directory that tools (IDE, `mise`, search) browse, and the repo list does not see directories outside the projects folder anyway. A branch from the default branch, not from the checked-out one, because a task is new work, and the user's feature branch could have their unfinished changes; the local default branch, because that is what sync moves. The prompt as an argument instead of a file, because Copilot CLI in interactive mode does not read a prompt from a file, and `-p` (non-interactive mode) turns off the TUI, approvals and conversation (ADR-17). Polling instead of a file watcher, because git computes the diff anyway, and a recursive watcher on a worktree with `node_modules` or `target` is expensive and different on every system; a temporary index with `--intent-to-add`, so that the agent's new files are in the diff without touching the index the agent uses for commits, and without writing objects to `.git` on every request. The cost of one request is a copy of the index and a few git commands (`add -N`, `diff`, `rev-list`, `status`), on the Trackwall repo (350 files, Linux) about 30 ms; only an open "Changes" tab polls, and the next request goes after the answer. The task did not get a time budget, a warning about path conflicts between tasks or a task token for MCP: those are the next steps. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI with its own worktree management that gives the same without the daemon (the 1.0.103 help mentions a `--worktree` directory, but with no option), `--interactive` stopping sending the prompt (then typing the prompt into the PTY once Copilot CLI shows its input box, since without a starting prompt `sessionStart` comes only with the first prompt, ADR-17), polling too expensive on large repos on Windows (then a watcher only as a signal to recompute) or a need for a worktree next to the repo, for example for tools that look for configuration in parent directories. Clarification (2026-10-24, #36): the first version added new files through `git add --intent-to-add`, which on every request writes (or refreshes) an empty blob in `.git/objects`; several "Changes" windows at once thus wrote the same object file, and on Windows the second write ended with "Permission denied" and a momentary `repo_unavailable`. Entries through `ls-files --others` and `update-index --index-info` give the same diff without any write (request cost: `ls-files` and `update-index` instead of `add -N`), so neither a queue of computations in one repo nor a retry after an error is needed, and a write of the empty blob by the agent itself (`git add` of an empty file) no longer has anyone on the daemon side to race with. Clarification (2026-10-24, ADR-39): instead of a task token every session, also in a repo root, gets a session token; a task can come from a task proposal, with its identifier and title (the branch slug from the title), and has a summary from its session (`Task.summary`). Superseded in part by ADR-84: worktrees go to the task worktrees folder, by default in the projects folder.

**ADR-32 The product speaks English, the project documentation stays in Polish.** (2026-10-24) Everything a user or an agent using Trackwall sees is in English: UI texts, the desktop shell (tray, dialog windows), daemon messages and logs, validation messages (the default zod locale instead of `z.locales.pl`), MCP tool descriptions and instructions, `README.md` and `README.txt` in the portable build. In Polish stay the documentation in `docs/`, ADRs, `AGENTS.md`, code comments, commit messages, PR descriptions, test names and tool scripts for people developing Trackwall (build, icons, smoke, recording). English, because the repo is public, and the tool goes to teams where not everyone reads Polish; MCP descriptions are also read by an agent, which works in English anyway. The project documentation stays in Polish, because it is written and read today by the same people and agents as before, and translating it now is a lot of work with no user waiting for it. `README.md` says only what Trackwall is, that it is an early version and where the releases with installation instructions are; the feature description is in `docs/features.md`, and running from source in `docs/development.md`. `CONTEXT.md` gives, for every term visible in the UI, daemon messages and MCP, the English word we use there. `SECURITY.md`, the issue forms in `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/` and the release notes in `docs/release-notes/` (the release text on GitHub) are read by users, so from this ADR they are in English too; earlier notes stay in Polish. Plurals are English `one` / `other` (`pluralNoun` and `countLabel` in `protocol`, shared by the daemon and the UI) instead of the three Polish forms. What would reverse it: contributors from outside Poland (then documentation and comments in English too, in a separate PR) or a need for several UI languages (then texts in a translation catalogue instead of in code). The project-language part (documentation, ADRs, `AGENTS.md`, comments, commits, PR descriptions, test names, scripts) is superseded by ADR-43.

**ADR-33 A 3D dashboard on Pawn from npm, fed with a `HomeModel` snapshot instead of an event stream, in the "Orbit" layout of models built from simple solids.** (2026-10-24) The first screen of Trackwall is to be a 3D dashboard (the view in the UI came after the scene package, see the clarification below), which says at once whether the environment works, which Copilot sessions are waiting for a human and what state the repos are in. The scene is a separate package `@trackwall/scene` on `@pawnjs/core` at an exact version from npm (the three.js + koota engine); Trackwall uses nothing outside the published Pawn packages, and what they lack it reports to the Pawn project and works around on its side until a decision (rendering on demand and WebGL context loss on the UI side, arcs and wires as our own three.js models). The scene gets a snapshot, not events (a change from the original §5.5), because the UI does not have `/ws` yet, and a snapshot is the same value when polling and with events; the comparison with the previous snapshot is done by `KeyedEntities` and an object's appearance signature, so the cost is one `JSON.stringify` per object at the scale of 10–15 repos and about 10 sessions. The "Orbit" layout won over layers in rows and a grid, because at this scale it fits everything in one frame, and sessions stand next to their repo. The models are built from simple solids (no glTF) while the look settles; the same shape can later come through Pawn's `Model` without changing the rest of the scene. Colours come from UI tokens (ADR-16), given as a `ScenePalette` in `#rrggbb`, and the label text is written by the UI, so the scene has no texts or colours of its own. What would reverse the decision: a scale much larger than a few dozen repos (then a layout with layers in rows and grouping), the cost of comparing snapshots visible in a profile (then events from `/ws` straight onto entities) or the project dropping Pawn. Clarification (2026-10-26, release 0.4.1): koota, the ECS under Pawn, builds the accessors of its traits with `new Function`, which the CSP of the Tauri window refuses, so in 0.4.0 the scene threw as it was built and the window went blank. Instead of allowing `'unsafe-eval'` for the whole window (whose README tab shows content from repos), `patches/koota@0.6.6.patch` (pnpm `patchedDependencies`) turns them into closures that do the same; the patch is tied to that version, so pnpm refuses an upgrade until it is reviewed, and the E2E test of the window under its CSP (ADR-71) fails if code from strings comes back. To be reported to koota; the patch goes when koota stops generating code.

**ADR-34 The sync result says what was left in the working directory; working directory changes in the "Changes" tab; the `no-mise` tag from the card.** (2026-10-24) Every sync result without an error has the state of the working directory after it (the checked-out branch, changed and, separately, new files), and the UI shows a sentence about the result for every repo, including an up-to-date one; the "Changes" tab in the repo details shows the files and diff of the working directory against HEAD; a repo without mise tasks in the commit gets the `no-mise` tag. Reasoning: after "Sync all" the user saw "4 changes" on the list and got no message, because the repo was up to date (the list hid "up to date"), and the number of changes could not be opened; nor did the result itself make clear that a moved default branch does not change the files when another one is checked out. The working directory state comes from the daemon as part of the result, not from the repo list in the UI, because it describes the moment of the sync, also goes to `list_repos` in MCP (`lastSync`) and costs one `git status` per repo; when this `git status` fails, the result has `workingTree: null`, not an error, because the branch has already moved. The tab uses the task changes read (ADR-31) instead of `git status` with a file list, because it gives the diff and new files at once without writing to the user's index; it is read-only, because committing and discarding changes in the background is easy to lose track of, and an editor does it better. `no-mise` is a tag from the commit's card, not a result of `mise tasks ls`, because the repo list cannot run mise in every repo (trust, time, global configuration, ADR-20, ADR-25), and the tag gives a "repos to normalise" filter without a new field in the list; it is negative, because it is the absence that must be found. What would reverse it: a need to commit or discard changes from Trackwall (then actions with confirmation in `vcs`), tasks in repos kept mainly in `task_config.includes` (then reading the included files or `mise tasks ls` in the background after trust) or the `git status` at the end of a sync noticeable in time with hundreds of repos (then the working directory state from the repo list in the UI).

Clarification (2026-10-24, Home view): the dashboard is the first view on the bar (Ctrl+1), and Repos, Sessions and Overview moved to 2, 3 and 4, because it is the screen the day starts with. The snapshot no longer has a "repo has mise" field: the only cheap source would be `mise tasks ls` per repo, and that runs mise (and `[env]` in a trusted repo) every few seconds, so a repo tile without mise does not differ from the others. The running task comes from the new `mise/running`, a read from daemon memory. The tokens are converted to `#rrggbb` by the browser's 2D canvas instead of our own `oklch` conversion, because the browser draws them anyway and knows the screen's colour space.

Addendum (2026-10-24, ADR-36): the UI has `/ws` now, and sessions and running mise tasks reach the dashboard from events; the scene still gets a `HomeModel` snapshot, because events carry topic snapshots too.

**ADR-35 The README in the repo card renders the ready-made Streamdown with the Mermaid plugin; links are opened by the daemon in the system browser, images are replaced by a description.** (2026-10-24) The README tab showed raw Markdown. Instead of assembling our own renderer, the UI uses the ready-made Streamdown component (`streamdown`, Apache-2.0) with `@streamdown/mermaid` (Apache-2.0; Mermaid MIT; the other dependencies MIT, ISC, BSD and Apache-2.0, no copyleft): GFM (tables, task lists), code blocks and Mermaid diagrams in `strict` mode with a dark theme, with copying, full screen and zoom. The component is loaded lazily (`ReadmeMarkdown.tsx`), because with Mermaid it is about 4 MB of JS, and the rest of the UI does not need it; a loading or rendering error shows the raw text (`ReadmeFallback.tsx`). The format is decided by the daemon (`readme.format` in `overview`): `.txt`, `.rst` and `.adoc` are text, the rest Markdown, as on GitHub. The README is content from the repo, so untrusted. Raw HTML goes through `rehype-raw` and `rehype-sanitize` with GitHub's schema (`defaultSchema`, with the `user-content-` prefix for `id` and `name`), given explicitly instead of Streamdown's default plugins, because those remove the prefix and add `rehype-harden`, which without a base address turns relative links and images into "[blocked]". Nothing from the README navigates the window: the container intercepts every click (also with the middle button and Enter) on an `<a>`, also in a diagram's SVG, where Mermaid in `strict` mode leaves links from `click … href`; an `http` or `https` address (`ExternalLink` in `protocol`, in canonical form from `normalize`) is opened by the daemon with the `workspace/open-link` action using the same `openUrl` as VS Code, and other links (relative, anchors, `mailto:`) are text. The Tauri window has no browser, and a clicked link would replace the app in it. Images from Markdown and HTML are not loaded, only described (`alt`): the Tauri window lets in images only from `self` and `data:` (CSP), and an image from the internet is also a tracking pixel. Downloading diagrams, tables and code to a file is turned off, because it is not known whether WebView2 and WKWebView support it. What would reverse the decision: a need for images from the repo (then a daemon action with an image from HEAD as `data:`), links inside the repo (then going to the "Files" tab) or the cost of the UI bundle size.

**ADR-36 Sessions in MCP read-only and `/ws` events as topic snapshots, detected by comparison in the daemon.** (2026-10-24) The "Sessions" view is to be the registry of all sessions, and an agent (the supervising agent or another session) is to know about them, so the MCP server gets two read-only tools: `list_sessions` (sessions and tasks, the same `Session` and `Task` as in the UI, optionally for one repo) and `get_session` (a session, its task and the task's changes without the diff: a diff of up to 1 MB does not fit sensibly into an agent's context, the agent can read the worktree itself, and git then does not have to compute it). The tools do not return the session screen, nor the last lines of an ended group (`AgentSession`): the last lines are masked only with the values of secrets from the process environment (§4.2), and the agent also prints in the terminal what it read from files (`.env`) or got in the prompt, so the screen could carry a secret printed in the terminal to another session (§8). The tools do not start, answer or stop sessions: a main session that commissions a task (`propose_task` with approval in the UI), reports a result (`report_result`) and waits for a session (`wait_for_session`) is the next step, which needs a task token so that the daemon knows who is calling; today a session's stdio bridge does not tell the daemon which session it is from, so `list_sessions` does not mark "this is me". The `/ws` events (§5.2) carry the whole topic snapshot (`sessions.changed`, `mise.running.changed`), not a difference or a domain event like `task.state`: after every connection the client gets the snapshots, so the event buffer, `seq` and `resync` from the original §5.2 are not needed (an event has no number, because one connection gives the order, and nobody read it), and the UI gets the same value as from the HTTP action and the same view code works with both sources (polling stays as a fallback for the time without a connection and for a topic the daemon cannot read). The daemon detects a change by comparing the snapshot JSON every 200 ms while someone listens, instead of notifications from modules: session state changes in many places (hooks, OSC, keys, the `Stale` threshold, the end of a group in `procs`), snapshots are a read from memory, and one comparison will not miss a change a module forgot to notify about. Deliberate cost: two `JSON.stringify` calls and a session read five times a second the whole time the UI window is open, also when hidden in the tray (before, the UI did not ask with the window hidden); with a dozen or so sessions that is fractions of a millisecond. The first topics are the ones the UI polled most often and that the Home dashboard reads (§5.5); the Tauri shell still polls HTTP (§5.4). WebSockets have one `upgrade` handler (`api/sockets.ts`) with the same checks for events and terminals, and the address with the socket subprotocol has one place in `protocol` (`DaemonSocket`). The event source (`EventHub`) is in `api/` today, because only `/ws` listens to it; `wait_for_session` in MCP will get the same object from `main.ts`, just as `createMcpEndpoint` gets the facade, and the hub will then move to `infra/`. What would reverse the decision: a topic whose snapshot is expensive (the repo list with `git status`, the graph; then a notification from the module or watching the repo and an event without a snapshot), a topic with a large snapshot or frequent changes (logs: then `log.append` with records from a cursor, like `logs/tail`), the cost of comparison with the window hidden visible in a profile (then rarer comparison or disconnecting the UI with the window hidden) or a need for the agent to wait for an event (then `wait_for_session` in MCP on the same event source). Clarification (2026-10-24, ADR-39): `wait_for_session` does not listen to `EventHub`, it reads the state of one session, task or proposal from daemon memory every 200 ms, because it waits for one object, and comparing the snapshots of all topics would gain nothing here; the hub stays in `api/`, and `list_sessions` marks the calling session (`caller`) by the session token.

**ADR-37 Java and Kotlin syntax trees from tree-sitter in WASM in the daemon process, grammars embedded in the binary.** (2026-10-24) When the tokens from `java-tokens.ts` are not enough for a card extractor (DTOs: `@RequestBody` and response types, fields of records and classes, `data class`), it will take a syntax tree from `modules/cards/syntax-tree.ts`: `web-tree-sitter` with the grammars `tree-sitter-java` and `@tree-sitter-grammars/tree-sitter-kotlin` (all MIT) as `.wasm` files. The runtime and the grammars are loaded once per process by `platform/tree-sitter.ts` (`Parser.init` replaces the WASM module of the whole library): under Bun the files come through an import with the `type: "file"` attribute (`bun-grammar-files.ts`), which `bun build --compile` embeds in the binary, under Node the same files from `node_modules` through `import.meta.resolve`. The extractors stay synchronous: `loadSyntaxParsers()` is asynchronous only because of loading the grammars, and `read` gives the tree to a read function and frees it right after it (GC does not clean up WASM memory), so a read that returns a node or a promise is an error (the result itself is checked; a node in an array or an object field is left to the review). pnpm does not run install scripts outside `onlyBuiltDependencies` anyway, so `ignoredBuiltDependencies` for the grammar packages only records that we knowingly do not build their native addons; the author's tool, which the Kotlin package has in `dependencies`, is removed by `overrides`. This PR is a spike: no extractor parses yet, so the daemon binary does not contain the grammars today, and the first extractor (DTOs) will add a parser check to `scripts/smoke.ts`, so that the evidence is the real binary, not a probe. Reasoning: research of tools for Java code analysis (jQAssistant, Joern, CodeQL, scip-java, Spoon, JavaParser, jdtls, spring-service-graph, GitNexus and others) did not find a tool that would replace the extractors from ADR-30 without a JDK, a repo build or a license that rules out company code; tree-sitter is the only full Java and Kotlin parser that runs in the daemon process under Bun and Node, without compiling the repo and without a native addon (ADR-1), and on syntactically invalid code it gives a tree with `ERROR` nodes instead of an exception. The tokenizer stays where it is enough (annotations, HTTP client calls), because it is about four times faster. Spike on Linux x64 (Bun 1.4.4, Node 22.22; Windows and macOS to be measured by hand): a probe compiled with the grammars is 4.1 MB larger than an empty binary (Kotlin grammar 3.4 MB, Java 0.4 MB, runtime 0.2 MB), and the daemon binary will grow by that much; loading the parsers 30–60 ms; 3000 files of 12.8 KB (38 MB of code) in 8.3 s under Bun and 11.4 s under Node, at 69 and 168 MB RSS of the whole process, that is about 4.6 MB/s, against 2.1 s for the tokenizer on the same input. A card is computed once per commit (`card-cache.ts`), and the source file limit is 3000, so the worst case is a few seconds per repo on the first read. The test `syntax-tree.test.ts` checks on Linux (the `check` job) and in the Windows and macOS matrix (the `test` job) the parser under Bun, under Node and in a binary from `bun build --compile --minify` run from another directory, with the WASM files in the binary's virtual file system; the review also checked cross-compiling `bun-windows-x64` with the flags of `scripts/build.ts` (files in `B:\~BUN\`). What would reverse it: parsing that clearly lengthens the first card read for larger repos at work (then a tree only for files the tokenizer points to as DTOs, or the parser in a worker thread), a grammar that stops keeping up with Java or Kotlin syntax, or a change in `bun build --compile` after which `type: "file"` files are no longer embedded. Addendum (2026-10-24, ADR-38): the first extractor with a tree is endpoint contracts and DTOs, `scripts/smoke.ts` computes a real card in the compiled daemon binary, and the parsers are loaded once per process.

**ADR-38 Endpoint request and response types and DTOs on the repo card from the syntax tree.** (2026-10-24) An HTTP endpoint on the card has `request` (the type of the `@RequestBody` parameter) and `response` (the return type without wrappers that do not change the body), and the card a `dtos` list: types from the repo reachable from these types through field types, with fields and their types or with enum constants (§4.6). They are read by `endpoint-contracts.ts` from tree-sitter syntax trees (ADR-37), only in files that have endpoints and in the type files that follow from them; the commit read pulls in these files by type name (`OrderDto` → `OrderDto.java`/`.kt`) in at most three rounds. Types are the text from the syntax tree (`List<OrderLine>`), not resolved symbols: without imports, packages and inheritance, so a type with the same simple name in two packages gives the first one found, and the card does not know fields from a superclass or those generated by annotation processors. `find_endpoint` and `get_repo_card` give the types to the agent, the "Card" tab shows them at the endpoint and in the DTO section. An endpoint's contract is the method with the handler's name that contains the line of its mapping, not the first method with that name, because one file can have several classes and overloads. A type without fields is not a DTO, and a qualified name points to its last segment, so that the list does not spill over to services injected into the controller. All card parsing has a total budget of 10 s, which bounds the gap from §8 described in ADR-37, and a card that missed a tree because of time does not go to disk, because it depends on the machine's load (nor does a card without grammars, when it tried to parse Java or Kotlin files: endpoints or types); type files pulled in for contracts do not go to the Spring extractor, so that HTTP facts do not depend on guessing files by name; `scripts/smoke.ts` computes a real card of a repo with a record in the compiled binary, so the evidence of embedding the grammars is now the daemon binary. `EXTRACTOR_VERSION` 4. Reasoning: the tool research (ADR-37) ended with DTOs as the first extractor with a tree, because an agent changing an API asks what shape of data an endpoint takes and returns, and tokens will not separate a parameter type from an annotation or a field from a method. Simple names are enough for what is visible on one repo's card; resolving symbols (LSP, scip-java) is left to the task session. What would reverse it: repos at work where DTOs come from generated code (OpenAPI generator, files outside the commit) or from a package other than the controller's, which would require reading the contract from `openapi.yaml` or from the card of the package's repo; cards whose first computation reaches the budget (then the parser in a worker thread).

**ADR-39 A main session commissions tasks through MCP: a proposal approved in the UI, a summary from the task session and waiting for a session, with a session token in the stdio bridge.** (Superseded in its bridge part by ADR-89: a session sends the token as a header of its HTTP MCP entry.) (2026-10-24) Modelled on the coordinator in Claude, which talks to a human, opens threads and reads their results, a Copilot CLI session with the Trackwall MCP server (in Trackwall or a supervising agent outside it) gets three tools (§4.4, §5.3): `propose_task {repo, title, prompt}` stores a task proposal, which the user in the "Sessions" view reads, can edit and approves ("Start task", then an ordinary task from ADR-31 is created with the proposal's identifier) or rejects; `report_result {summary}` stores a summary in the session's own task (`Task.summary`, in `tasks.json`); `wait_for_session {session | task, timeoutSeconds}` waits at most 55 s until the session or task needs someone. The daemon learns which session is calling by the **session token**: a random token in a file in the session directory, which the session's stdio bridge reads (`--session-token-file`) and sends in the `x-trackwall-session-token` header; the daemon keeps only a SHA-256 hash. Reasoning: the decision about work is made by a human (ADR-9), so a session proposes, it does not start, and this is not a planner; the rule from AGENTS.md that no MCP tool changes the configuration or processes stays, because a proposal only waits in memory, and after approval the daemon builds the command from the repo identifier as with the form (§1.2), with the prompt as the content of `--interactive=`. Approval is a gate for an agent that works through MCP, not a security boundary: the session's process can read the daemon token itself and call the HTTP API (gap in §8). The result going up has a fixed shape (a summary and changes from git through `get_session`), not typing text into another session's PTY, because then one agent's text would become another's input without a human taking part. A session token instead of the planned task token, because any session can propose, also in a repo root; a file instead of an argument, because other users of the system can see process arguments; a hash in memory, so that looking it up does not reveal the token through comparison time. An unknown token is a refusal, not an anonymous call, so that a closed session cannot pass itself off as an agent outside Trackwall. Proposals only in memory (at most 20 waiting), because a session that waits for an answer does not survive a daemon restart anyway. Waiting polls daemon memory every 200 ms instead of `EventHub` (clarification of ADR-36), because it concerns one object; the 55 s limit is under a minute, so that the MCP client does not abort the call with its own limit, and the agent calls it again. A task session with a starting prompt gets the `Working` state from the `sessionStart` hook, because otherwise waiting would end with `needs_attention` before the agent starts. The `audit` table in SQLite, which ADR-24 put before the first state-changing tool, is not a precondition: both new tools store only text for a human, who sees it in the UI, and the daemon log has the tool and the session; the audit will come before `run_task`. Clarified by ADR-41: the audit is not a precondition for `run_mise_task` either. Knowingly outside this step: a main session in the projects folder started from the "Sessions" view (today a session always has a repo, so the main session is a session in a repo or a supervising agent outside Trackwall), proposals in the dashboard's "Needs you" queue and in the tray menu, indenting threads under a session in the list, a warning about the same files in two tasks and automatic approval (if it ever exists, only as a setting in the UI). Clarification (2026-10-25): the result going up also has the task changes with their diff, `get_task_changes {task, paths, maxPatchBytes}`, so the main session or the supervisor reviews what a task session did before reporting it to the user; it is the diff of the "Changes" tab, narrowed by git pathspecs and cut at a whole line within a byte limit (default 32,000, at most 200,000, because the result reaches the agent twice), and a separate tool rather than a field of `get_session`, so that following a session stays cheap and the agent asks for the diff of the files it cares about. The diff is the task's work product, not text one agent writes to another, and it passes unchanged (the gap in §8). What would reverse it: Copilot CLI with its own commissioning of work to other sessions that gives the same with visibility in Trackwall; an MCP client with a call limit shorter than 55 s (then shorter waiting or server notifications in a Streamable HTTP session); a need for proposals that survive a restart (then a file like `tasks.json`); a session in a loop flooding the user with proposals despite the limit (then a per-session limit or muting a session in the UI). Partly superseded by ADR-68: the proposer now sends text down to its task's session (a task message, read through a tool after the daemon's fixed prompt), and the result going up also arrives without polling, as task updates.

Clarification (2026-10-24): proposals also show in the dashboard's "Needs you" queue and in the tray menu, and the "Sessions" list is a tree: tasks and proposals sit under the entry with the session that proposed them (§4.4, §5.4, §5.5). The origin comes from the existing `proposedBy`, so the API contract does not change; the tray reads `proposals` from the same reply as the sessions. Proposals are not on the 3D scene, because they have no session or worktree yet, and the decision with an edited prompt is made in "Sessions", so a click in the queue or the tray leads there. A new proposal draws attention like a session that started waiting, because it also waits for a person.

**ADR-40 Code search through `git grep` in the HEAD commit, without an index and without ripgrep.** (2026-10-24) The `search_code` MCP tool searches for a fixed string or a regular expression in many repos of the projects folder at once, with `git grep` over the objects of each repo's HEAD commit (§4.5, §5.3). Git is required anyway, and `git grep` over a commit gives the same guarantees as browsing (ADR-14): paths only from the commit tree, no ignored files, no programs run from the repo (`--no-textconv`), and machine-readable output (`-z`) that does not depend on git's language. ripgrep is faster on a working tree, but it is a second native binary to ship and sign for every platform, and it would search the working tree, including files the repo does not track (`node_modules` without `.gitignore`, secrets in `.env`). There is no index (e.g. trigrams, as in Zoekt): the pool holds dozens of repos, not thousands, and an index would need refreshing after every sync. What would reverse it: searches over hundreds of large repos taking longer than a few seconds (then an index), a need to search uncommitted changes (then `git grep --untracked` in the working tree), or a need for PCRE (`-P` depends on git being built with PCRE2). Without an index every query costs a full read of each commit, so the search is bounded: per-repo output and time limits, an overall budget after which no new repo starts, and a stop once the limit of matches is reached (§4.5).

**ADR-41 The agent runs and stops mise tasks over MCP from the list of a trusted config, without a separate approval in the UI.** (2026-10-24) The tools `run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `wait_for_mise_task` (waits up to 55 s for the group to end) and `list_running_mise_tasks` (§4.7, §5.3) map to `mise.start`, `mise.stop`, `mise.waitForEnd` and `mise.running`, like the buttons in the "mise tasks" tab. An agent needs the loop "change, run the tests or the build, read the result" without asking a human to click, and with `tail` (ADR-28) and the exit code from `wait_for_mise_task` it has all of it. The consent to run repo code is the mise trust given in the UI: without it mise does not list the tasks, and the daemon runs only a task from that list, with the command built from its name (§1.2), so the agent cannot run anything the user could not run with the button; MCP has no tool for `mise trust`. A separate "agents may run tasks" switch was rejected, because an agent with shell access can type `mise run` anyway, and the MCP client asks for approval of the tool (`destructiveHint` on `run_mise_task`, and on `stop_mise_task`, which can kill a dev server or a migration the user started) as it does for a shell command. The names carry `mise_` instead of the planned `run_task` (§5.3 before this decision), because in English text a bare *task* is an agent task (`propose_task`, `CONTEXT.md`); for the same reason the allowlist of names (`setup`, `test`, `build`, `lint`, `health`) was dropped: repos name their tasks their own way (`test:unit`, `build:ui`), and the services that were to take over `dev` do not exist yet. A Trackwall session (by its session token, ADR-39) runs and stops only tasks of the repo it works in, as planned in §8; an agent outside Trackwall is the user's program and reaches every repo. Like the proposal approval in ADR-39, this is a guard for an agent using its own bridge, not a security boundary: a session process can call `/mcp` with the daemon token alone. The `audit` table that ADR-24 and ADR-39 put before `run_task` is not a precondition: before acting, a destructive tool writes its arguments (repo and mise task, never free text) to the daemon log with the tool and session, and the run shows in the UI (state, log, Home) like a run from the button. The run is always in the repo root in the projects folder, also for a task session in a worktree, because mise trust is per path and a worktree in the state directory is not trusted. The gap of trusting a path rather than its content is in §8. What would reverse it: abuse by an agent that changes a trusted config (then only tasks from the commit, or mise `paranoid` mode), a need to run tasks in a task worktree (then trusting the worktree when it is created), services with leases (then `dev` through a lease instead of `run_mise_task`) or an audit the user requires (then the `audit` table before these tools). Addendum (2026-10-25): ADR-59 runs mise tasks in a task worktree, with the worktree trusted per run instead of when it is created.

**ADR-42 Errors from logs for the agent as blocks of in-memory records, detected by line heuristics.** (2026-10-24) The MCP tool `get_errors` returns errors from the buffers of all log sources (filters `repo` and `miseTask`, a `sinceSeq` cursor across the workspace): a block from an `error` (or `warn`) record, an exception header or a Spring/Gradle report, together with the lines that belong to it (stack trace, `Caused by:`, indented compiler context), the same errors of a source merged with a count, the stack trace trimmed and the cause in `exception` (§4.2). The `errors` tool planned in §5.3 is named `get_errors`, like the other tools that read one thing, and takes `repo`/`miseTask` instead of `service`, since there are no services yet. Rationale: an agent asking what broke needs the stack trace and the cause, not 2000 `tail` lines from one task; the planned single records with `level>=error` would cut a Java stack trace into a header without its cause (frames and `Caused by:` have level `info`), and a flood of the same error from every request would eat the context. Blocks computed on each request from the buffer (at most 2000 records per source) cost less than an index and keep no state of their own, and line heuristics work without changes to the user's repos until there is an ECS parser. Compiler levels (javac, tsc, kotlinc, `npm error`) and the mise task prefix went into `logLevel`, because the UI uses the same rule. Would reverse it: the ECS parser (then `error.stack_trace` from the record replaces block building for structured logs), service logs longer than the buffer in which the agent looks for older errors (then an error index or reading the JSONL), or formats the heuristics miss that recur in work repos (then block rules from the workspace config).

**ADR-43 The whole project is in English, migrated in stages.** (2026-10-24) Documentation in `docs/`, ADRs, `AGENTS.md`, `CONTEXT.md`, comments, commit messages, PR titles and descriptions, test names and developer scripts move to English, like the product already did in ADR-32. The repo is public and an English-only reader should be able to understand a change and trust it without a translator; a split where the UI says *session* and the glossary, comments and tests explain it in another language doubles every term and makes every review switch languages. The migration is staged so that it does not block parallel work or the 0.3.0 release: first the rule itself with `AGENTS.md` and the PR template, then `CONTEXT.md` and the shorter documents, then `docs/architecture.md` with the ADRs once the ADRs in flight are merged, then comments and test names one package per PR. Until then new and rewritten text is in English and untouched Polish text is left for its migration PR, so that feature PRs do not conflict with the translation. History is not rewritten: old commits, PR descriptions and the v0.2.0 release note stay in Polish. What would reverse it: a team that works in Polish taking over the project (the product texts would stay in English for their users, per ADR-32). Addendum (2026-10-24): `docs/architecture.md` with all ADRs, `CONTEXT.md` and `docs/features.md` are translated; the Polish text left in the repo is code comments, test names and developer scripts, migrated one package per PR after 0.3.0, plus the history above.

**ADR-44 Shared files of two tasks as a warning in the task panel, computed on request from the worktrees' lists of changes.** (2026-10-24) Two tasks in one repo work in separate worktrees, each from its own base commit (the default branch when it was created), so they do not get in each other's way while working, but the one merged second will likely get a conflict in the files both changed. The daemon answers `task-shared-files` with the intersection of the file lists from git (the same read as the "Changes" tab, without the diff), and the UI shows a warning above the selected task's tabs. At the level of files, not lines: two diffs in one file usually need attention anyway, and comparing line ranges against two different bases would give false certainty. A list of changes cut off at its limit (`truncated`) can miss a shared file, which is acceptable for a warning. On request, not in the background or on the session list, because the list refreshes every second, and a git read of every task on every list change would cost more than the warning is worth; a marker on the list and a warning for the main session in MCP (`get_session`) are possible next steps. Only a warning: Trackwall does not stop a task or merge anything by itself (ADR-9). This would be reversed by many false warnings on files that always change together (then line ranges or a trial merge with `git merge-tree`), or by a need for the warning without opening the task (then computing in the background with a cache and a `/ws` event).

**ADR-45 A service is a repo's mise task `dev` with readiness observed on its port, started together with the services it calls from the graph, and an agent calls its endpoints through the daemon with the errors from all service logs in the result.** (2026-10-24) The module `services` (§4.3) gets its first slice and four MCP tools (§5.3): `start_service {repo, dependencies}` starts the repo's `dev` and, transitively, the `dev` of the repos it calls over `http` and `manual` edges (not `package`, not `low` confidence), in waves from the dependencies, and waits up to 55 s; `stop_service`, `list_services`, and `call_endpoint {repo, method, path, headers, body}`, which sends the request to the port that answered and returns the response with the errors (ADR-42) that the `dev` tasks of all repos logged during the call. Justification: an agent working across microservices needs the loop "change, start the slice, call the endpoint, read the response and the downstream stack trace, fix" without a human starting five terminals; the graph (ADR-29, ADR-30) already knows who calls whom, `run_mise_task` (ADR-41) set the consent rule (a trusted config, a command built by the daemon), and `get_errors` (ADR-42) turns a log into a cause. `dev` is a convention like `setup` and `build` in the batch command (ADR-27), so a service needs no new config; readiness is a TCP connection to the port from the log or from `server.port` on the card instead of the planned `mise run health`, because no repo has that task and an open port says the same for a web service; the port from the log wins because profiles and `--server.port` change it and the card reads the default profile. The service is the same process group and log as the mise task (`mise.start`), so the UI tab, `tail` and `get_errors` see it without a new log source. The daemon builds the URL (loopback, the probed port), so `call_endpoint` is not a general HTTP client; the response is masked like a log line, and only `repo` and `method` go to the audit line. State lives in memory, like the process groups (§4.1). Not in this slice: a service from a task worktree (mise trusts by path, and auto-trusting the worktree config would let the agent run any command through `mise.toml`), several services per repo, leases (ADR-5), secrets. What would reverse it: services that need a health URL rather than a port (then a `health` task or `management.endpoints` from the card), or repos where `dev` is not the service (then a `services` key in `workspace.yaml`). Clarification (2026-10-25): a service from a task worktree came with ADR-61, once mise in a worktree ran only with the config the user trusted (ADR-59). Clarification (2026-10-26, ADR-72): `stopped` also waits for the port to close, because Gradle's daemon runs `bootRun`'s app outside the group of `dev` and ends it only after the group is gone; a stop waits up to 15 s and otherwise returns the service `stopping` with the reason, and the watch goes on until the port closes; another stop checks again. Killing the process that holds the port was rejected: nothing but the port ties it to the service.

**ADR-46 The agent learns how to work with Trackwall from the MCP server and from session instructions, without changes to the user's repo.** (2026-10-24) One MCP instruction (`MCP_INSTRUCTIONS` in `protocol`) says what Trackwall is and when to reach for which tool; the MCP server announces it in `instructions` (§5.3), and every Trackwall session gets it in its **session instructions** together with the session's role (§4.4): the file `trackwall.instructions.md` in the session directory, whose directory the daemon appends to `COPILOT_CUSTOM_INSTRUCTIONS_DIRS` in the session environment. Rationale: tool descriptions say what a tool returns, not in which order and why to use them (the repo card instead of reading build files, `find_endpoint` and `who_calls` before changing an API), and a task session did not know it should stay in its worktree and end with `report_result`. Checked in the Copilot CLI 1.0.103 binary without signing in: MCP server `instructions` reach the prompt only for servers on an allowlist (`mcpRegistryServerInstructionAllowlist`; the `--allow-all-mcp-server-instructions` workaround would also turn on the instructions of every other server the user has), so the MCP server alone is not enough; `copilot instruction list` shows a file from a `COPILOT_CUSTOM_INSTRUCTIONS_DIRS` directory as a repository instruction, and the variable separates directories with commas. The variable rather than a file in the repo (`AGENTS.md`, `.github/instructions`), because the repo and worktrees belong to the user and a file in a worktree would show up in the task's changes; rather than `~/.copilot/copilot-instructions.md`, because that is the user's configuration and would apply to sessions outside Trackwall too; rather than a skill in the session plugin, because a skill loads only when the model finds its description relevant, while the role must hold from the first turn; rather than a custom agent (`--agent`), because an agent replaces Copilot CLI's default agent and its tools. The role is built only from the repo and task ids, so agent text never becomes another session's instruction (§1.2). A test requires the MCP instruction to mention every tool in `mcpTools`, so a new tool comes with its instruction. No `llms.txt` in the Trackwall repo: agents that develop Trackwall have `AGENTS.md`, and agents that use it get the instruction from Trackwall. To check by hand with a signed-in Copilot CLI: whether the file with `applyTo: "**"` is in the prompt from the first turn (not only after a file is opened) and whether the agent follows its role. What would reverse it: a Copilot CLI that takes `instructions` from every local MCP server or lets the user allow a single server (then the server is enough and the file keeps only the role); a change or removal of `COPILOT_CUSTOM_INSTRUCTIONS_DIRS` (then an instruction file in the session plugin, if Copilot CLI starts reading one there, or a preamble in the task prompt). Addendum (2026-10-25): a session also learns at start which environment it runs in, so that it does not have to ask or guess. The session instructions open with the Trackwall version that started the session and where the user sees it, and the MCP instruction ends with the address of the documentation site and its `llms.txt`, so every MCP client, not only a Trackwall session, can look up a feature instead of guessing. The version comes in through the facade options rather than from `infra/version.ts`, because a module takes only the listed infrastructure files (§4). The site is published from `main`, so it describes the development version rather than the session's; a versioned site would let the link carry the version. The ADR's "no `llms.txt` in the Trackwall repo" still holds for developers: the `llms.txt` agents get is the documentation site's (ADR-56), for people using Trackwall.

**ADR-47 "Waiting for you" ends when the user has seen the end of the turn; approval and plan wait for an answer.** (2026-10-24) A session in `Input` (the turn ended, the agent asked a question, or the user interrupted it) stops waiting for you once a session terminal window has been in front of the user (focused and visible) or the user typed into the session; `Approval` and `Plan` keep waiting until the answer, which the hooks and the session keys already report. Before this, `Input` waited until the next prompt, so a session the user had already looked at kept the warning, the counter and the tray entry for as long as it stayed idle, and "waiting for you" stopped meaning anything. The rule follows tools that solved the same problem: Agent Deck has `waiting` (yellow, not acknowledged) and `idle` (grey, acknowledged on attach), and a tmux status indicator for Claude Code clears a finished session when its pane is viewed but keeps the permission prompt until work resumes. The window counts as in front only after 1 s, because a click on "Waiting: <repo>" in the tray first brings the window to the front with the previous session's terminal and only then switches to the chosen session. Focus comes from the window over the existing PTY WebSocket instead of a separate HTTP action: the daemon sees when the window disconnects, so a closed window cannot leave the session "watched", and the window that shows the session is the one that knows whether the user can see it. Only a change to another state makes a seen session unseen again, so a hook that confirms `Input` after a session key does not bring the warning back. The state stays `Input` (the detectors do not change); "idle" is how the UI and the dashboard show seen `Input`. Reversed if Copilot CLI reports turns in a way that tells a question apart from an ended turn (then a question could wait like `Approval`), or if the user wants an explicit "mark as read" instead of focus.

**ADR-48 Sessions switch from the keyboard with Alt + digit and Alt+J, and show several terminals in a grid of pages.** (2026-10-24) The Sessions view gets a layout switch: `1` is the selected entry with its full panel as before, `2` and `4` show a page of that many entries as a grid of terminals, and `auto` shows the whole list, up to nine per page (`lib/session-grid.ts`). The page is always the one with the selected entry, so a shortcut to any position also turns the page and the selected tile never leaves the screen. Columns and rows come from the size of the area (`gridShape`: the shape with the largest tile of ratio 1.6 that fits a cell), so two tiles sit side by side on a wide window and stack on a tall one, and six fill three columns and two rows on a 34-inch ultrawide. Alt + digit (⌥ on macOS) selects the entry at that position in the list, and Alt+J opens the next session that is waiting for you from any view, after the selected one like the "Next waiting" button (`lib/session-shortcut.ts`; `App` owns Alt+J, the view the positions). The selected tile has the accent ring and the keyboard (a tile of a task without a session takes the focus itself, so keys stop going to the terminal selected before); a waiting tile keeps its warning frame also when selected. The last page keeps the tile size of a full page, and tiles appear only once the grid area is measured, so that no terminal reports a size to its session from a shape that is about to change. Rationale: switching between sessions that need you was a mouse click each time, and a large screen fits several Copilot CLI sessions at once. Ctrl/⌘ + digit already switches views (ADR-16), and Ctrl+J, proposed first, is a line feed in a terminal, which a TUI can read as Enter; Alt + digit and Alt+J have no meaning in Copilot CLI 1.0.103 that we know of, and on macOS Option + key on a US layout only types a character such as `¡`, so the keys are matched by position (`code`). What Alt and Option exist for stays a key: AltGr (Ctrl + Alt on Windows), the keypad (Alt + keypad digits type a character by its code on Windows) and, on macOS, an Option + key that types an ASCII character (⌥5 is `[` on a German layout, ⌥2 `@` on a Swedish one), which on such layouts leaves those positions without a shortcut. Pages instead of choosing the tiles by hand, because the list order is already the order the user knows and the shortcut numbers match it without a second list to maintain. The shortcuts listen in the capture phase on `window` and stop the event: xterm.js turns Ctrl + digit into a control character and stops its propagation, so the view shortcuts did not work while a terminal had the focus, and a session shortcut must not also reach the terminal. In Sessions the session shortcuts are always taken, also when there is nothing to switch to; outside Sessions Alt+J is taken only when a session is waiting, so ⌥J still types in a text field. The capture listener is one function for view and session shortcuts (`lib/window-shortcuts.ts`). Only the selected tile counts as in front of the user for ADR-47, so a session that waits in another tile keeps its warning frame until the user selects it. A terminal in a grid tile reports its size to the PTY like any other window (§5.2), so a session shown in a small tile is resized; switching back to layout 1 resizes it again. Known gap: a tile smaller than the PTY minimum (10 columns, 4 rows; nine tiles in a window about 400 px tall) sends a size the daemon rejects, so the PTY keeps its last size and the terminal shows a fragment of the screen. The layout lives in `App`; the main window loses it when the app closes, and an extra window gets it back at the next start (ADR-49). Not done yet, from the same proposal: opening a main session with all its tasks as a grid, and pinning a session to a panel on the right that stays visible in other views. Clarification (2026-10-25, ADR-55): Alt+J and "Next waiting" also open task proposals, a group of one session being one stop, and a grid tile with proposals shows their prompts with "Start task" and "Reject…" instead of only leading to the full panel. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI giving Alt + digit or Alt+J a meaning (then another modifier, or shortcuts that work only outside the terminal), more than nine sessions being the usual case (then chosen tiles instead of pages), or terminals that need a minimum size the grid cannot give (then a minimum tile width). Clarification (2026-10-25): a grid tile and a list row with a session have an X that closes that session, because in a grid there was no way to close one session without switching to layout `1`, and the list had none at all. One click stops the process group if it still runs and removes the session from the list; while the agent is working or asking for approval of a step or a plan, the X first asks "Stop it and close the session?" in a strip that lies over the top of the terminal (so the PTY is not resized mid-turn) or sits under the row, so a stray click does not cut off a turn, while an idle, waiting or ended session closes at once. A task's X closes only its session: the task and its worktree stay on the list, because ending a task deletes the worktree and keeps its own confirmation in the task panel; a task proposal has no X. A task without a session (its session closed, or after a daemon restart) has an X that ends the task: it opens the same End task question as the task panel (`TaskRemoval`) in the tile or under the row, and the ended task selects its neighbour like a closed session (`selectionAfterRemoval`; clarification 2026-10-25, because a task whose session was closed otherwise stayed on the grid with nothing to close). A press on the X does not take the focus, so closing a tile that is not selected does not select it first; closing the selected session selects its neighbour on the list, so the grid stays on the same page (`selectionAfterClose`, decided with the selection as it is when the stop ends; the Close of an ended session in layout `1` follows the same rule). There is no keyboard shortcut yet, because keys in a terminal go to Copilot CLI. The UI calls `stop` and then `dismiss` instead of a new daemon action, so a window closed during the stop leaves the session stopped on the list, where its X closes it.

**ADR-49 Trackwall opens views in several windows, each remembering its monitor.** (2026-10-24) The desktop shell can open extra windows besides the main one: "Open in new window" in the native context menu of a view icon on the view bar, Ctrl+Shift + the view's digit (Ctrl+Shift+2 opens Repos; ⌥⌘ on macOS, because ⌘⇧3 and ⌘⇧4 are the system's screenshot shortcuts), Ctrl+Shift+N (⌘⇧N) for the current view, and "New window" in the tray. A held key opens one window, not one per autorepeat. Every window is the full UI with the view bar, starts with the view from its address (`?view=`) and switches views on its own, so the session grid can fill a 34-inch ultrawide while Home or Repos sits on the second monitor. Extra windows have stable labels (`window-2`, `window-3`…, the lowest free number), and `tauri-plugin-window-state`, the official Tauri plugin, restores the monitor, position and size of the last window with the label, falling back to the default position when the monitor is gone. Decisions taken with the user: an extra window closes for real and only the main window hides to the tray, so the tray does not collect invisible windows; the same session may be open in two windows, and the window that comes to the front resizes the PTY again, because the PTY has one size and the window the user works in should get it; an extra window keeps the view bar; a session from the tray (or a proposal) opens in the last focused window that shows Sessions, otherwise the last focused window, so a click on "Waiting: <repo>" does not turn the Home window on the other monitor into Sessions. Rationale: the daemon already serves any number of clients (`/ws` broadcasts to every connection, `/ws/pty` takes several windows per session, ADR-47 counts several windows), so a second window needs no daemon change, only the shell. A ready plugin instead of our own position file, because it already handles monitors, maximized and minimized windows and the order of restore and show; it runs in Rust only, and the UI needs no permission beyond `core:default`, which already covers events and the native context menu. The tray's event goes only to the chosen window, so the UI listens on its own window; a global listener would turn every window into Sessions. The window registry (what each window shows, the list of extra windows saved for the next start and the focus order) lives in the shell, because only the shell sees focus across windows and the tray needs it while windows may be hidden. Each window keeps its own UI state (view, session layout, selection) in memory. Clarification (2026-10-24): the next start of the app opens the extra windows that were open when it quit, each under its label (so on its monitor) with its view and session layout from the page address (`?view=…&sessionLayout=…`); the list is the shell's file `windows.json`, because the window state plugin keeps geometry only and the UI's `localStorage` is not readable before a window exists. The main window still starts with Home. The UI served by the daemon in a browser (`--ui`) has no shell and so no extra windows. Clarification (2026-10-25): the desktop shell has a native menu bar on macOS with the views, Settings and a Window menu that lists the open windows, and every window's title names its view; Tauri's default macOS menu had no views and showed every window as "Trackwall". Windows gets no menu bar, since there it would sit inside every window and duplicate the view bar. What would reverse that part: users on Windows asking for a menu bar, or Tauri offering an app-wide menu bar there that does not sit inside every window. What would reverse it: Tauri gaining multi-webview layouts inside one window that make docking easier than separate windows, or one session shown in two windows turning out to need one owner (then the second window would show a placeholder instead of the terminal).

**ADR-50 Waiting sessions reach you outside the window: a system notification, a global shortcut and a tray panel.** (2026-10-24) A session that starts waiting for you and a new task proposal show a system notification while no Trackwall window has the focus, and clicking it opens that session or proposal; Win+Alt+J (⌃⌥J on macOS) from any app opens the next waiting session; a left click on the tray icon opens a small panel with the waiting sessions and proposals (§5.4). Rationale: the sessions work while the user is in another app (the IDE, the browser), and a flashing taskbar button or a bouncing Dock icon is easy to miss and does not say which session waits; the notification says it and leads there in one click, the shortcut does it without the mouse, and the panel shows the whole queue without bringing up the big window. The notification is skipped while a Trackwall window has the focus, because the badges are on screen then. The shell talks to the system itself instead of using `tauri-plugin-notification`: on the desktop the plugin does not report a click, and on Windows it shows toasts only under an AppUserModelID that an installer registered, which the portable build does not have (the reason ADR-26 left toasts out). Registering the AppUserModelID in HKCU with a display name and an icon is the documented way for unpackaged apps and needs no Start menu shortcut; reopening Trackwall from a toast after it has quit would also need a COM activator, which is left out. On macOS `mac-notification-sys` reports the click and works from a background thread, at the cost of a waiting thread per notification, hence the limit of eight. `tauri-plugin-positioner` is not used either: the tray click carries the icon's rectangle, placing the panel is one tested function (`panel_position`), and the plugin computes in physical pixels, which on macOS go wrong across displays of different scale. The shortcut is not Ctrl+Alt+J on Windows, because Ctrl + Alt is AltGr there and a system-wide AltGr+J would stop layouts that type a character with it (ADR-48 keeps AltGr a key); Win + Alt types nothing. On macOS ⌃⌥J types nothing either, while ⌥⌘J opens a browser's console. The panel is a second WebView with the same UI and the same capability as the main window, so it reads the daemon the same way and no new transport is needed; it is created with the app, so a click shows it at once, which costs one more WebView kept in memory. Clarification (2026-10-25, ADR-55): new task proposals are held until the main session stops proposing for about six seconds and then make one notification, and the global shortcut also opens task proposals. What would reverse it: the notification plugin reporting clicks on the desktop and working without an installer (then the plugin replaces `notification.rs`), the shortcut colliding with tools users rely on (then it becomes a setting), or a panel nobody opens (then the left click shows the window again). Clarification (2026-10-25, ADR-66): the panel lists the sessions asking for approval first and answers their approval requests itself; the other entries still open in the main window.

**ADR-51 The projects folder is chosen in the system picker, a repo opens in Explorer or Finder, and dropped files go to the session or the repo list.** (2026-10-24) Settings gets "Choose…" next to the path field in the Tauri window: the app command `pick_projects_folder` (Rust, `tauri-plugin-dialog`) opens the system folder picker and the chosen folder is saved at once. The repo header gets "Show in Explorer" (Finder on macOS): the daemon opens the folder of the listed repo, like "Open in VS Code" (`open-folder`), so it also works in the browser. A file or folder dropped on a session terminal is pasted into it as its path (quoted when it has a space or a quote, with a space after it), and a folder dropped anywhere else goes to `dropped-folder`: a listed repo opens in Repos, and a folder with repos, or the folder that holds a dropped repo, is offered as the projects folder. Reasoning: Trackwall lists the repos of one folder (ADR-18), so a single repo from elsewhere cannot join the list without changing that rule; offering its folder keeps the rule and still makes the drop useful. The picker is an app command rather than the plugin's JavaScript API, so the window's capability stays `core:default` and the page gets no general dialog or file access; Show in Explorer/Finder goes through the daemon rather than `tauri-plugin-opener` because the daemon already opens things in the system from its own configuration (`open-editor`, `open-link`) and the page opened from the daemon gets it too. `clipboard-manager` is not needed: the terminal already copies with `navigator.clipboard`. What would reverse it: a list of repos from several folders (dropping a repo would then add it), or a need for the plugin dialog in the page (file pickers in several views). Clarification (2026-10-27): the terminal no longer copies with `navigator.clipboard` alone. Copilot CLI copies its own selection with OSC 52, and that copy reaches the page over the WebSocket, outside the key handler where WKWebView allows `navigator.clipboard`, so the window copies through the app command `copy_text`, which writes text with `arboard` and cannot read; `arboard` directly rather than `clipboard-manager`, because the plugin brings image decoders and a read command the page does not need.

**ADR-52 `trackwall://` links show a repo, session or task in the window, and only navigate.** (2026-10-24) `trackwall://repo/<repo id>`, `trackwall://session/<session id>` and `trackwall://task/<task id>` open Trackwall at that thing, so an agent's answer or a notification can point the user at it. The MCP instruction tells agents the format (`trackwallLink` in `protocol`), and a link printed in a session terminal opens on Ctrl+click or Cmd+click. The shell takes links with `tauri-plugin-deep-link` and, on Windows, `tauri-plugin-single-instance` with its `deep-link` feature, and turns them into the same jump as the tray (`Jump`, §5.4). A notification shown by the shell should call `jump::request` directly; text for the user and other apps use `trackwallLink`. Jumps go through a slot in the shell that the UI takes with `take_jump`, instead of an event payload, because a link that starts the app arrives before the page listens. Why: the alternative, a URL the daemon serves, would open a browser tab instead of the window and need the token. A link is untrusted, so it is a closed set of kinds; the shell checks the shape and the UI the id, with the protocol schemas as the only definition of an id, and a link can never run anything: a link that ran a task or approved a proposal would let any web page act for the user (§8). The Windows portable build has no installer, so the release shell registers the scheme for the current user at every start; macOS takes it from `Info.plist`. Linux is not registered (no release there). Reverse if: Trackwall gets an installer that registers the scheme (then drop the registration at start), or links need actions (each would need a confirmation in the UI, like a task proposal).

**ADR-53 The app updates itself from GitHub Releases with the Tauri updater and a minisign key; the portable Windows folder is swapped by renames.** (2026-10-24) The UI of the main window asks the shell to check `latest.json` of the latest release (`releases/latest/download/latest.json`) 10 s after start and every 6 h (an extra window, ADR-49, checks only from "Settings > Updates"); when a newer version is there, a strip above the views and "Settings > Updates" offer "Update and restart". The click downloads the update package, the updater plugin (`tauri-plugin-updater`) checks its minisign signature against the public key in `tauri.conf.json` and, with `requireSignedVersion`, that the signature was made for the version the manifest announces, and only then is anything installed. macOS installs through the plugin (`trackwall-<version>-macos-arm64.app.tar.gz`, the bundle swapped in place). On Windows the plugin installs only through NSIS or MSI, and Trackwall ships a folder without an installer (docs/release.md), so the shell installs the release zip itself (`portable_update.rs`): it unpacks it into `<app folder>/.update/new`, moves the entries of the current build (the running `trackwall.exe` and `trackwall-daemon.exe` among them; a file of the user's in the folder stays) to `.update/old` and the new ones into the app folder, and the next start deletes `.update`, unless putting the files back after a failed move failed too and `.update/old` holds the only copy (then the error says how to recover, docs/release.md). Windows lets the exe of a running process be renamed but not overwritten or deleted, and renames inside one folder never cross volumes, so nothing is copied over a file in use; a failed move puts back what has moved. Then the shell restarts through `request_restart`, whose `RunEvent::Exit` stops the old daemon before the new shell starts its own, because a shell refuses a daemon of another version (§5.4). Installing waits for the click because the restart stops services and sessions. The release workflow signs the two update packages with the private key from the repo secrets (`tauri signer sign --app-version`) and writes `latest.json` (`scripts/update-manifest.ts`, in a job without write access, which refuses a signature made with another key than the one in `tauri.conf.json`); `tauri build` itself does not sign (`createUpdaterArtifacts` is off), so CI on a PR needs no key. Rationale: the updater plugin already does the parts that are easy to get wrong (manifest, version comparison, download over HTTPS with the system proxy, signature and the signed version, the bundle swap on macOS), so Trackwall adds only the Windows install step, which the plugin does not have for a portable app. An NSIS installer per user would make the standard updater work on Windows too, but it changes how Trackwall is installed and adds a second artifact to test, and the swap is a few renames tested on every system. The shell's own key rather than code signing: the signature protects the update even before the app has a Windows or Apple certificate, and it would stay after one. `TRACKWALL_UPDATE_ENDPOINT` points the check at another manifest, such as a pre-release's, so that an update can be tried before it is the latest release; its packages still need the release key.
