Would reverse it: a code-signed Windows build with an installer (then the plugin's NSIS path), the plugin gaining a portable install on Windows, a lost or leaked private key (then a new key in `tauri.conf.json`, and installed versions move to it only by a manual install, docs/release.md), or a corporate proxy that the updater cannot pass (then the manual install stays the way, and perhaps a download through the daemon). Clarification (2026-10-26): the install shows its stage, not only a growing number. The first progress used to come after a whole megabyte, so a download held back by a proxy looked like "Installing…" forever: the read timeout of 30 s resets with every few bytes, so a trickling line never fails. The shell now reports each stage and the download by time (every 250 ms) rather than by size, the UI marks a download with no new bytes for 20 s as stalled, and `cancel_update` stops the download (`until_cancelled`, a `tokio::select!` against a `Notify` in the install phase) but not an install already replacing files, which must finish or put the old files back.

**ADR-55 The task proposals of one main session are reviewed together, and a rejection carries a reason.** (2026-10-25) The Sessions view shows the proposals of one session as one entry with one panel: every prompt, readable and editable, with "Start task" for one and "Start N selected" for the ticked ones, started one after another. "Reject…" asks for an optional reason, which the daemon keeps with the rejection and `wait_for_session` returns as `rejectionReason`. After a decision the view opens the next waiting proposal of that session. A grid tile with proposals shows the prompts with "Start task" and "Reject…", "Next waiting" and Alt+J go through proposals too (a group is one stop, while the counts on the button and the view bar count each proposal), and new proposals are announced in one system notification once the main session stops proposing for a few seconds. Rationale: a main session usually proposes a plan of several tasks at once, and the old flow (one list entry and one panel per proposal, Home and the tray only leading to it, a dead grid tile, Alt+J skipping proposals while the view bar counted them, "Reject" with no way to say why) made the user hunt for each proposal and left the main session guessing after a rejection. Considered and left out: approving from Home and the tray panel (the prompt comes from an agent and is to be read before it runs, ADR-39, and there is no room to read it there; they keep leading to the panel), and a drawer next to the main session's terminal instead of list entries (better context, but proposals from an agent outside Trackwall have no session to sit next to, and it reshapes the list and the grid more). Nothing is ticked at first and a proposal can be ticked only once it has been opened, so starting several still takes a look at each; starts go one at a time because each runs `git worktree add` and starts a Copilot CLI session, which on a slow Windows machine is felt, and worktree commands on one repo wait for git's lock anyway. While a decision runs the group stays selected by its proposer, and once its last proposal has become a task, the newest task from that proposer stands in for it until the decision moves on. What would reverse it: users mostly approving single proposals (then the per-proposal entries were simpler), a main session that needs an answer richer than a reason (then a reply that reaches its terminal), or plans larger than one panel can show (then a dedicated view).

**ADR-56 Features are documented on a VitePress site in `apps/docs`, published on GitHub Pages.** (2026-10-25) The single `docs/features.md` grew into one long page with a paragraph per feature, which was hard to browse and to link to. The feature documentation moves to `apps/docs`, a VitePress site with one page per feature (`features/`) and a short guide (`guide/`: what Trackwall is, installation, connecting Copilot CLI), and `docs/features.md` is removed so that a feature is described in one place. The site is Markdown in the repo, so a PR that changes a feature changes its page, and the reviewer sees both. It wears the app's look on the VitePress default theme: the dark theme takes the app's tokens (ADR-16, a test keeps the copies equal), the HUD shows only in small labels and the screenshot frame, and a light theme is offered too. `.github/workflows/pages.yml` publishes it at `https://jakubprill.github.io/trackwall/` after a push to `main` that changes it or its address (`TRACKWALL_DOCS_URL` in `protocol`); the `check` job in CI builds it on every PR, so a broken link between its pages fails CI (links to GitHub are not checked). Turning Pages on (Settings → Pages → Source: GitHub Actions) is a repo setting the owner makes. VitePress 1.6 asks for Vite 5, whose dev server has known vulnerabilities fixed only in 6.4.4+, so `pnpm-workspace.yaml` overrides VitePress's Vite to 6.4.5 (the site builds and `vitepress dev` serves with it); moving to VitePress 2 once it is stable makes the override unnecessary. Considered: WordPress (cannot be served by GitHub Pages and keeps the text outside the repo), Starlight (Astro, another framework next to Vite) and MkDocs Material (needs Python in CI, against "scripts in TypeScript"). Architecture, ADRs, development and release documents stay in `docs/`, for people who work on Trackwall. Would be reversed by: a need for content the default theme cannot carry well (interactive demos, versioned docs), or the site moving out of the repo. Addendum (2026-10-25): the pages moved over from `features.md` read like a specification, with ADR numbers, so they are rewritten for people using Trackwall, to be read on their own or in a technical meeting. Every feature page follows `apps/docs/TEMPLATE.md` (a one-sentence description, then "What you get", "How it works", "Use it", optional reference tables, "Good to know") and cites no ADRs; the why stays here, and `apps/docs/test/pages.test.ts` holds the pages to the template. "All features" lists every feature with its description, grouped like the sidebar (`.vitepress/sidebar.ts`), so it cannot miss a page. For AI tools the site also publishes `llms.txt` (the pages with their descriptions), `llms-full.txt` (every page in one file) and a Markdown copy of each page with a "Copy page" button, from `vitepress-plugin-llms` (MIT); `@types/markdown-it` is pinned to 14.1.2, the types VitePress 1.6 bundles, so the plugin's markdown hook type-checks. The plugin types itself against Vite without declaring it, so `packageExtensions` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` gives it VitePress's Vite 6.4.5 instead of whichever Vite pnpm hoists. Addendum (2026-10-25): the feature pages show screenshots of a made-up shop of twenty repos (`packages/daemon/scripts/demo.ts`, with a stand-in for Copilot CLI), taken by `apps/docs/scripts/screenshots.ts` with Playwright, so the pictures are reproducible, show a busy workspace and never come from someone's real repos. The demo builds its daemon from the same pieces as `main.ts` (`selfCommand` always points at `src/main.ts`, and `infra/shutdown.ts` holds the stop sequence), and the script stops it through `POST /api/daemon/shutdown`, so on Windows too it stops its services and sessions itself. The picture of two windows is composed from two screenshots, not taken from the desktop shell; `packages/daemon/test/demo-workspace.test.ts` checks that the demo's graph shows every call between its services. "All features" is drawn by Vue from the sidebar, so its Markdown source holds no list: it is left out of the Markdown copies, and `llms.txt` lists the features anyway. The site links to no document in `docs/`: the installation steps for users move from `docs/release.md` to the site's "Install" page, and `docs/release.md` and `README.md` point there.

**ADR-58 One supervisor session runs in the projects folder above all sessions, in a panel on the right of every view.** (2026-10-25) Trackwall gets a dedicated supervisor: Copilot CLI in the projects folder, not in a repo (`Session.repo: null`), at most one at a time, started with "Start supervisor" in a panel that is pinned on the right of every view of the window, with Alt+0 to reach it. Its proposals carry `ProposedBy.supervisor`, and the Sessions list shows its work under a "Supervisor" heading at the top, so the hierarchy is supervisor → the tasks it delegated → the work those delegated further. Until now any session that proposed a task became a main session (ADR-39), so there was no one place to talk to: a main session sat in one repo with that repo's context, and the user would have had to pick one of several sessions as "the" main one. A supervisor in the projects folder sees all repos by where it runs, and one fixed place for it lets the UI keep it in view while the user works in other views, which a list entry in Sessions cannot. It gets no new rights: it lists and waits for sessions like any session, every proposal still waits for the user (ADR-9, ADR-39), it does not type into other sessions (since ADR-68 it sends task messages to the tasks a supervisor proposed, read through a tool) and changes no configuration (§8); its session instructions tell it to coordinate rather than change code. Other sessions keep proposing their own work, which stays under them. Marking the supervisor's work on the proposal and the task, rather than finding it through the proposing session's id, keeps the hierarchy after the supervisor is replaced or the daemon restarts. The supervisor is left out of the Sessions counter, because its panel shows when it waits, and Alt+J reaches it only after the list entries; the tray and notifications still name it. What would reverse it: needing several supervisors at once (one per workspace or per goal), which would make the supervisor a kind of list entry with its own selection again, or the supervisor needing to act without approval, which ADR-9 rules out. Clarification (2026-10-26): the pinned panel is 40rem wide (about 80 terminal columns) but at most 36 % of the window, so on a 1280-pixel window (1920×1080 at 150 %) a task panel beside it keeps about 480 pixels and its header's buttons wrap under the task, instead of a 300-pixel column with overlapping buttons. An overlay over the view in a narrow window was rejected: a pinned panel would then cover the task terminal whose approval the user is answering. Clarification (2026-10-26, ADR-78): "coordinate rather than change code" became a boundary: the supervisor starts without Copilot CLI's shell and file-writing tools unless the user sets `supervisor.changesCode`.

**ADR-59 A task session builds and tests in its own worktree: mise tasks take a `task`, and the worktree's mise config is trusted per run while it is the one the user trusted.** (2026-10-25) `run_mise_task`, `stop_mise_task`, `wait_for_mise_task`, `tail` and `get_errors` take an optional `task`, and `list_running_mise_tasks` shows it: with it, the mise task runs in the task's worktree (owner `task-mise:<task>:<repo>:<mise task>`, so a run is reached only through its task's repo; log source `task-mise-task`), without it in the repo root as before (§4.7). A task session may pass only its own task. Rationale: a task session changes code in its worktree, while ADR-41 ran its tests in the repo root, on another branch, so the loop "change, test, read the result" tested the wrong code, and the agent fell back to its own shell, where Trackwall sees neither the run nor its log. Trust: mise does not load a config it has not trusted, and `mise trust` on a worktree would trust whatever the agent writes there later. Instead the daemon hashes the worktree's mise files (config files, `.tool-versions`, file tasks) when it creates the task, keeps the snapshot in `tasks.json`, and for each run passes the worktree in `MISE_TRUSTED_CONFIG_PATHS`, only if the repo root's config is trusted and the files still match the snapshot. When the agent changed them, the run is refused with the list of files and the task panel shows them with their text and "Trust this config", which sends the digest of the version on screen (the panel shows a newer one only when the user asks), so a newer change is refused rather than trusted unseen. File names count without regard to case and a symbolic link counts by its target, only inside the worktree, because mise finds `Mise.local.toml` on Windows and macOS and follows links. A task created before this has no snapshot and needs one click. The daemon never runs `mise trust` for a worktree, so nothing outlives the run in mise's own state. Considered: trusting the worktree with `mise trust` on creation (simple, but trusts every later change by the agent), refusing any worktree whose config differs from the base commit (blocks a task whose job is to change the mise config), and a copy of the root's trust by `git diff` (misses `mise.local.toml`, which git ignores). Knowingly not covered (§8): the code the mise task runs, tests and build scripts, is the agent's work anyway, so this gate keeps the command line consented, not the build. A task cannot be ended while a mise task runs in its worktree, since on Windows the running files lock the worktree. A service from a worktree (`start_service`, ADR-45) is left out: two `dev` runs of one repo collide on ports, which needs leases. What would reverse it: mise offering a per-run trust of its own (then that instead of the env variable), agents that mostly change the mise config (then a lighter review than one click), or leases (then services from a worktree too). Clarification (2026-10-25): services from a worktree came with ADR-61, on a task port next to the root's service rather than through leases.

**ADR-60 Test results for an agent from the JUnit XML reports on disk, a module that reads them on request.** (2026-10-25) An agent that ran `mise run test` learned what failed only from the log, through `tail` and `get_errors`: a Gradle or Maven log of thousands of lines where the failed test, its assertion and its cause are scattered. The new `test-results` module (§4.9) and the MCP tool `get_test_results {repo, task?, since?}` read the JUnit XML reports the build tools write anyway (Surefire, Failsafe, Gradle, Ant, jest-junit, Vitest) in the repo root or a task's worktree, and give totals, the reports with their times and the failed tests with type, message and a trimmed stack trace. Reasoning: JUnit XML is the one format every build tool on the stack writes without configuration, so no parser per tool and no guessing from log text; reading on request instead of after every run keeps the module free of state and also covers a run the agent started in its own terminal or the user in the IDE; the reports' modification time with `since` handles stale reports (a report stays until its class runs again) without tracking runs; known locations and skipping `src`, `node_modules` and compiled output keep the walk short in a large multi-module repo. A separate module rather than part of `mise`, because the reports do not belong to a mise run, and rather than part of `logs`, because they are files, not a stream; the XML parser of the card moved to `src/infra/xml.ts` and gained attributes, so both modules share it. Clarification (2026-10-25, review): every `<testcase>` counts, also a repeated title, because merging same-titled cases as retries hid real jest failures; and reports older than `since` are dropped during the walk, before its report limit, and counted as `earlierReports`. Knowingly outside this step: test reports in the UI, other formats (TRX of .NET, TAP, Go's `test2json`), coverage, history across runs, flaky-test detection (a Surefire test that passed on a rerun, with only `flakyFailure` children, counts as passed). What would reverse it: a build tool on the stack that writes no JUnit report by default (then its format or a log parser for it), or reports so large that reading them on request is too slow (then reading after a run's end, kept in memory).

**ADR-61 A task worktree is ready to run: ignored files from the repo root named in `.worktreeinclude`, a port of its own for its mise tasks, and its service next to the root's.** (2026-10-25) A new task copies into its worktree the files the repo root's `.worktreeinclude` names that git ignores (local config, `.env`, `mise.local.toml`), gets the lowest free port of 41000–41999 for as long as it exists, and every mise task run in its worktree gets that port in `SERVER_PORT` and `PORT`. `start_service`, `stop_service` and `call_endpoint` take an optional `task`: the service runs from the task's worktree on the task port, next to the same service from the repo root, while its dependencies run from their roots. Reasoning: since ADR-59 a task's agent builds and tests its own code, but could not run it: a worktree is a checkout of committed files, so a service that reads local config did not start there, and a second `dev` of a repo took the root's port. A comparison with Conductor and Claude Code, which run parallel agents in worktrees, showed the same three answers there (a list of files to copy, a setup step, a port range per workspace); the format of the list is Claude Code's, so a repo needs one file for both, and the file may stay untracked, so the user does not have to commit it to a shared repo. Only ignored files are copied, so the list cannot overwrite tracked files or take files git would commit. A port per task instead of the lease of §4.3 (stop the root's service, start the task's on its port): parallel tasks of one repo can each run their service, and the user's own instance keeps running; the cost is that callers in the slice still call the root's instance, so a task reaches its service directly through `call_endpoint`. `SERVER_PORT` and `PORT` cover Spring Boot and most Node servers without changes to the repo; a fixed range keeps a task's port stable across restarts and readable. Considered: running a setup mise task automatically after the worktree is created (left out: the agent can run `setup` itself with `run_mise_task` and its task, and a setup that starts containers should not start unasked), copying the whole root's ignored files (would copy `node_modules` and build output), a free port from the system (changes on every run and can be reused by outgoing connections), a port only for the service rather than every mise task (tests with a defined port would still collide). Only ignored files in both the root and the worktree are copied, since the worktree has the base commit's ignore rules and index, not the root's. Knowingly not covered: a repo whose mise `[env]` sets `PORT` itself (mise's own environment wins, so its service keeps its fixed port), rerouting callers to the task's service (leases), ports other than the HTTP one (management, debug), a setup step, `.worktreeinclude` patterns that cross into an ignored directory as Claude Code handles them (git's own pattern matching decides). What would reverse it: services that do not read `SERVER_PORT` or `PORT` on the stack (then a port named in the workspace config per repo), a need for end-to-end runs through the task's service (then leases on top of the task port), or many tasks whose repos need a slow setup (then a setup step after creation).

**ADR-62 A review of a task's changes goes to its agent as comments it reads with an MCP tool, after a fixed prompt the daemon types when the session waits for input.** (2026-10-25) In the task's "Changes" tab the user comments on lines of the diff and sends the comments as one review; the daemon queues them per task, types the one-line prompt "call `get_review_comments` and address every comment" into the task's session when it waits in its input box after a hook (at once, after the current turn, or in the session the user resumes; one prompt at a time), and the tool returns the unread comments with file, side, line number and the line's code. Reasoning: the comparison with Conductor and Vibe Kanban (2026-10-25) showed the loop review → fix as the weak spot of Trackwall's tasks: the user saw the diff but had to retype the remarks into the terminal with file names and lines. Comments are collected and sent as one review, as on GitHub, rather than one by one, because every message to the agent starts a turn, and a review read at once lets the agent plan the fixes together. Typing the comments themselves into the PTY was rejected: Copilot CLI's input box treats `@` (the file picker, common in Java annotations), a leading `/` or `!` and line breaks specially, and keystrokes landing in the middle of a turn or on an approval prompt would answer it; a fixed prompt typed only in `Input`, with the content through the tool, keeps the user's text out of the terminal and lets the agent get structured data. Also considered: writing the comments to a file in the worktree (the agent would commit it, and the diff would show it), a file in the session directory (outside the worktree, so Copilot CLI asks for permission to read it), only the tool without the typed prompt (an agent at rest does not poll), MCP notifications (Copilot CLI does not act on them). The comments live in memory like task proposals: a review is minutes old, and a daemon restart while one is unread costs the user a resend. Knowingly not covered: comments on a file without a changed line (the agent gets the whole review's context through `get_task_changes`), the comments sent (the UI shows them only until they are sent), comments the agent's cancelled `get_review_comments` call took (they count as read once returned), a review after an Esc or while the user has unsent text in the input box (it waits for the end of the next turn or the user's next message), a review sent while the task is being ended (it is dropped with the task), and a retyped prompt after one the TUI took as a paste (the first copy may still be in the input box), threads and replies from the agent on a comment (the agent answers in its turn and in `report_result`), keeping comments across a daemon restart, a key to clear text the user is typing in the session at the same moment. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI gaining a way to send a message to a session (an SDK or a socket), which would replace the typed prompt, or users wanting comment threads, which would make reviews a stored part of the task. Clarification (2026-10-25): `get_review_comments` is read-only to MCP, although it marks the comments read, because the contract test showed that the real Copilot CLI asks the user to approve every MCP tool that is not read-only, so the task agent asked to read a review waited for that approval (as with `get_messages` and `get_updates`, ADR-68). Clarification (2026-10-25, ADR-69): Copilot CLI sends no hook before the first prompt, so a session the user has just resumed is in its input box when its screen shows it, and the review prompt is typed there without waiting for the user's first message.

**ADR-63 The daemon watches every repo's HEAD and refs by comparing `stat` results every second, and warms up missing cards in the background; the UI fetches commit-dependent data on the `repos.changed` event.** (2026-10-25) With a projects folder of many repos the first graph, the first `list_repos` and the first `get_repo_card` waited for the cards of every repo (a few `git` processes and parsing each, slow on Windows with an antivirus), and the UI kept the daemon busy without a reason: the list every 10 s (`git status` of every repo), tags every minute and the graph every 30 s (two `git` processes per repo), while a commit still reached the screen only at the next poll. Now the repo watch (§4.5) compares the set of repos and the HEAD and refs of each from the files of its git directory every second, bumps a revision on a change (`repos.changed` on `/ws`, a topic without an HTTP action) and hands the new repos and those whose HEAD moved to the card warm-up (§4.6), which computes them two at once, the most recently used first (`logs/HEAD`); a request does not wait for the queue, the card cache gives both the same job. The UI fetches the list, tags and the graph on a new revision and polls them only as a safety net (the list every 30 s for uncommitted changes, which the watch does not see on purpose; tags and the graph every 5 minutes for hand edits of `workspace.yaml`), and as before when the event connection is down. `stat` instead of `fs.watch`: on Windows a watch handle on `.git` leaves a deleted `.git` pending and fails the deletion of the repo folder in Explorer; a recursive watch of the projects folder would bring every write in `node_modules` and `build` and overflow its buffer during a build; change notifications do not work on network drives, where the projects folder sometimes lies; a recursive watch on Linux is one inotify watch per directory. A few `stat` calls per repo per second do not start processes or open files, behave the same under Bun and Node and on all three systems, and the file of the checked-out branch is compared by identity, so a commit is seen even when the directory's time does not change within the timestamp granularity. Uncommitted changes are not watched, because that needs the working directory (rejected above) or `git status` (the polling this replaces). The graph does not come in parts during the warm-up, because a missing repo would look like a repo nobody calls. The first graph right after a start still waits for every card (the warm-up only makes it likely that they are computed already), and the list still polls `git status`, every 30 s instead of every 10 s, so the polling is reduced, not gone. Known limits: card parsing still runs on the daemon's main thread (§8), and now also without a request, after the start and after a commit; the file identity on Windows depends on the runtime giving a stable `ino` (otherwise time and size remain, and the directory times of `refs/`); a hand edit of `workspace.yaml`, which no event reports, reaches the graph after up to 5 minutes or the refresh button. What would reverse it: a projects folder so large that a second of `stat` calls becomes noticeable (then a longer interval or native notifications per repo), parsing in a worker thread (then the warm-up can be wider), or a need to see uncommitted changes live (a watch of selected working directories).

**ADR-64 A task's verification: one button and one MCP tool run the mise task `test` in its worktree and give one verdict from the exit code, the JUnit reports and the log errors.** (2026-10-25) "Verify" in the task panel and `verify_task {repo, task}` start the run (or join the running one), and once the group has ended the `verification` module (§4.10) sums up the exit code, the reports the run wrote (`get_test_results` with `since` at the run's start, ADR-60) and the errors of that run's log (`get_errors` by `runId`, ADR-42) into `passed`, `failed` or `cancelled` with the first failed tests and errors, kept in memory per task and shown in the panel; `get_session` returns it to the supervisor, and a task session is told to verify before it reports. Reasoning: the comparison with Devin, Factory and Conductor (2026-10-25) showed a check step after the agent's work as the missing piece between "the agent says it is done" and the user's review; all its parts were in place (task worktrees with trusted mise config, ADR-59; JUnit reports, ADR-60; log errors, ADR-42), but the user had to read three places and the agent had to chain four tools. One verdict that both read means the user sees what the agent saw. A module of its own rather than part of `mise` or `test-results`, because it composes three modules and holds state of its own, as `batch` does. The mise task `test` by convention, like `setup`, `build` and `dev`, rather than a setting, because every repo on the stack has it or can add it in one line; a pass on exit code 0 with no failed test rather than on the exit code alone, because some builds ignore test failures. Considered: running `build` and `test` as two steps (`test` in Maven and Gradle compiles first, and a repo whose `test` does not can depend on `build` in mise), starting the task's service and probing it (slow, and a service is not a check of the changes; `start_service` stays a separate step), storing the verdict in `tasks.json` (a verdict after a restart may be about code since changed, so a stale green would mislead), auto-verifying after every commit (the agent's turns would race the runs, and nobody asked for the cost), and a separate wait tool (`verify_task` called again joins the run, as `wait_for_mise_task` does). Knowingly not covered: marking a verdict outdated after later edits, a verdict on the Sessions list, another mise task per repo, verifying the repo root, merging or MR creation on a pass. What would reverse it: repos where `test` is not the check the user wants (then a mise task named per repo in `workspace.yaml`), or verdicts users trust after the code moved on (then a fingerprint of the worktree with the verdict).

**ADR-65 Trackwall starts at sign-in hidden in the tray, shows the number waiting on its icons, and zooms each window.** (2026-10-25) The daemon is useful before the user opens a window (repo watch, card warm-up, notifications), and on a work machine Trackwall is the first thing opened every morning, so Settings gets "Start Trackwall when I sign in": the shell writes the system's start entry for the current user and a start from it keeps every window hidden until the user opens Trackwall, which then restores the extra windows. The `auto-launch` crate directly, not `tauri-plugin-autostart`: the plugin builds the same crate without quotes around the Windows path (ambiguous for a folder with a space), writes for all users when the app runs elevated, and registers JavaScript commands that the UI does not need; the system's entry is the only record, so Settings shows what the system will do. The taskbar flash and Dock bounce last a moment, so the number of waiting sessions and task proposals stays on the icons: a Dock badge, a taskbar overlay the shell draws itself (Windows takes only an image; a 3×5 pixel font draws 1 to 9 and "9+" without a font file or an image crate), and a dot on the tray icon. Zoom per window because one window sits on a 34-inch ultrawide and another on a laptop; the shell keeps the level, because a web view cannot report its own zoom and Tauri's zoom hotkeys would not remember it. Reversed if the start entry proves blocked by policy on managed machines (then a scheduled task, or a note in the docs), if Tauri gains a per-window zoom that persists, or if the number on the icons turns out to be noise next to the notifications (then only the tray dot stays).

**ADR-66 Approvals answered outside the terminal: the daemon reads the agent dialog from the session's screen and types the chosen key; tool permission rules go to Copilot CLI as `--allow-tool` and `--deny-tool`.** (2026-10-25) With several sessions most of the waiting is approval requests, and answering one meant finding its terminal to press "1". Now the tray panel and Home show the request with Copilot CLI's options, and a click types the option's digit (or Esc) into the session, once the daemon has checked the screen still shows that request (§4.4). Rules in `workspace.yaml` (`toolPermissions`) give every new session Copilot CLI's own `--allow-tool` and `--deny-tool`, so a command allowed for a repo is not asked about in any of its sessions or task worktrees. Reasoning: the first plan was a `permissionRequest` (or `preToolUse`) hook that waits for the daemon's decision and falls back to the terminal on a timeout. Copilot CLI 1.0.103, driven in tmux with a stand-in model (`COPILOT_OFFLINE` with `COPILOT_PROVIDER_BASE_URL` on a local fake of the chat completions API, so no account), showed why not: the hook gets `toolName`, `toolInput` and `permissionSuggestions` and decides with `{"behavior": "allow"}` or `{"behavior": "deny", "message": …}` (`permissionDecision` is ignored), but the terminal shows the approval request only after the hook has returned, so a hook that waits for the user's click freezes the terminal for as long; a hook that times out falls back to the request, but one that exits with an error or prints anything that is not JSON denies the tool, and the hook is the daemon binary, which on the slow test machine sometimes needs over 5 s to start; and `permissionRequest` fires also when `--allow-tool` approves anyway. Typing the key keeps the terminal and the panel live at once, adds no hook that can deny tools, and uses Copilot CLI's options as they are, including "Yes, and don't ask again", which Copilot CLI keeps per folder in `~/.copilot/permissions-config.json` (so per task worktree; rules cover every worktree of a repo). The price is reading Copilot CLI's layout from the screen: a changed layout means no buttons until the reader follows it, never a wrong answer, because the daemon types only a digit it read as an option, and only into the dialog the user saw. Esc for "Stop the agent" instead of "No, and tell Copilot…": that option takes the text in the terminal (the digit only moves the selection there), so it stays in the terminal. Questions from the agent (`ask_user`, `elicitation_dialog`, now seen on the binary, so `high` confidence) are a form, shown with "Answer in the session". The id counts the wait as well as the text, since an agent repeating a command asks the identical question again. The Sessions view gets no card: its selected tile is the terminal with the dialog in it, and a card on every tile would repeat it. No one-click answer in a system notification (rejected with the Tauri features): a notification shows no command, and approving what one has not read is the habit this should not teach. Rules by hand in `workspace.yaml` like `repoTags`; a button that writes a rule from the panel waits until the hand-written rules prove their shape. What would reverse it: a Copilot CLI whose approval request appears while a `permissionRequest` hook runs and which treats a failed hook as "ask" (then the hook, with structured requests and no screen reading), the ACP mode (ADR-17) becoming the session view, or a layout change per release that keeps the reader behind.

**ADR-67 Contract tests with the real Copilot CLI in BYOK mode against a scripted model, in CI on every PR.** (2026-10-25) Feature tests keep the fake Copilot CLI (ADR-26), because it is fast, deterministic and can produce cases a live session hardly gives (a crashing hook, a long command). What the fake assumes about Copilot CLI (hook events and payload fields, the approval request, flags, MCP, custom instructions) is checked by contract tests in `packages/daemon/contract/`: the daemon starts the real native Copilot CLI of the version pinned in `scripts/copilot-cli.ts`, with the session plugin, the MCP stdio bridge and the session instructions as `main.ts` wires them, and Copilot CLI runs in offline BYOK mode (`COPILOT_OFFLINE`, `COPILOT_PROVIDER_BASE_URL`) against `contract/support/scripted-model.ts`, a chat-completions endpoint on 127.0.0.1 with replies queued by the test. No account, secret or model cost is needed, so the job runs on Linux, Windows and macOS in every PR and is part of `CI ok`; the binary comes from the npm registry, checked against the registry's sha512 and cached by version. Why: stable Copilot CLI versions come out almost every working day (1.0.93 to 1.0.103 within 15 days), and a fake written from memory stays green when a version changes a payload field or the approval window, while the app shows "state unknown"; the first run already found that `sessionStart` comes only with the first prompt (clarification in ADR-17). The tests are not in `pnpm test` (`*.contract.ts`, run by `pnpm test:contract`), so a checkout without network can still run the rest. A Copilot CLI upgrade is a PR that changes the version and passes the contract tests, updating the fake in the same PR. The version is pinned in that script, not in `mise.toml`: mise would install the npm wrapper for everyone working on Trackwall and put it on their `PATH` in the repo, while the tests need only the native binary in a cache. A real model with an account stays out of CI (a personal secret in a public repo, cost, non-deterministic answers). What would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping BYOK or offline mode (then a recorded replay of the Copilot API), or the job turning flaky on a system for reasons outside our code (then that system runs it nightly instead of on every PR). Clarification (2026-10-26): the fake no longer describes Copilot CLI from memory but replays the Copilot CLI reference of its system (hooks and their payloads, window titles, the input box, the approval dialog), which the contract tests take with `TRACKWALL_RECORD=1` and compare a live session with on every run; a reference of another version fails `copilot-reference.test.ts`. References are structural, not TUI screenshots: only the bottom lines the daemon reads, paths replaced by `/projects`. Clarification (2026-10-26): users update Copilot CLI on their own, usually ahead of the pinned version, so `.github/workflows/nightly.yml` runs the contract tests every night on the newest stable version on npm, resolved once for Linux, Windows and macOS and passed as `TRACKWALL_COPILOT_CLI` (an exact version; `scripts/copilot-cli.ts` refuses a dist-tag or a range, since the value goes into the registry URL and the cache path). It is not part of `CI ok`, because a Copilot CLI release must not turn every PR red for a change nobody made in the repo; instead a failure opens one issue per version (a later failing night comments on it) and a green night on the newest version closes the open ones, so the signal is not lost in a failed scheduled run that only its last editor gets an email about. The failed jobs print the reference they saw, which a PR raising the pin starts from. Only a run of `main` reports; a run that cannot resolve the version only fails. What would reverse the reporting: issues that are mostly noise from a flaky system (then a retry before reporting). Clarification (2026-10-26): since ADR-88 and ADR-89 the session reaches the daemon over HTTP, hooks and MCP alike, instead of the bridge and hook processes named here.

**ADR-68 A task's proposer sends its session messages and hears about its work through updates, both read with MCP tools after a fixed prompt the daemon types.** (2026-10-25) `send_message {task, text}` queues a task message for the session of a task the caller proposed, which reads it with `get_messages`; the daemon queues a task update for the proposer when the task's session ends a turn it worked on, errs or exits, reports its result, gets a verification verdict, or when the user rejects one of the proposer's proposals, and the proposer reads them with `get_updates`. Either way the daemon types the one-line prompt naming the tool into the session's input box, with ADR-62's rules (only in `Input` after a hook, never into the user's text), so the supervisor or a main session wakes up when its work moves instead of polling (§4.4). Reasoning: with the supervisor (ADR-58) the user had one place to plan, but the supervisor could only watch: it had to sit in `wait_for_session` calls inside a turn (each call another model step, and a turn the user cannot use meanwhile) or be asked by the user, and it could not pass a correction to a task without the user retyping it in the task's terminal. Both directions reuse what ADR-62 proved: Copilot CLI's input box mangles `@`, a leading `/` or `!` and line breaks, and keys landing mid-turn answer dialogs, so the text goes through a tool and only a fixed prompt is typed; the prompt is read when it is typed and names every tool with something unread, so a review, a message and an update share one prompt instead of racing for the input box. The reading tools `get_messages` and `get_updates` are read-only to MCP (marking read changes only Trackwall's own state): the contract test with the real Copilot CLI showed that it asks the user to approve any other tool, so an agent the daemon woke would stop at an approval and the supervisor could not work while the user is away. `send_message` keeps the approval, so the user sees what one agent tells another unless they allow it for the session. Where the hooks and the screen disagree (the agent's question still on the screen after the hooks put the session in its input box), the dialog reader of ADR-66 holds the prompt back, so it never answers the question. Who may send: only the task's proposer, by the session token, and for the supervisor's work any supervisor (the mark of ADR-58 already survives a replaced supervisor). Writing to any session was rejected: a session the user started (or a task they typed) is the user's conversation, and a message there would steer work no agent was asked to coordinate; a task another session delegated belongs to that session's plan. The cap of 5 messages per task until the user takes part (typing into either session, or a review) bounds two agents keeping each other busy, since each message starts a turn whose end wakes the proposer; the user typing in the supervisor counts, so a user who talks to the supervisor refills it; an answer to an agent's question does not, since it leaves no text in the input box and is told apart from it only by the screen. What wakes the proposer: the end of a turn the agent worked on (its `agentStop` hook, not a change of state, since a question dialog and an Esc give `Input` mid-turn; the task may be done or asking, the summary says which), an error, the end of the process, a summary, a verdict, a rejection. Not an approval request or a stale session: the supervisor cannot answer an approval (the user does, ADR-66) and a stale agent is often a long build; and not a turn the user drove by typing in the task's terminal, because the user is handling that task then and every turn would otherwise wake the supervisor too. Considered: messages typed as keys (rejected above and in ADR-62), MCP notifications or sampling (Copilot CLI does not act on them, ADR-62), a planner that decides on its own (ADR-9 rules it out; every proposal still waits for the user), keeping messages and updates in a file (a daemon restart ends every session that would read them, as for proposals), and an update for every state change (noise that costs a model turn each). Knowingly not covered: a message to a task the caller's task delegated further (only the direct proposer writes), a task asking its proposer a question through a tool (it reports with `report_result`, which reaches the proposer as an update), an approval waiting in a task (the user sees it in the tray panel), the user's text typed during a turn and sent only after it (that turn counts as the user's), a task a resumed proposer proposed in its earlier session (its key is the earlier session id, so the updates stop when the user closes that session), updates and messages for an agent outside Trackwall, and messages or updates across a daemon restart. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI gaining a way to deliver a message to a session (an SDK or a socket), which would replace the typed prompt; agents that keep each other busy within the cap (then a lower cap, or a per-message approval in the UI); or users wanting to steer delegated work only themselves (then `send_message` goes and the updates stay). Clarification (2026-10-26): on a real Windows machine a task's agent twice refused a rename its supervisor asked for, because it took the task's prompt, which Copilot CLI shows as the first user message, for the user's word, and its instructions said to follow messages "unless they contradict the user". A message is now an update of the task: the task's session instructions and the description of `get_messages` say that the proposer drafted the prompt (the user read and approved it), so where a message and the prompt differ the message wins, and only what the user typed into the task's session or sent as a review ranks above it (`TASK_MESSAGE_RULE` in `protocol`, one text for both); the prompt the daemon types asks the agent to follow the message as an update of its task. The agent cannot tell the user's edits of a proposal's prompt from the proposer's draft, so a message wins over those too; the user sees every message in the task's panel and can correct the agent there in its terminal. The bounds of §8 stay: only the proposer sends messages, and the user sees each one in the task's panel. That an agent follows the rule is a model's judgement no test can show; the unit tests check the text and the contract test that it is in the agent's instructions when it reads a message. Clarification (2026-10-26): on a real machine a message to an idle task did not arrive until the user clicked into it, and a task's result did not wake the supervisor. The typed prompt had no delivery check: after the first hook the daemon trusted `agentStop` alone, but Copilot CLI sends it before it draws the input box again (the contract test of a review sent mid-turn shows the daemon typing while the screen still shows `Working`), so the prompt could be lost; a lost prompt counted as typed and was tried again only when another request came; and the user's draft blocked every prompt until a `userPromptSubmitted` that Ctrl+C, a slash command or text taken into a running turn never send. Now the screen must show the empty box too, the read cycle presses Enter again or types the prompt again when it was not taken after 5 s (at most twice), and the draft counts as gone once the box has stayed empty for 2 s. Delivering at a turn's end and mid-turn through hook output instead of typing followed in ADR-85.

**ADR-69 A new session's state and input box come from its screen until the first hook.** (2026-10-25) Copilot CLI 1.0.103 sends its first hook, `sessionStart`, only together with the first prompt (ADR-17, found by the contract tests of ADR-67), so a new session or a resumed task session had no state ("state unknown") until the user wrote to it, and the daemon never typed into it: a review sent to a task whose session the user had just resumed waited until the user's own first message (ADR-62). Until a session has a state, the daemon reads its screen image for Copilot CLI's input box over the footer with hints (`copilot-prompt-box.ts`): that gives `Input` from the `screen` source with `medium` confidence, waiting for you like the end of a turn, and counts as being in the input box. The footer decides, not the box alone, because Copilot CLI draws the box already while it is still loading (`● Loading: …`). A session started with a prompt (`--interactive=`, a new task) is left out, because its first `sessionStart` is on its way. A first `sessionStart` without a known prompt (the user's own first prompt, or a typed one older than 5 s) still gives `Input`, because whether `/resume` or `/clear` as the first command sends it is not checked, and `Working` with no end of turn coming would be worse than a moment of `Input`. Once any hook gives a state, the screen is not read for this any more, and the hooks rank above it anyway (§4.4). The daemon types its prompt (ADR-62) before the first hook only while the screen shows the empty box at that moment, not because it showed it once: in a session whose hooks do not work the state stays `Input` from the screen, and a later approval dialog must not get the prompt's Enter. For up to 5 s after typing such a prompt the box does not give `Input` and nothing is typed again, so the session does not wait for you while its agent is about to read the review, and that prompt's `sessionStart` gives `Working`; a prompt not taken by then lets the box speak again (a box still holding its text is waiting for you, but takes no other prompt). The fake Copilot CLI of the tests behaves the same way, and the contract tests check both the state and a review typed into a resumed session on the real binary. Rejected: `Input` from the start of the process without looking at the screen (a prompt typed while Copilot CLI is still loading or asking for folder trust could be lost or answer the trust question), and a timer after the start (Copilot CLI starts in 1–2 s on Linux and far slower on a cold Windows machine). Knowingly not covered: Copilot CLI that is not signed in shows the same box and footer under its sign-in message, so a review typed there is refused by Copilot CLI and not typed again (the user sends the review again after signing in). What would reverse it: a Copilot CLI version that sends `sessionStart` at the start again (then the screen is not needed, and does no harm: the hook ranks above it), or one whose input box or footer changes so that the contract test fails (then the pattern changes with the version).

**ADR-70 "Start my day": the daemon syncs, computes a day report against the last day start and starts a new supervisor whose first message asks for a briefing from it.** (2026-10-26) One button in the supervisor panel runs three steps: the sync of every repo, awaited to its end; the day report (`get_day_report`, §4.11): commits new on each repo's remote default branch since the last day start (total and the newest five), repos the sync did not update with the reason, and every open task with its session's state and how many commits its repo's default branch has that its base does not; and a new supervisor started with `--interactive=<first message>`, which tells it to read the report, brief the user and propose at most three tasks, followed by the user's `dayStart.prompt` from `workspace.yaml`. The report and the commit of each repo's remote branch are kept in `day-start.json`, the rest in memory. The daemon computes and the supervisor judges, because a supervisor that gathered the same facts with tools would call `get_repo` for every repo, which carries the README and only the last ten commits without a "since" filter: tens of thousands of tokens and minutes of turns for an answer the daemon gives deterministically in seconds. The remote default branch rather than the local one, because sync leaves a dirty or diverged local branch where it was, and what the user needs to know in the morning is what came onto the server. The issue tracker and the repo host stay out of the daemon: Copilot CLI already reaches them through the user's own MCP servers, which `--additional-mcp-config` adds to rather than replaces, so the supervisor's instructions only say how a repo and a task map onto them (the remote's path is the project, a task's branch is the merge request's source branch), and the user's query lives in `workspace.yaml` (ADR-7). The contract test shows a server from the user's own Copilot CLI config next to Trackwall's in the day start's supervisor. A new supervisor every day rather than resuming yesterday's conversation, because a long history makes every turn slower and dearer, and the report carries what is worth carrying over. A running supervisor refuses the day start instead of being closed by it, so no conversation ends without the user. Left out of this first step, as the analysis before it planned: differences of cards and graph (rare, and `subgraph_for` answers them on demand), task proposals and rejections that survive a restart, a "Today" card in Home (to be decided after the briefing in the panel has been used for a while), and a day start at sign-in, rejected because the VPN and the issue tracker are often not reachable yet and the briefing would be read hours later. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI getting an API to send a session a message, which would let the day start brief a running supervisor; or the briefing needing data that only the daemon can get from the repo host, which would bring its own adapter back.

**ADR-71 E2E tests of the UI: three key paths in Chromium from Playwright, on the daemon with the fake Copilot CLI, in CI on every PR.** (2026-10-26) `packages/daemon/e2e/*.e2e.ts` (`pnpm test:e2e`, which builds the UI first) start the daemon of the session tests (`test/support/session-api.ts`: the real facade, PTY, git and process groups; Copilot CLI is the fake that replays the reference of ADR-67, mise the fake of the mise tests) serving `packages/ui/dist`, and drive it in headless Chromium through Playwright's library under `bun test`, as a user would: the Sessions list, the forms, the terminal (xterm.js draws its rows in the page) and the tray panel page. What the agent got is checked through the MCP tools its session uses, as its bridge does, and the agent's own work (a commit in its worktree) is done by the test. Three paths: a session in a repo asks for approval and the tray panel answers it (the dialog read from the screen, ADR-66); a main session proposes a task through MCP, and the user edits its prompt and starts it in Sessions, after which the main session follows the task with that prompt (ADR-55); and the user creates an agent task, verifies it (ADR-64), comments on a line of its commit in Changes, and the review reaches the agent once its turn ends (ADR-62). Reasoning: the test plan of 2026-10-25 put E2E after the contract tests, on 3–5 paths. The UI's logic is already in `packages/ui/src/lib` with unit tests, and the daemon's flows have tests through its API, but each sees one side only: a renamed field in a protocol schema, a WebSocket message the view stops handling or a button wired to the wrong action passes both. These three paths are the ones the user relies on every day and where the daemon, both WebSockets, the PTY and several views meet. They live in `packages/daemon` beside `contract/`, because the harness is the daemon's own test support with the facade in the same process, and only the daemon may import its code (the UI may not). Playwright's library under `bun test`, not the `@playwright/test` runner, keeps one test runner in the repo, and the documentation site's screenshots already use the same library at the same version. CI runs the `e2e` job on Linux, Windows and macOS, in `CI ok`: the fake's screen reaches the browser through the PTY, which is ConPTY on Windows, and the three tests take about 20 s locally. Rejected: tauri-driver (no macOS driver, and the tray, notifications and global shortcuts are out of reach of WebDriver anyway), an E2E test for every feature (slow and brittle for what unit tests already cover), the demo daemon of the screenshots (twenty repos and real mise services, a minute to start, and a scene to look at rather than steps to check), and `trackwall://` links (a session terminal opens them only in the Tauri window; their parsing and routing have unit tests). Found while writing them: the fake Copilot CLI draws at a fixed width and does not redraw when the terminal shrinks, as Copilot CLI does, and its approval dialog wrapped over two rows is no dialog the daemon can read, so the tests fold the supervisor panel to keep the terminal wide; and a key for an approval that reaches the daemon before the request's hook counted as text typed into the input box (the state was still `Working`, so there was no dialog), so a review then waited for the user's next message; with slow hooks (several seconds on a slow Windows machine) a real user can be that fast too (closed below). Clarification (2026-10-26, release 0.4.1): a fourth path opens the main window as the Tauri shell shows it, which the daemon's own page does not (`e2e/support/desktop-window.ts`): the built files under the window's origin (`http://tauri.localhost`) with the CSP of `tauri.conf.json`, the daemon on its own origin, the connection from a fake `daemon_connection` command; Home has to draw the 3D scene and receive the events of `/ws` with no CSP violation and no uncaught error, and a second test fails the scene's chunk and expects the message in its place with the other views working. 0.4.0 shipped with a blank window because nothing ran the UI under that CSP: the scene's code from strings and the `ws://` socket outside `connect-src` passed every other test. With this fourth layer, point 1 of the Definition of done in `AGENTS.md` names the test each layer a change touches gets (logic, a daemon flow, what the daemon relies on from Copilot CLI, a key path through the UI), and the PR description names them, so every new feature comes with its tests. What would reverse it: the tests failing intermittently more often than they find a fault (then fewer paths or Linux only), or a need to test the shell itself (then one tauri-driver smoke test on Windows). Clarification (2026-10-26): a digit or Enter in the middle of a turn (`Working` or `Stale`) while the screen already shows an approval is no text in the input box, and when the approval's hook comes after that answer, the answer moves the session on to `Working` at once instead of leaving it in `Approval` until the command ends (`LiveSession`); so the review test answers as soon as the dialog is drawn. Ctrl+C and Esc are not answers and count as before. Clarification (2026-10-26): a fifth test runs an agent task on a small screen (1280×650 CSS pixels, a 1920×1080 screen at 150 % without the taskbar) with a summary, a verdict and a running mise task above its terminal, and expects the whole approval dialog in the terminal and its buttons in the tray panel; before the task panel kept those sections to a third of its height, the terminal had 12 rows there. The fake Copilot CLI cuts its dialog above in a terminal too short for it, as the real one does (ADR-66 in §4.4). A second test there opens Home on that screen with the supervisor panel pinned: its side panels shrink to 12rem before the scene goes under 20rem, where the scene used to be 16 pixels wide. Clarification (2026-10-26): since ADR-88 and ADR-89 the session reaches the daemon over HTTP, hooks and MCP alike, instead of the bridge and hook processes named here.

**ADR-72 Tests with real Java builds: two Spring Boot projects built by real Maven and Gradle through real mise, every night and on PRs that touch the daemon, outside `CI ok`.** (2026-10-26) Half of what Trackwall offers a Java team depends on what a build tool writes: the Spring Boot log lines that tell a service's port and start (§4.3), the Surefire and Gradle report directories (§4.9), the compiler and test failures in a log (§4.2), and the exit code of `mise run test` (§4.10). The unit tests check those against handwritten lines and files, so they prove only that the daemon agrees with what its author believed. `packages/daemon/jvm/` holds two small made-up projects in the shape Spring Initializr gives (`orders` with Maven, `checkout` with Gradle Kotlin DSL calling `orders` through a `RestClient` whose address comes from `application.properties`) and one test file that drives the daemon through the MCP tools as an agent does, from the card to a failed verification. The projects name their JDK, Maven and Gradle in their own `mise.toml`, which the test installs with `mise install`, so the path is the one of a user's workspace, including mise on Windows. Maven, Gradle, Spring Boot and the workflow's mise are pinned, so a red night means a change on `main` or something outside the repo (a new Java 21 build, the dependency repositories); Dependabot proposes new Spring Boot and plugin versions, and the PR runs the tests on them. Not in `CI ok`, because a run needs network and minutes (a JDK and the Spring Boot dependencies, cached between runs) and an outage of Maven Central must not block every PR; instead `jvm.yml` runs on a PR that touches the daemon, the protocol or the dependencies, which the tests go through end to end, and `nightly.yml` calls it every night and keeps one issue open while it fails on `main`, like the contract tests (ADR-67). Rejected: projects generated by start.spring.io at every run (the output changes with every Initializr release, and a failure could not be reproduced from the commit), the Maven and Gradle wrappers (Gradle's needs a binary jar in the repo, and mise is what users run), public open-source Spring projects (minutes of build for features two small projects show, and nobody controls when they change), and a JDK from the runner image (it would skip mise's installation, which users rely on). Would be reversed if the tests became fast and stable enough for `CI ok`, or if a feature needed a project whose build takes longer than a night's budget.

**ADR-77 From a finished task to a merge request: the user pushes the task branch and opens the repo host's own form filled in from Trackwall, and the task panel follows the merge request and its pipeline, found by the task branch.** (2026-10-26) The task panel gets "Push" (the task branch to the main remote, never forced, without hooks, only where the remote fetches from) and "Open merge request" (the repo host's new merge request page in the browser, with the source and target branch, the title and the description the user approved: the agent's `report_result` summary and the last verification's verdict); the user creates the merge request there. The panel then reads the merge request whose source branch is the task branch, with its pipeline, through `glab api` or `gh api`, and shows its state and pipeline with links (§4.12). Reasoning: the step after "Verify" was the user pushing by hand and retyping the agent's summary into GitLab. Three ways to create the merge request were weighed. Creating it through the API from Trackwall needs a write call per host, with its error cases (an existing merge request, a missing target, permissions), and loses what the host's form gives: the project's merge request template, reviewers, labels, draft and squash settings, which at work often have rules. Leaving it to the user's GitLab MCP server makes an agent the one who creates it, with Copilot CLI's tool prompt as the only approval, and it exists only where that server is configured (not for GitHub). The host's form needs no write API and no permission beyond what `gh` and `glab` already read with, and the user's "Create" there is the approval. Finding the merge request by its source branch rather than storing what Trackwall created means no state to keep and a merge request made any other way shows too, and it follows the mapping the supervisor's instructions already state (ADR-70). No MCP tool for proposing a merge request: the task's `report_result` is already the agent's account of its work, so the session instructions tell it to write that summary for a reviewer, and the supervisor that wants a better one asks the task with `send_message` (ADR-68). Hooks are skipped because a hook path can point into the worktree, where the agent writes (the pre-PR security review found that `--no-verify` alone still runs `reference-transaction`), and a push address other than the fetch address is refused because the agent can set one; the host's pipeline runs the checks instead. Knowingly not covered: Git LFS on push, forks (the merge request goes into the same project), the merge request in `get_session`, a notification on a failed pipeline, and its failed jobs for the agent. What would reverse it: a description longer than an address carries being common (then the API with the host's template read first), or the user wanting merge requests created without opening the browser (then a create call per host behind the same draft), or repos that rely on pre-push hooks that are not in the worktree (then running hooks from the repo's own `.git/hooks` only).

**ADR-78 The supervisor starts without Copilot CLI's shell and file-writing tools, unless the user lets it change code.** (2026-10-26) The supervisor (ADR-58) starts with `--deny-tool=shell` and `--deny-tool=write` after the user's `toolPermissions`, and its session instructions say so: it reads files with Copilot CLI's viewing and search tools and with Trackwall's (`search_code`, `get_repo_card`, `get_task_changes`), and hands every command, build, test and change to a task. `supervisor.changesCode: true` in `workspace.yaml` starts it without the denial and with instructions that let it work itself on what the user asks for. Reasoning: an analysis of what the supervisor knows of its role found that it knows it well (its role in the session instructions, the MCP instruction, the day start's first message, and the MCP tools that refuse it mise tasks, services and verification), but that "you coordinate, you do not change code" was only a sentence to the model, while the supervisor runs in the projects folder, where Copilot CLI's shell and edit tools reach every repo, outside any task's worktree and so outside the user's review in Changes; one "Yes, and don't ask again" or an `allow: [write]` rule meant for all sessions would let it edit any repo directly. Copilot CLI applies a denial before every approval (`copilot help permissions`), and the contract test shows both the shell and the file creation refused without an approval prompt and nothing written. The whole `shell` kind rather than a list of writing commands, because any command can write; reading through the shell (`git log`) goes too, which Trackwall's tools cover. The supervisor was not given more rights instead, as was asked in passing: what makes it the supervisor is that it sees all the work and the user approves what it delegates, and a supervisor that also builds and edits would be one large agent with a long context working outside the worktrees. Rejected: a Copilot CLI custom agent (`--agent`) for the supervisor, whose prompt the session instructions already give and whose tool list adds nothing to `--deny-tool` here; `--excluded-tools`, which names tools (`bash` or `powershell`, `create`, `edit`) that differ by system and version, where `--deny-tool` names kinds Copilot CLI documents. Left as before: the user's own MCP servers (§8). What would reverse it: users who keep setting `changesCode` because the supervisor regularly needs a command that Trackwall's tools do not cover, which would bring a narrower denial (`write` and named commands) or a Trackwall tool for that need.

**ADR-79 The daemon keeps a git budget: the real git binary on Windows, at most 6 short git calls at once, and HEAD, the remote address and the HEAD commit's details read without a process when they are known.** (2026-10-26) On a managed Windows machine an antivirus or EDR scans every process start (an empty `cmd /c ver` took about a second), and with 20 repos in the projects folder Trackwall stayed on "Loading": a refresh of the repo list started three git processes per repo, and the tags and the graph two per repo even with every card cached, and the launcher `cmd\git.exe` on `PATH` starts the real git as a second process, so each call paid the start twice. Dozens of starts at once took the machine and the daemon's event loop with them (`/api/health` answered in 12–60 s), and the card warm-up hit the 15 s limit. Since ADR-63 the UI refreshes the list, the tags and the graph after every commit, so the cost came at every change. Now: on Windows the daemon asks the launcher once for `--exec-path` and starts `<root>\mingw64\bin\git.exe` (or `ucrt64`, `clangarm64`) itself with the launcher's `PATH` additions; network commands keep the launcher, so credential helpers and ssh run as the user knows them. The HEAD commit comes from the git directory's files (`HEAD`, the loose ref, `packed-refs`; the reftable backend still asks git), so tags and the graph from cached cards start no process. `git config --list` is kept per repo until one of the configuration files it usually comes from changes. The list's `git log -1` is replaced by commit details remembered by hash, so a list refresh is one `git status` per repo. A daemon-wide limit of 6 short git calls at once keeps the API answering between them. `git-budget.test.ts` counts the git processes of a refresh with 20 repos: one per repo for the list, none for the tags and the graph, and after a commit only the changed repo costs more. Rejected: a longer time limit (it only hides the queue), a persistent `git cat-file --batch` per repo (a process per repo held open, and stale answers after `gc`), libgit2 or isomorphic-git (a native addon or a second git implementation for what files and one process give). Would reverse it: a git that starts without a launcher on Windows by default, or a status source that needs no process (an `fsmonitor` the daemon could trust); a configuration change only in the system file or an `include.path` file that must show without a restart would need those files in the key.

**ADR-80 Every card problem has a code: `files-not-read`, `file-invalid`, `parse-incomplete` or `truncated`.** (2026-10-26) A card problem was a path and a sentence, so the UI could only print it and an agent had to read English to tell a file the card never read from a `pom.xml` that does not parse or a DTO list cut at 500. With a code the UI names the kind next to the path, and MCP clients (`get_repo_card`) can tell without the message whether a file was not read, did not parse, had no syntax tree, or a list hit its limit. The codes describe the gaps the extractors already report; new detections (no endpoints found, a call whose target the graph cannot resolve, an ambiguous route) are separate changes that add a code when they come. `EXTRACTOR_VERSION` goes to 5, so cards on disk are computed again with codes. Rejected: a code per limit (`max-dtos`, `max-source-files`…), which would leak limits into the contract and change with every new limit; the message keeps that detail. Would reverse it: a consumer that needs to act on one specific limit, which would split a code.

**ADR-81 The supervisor works out what a request changes before it delegates, and sends a follow-up to the running task.** (2026-10-26) The supervisor's session instructions and the descriptions of `propose_task` and `send_message` say what a task is for: a task is a new worktree of one repo on a new branch, so it changes files inside that repo and cannot remove, move or rename the repo or work in the projects folder around it; the supervisor does not propose a task for such a request and tells the user what it takes (with `supervisor.changesCode` it may do it itself when asked). Before proposing, it reads the tasks a supervisor proposed with `list_sessions`, and a follow-up on the same topic in the same repo goes to that task with `send_message` while the user has not ended it, so it lands on the same branch and merge request; a follow-up on a waiting proposal or a task the user started goes to the user, and a new topic stays a new task. The uses of `send_message` (a correction, an answer, a follow-up, a change elsewhere) are one constant, `TASK_MESSAGE_USES` in `protocol`, shared by the tool's description, the MCP instruction and the supervisor's role. Reasoning: asked to remove a folder of the projects folder, the supervisor, which since ADR-78 has no shell, proposed a task in that repo, whose session then started in a worktree of the very repo it was to remove (against its own rule to work only in its worktree, and leaving the task behind); asked for a second removal, it proposed another task instead of messaging the first. Nothing in its instructions asked it to check what a task can do or which tasks already run, and `send_message` was described only as a correction or an answer. Not in this decision: an action on the projects folder (removing a repo first) that the supervisor proposes and the user approves in the UI, which comes as its own ADR (ADR-83). What would reverse it: tasks that keep getting follow-ups unrelated to their merge request (then the rule narrows to answers and corrections again), or a supervisor that still delegates projects-folder requests to tasks despite the instruction (then `propose_task` would need a check, not a sentence).

**ADR-82 An endpoint's card lists the client calls its handler reaches, from a call graph of the repo's Java code, so the graph says which endpoint calls which.** (2026-10-26) `who_calls` and `find_endpoint` tied a client to its repo, so every endpoint of a repo looked like it might call everything the repo calls, and "what does a change to this endpoint affect" had no answer below the repo. Now `call-files.ts` parses every Java file of the commit with tree-sitter (ADR-37), `call-graph.ts` builds a static call graph (receiver types from locals, parameters, fields and return types, resolved through imports; a call on an interface to each implementation, up to 8), and `endpoint-reach.ts` gives every endpoint `reaches`: the card's client calls reachable from its handler, computed once per strongly connected component. `find_endpoint` adds to each caller the endpoints of its repo that reach the call (`from`) and to each endpoint the endpoints of other repos it calls (`calls`). A spike on 8 public Spring repos (22 to 5,500 Java files) found 41 endpoint–call pairs, all correct on review; the one call it missed came from a client the card does not see, and every call no endpoint reached was made by a scheduled job, startup code, a security filter, an interceptor or a listener. On the largest repo 3 of 931 endpoints reach a call, where per repo every endpoint seemed to reach 211. Time: parsing every Java file is the cost (7 s for 5,500 files on a fast machine, against about 1 s for the card before), the graph and its components 0.6 s; a trace from each endpoint with a depth and step limit took 20 s there and was cut for 504 of 931 endpoints, so the reach is a closure over the whole graph instead. The parse therefore keeps reads by blob id, gives way to other work every 50 ms and stops after 60 s. A repo with Kotlin code gets no reach (`reachTraced: false`, `reaches: null`) rather than one missing the Kotlin paths: the repos in use are Java. The answer is "may call": branches and implementations all count, and calls the framework makes (listeners, schedules, filters, AOP) are reached by no endpoint. `EXTRACTOR_VERSION` goes to 7. Rejected: a bounded trace per endpoint (above), compiling the repo or a language server (a JDK and a build per repo, ADR-37), reading only the files from the search (a handler reaches a client through services without annotations), and an LLM for the call paths (evidence must come from code). Would reverse it: Kotlin in the repos (read it the same way through its grammar), many false pairs from interfaces with many implementations in real repos (lower `MAX_DISPATCH` or resolve beans by qualifier), or a parse that cannot stay under a few seconds per commit with the reads kept (move it to a worker thread).

**ADR-83 Work on the projects folder itself is a typed folder action the supervisor proposes and the user approves; the first one removes a repo by moving it into `.trackwall-removed`.** (2026-10-26) The MCP tool `propose_folder_action` takes an action from a closed list (today only `remove_repo`), a repo and a reason, and adds a proposal of the kind `remove_repo` to the proposals that wait for the user (`TaskProposal.kind`; any session but a task session may propose it, like `propose_task`), so it shows in the same places as a task proposal, tray and dashboard included. "Remove repo" in the UI (`approve-repo-removal`) makes the daemon check that no session runs in the repo's root, no task belongs to the repo and no process of `procs` runs in it, and then move the folder into `<projects>/.trackwall-removed/` under a free name; the proposer gets `repo_removed`. Reasoning: since ADR-81 the supervisor no longer hands a projects-folder request to a task, but it had nothing to hand it to instead; a task cannot do it (it is a worktree of the repo it would remove), and a session with a shell in the projects folder would undo ADR-78. A closed list of actions keeps the daemon in charge of the command (§1.2), the checks are the daemon's, not the agent's, and the user approves one concrete change in the place where they already approve proposals. Reusing `TaskProposal` instead of a new list keeps every view of waiting work, the limit of 20 and the rejection path without new code; the kind tells the views and the approval apart. The repo is moved, not deleted: a move inside one folder is quick and atomic, can be undone by hand and behaves the same on Windows, macOS and Linux, while the system trash needs native APIs per system and a permanent delete loses unpushed work the daemon does not check for. Rejected: approving any shell command a session proposes (the user would review commands, and the daemon would run text from a session), and the system trash (above). What would reverse it: folder actions that need parameters the closed list cannot express (then a small schema per action rather than free text), or a `.trackwall-removed` folder that grows unnoticed (then Trackwall offers to empty it, or moves to the system trash).

**ADR-84 Task worktrees go to a task worktrees folder, by default `.trackwall-worktrees` in the projects folder, and each task remembers its folder.** (2026-10-26) A new task's worktree is created in `<folder>/<repo>/<branch without prefix>`, where the folder is `worktreesDir` from `workspace.yaml` ("Task worktrees folder" in Settings) or, without the key, `.trackwall-worktrees` in the projects folder; `tasks.json` keeps the folder of each task, and a task saved before this decision keeps its worktree in `worktrees/` of the state directory until it ends. Reasoning: a worktree in the state directory (ADR-31) sits under the user's profile, which an antivirus scans on every file access, while users who work with many repos usually exclude the folder that holds them, so git and builds in a task were many times slower than in the repo next to it; the projects folder also lies on the disk the user chose for code and is usually what the user trusts in Copilot CLI, which covers its subfolders. The repo list already skips names starting with a dot and only looks one level down, so the folder never shows up as a repo, and since it is not inside a repo, no repo's `git status` gets an untracked directory, which was ADR-31's reason against `<repo>/.worktrees`. The setting stays for a projects folder on a slow or network drive; a folder that is the projects folder itself or lies in one of its repos is refused, because a worktree would land in a repo. The folder is stored per task instead of being recomputed, so changing the setting or the projects folder never loses an existing worktree, and nothing is moved with `git worktree move`, which on Windows fails on a file a running process holds. The folder in `tasks.json` decides which directory ending a task deletes, like `repoDir` decides where git runs: the file is the daemon's own state, and a task with a relative folder is refused when it is read. Rejected: asking the user to move every repo into a `repos` subfolder with worktrees next to it, which changes nothing the default does not and breaks the paths IDEs, mise trust and Copilot CLI trust remember; the worktree inside its repo (ADR-31's reasons). Known cost: an IDE or a tool opened on the whole projects folder shows the worktrees too (they are a hidden folder only on macOS and Linux). The supervisor (ADR-58) works in the projects folder, so the default folder puts task worktrees, with the files `.worktreeinclude` copied into them (ADR-61), within its file tools; that adds nothing it could not read before, because the same files are in the repo roots next to it. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI or git tooling that needs worktrees elsewhere, or projects folders that are commonly on network drives, which would make the state directory the better default.
