**ADR-85 What waits for a working session goes to its agent through the hooks' output: a block at the end of its turn and context with a tool's result, with the typed prompt as the fallback.** (2026-10-26) When a review, a task message or a task update waits for a session that is working (`askToRead` returned `queued`), the daemon answers the session's next `postToolUse` hook with `{ additionalContext: <prompt> }`, once per turn and prompt, and its `agentStop` hook with `{ decision: "block", reason: <prompt> }` (`LiveSession.hookOutput` applies `hookOutputFor` of `copilot-hook-output.ts`); the context and the block can both come in one turn, since an agent may not act on the context. A blocked `agentStop` is not reported as `turn_ended` to the proposer, since the turn goes on. The prompt is the same fixed one-line text ADR-62 and ADR-68 type, so the agent still reads the content through the tools. The block is skipped while the user has a draft in the input box and when the `agentStop` payload says `stop_hook_active` (the turn was itself started by a block); then, with the prompt still unread at the end of the turn a block started, the typed prompt of ADR-68 delivers it once the box is back. The hook record keeps `stop_hook_active`. Known gaps: between the blocked `agentStop` and the next `userPromptSubmitted` the session shows `Input` for a moment, and when the user interrupts the turn a block started with Esc (no `agentStop`), the proposer hears of neither turn's end until the next one. Reasoning: on a real machine typed prompts were lost in the gap between `agentStop` and the redrawn input box, and a session busy for a long turn heard nothing until it ended; the supervisor asked a task something and the answer waited for a click (the review of the communication between the supervisor and its tasks, 2026-10-26). A spike on Copilot CLI 1.0.103 showed that a blocked `agentStop` starts a new turn with the reason as the user's message and a `userPromptSubmitted` hook, that Copilot CLI stops after about eight blocks in a row, and that `additionalContext` from `postToolUse` is appended to the tool result the model reads next; the contract tests check both. Answering from the hook needs no screen, no keys and no timing, and works the same on Windows, where hooks were the slow part until ADR-88. The typed prompt stays for a session that is idle, since no hook comes then. Considered and rejected: Copilot CLI's hidden UI server port with the SDK's `send` (it injects into an idle session, but the port has no token, and any local process could type into the session), and agents polling with `wait_for_session` (a model step per poll and a turn the user cannot use). Would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping or changing the hook outputs (the nightly contract run on the newest version would show it), or a supported, authenticated way to send a message into a running session.

**ADR-86 A repo's local config files are backed up into a git repo in the projects folder, and restored from it into a new clone.** (2026-10-26) The daemon compares `mise.local.toml` and `.worktreeinclude` of every repo every 5 s, commits a changed file that git does not track into `<projects folder>/.trackwall-local/<repo folder>/`, a git repo it checks before every use, and pushes the store to its remote over HTTPS or SSH when the user added one, the repo's file winning over the store's; the repo panel shows each file against the store, restores what a new clone lacks and adds the files to `.git/info/exclude`, and the `no-mise` tag counts the mise tasks of `mise.local.toml` (§4.13). Reasoning: where a team does not accept mise configuration in its repos, the mise tasks a user works out with an agent have to live in `mise.local.toml`, which git ignores, so nothing kept it: a deleted folder or a new clone lost it, and so did the second computer. A git repo gives the history of every version and a remote without anything new to install; the projects folder is the one place where git is fast on a machine whose antivirus skips only that folder. Considered: the user's global mise config per directory (not per repo, and outside the fast folder), `task_config.includes` pointing into a store outside the repo (mise would read files the worktree's trusted config does not cover, ADR-59), links from the repo to the store (git on Windows turns them into text files), a scheduled task or a `post-commit` hook running a script (another thing to keep working on each machine; a hook misses changes without a commit), and a `mise.local.toml` generated from the stack (planned in §4.7, but it would not keep what the user wrote by hand). Reversed if teams accept mise configuration in their repos, or if mise gains a supported way to load a per-repo config from outside the repo.

**ADR-87 Endpoints come also from OpenAPI specs in the repo, and `find_endpoint` matches the end of a path, a whole address and words.** (2026-10-26) An agent asked `find_endpoint` for an endpoint and got nothing until the user narrowed the question. Four reasons, all in the code: a controller implementing an interface the OpenAPI generator writes to `target/` during the build has no mapping in the repo, so the card had no endpoint at all; `${…}` in `@RequestMapping` stayed in the path as text, so the path never matched; the card's path starts with the context path and a gateway adds its own prefix, so a path the agent had from a log or a colleague had a different number of segments; and a whole URL, `GET /path`, a path without the leading `/` or a few words found nothing. Now the card reads specs as a second source of endpoints, found by content (a top-level `openapi` or `swagger` key, through `git grep`) because repos name them differently: an operation the code maps describes that endpoint (`operation`: `operationId`, summary, spec line), any other becomes an endpoint with `source: "openapi"` whose handler is the controller method named like the `operationId`, which is how the generator names the interface method, so its reach is traced like a mapped one (ADR-82). A spec the build generates a client from (read from the generator plugin's configuration in `pom.xml` and `build.gradle(.kts)`) describes another app and gives no endpoints, and so does a spec in a repo without a Java build. The path follows the generator, as a real Maven build showed: with `interfaceOnly` the app serves the bare spec path under the implementing controller's mapping, without it under `openapi.<title>.base-path` (by default the server path); only a spec the build does not generate from is taken with its server path. Mapping paths expand `${…}` from the module's configuration. `find_endpoint` parses the query (method, address, path or words), matches the whole path, then the end of either path with one fixed segment equal on both sides (so `/42` alone matches nothing), then every word in the path, handler, app, `operationId` or summary, and says how it matched (`match`), best first. `ProvidedEndpoint` gets `source` and `operation`, and `handler` becomes nullable for an operation without a controller method. `EXTRACTOR_VERSION` goes to 8. Not in this decision: the spec a running app serves (`springdoc` at `/v3/api-docs`), which is not in the repo (`call_endpoint` can fetch it from a running service), specs split over several files (`$ref` to another file gives no operation), and client specs as the calls of a generated client, which the card does not see either (a later step: the calls of the client generated from a spec). Rejected: choosing specs by file name (repos name them differently, and the search costs one `git grep`), fuzzy matching by edit distance (wrong endpoints with the same confidence as right ones, where `match` says how far a result is from the query), and trusting every spec as provided (a BFF keeps the specs of the services it calls, and their endpoints would point callers to it). Would reverse it: specs in real repos that disagree with the code often enough that their endpoints mislead (then only `operation` on mapped endpoints), or `path-end` matches that bury the right endpoint in large workspaces (then require two fixed segments).

**ADR-88 The session plugin's hooks are HTTP hooks that POST to the daemon, not a daemon process per event.** (2026-10-26) The plugin registers every hook as `{ type: "http", url, headers, timeoutSec }` (`HttpHook` in `copilot-plugin.ts`) to `POST /hooks/<event>` of the daemon (§5.2) with the daemon token and the session token, and the session environment gets `COPILOT_HOOK_ALLOW_LOCALHOST=1`. The daemon writes the same hook record as before to the session's `hooks.jsonl` and then answers with the hook's output, `{}` at first and since ADR-85 also the daemon's prompt; the `copilot-hook` subcommand is gone, and only the recording script still registers a program, because it has no daemon. Why: Copilot CLI waits for every hook before it goes on (checked on the real binary: a hook 2 s slower delays every next model request by 2 s), and every hook was a start of the whole daemon binary. On a test Windows machine without a company antivirus a hook took 0.3–2.0 s (median 0.68 s over 44 hooks of one task); on a managed machine an antivirus scans every process start outside its excluded folder. A turn waits for its first two hooks, one per tool call and approval request, and one at its end, so a supervisor turn with ten tool calls waited over ten seconds for hooks alone. Spike on Copilot CLI 1.0.103 with the scripted model: the POST carries the same JSON as a command hook's stdin and the headers from `hooks.json`; without `COPILOT_HOOK_ALLOW_LOCALHOST=1` a hook to `127.0.0.1` is dropped without a word; the hook does not go through `HTTP_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY` (with a dead proxy and no `NO_PROXY` the hooks still arrived), so the session needs no `NO_PROXY`; a hook the daemon answers with 500 does not stop the turn, it only leaves no record; a turn with one tool call took 0.7 s instead of 2.4 s with hook processes run from source. The answer being the hook's output is what the delivery of messages to a task through its hooks builds on (ADR-85): an `agentStop` or `postToolUse` answer can carry a decision or `additionalContext` from the daemon's memory, while an answer without one stays `{}`, and since a failed hook gives no decision, a daemon that is down never blocks a turn. The fake Copilot CLI posts the hooks the same way and drops them without the variable. Gaps: the daemon token lies in `hooks.json` next to the session token (§8). The daemon still reads the records back from the file every 200 ms (`HookTail`); handing a received record straight to its session would save that delay and is a next step. Copilot CLI's own start is slowed down by an antivirus the same way: the binary unpacks its package (236 MB for 1.0.103 on Linux: `cli-main.js`, native `.node` modules, ripgrep) into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\copilot\pkg` on Windows and loads it at every start, and `COPILOT_PKG_CACHE_HOME` moves it (read in the binary's loader, `sea-loader.js`); the sessions page tells the user how to put it in the projects folder, where the repo list skips dot-named folders, and Trackwall does not set it, since it is the user's Copilot CLI setting for the whole machine. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping or changing HTTP hooks (the contract tests on the newest version every night would show it), then a command hook again, best a small program in the projects folder rather than the daemon binary.

**ADR-89 A Trackwall session reaches the daemon's MCP server over HTTP, not through the stdio bridge, with `127.0.0.1` in its `NO_PROXY`.** (2026-10-26) The session's `mcp-config.json` is an HTTP entry, `{ type: "http", url, headers, tools: ["*"] }` (`copilotHttpMcpServers` in `protocol`), for `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/mcp` with the daemon token and the session token as headers (`sessionMcpRequest`), written user-only; `mcp-stdio --session-token-file` and the session token file are gone. Reasoning: the bridge was one more daemon binary that Copilot CLI starts with every session, which an antivirus scans like the hook processes of ADR-88, and one more hop for every tool call; ADR-24 chose it so that the daemon token stays out of the user's `~/.copilot`, which still holds for agents outside Trackwall, but a session's config is the daemon's own file in the session directory, made for one daemon lifetime, so the reason does not apply to it. Checked on Copilot CLI 1.0.103: an HTTP entry with headers reaches `/mcp` and lists every tool, but its HTTP client sends a request to `127.0.0.1` through `HTTP_PROXY` unless `NO_PROXY` names `127.0.0.1` (`localhost` is not enough), and then fails to connect; hooks ignore the proxy (ADR-88). So the daemon adds `127.0.0.1` to every spelling of `NO_PROXY`/`no_proxy` the user's environment has, or sets `NO_PROXY` (`withLoopbackNoProxy`); the contract tests run a session behind a proxy that answers nothing. What would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping HTTP MCP entries or their headers, or a proxy setup in which a loopback request through `NO_PROXY` is still refused; then the bridge again, with the session token in a file.

**ADR-90 A session resumes a task with the MCP tool `resume_task`: the supervisor any task, a main session the tasks it proposed.** (2026-10-27) `resume_task {task}` does what Resume in the task's panel does (`--resume=<task UUID>` in the task's worktree, §4.4) and returns the task with its new session; the session then reads what waited for the task (`on_resume`, ADR-62, ADR-68). The supervisor may resume any task, also one the user started, a main session only a task it proposed, a task session none; an agent outside Trackwall, the user's program like the HTTP API, any. The tool is destructive (`auditArgs: ["task"]`), because the resumed agent runs commands in the worktree. The supervisor's role and the tool's description ask it to resume only when the user asks to continue a task, and to resume rather than propose the work again. Reasoning: after an update or a restart of Trackwall every task is left without a session (sessions live in daemon memory, §4.4), and asked to continue one, the supervisor could only propose a new task, which starts over on a new branch in a new worktree and loses the conversation and the uncommitted changes' context. Resuming starts no new work: the user approved the task when it was created, the session runs in the task's own worktree, which the user reviews in Changes, and the user can stop it at any time; that is why it needs no new approval in the UI, unlike `propose_task`. A user-started task is resumable by the supervisor although it does not get the supervisor's messages (ADR-68): resuming does not steer the task, it only gives it its session back. This is the first part of keeping sessions across a restart; the daemon resuming the sessions that ran when it stopped, on its own, is the next step. What would reverse it: a supervisor resuming tasks the user did not ask about; then the tool waits for the user's approval like a proposal.

**ADR-91 A task's sections above its terminal fold into task notices: a strip in the one-entry layout, a badge with an overlay and a banner on a grid tile.** (2026-10-27) `useTaskDetails` in the UI loads what the sections show (shared files, mise config, verification, merge request, running mise tasks) once for the task panel or a grid tile, and the pure `taskNotices` sums it up as one `TaskNotice` per section, warnings first; the sections (`TaskDetails`) take their data from the hook instead of loading it themselves. In the one-entry layout the notices are one line of chips above the tabs (`TaskNoticeStrip`), folded by default; "Details" or a chip unfolds the sections, still capped at a third of the panel. A task's grid tile gets a badge in its header (`NoticeBadge`: the number of warnings, or of other notices) that opens the sections as an overlay over the terminal, and a warning whose key the tile has not seen (`newWarnings`; the warnings present when the tile's data first came count as seen) lies over the terminal as a one-line banner until the user opens or dismisses it. Reasoning: the sections pushed the terminal down to a few lines in the one-entry layout, and in the grid the warnings (a changed mise config, shared files) did not show at all; an overlay instead of a row keeps the PTY's size, as the close question does (ADR-48). Folded sections and a closed overlay are hidden, not unmounted, so the mise config shown for trust and an open log survive folding. Every task tile now checks the two warnings, every 5 s when selected and every 15 s otherwise, and the verification and the merge request like the panel does; a tile whose task is being ended loads nothing. Reversed if that load on git is felt on Windows (ADR-79), by moving the warnings into the task list the daemon pushes.

**ADR-92 A session refused for an untrusted mise config is told when the user trusts it.** (2026-10-27) When `run_mise_task`, `verify_task` or `start_service` from a Trackwall session ends in `mise_unavailable`, the daemon remembers the session and the target (repo root or task worktree); when the user then presses "Trust this config" in the task panel or trusts the repo root with `mise trust`, each running session refused there gets the daemon's prompt (ADR-62, ADR-68, ADR-85: typed when it waits in its input box, otherwise as context with a tool's result and a block at the end of its turn) with a fixed sentence that the user trusted the mise config that refused its last run and that it should run it again if it still needs to (§4.4). Reasoning: trust is only the user's (ADR-41, ADR-59), so a refused agent told the user to trust the config and then stopped; after the click the user had to go back to the session and tell it to try again, a round trip that made nothing safer (a request from using it, 2026-10-27). The sentence goes in the prompt rather than behind a reading tool because it carries nothing to read, and no text of an agent or the repo goes into it. Only sessions refused since their last try are told, not every session of the repo or task, so a session that never needed the config does not get a turn for it; a trust of the repo root tells sessions refused in its task worktrees too, since a worktree run needs the root trusted. Knowingly not covered: the refusal is any `mise_unavailable` (also no `mise` on `PATH` or a broken `mise.toml`), so a trust after such an error tells the agent to try once more and it gets the same error, and a trust of the repo root also tells a session refused because its worktree's own config changed; a `mise trust` the user runs outside Trackwall tells nobody; a service refused because a dependency in another repo is not trusted is remembered for the session's own repo, so trusting the dependency tells nobody; a session that ends before the trust, or a task resumed in a new session, is not told; and an agent outside Trackwall is not told (it has no input box). Considered: telling every running session of the repo or task (a wasted turn for one that did not ask), an MCP notification (Copilot CLI does not act on it, ADR-62), and the agent polling with `wait_for_mise_task` or a new tool (a model step per poll and a turn the user cannot use). What would reverse it: agents told to try again for refusals the trust did not cure, often enough to matter (then a distinct error code for an untrusted config), or a trust that mise keeps by content (then nothing to tell).

**ADR-94 Trackwall keeps its own errors in an error journal on the computer, anonymized, with where in its code each came from; nothing sends them.** (2026-10-27) The daemon's unexpected errors (an API action or MCP tool failing with something other than a `DomainError`, an exception nobody caught, a failed start), the UI's (React's root handlers, `window.onerror`, unhandled rejections, sent to the daemon) and the desktop shell's (its stderr messages for failed calls and its panics, in a file of its own that the daemon takes in) go into one list in `logs/errors.json`, the same error counted once, each with up to 8 frames of Trackwall's own code as paths in its repo; the UI's built chunks are mapped back to their sources through the source maps the daemon already serves. Every message is anonymized before it is kept: the values the daemon knows (state, home, projects and worktree folders, repo names and folders, user and computer names, repo hosts, environment variable values) and then patterns (paths, addresses, hosts, e-mail, IP addresses, token shapes). Settings lists the journal. Reasoning: before, Trackwall's errors were lost: the daemon wrote them only to stderr, which the shell sends to `daemon-start.log` and replaces at the next start, the shell's `eprintln!` has nowhere to go in a release on Windows, and the UI kept nothing; an error seen at work could not be looked into a day later. Structured records instead of log lines: a record is made of fields Trackwall writes itself (area, kind, frames, counts) plus one message, which can be anonymized; a log line is free text that carries anything, including the output of the user's code. An issue on GitHub filled from the journal was proposed with it and put off by the user: the repo is public, an issue stays in indexes after it is deleted, and anonymization of free text is best effort. `tauri-plugin-log` was not taken: it writes free text, while the journal needs fields it can anonymize, and the shell needs only a few lines of its own code. Environment checks ("doctor") are a separate step that will show in the same part of Settings. What would reverse it: a need to read the daemon's full log after the fact (then a rotated log file next to the journal, still never sent), a crash reporter of the runtime that keeps its own records, or the user asking for the journal to be sent somewhere (then a preview, a check of the known values in the finished text, and a send only by the user's click).

**ADR-95 The next daemon resumes the sessions that ran when the previous one stopped, from a list in the state directory and Copilot CLI's `--resume`.** (2026-10-27) `resumable-sessions.json` follows the running sessions (session, repo, task, `hasConversation`); the daemon's stop stops following before it stops the groups, and the next daemon starts each session again once its API listens: a task's session with Resume (`--resume=<task UUID>`), the supervisor and a main session under their old Trackwall id, which is now also their Copilot CLI session id (`--session-id=<id>` at the start, `--resume=<id>` after), or with `--session-id` again when the session had no prompt, because Copilot CLI keeps no conversation for it and refuses to resume it (found by the contract test of the restart; the fake Copilot CLI refuses it the same way). Reasoning: an update (ADR-53), a quit or a crash ended every session, because the daemon owns their PTYs and process groups and a new version is a new daemon, so the user lost the supervisor's and the main sessions' conversations and had to resume each task by hand. Keeping the processes alive across the restart was rejected: the hooks and the MCP entry of a running session carry the old daemon's port and tokens (ADR-88, ADR-89), the Job Object that ends the group with the daemon (ADR-20) would have to move to a separate PTY host that itself could never be updated without ending the sessions, and on Windows that host would lock files in the install folder. Copilot CLI already keeps every conversation by session id, which survives a stop of the group (a Job Object kill on Windows) as the contract test shows. Kept: the same id for a main session and the supervisor, so links, `ProposedBy.session` of their proposals and the Sessions list stay the same; a task's session gets a new id as with Resume. Lost: the turn a stop cut off (the agent is idle in the resumed session, the user asks it to go on), an open approval, and what lives only in daemon memory (proposals waiting for approval, unread reviews and messages, updates). The file is untrusted input: the schema takes only UUIDs and repo ids, a repo goes through the projects folder and a task through `tasks.json`, and the command is built from configuration as for any session (§1.2). What would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping `--session-id` or `--resume=<id>`, or users who do not want the sessions back (then a setting to turn it off).

**ADR-96 A start of the supervisor goes on with the last supervisor's conversation, or starts a new one, as the user chooses.** (2026-10-27) `start-supervisor` takes `conversation`: `last` launches the newest supervisor again under its id with `--resume=<id>`, `new` starts a new one; the panel shows Resume and New while Copilot CLI keeps the newest supervisor's conversation (`SessionList.lastSupervisor`), otherwise "Start supervisor" (or "Start again") for a new one. `last-supervisor.json` in the state directory follows the newest supervisor the daemon lists (id, `hasConversation`) and keeps it once none is listed, so a supervisor the user stopped and closed, or one that was not running when the daemon stopped, can still go on. Start my day stays a new supervisor, because its first message is a briefing of the day and the user picks it to start afresh. Reasoning: ADR-95 brings back the supervisor that ran when the daemon stopped, but a supervisor the user stopped, or one that ended on its own, came back only as a new conversation, and the user lost what it knew about the day's work. Kept: the same id, so `ProposedBy.session` of its proposals stays its own; the ended session is closed just before the new start, since one id is listed once, so a start that fails loses its last screen. `procs.startTerminal` therefore accepts an owner whose group has ended, as `procs.start` does. Rejected: always resuming (a long conversation grows stale and costly, and the user wants a clean one sometimes) and a list of past supervisors to choose from (more UI for a case nobody asked for). What would reverse it: users who never pick New (then one button), or Copilot CLI refusing to resume an ended session.
**ADR-97 An update can wait until no agent is in the middle of a turn.** (2026-10-27) When an update is available and an agent is in the middle of a turn (a running session in `Working`, `Approval` or `Plan`), the strip and "Settings > Updates" offer "Update after their turn" and "Update now"; the first waits in the window where the user clicked it and installs once the session list shows no agent in a turn, "Cancel" takes it back. With no agent in a turn the offer stays "Update and restart". Reasoning: ADR-95 brings the sessions back after the restart, but a turn the restart cuts off is lost, and the user had to watch the agents to pick the moment. The UI decides, not the daemon or the shell: the session list with the states is already in every window, the turn rule is the one Close uses (`closeCutsOffTurn`), and the install is already the UI's call to the shell. An approval counts as a turn, because the restart would end it too, so a waiting update also waits for the user's answer; "Update now" is there for that. Kept small: the waiting lives in the window's memory, so reloading that window drops it, and the restart still ends the services; a check meanwhile moves the waiting to the newer version, because the shell installs only the update it found last. Two windows that both wait both call `install_update` when the last turn ends; the shell installs once and the second gets "already being installed". Rejected: waiting in the shell (it would need the turn rule in Rust or a new daemon field for a case the window already sees) and holding new prompts back while waiting (it would surprise a user typing into a session). What would reverse it: a hidden window that stops getting the session list, so the update never starts (then the shell or the daemon waits instead).

**ADR-98 A task proposal can name the Copilot CLI model its task runs on.** (2026-10-27) `propose_task` takes an optional `model`, a Copilot CLI model id (`TaskModel` in `protocol`: letters, digits, dots, dashes and underscores, up to 64 characters, such as `claude-opus-5.5` or `auto`). The proposal carries it, the user sees it in a field on the proposal and may change or clear it before "Start task" (`approve-proposal` takes `model`, `null` for the default), and the task keeps it (`Task.model`, in `tasks.json`, `null` in a task saved before). Every start of the task's session passes it as `--model=<id>`: the first one and each resume, because Copilot CLI resumes a conversation on its default model otherwise. The supervisor's instructions say to pass the model the user named for the work; a main session has the rule only in the tool's description, since it delegates less. Reasoning: the user asked the supervisor for a review on a stronger model; the supervisor could only write that into the prompt, and a prompt does not switch the agent's model, so the review ran on the default. Most tasks need no model (the user switches it in the task's session with `/model` when they want), so it stays optional. Trackwall checks only the shape of the id, not a list of models: the catalog depends on the Copilot CLI version and the user's account and changes often, so Copilot CLI judges the id itself in the task's terminal (what it does with an id outside the account's catalog no contract test covers: those run against a custom provider, which has no catalog). The shape check keeps an id from reading as another option. A contract test shows that the real Copilot CLI asks the model provider for the `--model` it was given, on the first turn and after a resume. Not now: a model in the "New task" form, changing a task's model after its start (a `/model` switch in its terminal lasts until the next resume), and a reasoning effort (`--reasoning-effort`). What would reverse it: Copilot CLI dropping `--model`, or a model setting per task kind in the workspace that makes picking per proposal unnecessary.

**ADR-100 A session can start without a worktree: in a repo's own working copy with a prompt, or as a free session in the projects folder.** (2026-10-27) The New session form gets "In its own worktree", checked by default: checked, a repo and a prompt make a task (ADR-31); unchecked, `start {repo, prompt?}` starts a main session in the repo root with the prompt as its first message (`--interactive`, so it works from the start and Copilot CLI keeps its conversation for a restart); the separate "Session in the repo, no task" button, which took no prompt, goes. The place choice also offers "No repo: the projects folder", a **free session** (`start {repo: null}`): Copilot CLI in the projects folder that is not the supervisor, `Session.supervisor: false` next to `repo: null`, since `repo: null` alone no longer names the supervisor. Without its own worktree the form says what the session gives up (no Changes tab, review, Verify or merge request) and warns when another running session works in the same folder (the main sessions of that repo's root, or for the projects folder the other free sessions), because two agents in one working copy change each other's files and branch. Reasoning: the user wanted one Copilot CLI to talk to without a repo, and a session in a repo without a new branch; the supervisor is one per Trackwall, coordinates, and by default may not change files (ADR-78), so it does not fill that place. A free session gets the rights of a main session, not more: what it changes in a repo goes through no review, which its instructions and the form say, and its delegated work is approved by the user like any other. It stays in the projects folder, so Copilot CLI's trusted folder and the antivirus exclusion of that folder cover it. Rejected: a free session in any folder the user picks (outside the projects folder an antivirus scans every file and Copilot CLI asks for trust again), and several supervisors (the supervisor panel and its delegated work assume one). What would reverse it: free sessions that keep changing repos without review, which would call for a worktree per repo they touch, or for a lock like the supervisor's.

## 13. Open questions

All questions from the vision were settled on 2026-10-23 (ADR-8, ADR-9, ADR-10).
